(Ibrāhīm) ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf was married to Umm al-Qāsim, daughter of Saʿd, then he married her sister, Umm Kulthūm, daughter of Saʿd. Thus he became Saʿd’s son-in-law twice.

(ʿAbd al-Raḥmān) ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿUtba ibn Nawfal ibn Uhayb was married to Umm ʿImrān, daughter of Saʿd. His brother (Ṭulayb) ibn Hāshim married her after him.

(ʿUthmān) ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf was married to Umm al-Ḥakam the Younger, daughter of Saʿd.

(Jābir) ibn al-Aswad ibn ʿAwf married her after him.

(Hāshim) ibn Hāshim ibn ʿUtba ibn Abī Waqqāṣ was married to Umm ʿAmr, daughter of Saʿd.

(ʿAbd al-Raḥmān) ibn ʿĀmir ibn Abī Waqqāṣ married her after him.

(ʿIyāḍ) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿIyāḍ ibn Thumāma ibn al-Aswad ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Muʿāwiya, an ally of Banū Umayya, was married to Hind, daughter of Saʿd.

(ʿAbd al-Raḥmān) ibn al-Aswad ibn ʿAbd Yaghūth was married to Ḥamīda, daughter of Saʿd.

(Jubayr) ibn Muṭʿim ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal was married to Umm ʿAmr, daughter of Saʿd.

(Sulaymān) ibn ʿĀmir ibn Abī Waqqāṣ married her after him.

(Muḥammad) ibn Jubayr ibn Muṭʿim was married to Umm Ayyūb, daughter of Saʿd.

(Hāshim) ibn ʿUtba ibn Abī Waqqāṣ was married to Umm Isḥāq, daughter of Saʿd. Then (ʿUthmān) ibn Ḥunayf married her. Then, after ʿUthmān, (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Abī Aḥmad ibn Jaḥsh ibn Riʾāb al-Asadī married her.

(ʿAbd al-Raḥmān) ibn ʿĀmir ibn Abī Waqqāṣ was married to Ramla, daughter of Saʿd.

(Suhayl) ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf was married to ʿAmra, daughter of Saʿd.

The Sons-in-law of Saʿīd ibn Zayd ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl

(Al-Mundhir) ibn al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām had ʿĀtika, daughter of Saʿīd, as his wife.

(ʿAbd al-Raḥmān) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥārith al-Murādī, an ally of Banū ʿAdī, was married to Umm Ḥasan, daughter of Saʿīd.

(ʿAbd al-Raḥmān) ibn Ḥuwayṭib ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, brother of Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, was married to Umm Ḥabīb the Elder, daughter of Saʿīd.

(ʿAbd al-Raḥmān) ibn Abī Sufyān ibn Ḥuwayṭib married Umm Ḥabīb after his paternal uncle.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd ibn al-Khaṭṭāb was married to Umm Zayd the Elder, daughter of Saʿīd.

(Al-Mukhtār) ibn Abī ʿUbayd ibn Masʿūd was married to Umm Zayd the Younger, daughter of Saʿīd.

(ʿĀṣim) ibn al-Mundhir ibn al-Zubayr was married to Umm ʿAbd, daughter of Saʿīd.

An Account of the Prophet’s Establishment of Brotherhood

Among his Companions who had emigrated, before the Hijra. He established brotherhood among them on the basis of truth and mutual support, and this was in Mecca.

The Prophet ﷺ established brotherhood between himself and ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁. He also established it between Ḥamza ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ﵀ and Zayd ibn Ḥāritha, the client of the Messenger of God ﷺ. / For this reason, Zayd disputed with ʿAlī and Jaʿfar, the two sons of Abū Ṭālib, may God have mercy on them, over Ḥamza’s daughter, and the Messenger of God gave her to The Prophet ﷺ sent [her] to Jaʿfar because her maternal aunt, Asmāʾ bt. ʿUmays, was with Jaʿfar. He made Abū Bakr and ʿUmar brothers ﵏; ʿUthmān b. ʿAffān and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. ʿAwf; al-Zubayr b. al-ʿAwwām and ʿAbd Allāh b. Masʿūd; ʿUbayda b. al-Ḥārith b. al-Muṭṭalib and Bilāl, the freedman of Abū Bakr; Muṣʿab b. ʿUmayr and Saʿd b. Abī Waqqāṣ; Abū ʿUbayda b. al-Jarrāḥ and Sālim, the freedman of Abū Ḥudhayfa; and Saʿīd b. Zayd and Ṭalḥa b. ʿUbayd Allāh.

This was the brotherhood established at Mecca.

When the Prophet ﷺ arrived in Medina, he made the Emigrants and the Helpers brothers, on the basis of truth and mutual support, and so that they would inherit from one another after death, to the exclusion of their blood relatives. None of those who had been paired in brotherhood died before Sūrat al-Anfāl was revealed; thereafter, inheritance went to blood relatives rather than to brothers by covenant.

He made ʿAlī b. Abī Ṭālib ﵁ and Sahl b. Ḥunayf brothers; Zayd b. Ḥāritha and Usayd b. Ḥuḍayr; Abū Marthad al-Ghanawī, the ally of Ḥamza b. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, and ʿUbāda b. al-Ṣāmit; Marthad b. Abī Marthad and Aws b. al-Ṣāmit; ʿUbayda b. al-Ḥārith b. al-Muṭṭalib, who was martyred at Badr, and ʿUmayr b. al-Ḥumām al-Sulamī; and al-Ṭufayl b. al-Ḥārith b. al-Muṭṭalib and al-Mundhir b. Muḥammad b. ʿUqba b. Uḥayḥa b. al-Jallāḥ; al-Ḥuṣayn b. al-Ḥārith b. al-Muṭṭalib and Rāfiʿ b. ʿUnjada; ʿUthmān b. ʿAffān ﵀ and Aws b. Thābit—though it is said that it was Abū [reading uncertain: Abī?] ʿUbāda Saʿd b. ʿUthmān al-Zuraqī; Shujāʿ b. Wahb and Aws b. Khawli; ʿAbd Allāh b. Jaḥsh al-Asadī and ʿĀṣim b. Thābit b. Abī al-Aqlaḥ, whose name was Qays; Muḥriz b. Naḍla and ʿAmāra b. Ḥazm, who was killed at al-Yamāma; Abū Ḥudhayfa b. ʿUtba b. Rabīʿa b. ʿAbd Shams and ʿAbbād b. Bishr b. Waqsh; Sālim, the freedman of Abū Ḥudhayfa, and Muʿādh b. Māʿiṣ; ʿUtba b. Ghazwān and Abū Dujāna Simāk b. Kharasha; Saʿd, the freedman of ʿUtba, and Tamīm, the freedman of Khurāsh b. al-Ṣimma; Ṭulayb b. ʿUmayr b. Wahb and al-Mundhir b. ʿAmr; al-Zubayr b. al-ʿAwwām and Kaʿb b. Mālik; Ḥāṭib b. Abī Baltaʿa and Rukhayla b. Yakhlad; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. ʿAwf and Saʿd b. al-Rabīʿ; Saʿd b. Abī Waqqāṣ and Saʿd b. Muʿādh; ʿAbd Allāh b. Masʿūd and Muʿādh b. Jabal; ʿUmayr b. [ʿAbd] ʿAmr b. Naḍla al-Khuzāʿī, who was called Hūdh, and al-Shimālayn, an ally of Banū Zuhra who was killed at Badr; and Yazīd b. al-Ḥārith, who was called Ibn Fasḥam, who was killed at Badr; Khabbāb b. al-Aratt and Jabbār b. Ṣakhr; al-Miqdād b. ʿAmr and Jabr b. ʿAtīk; ʿUmayr b. Abī Waqqāṣ, who was killed at Badr, and ʿAmr b. Muʿādh, the brother of Saʿd b. Muʿādh; Masʿūd b. Rabīʿ al-Qārī and ʿUbayd b. al-Tayyihān; Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq ﵀ and Khārija b. Zayd b. Abī Zuhayr; Ṭalḥa b. ʿUbayd Allāh and Ubayy b. Kaʿb; Bilāl, the freedman of Abū Bakr, and Abū Zurʿa al-Khathʿamī; ʿĀmir b. Fuhayra and al-Ḥārith b. Aws b. Muʿādh; Ṣuhayb b. Sinān and al-Ḥārith b. al-Ṣimma; Abū Salama b. ʿAbd al-Asad and Saʿd b. Khaythama, who was killed at Badr; Shammās b. ʿUthmān b. al-Sharīd and Ḥanẓala b. Abī ʿĀmir; al-Arqam b. Abī al-Arqam and Abū Ṭalḥa Zayd b. Sahl; ʿAmmār b. Yāsir and Ḥudhayfa b. al-Yamān; Muʿattib b. Ḥamrāʾ al-Khuzāʿī and Thaʿlaba b. Ḥāṭib; ʿUmar b. al-Khaṭṭāb ﵀ and ʿUwaym b. Sāʿida—though it is said that it was ʿUmar b. al-Khaṭṭāb and Muʿādh b. ʿAfrāʾ, and it is also said that it was ʿUmar and ʿItbān b. Mālik; Zayd b. al-Khaṭṭāb and Maʿn b. ʿAdī, both of whom were killed at al-Yamāma; Wāqid b. ʿAbd Allāh al-Tamīmī—or Ḥiṣn, an ally of Banū ʿAdī—and Bishr b. al-Barāʾ b. Maʿrūr; and ʿĀmir b. Rabīʿa al-ʿAnazī, an ally of al-Khaṭṭāb, and Yazīd b. al-Mundhir b. Sarḥ; and Saʿīd b. Zayd b. ʿAmr b. Nufayl and Rāfiʿ b. Mālik; ʿĀqil b. Abī al-Bukayr, who was killed at Badr; Mubashshir b. ʿAbd al-Mundhir, who was killed at Badr—though it is said that it was rather al-Mujadhdhar b. Dhiyād; ʿĀmir b. Abī al-Bukayr and Thābit b. Qays b. Shammās; Khālid b. Abī al-Bukayr and Zayd b. al-Dathinna; Iyās b. Abī al-Bukayr and al-Ḥārith b. Khazma; ʿUthmān b. Maẓʿūn and Abū al-Haytham b. al-Tayyihān; ʿAbd Allāh b. Maẓʿūn and Sahl b. ʿUbayd b. al-Muʿallā; al-Sāʾib b. ʿUthmān and Ḥāritha b. Surāqa, who was killed at Badr; Muʿammar b. al-Ḥārith and Muʿādh b. ʿAfrāʾ; Khunays b. Ḥudhāfa and Abū ʿAbs b. Jabr; ʿAbd Allāh b. Makhrama b. ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā b. Abī Qays and Farwa b. ʿAmr; and Abū Sabra b. Abī Ruhm and Salama b. Salāma b. Waqsh; Wahb b. Sarḥ and Suwayd b. ʿAmr, both of whom were killed at Muʾta; Abū ʿUbayda b. al-Jarrāḥ and Muḥammad b. Maslama; Ṣafwān b. Bayḍāʾ, who was killed at Badr, and Rāfiʿ b. al-Muʿallā, who was killed at Badr; and Salmān al-Fārisī and Abū al-Dardāʾ.

The brotherhood was established before Badr, and none was established after Badr.

The Prophet’s messengers to kings and notables

The Prophet ﷺ sent Jarīr b. ʿAbd Allāh al-Bajalī to Dhū al-Kalāʿ and Dhū ʿAmr, in Yemen. Dhū ʿAmr was a Jew. He said to Jarīr, “If your companion is truthful, then he has died today. For I find in our books that the last prophet on earth will die on this day of this month.”

They recorded the day. Then caravans arrived bearing news of the Prophet’s death, saying that he had died on that very day. The two men accepted Islam.

Dhū al-Kalāʿ came as a delegate to ʿUmar, who sent him on a military expedition to Syria. He remained there until he was killed at Ṣiffīn, fighting alongside Muʿāwiya.

The Prophet ﷺ sent Diḥya b. Khalīfa al-Kalbī to Caesar, the great ruler of the Romans. Caesar took the Prophet’s letter and placed it against his side.

Diḥya reached him, and he said, “Were he in my land, I would follow him and support him.”

The Prophet ﷺ sent Shujāʿ b. Wahb al-Asadī to Jabala b. al-Ayham al-Ghassānī.

Ḥāṭib b. Abī Baltaʿa, an ally of Banū Asad b. ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, was sent to al-Muqawqis, the ruler of Alexandria. He received him hospitably and gave him a cordial welcome. He sent the Prophet ﷺ Māriya, the mother of Ibrāhīm; her sister, the mother of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. Ḥassān b. Thābit al-Anṣārī; and his mule and donkey.

ʿAmr b. Umayya al-Ḍamrī was sent to the Negus, whose name was Aṣḥama.

He married Umm Ḥabība bt. Abī Sufyān b. Ḥarb to him. She had emigrated with her husband, ʿUbayd Allāh b. Jaḥsh, who became a Christian and died as a Christian; God preserved her, and she held fast to Islam. The Negus paid her dowry on behalf of the Prophet ﷺ: four hundred dinars. He also sent him clothing—a shirt, trousers, a turban, and a cloak made of Sūwānī cloth, from a village called Sūwān, the last town in Egypt within the Muslim lands, adjoining the route to Nubia—and two plain leather socks. The Prophet ﷺ performed ablution and wiped over them.

Sulayṭ b. Qays, of Banū ʿĀmir b. Luʾayy, was sent to the people of al-Yamāma.

Al-ʿAlāʾ al-Ḥaḍramī, an ally of Banū Umayya, was sent to the people of Bahrain.

They accepted Islam and sent their land tax. The first revenue to arrive in Medina was the land tax from Bahrain: seventy thousand.

ʿAmr b. al-ʿĀṣ al-Sahmī was sent to Jayfar and ʿAbd, the sons of al-Julundā al-Azdī, in ʿUmān. They accepted Islam and took control of ʿUmān.

ʿAbd Allāh b. Ḥudhāfa al-Sahmī was sent to Kisrā b. Hurmuz.

When he read the Prophet’s letter, he said, “He began with himself before me,” and tore his letter into strips. The Prophet ﷺ said, “May God utterly shatter the kingdom of Persia.” They did not prosper after his prayer.

The wives of the Messenger of God ﷺ

The first of them was Khadīja bt. Khuwaylid b. Asad b. ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā b. Quṣayy b. Kilāb. Her mother was Fāṭima bt. Zāʾida b. al-Aṣamm b. Hirm b. Rawāḥa b. Ḥujr b. ʿAbd b. Muʿayṣ b. ʿĀmir b. Luʾayy. The reason he married her was that when Maysara, Khadīja’s servant, returned from his journey with the Prophet ﷺ they stopped beneath a tree. A monk saw him and said to Maysara, “Who is this man with you?” He replied, “He is one of my people.” The monk said, “He is a prophet. By God, no one other than he has sat in this place since Jesus son of Mary.” Maysara returned to Khadīja and told her what the monk had said. He also said to her, “I used to eat with him until we were full, and the food remained as it was.” Khadīja called for a bowl containing fresh dates, and summoned her sister Hāla, the mother of Abū al-ʿĀṣ b. al-Rabīʿ b. ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā b. ʿAbd Shams. She invited the Prophet ﷺ and they all ate until full, yet nothing was diminished. She said to him ﷺ “Ask my uncle ʿAmr b. Asad to marry me to you.” He was an old man, and by then no one remained of Asad’s direct descendants but him. So the Prophet went to her, accompanied by Ḥamza. She slaughtered a sheep and prepared food for him. Then she sent for ʿAmr, who ate, and then gave him a drink. When the drink began to take effect, Khadīja said to the Prophet ﷺ

“Tell your uncle Abū Ṭālib to ask him to marry me to you while we are here.” Abū Ṭālib came to him and asked for her hand. When the effect of the drink had worn off, ʿAmr heard voices and asked, “What is this?” They told him. He said to Khadīja, “You deceived me.” She replied, “Sir, by God, he is your equal, so complete the matter for him.” He did so. Before her marriage to the Prophet ﷺ she had been married to Abū Hāla Hind b. al-Nabbāsh b. Zurāra b. Waqadān4 b. Ḥabīb b. Salāma b. Ghawī b. Jarwa b. Usayd b. ʿAmr b. Tamīm. She bore him Hind b.

Abū Hāla.

After Abū Hāla, ʿAtīq b. ʿĀʾidh b. ʿAbd Allāh b. ʿUmar b. Makhzūm married her. She bore him a daughter called Hind. Hind married Ṣayfī b. Umayya b. ʿĀʾidh b. ʿAbd Allāh b. ʿUmar b. Makhzūm, and bore him Muḥammad. The descendants of Muḥammad b. Ṣayfī in Medina are called “the Banū al-Ṭāhir.” Khadīja had been pledged in marriage to Waraqa b. Nawfal b. Asad. She bore the Prophet ﷺ al-Qāsim, Zaynab, Umm Kulthūm, Fāṭima, and ʿAbd Allāh—also called al-Ṭāhir and al-Ṭayyib, which are two names for the same person.

At the time he married her, he was twenty-five years old, and she was forty. It is also said that he was twenty-three and Khadīja was twenty-eight. Her dowry was twelve ūqiyya. The dowries of his other wives were the same. She died in Mecca before the Hijra ﵂.

Then the Prophet ﷺ married Sauda bt. Zamʿa b. Qays b. ʿAbd Shams b. ʿAbd Wudd b. Naṣr b. Mālik b. Ḥisl b. ʿĀmir b. Luʾayy b. Ghālib. Her mother was al-Shamūs bt. Qays b. Zayd b. ʿAmr b. Lubayd b. Khudāsh b. ʿĀmir b. Ghanm b. ʿAdī b. al-Najjār, who was Taym Allāh b. Thaʿlaba b. ʿAmr b. al-Khazraj b. Ḥāritha. She was married to him in Mecca. Before the Prophet ﷺ she had been married to al-Sakrān b. ʿAmr b. ʿAbd Shams b. ʿAbd Wudd. Al-Sakrān was the brother of Suhayl b. ʿAmr. She had seen in a dream the Prophet ﷺ walking toward her until he stepped on her neck. She told her husband, who said, “By your father, if your dream is true, I shall die and Muḥammad will marry you.” Then, another night, she saw in a dream what seemed to be a moon falling down upon her from the sky as she lay on her side.

She told her husband, who said, “By your father, I have but a little while to live. Then, after I die, you will marry.” Al-Sakrān fell ill that very day and died shortly thereafter. The Prophet ﷺ married her and then divorced her with a single pronouncement, for she had grown old. When the news reached her, she gathered her clothes and sat on the path he took when going out to pray. As he approached, she wept and said, “Messenger of God, have I offended you in Islam?” He replied, “God forbid.” She said, “I beg you to take me back.” He took her back. She said to him, “Messenger of God, I give my day to ʿĀʾisha, to please you, so that I may look upon your face. By God, I have no desire for what women desire; I only wish that God raise me among your wives on the Day of Resurrection.” She cared for his child ﷺ.

Then the Prophet ﷺ asked for the hand of ʿĀʾisha, daughter of Abū Bakr b. Abī Quḥāfa—whose name was ʿUthmān b. ʿĀmir b. ʿAmr b. Kaʿb b. Saʿd b. Taym b. Murra b. Kaʿb.

Her mother was Umm Rūmān bt. ʿUmar b. ʿĀmir, of Banū Mālik of Kināna, in Mecca.

Abū Bakr said to him, “Messenger of God, I had already mentioned her—or promised her—to Muṭʿim ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf, for his son Jubayr ibn Muṭʿim. Let me go and release her from them.” He did so. The Messenger of God married her when she was seven years old, in Shawwāl. When the Prophet wanted to consummate the marriage with her, her mother, Umm Rūmān, went out to her while she was playing with the girls among the palm trees. She took her by the hand and brought her in to the Prophet in Shawwāl, a year after his arrival in Medina, when she was nine years old. The Prophet died when she was eighteen years old. She died in the year 57.

Al-Wāqidī said: “In the year 58, on the night of Tuesday, when seventeen nights of Ramaḍān had passed. She was buried that same night, after the witr prayer. Abū Hurayra, who was Marwān’s deputy over Medina, performed the prayer over her.”

Then the Prophet married (Ghāziya), daughter of Dūdān ibn ʿAwf ibn Jābir ibn Ḍibāb ibn Ḥujr ibn ʿAbd ibn Muʿāwiya ibn ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy.

Before him she had been married to Abū al-ʿAkr ibn Sumayy ibn al-Ḥārith al-Azdī ibn Maydaʿān ibn Mālik ibn Naṣr ibn al-Azd. She bore him Sharīk ibn Abī al-ʿAkr, and so she was given that kunya. She was the woman who offered herself to the Prophet. She would visit the women of Quraysh, calling them to Islam and encouraging them in it. Her affair became known to the people of Mecca, and they seized her and said, “Were it not for your people, we would have killed you. But we will return you to them.” They were people of the desert. She said: “They put me on a camel with no saddle and left me without food or water.

“I remained like that for three days. When the third day came, there was nothing on earth that I could hear.

“They camped at a place, bound me, and threw me into the sun. While I was like that, I suddenly felt something cool on my chest. I reached for it and drank from it. This happened several times, until I was quenched; I poured the remainder over my body and clothes. When they awoke, they saw the trace of water on me and saw that I looked well. They said, ‘Have you recovered and taken the water-skin and drunk from it?’ I said, ‘I did not. Rather, this is what happened.’ They said, ‘If you are telling the truth, your religion is better than ours.’ They looked at their water-skins and found them as they had left them. Then they embraced Islam and let her go. She came to the Prophet and told him what had happened, and offered herself to him. He accepted her and consummated the marriage, but saw that she was advanced in age, so he divorced her.

“These, then, are the wives whom the Prophet married in Mecca.”

She would visit the women of Quraysh and call them to Islam, encouraging them in it. Her affair became known to the people of Mecca, and they seized her and said, “Were it not for your people, we would have killed you. But we will return you to them.” They were people of the desert. She said: “They put me on a camel with no saddle and left me without food or water.

“I remained like that for three days. When the third day came, there was nothing on earth that I could hear.

“They camped at a place, bound me, and threw me into the sun. While I was like that, I suddenly felt something cool on my chest. I reached for it and drank from it. This happened several times, until I was quenched; I poured the remainder over my body and clothes. When they awoke, they saw the trace of water on me and saw that I looked well. They said, ‘Have you recovered and taken the water-skin and drunk from it?’ I said, ‘I did not. Rather, this is what happened.’ They said, ‘If you are telling the truth, your religion is better than ours.’ They looked at their water-skins and found them as they had left them. Then they embraced Islam and let her go. She came to the Prophet and told him what had happened, and offered herself to him. He accepted her and consummated the marriage, but saw that she was advanced in age, so he divorced her.

“These, then, are the wives whom the Prophet married in Mecca.”

Then the Prophet married (Ḥafṣa), daughter of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ibn Nufayl ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Riyāḥ ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qurṭ ibn Razāḥ ibn ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb ibn Luʾayy. Her mother was Zaynab bt. Maẓʿūn ibn Ḥabīb ibn Wahb ibn Ḥudhāfa ibn Jumāḥ. Before him she had been married to Khunays ibn Ḥudhāfa ibn Qays ibn ʿAdī ibn Saʿd ibn Sahm. She was among those who migrated to Abyssinia.

She died in the year 45.

Then the Prophet married (Zaynab), daughter of Khuzayma ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Hilāl ibn ʿĀmir ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿa. She was “the Mother of the Poor”; she had been called by that name in the Jāhiliyya. Before the Prophet, she had been married to Ṭufayl ibn al-Ḥārith [ibn al-Muṭṭalib], son of ʿAbd Manāf. Then his brother ʿUbayda ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Muṭṭalib, who was martyred at Badr, married her. ʿUbayda gave her to the Prophet. Of all his wives, none died while married to him except her and Khadīja ﵂.

Then the Prophet married (Umm Salama), whose name was Hind, daughter of Abū Umayya ibn al-Mughīra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. Her mother was ʿĀtika bt. ʿĀmir ibn Rabīʿa ibn Mālik ibn Judhayma ibn ʿAlqama, “the Spear-Brandisher” of the Banū Firās, ibn Ghanm ibn Mālik ibn Kināna. The reason for his marrying her was that when her husband, Abū Salama ibn ʿAbd al-Asad ibn Hilāl ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm, migrated, she had by him Salama, ʿUmar, Zaynab, and Durra. The Banū al-Mughīra rose up and kept her from migrating with her husband. The Banū ʿAbd al-Asad said, “We will take ʿUmar ibn Abī Salama, and he will stay with us.” The Banū al-Mughīra refused to let him be anywhere but with his mother, until conflict broke out between them. They tugged at the boy until they dislocated his hand—or his shoulder. It remained dislocated until he died. Every morning Umm Salama would go out, sit on al-Ṣafā, face the qibla, and say:

“O stone, O stone, stand firm;

Then let Hilāl and his sons be crushed.”

She meant the Banū Hilāl ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. When the Banū al-Mughīra saw this, they let her go. She came to Medina. Abū Salama was killed, leaving her a widow. Umm Salama used to say: “The Prophet would say, ‘There is no one who suffers a calamity and says, “O God, reward me in my affliction, grant me patience through it, and replace it for me with something better,” but God will do that for him.’” She said: “When Abū Salama died, I prayed to God with these supplications. Whenever I said them, I would think, ‘And who could be better than Abū Salama?’” The Messenger of God proposed to her, though she was advanced in age. She said, “Messenger of God, I am a bereaved woman, I am extremely jealous, and I am advanced in age.” He said to her, “As for the children you mentioned, do not fear that they will suffer want while God and His Messenger are with them. As for jealousy, I shall pray to God to take it away from you. As for your age, I am older than you.” The Prophet married her. She was the first woman to arrive in Medina as a migrant. He married her in Shawwāl and consummated the marriage in Shawwāl. She died ﵂ in the year 61. Abū Hurayra performed the prayer over her; the governor was al-Walīd ibn ʿUtba ibn Abī Sufyān. He buried her in al-Baqīʿ.

Then the Prophet married (Zaynab), daughter of Jaḥsh ibn Riʾāb ibn Yaʿmar ibn Ṣabra ibn Murra ibn Kabīr ibn Ghanm ibn Dūdān ibn Asad ibn Khuzayma.

Her mother was Umayma bt. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim. Before him she had been married to Zayd ibn Ḥāritha al-Kalbī, the client of the Messenger of God. The reason for his marrying her was that one day the Prophet came to Zayd, stopped at his door, and called him. He saw Zaynab wearing a shirt of hers dyed with saffron. She entered his heart. He said, “Glory be to the Turner of hearts,” three times. Zayd heard him while he was performing ablutions, and realized that she had entered his heart. Zayd went out to the Prophet. Some days later he said, “Messenger of God, I will divorce Zaynab.”

He said, “Why?” Zayd said, “Her character has become bad, and she has hurt me with her tongue.” He said, “Fear God and keep your wife.” Then Zayd divorced her. The Prophet married her.

Concerning her, God ﵎ revealed: “And when you said to the one whom God had favored and whom you had favored: ‘Keep your wife and fear God,’ while concealing within yourself what God was to disclose; and fearing the people, though God has more right that you should fear Him” (33:37), to the end of the verse. Her marriage was one of the distinctions of the Banū Asad. Zaynab used to boast over her companions, saying, “Your guardians married you off, but God, Mighty and Exalted, married me.” Among the distinctions of the Banū Asad were that Zaynab married and that God, Glorious and Exalted, gave her in marriage. The emissary was Gabriel ﵇ and ʿUkkāsha ibn Miḥṣan, whose sword broke. The Prophet gave him a piece of wood, which became a blade in his hand, and he fought with it. The Prophet prayed that God would admit him to Paradise and make his face like the full moon.

He would walk at a brisk pace. He is one of the people of Paradise, of whom there is no doubt. The first spoils taken in Islam were the spoils of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaḥsh al-Asadī, when he killed ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥaḍramī. The first to allot the Messenger of God a fifth of the spoils was ʿAbd Allāh, before the Qurʾān concerning it was revealed. The first to be called Commander of the Believers was ʿAbd Allāh. The first banner the Prophet raised was ʿAbd Allāh’s banner, and the first who pledged allegiance at the Pledge of Good Pleasure was Abū Sinān ibn Wahb. He came to the Prophet and said, “I pledge allegiance to you.” He said, “On what do you pledge allegiance to me?” He said, “On what is in your mind.” He said, “And what is in my mind?” He said, “Conquest or martyrdom.” The Prophet accepted his pledge. Then the people came and began pledging allegiance according to Abū Sinān’s pledge. On the day of Badr there were seven of the Emigrants: the Emigrants numbered seventy-seven, and the Banū Asad were eleven.

They embraced Islam willingly before the cavalry of the Messenger of God reached them.

It was concerning them that this was revealed: “They claim it as a favor to you that they have submitted. Say: ‘Do not claim your submission as a favor to me. Rather, God has favored you by guiding you to faith, if you are truthful’” (49:17). And the Prophet said, “Asad has the most eloquent orators among the Arabs.” He also said, “Among us is the finest horseman among the Arabs: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaḥsh.” ʿUkkāsha ibn Miḥṣan said to him, “Messenger of God, he is one of us.” He said, “Rather, he is one of us,” meaning that he was his ally through his alliance with the Banū ʿAbd Shams. Among them was Ḍirār ibn al-Azwar, the brother of the Banū Mālik. He came as a delegation to the Prophet, pledged allegiance to him, and embraced Islam. He said:

“I have cast aside the divining arrows and the singing-girls’ music,

and wine, its kindling and its invocation;

“O Lord, may my bargain not prove a loss:

I have sold my family and my wealth for a small price.

And my splendid mount amid the tumult,

and my striving in battle against the polytheists.”

The Prophet said, “May God not make your bargain a loss, Ḍirār.” The Messenger of God also said, “I shall give the banner to a man more steadfast than you in hunger and thirst.” He gave it to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaḥsh. He said, “Messenger of God, should I carry it, though I am a young boy?” He said to him, “Go.” He went, and God granted him victory. Zaynab died in the year 20. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵀ performed the prayer over her and had a tent pitched over her grave.

Then the Prophet married (Umm Ḥabība), daughter of Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb ibn Umayya. Her mother was Ṣafiyya bt. Abī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayya ibn ʿAbd Shams.

Before him she had been married to ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Jaḥsh ibn Riʾāb. She bore him a daughter whom she named Ḥabība, and after her Umm Ḥabība received her kunya. Ḥabība married Dāwūd ibn ʿUrwa ibn Masʿūd ibn Muʿattib al-Thaqafī. ʿUbayd Allāh had migrated with her to Abyssinia, where she was with him. After embracing Islam, ʿUbayd Allāh became a Christian and died, leaving her a widow. The Messenger of God sent ʿAmr ibn Umayya al-Ḍamrī to Abyssinia, and he married her to him. This was when Mecca was conquered, and the verse had been revealed to him: “Perhaps God will bring affection between you and those among them whom you have treated as enemies” (60:7). The affection was the Messenger of God’s marriage to Umm Ḥabība bt. Abī Sufyān. Abū Sufyān was favorably disposed toward the Messenger of God.

That, then, was the affection. He said: “When the Prophet proposed to Umm Ḥabība, the Negus summoned the Companions of the Prophet who were with him and said, ‘Which of you is most closely related to this woman?’ Khālid ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayya said, ‘I am the closest of them to her.’ He said, ‘Then marry her to your Prophet.’” He married her to him. The Negus paid the dower for him: four hundred dinars. Then she was brought to the Prophet. She died, may God have mercy on her, in the year 59.

Then the Prophet married (Juwayriyya), whose name was Barra, daughter of al-Ḥārith ibn Abī Ḍirār ibn Ḥabīb ibn ʿĀʾidh ibn Mālik ibn Judhayma al-Muṣṭaliqī of Khuzāʿa. Before him she had been married to a paternal cousin of hers called Musāfiʿ ibn Ṣafwān ibn Dhī al-Shafr ibn Abī Sarḥ ibn Mālik ibn Judhayma al-Muṣṭaliqī. The reason for his marrying her was that the Prophet captured her in the expedition against the Banū al-Muṣṭaliq at al-Muraysīʿ. She became the property of Thābit ibn Qays ibn Shammās and one of his paternal cousins. They made a contract of manumission with her. She came to the Prophet to ask him to help with her contract. She entered upon ʿĀʾisha while the Messenger of God was inside. ʿĀʾisha said, “What I saw of her displeased me, and I knew that the Messenger of God would see in her what I had seen.” The Prophet came out, and she said to him, “Messenger of God, I am the daughter of the chief of his people, al-Ḥārith ibn Ḍirār.

“What you have heard has happened to me, and I have come to the state you know. Help me with my contract, and may God help you.” He said to her, “Would you prefer something better? I will buy you, set you free, and marry you.”

She said, “Yes.” The Prophet married her. There was no woman from among the captives of the Banū al-Muṣṭaliq held by any of the Prophet’s Companions who was not set free. They said, “The Messenger of God has married into their clan, so it is not right for us to keep them.” ʿĀʾisha said, “I have never seen a woman who brought greater blessing to the women of her people than she did.” Juwayriyya died in the year 57. Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam performed the prayer over her.

Then the Prophet married (Ṣafiyya), daughter of Ḥuyayy ibn Akhṭab ibn Shuʿya ibn Thaʿlaba ibn ʿUbayd ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Khazraj ibn Ḥabīb ibn al-Naḍr ibn al-Naḥḥām ibn Yanḥūm, of the Children of Israel, from the tribe of Aaron ﷺ. Her mother was Barra bt. Samawʾal. Before him she had been married to Sallām ibn Mishkam al-Quraẓī. Then Kināna ibn al-Rabīʿ ibn Abī al-Ḥuqayq married her. The reason for his marrying her was that the Prophet took her captive on the day of Khaybar. With her was a female cousin of hers, whom he gave to Diḥya ibn Khalīfa al-Kalbī. The Messenger of God kept Ṣafiyya for himself and saw a green mark on her face. He said, “What is this on your face?” She said, “I saw a dream before your arrival.

“By God, I remember nothing about you. I told my husband, Kināna ibn al-Rabīʿ, of it, and he slapped my face and said, ‘You are only wishing for that king in Medina.’” He said— The Prophet ﷺ said to her, “And what did you see?” She said, “I saw the moon descend from its place and fall into my lap.” Her vision pleased the Prophet ﷺ. When Banū Qurayẓa were killed, he ordered Bilāl to bring Ṣafiyya to him. Bilāl brought her, passing her by the slain. The Prophet ﷺ said to him, “Bilāl, has compassion departed from you? Do you take her past the slain?” Then he drew the veil over her on the camel. They knew that he had married her. Ṣafiyya died in the year fifty.

Then he married (Maymūna) bt. al-Ḥārith b. Ḥazn b. Bujayr b. al-Hazm b. Ruwayba b. ʿAbd Allāh b. Hilāl b. ʿĀmir b. Ṣaʿṣaʿa. Her mother was Hind bt. ʿAwf b. Zuhayr b. al-Ḥārith b. Ḥamāṭa, from Jarash, of the family of Ḥimyar.

Before him, she had been married to Abū Ruhm b. ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā b. Abī Qays. She was the full sister of Umm al-Faḍl, the wife of al-ʿAbbās b. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. The Prophet ﷺ married her after Zaynab bt. Khuzayma had died. While he ﷺ was sitting, Ḥuwayṭib b. ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā b. Abī Qays came to him and said, “Muḥammad, your allotted time has passed. Leave our land.” Saʿd b. Zayd al-Anṣārī said to him:

“You lie! It is not your land, but his land and the land of his forefathers.” He ﷺ said to him, “Easy, Saʿd! Do not be rude to our visitors. Ḥuwayṭib, what harm is there in our staying among you, so that we eat and you eat?”

He said, “I adjure you to leave!” So he left, and they left. He left Abū Rāfiʿ behind and said, “Bring me to Maymūna.” Abū Rāfiʿ said: He seated her on a she-camel. The people of Mecca made it shy away, saying to her, “May God not bless you,” until he reached her at Sarif. This was during the truce, when Mecca had been vacated for the Messenger of God ﷺ for three days. Maymūna later died in Mecca. ʿAbd Allāh b. ʿAbbās carried her, saying to those carrying her, “Be gentle with her, for she is your mother.” He buried her at Sarif, one day’s journey from Mecca toward Medina.

These are his wives ﷺ ﵃ whom he consummated marriage with.

He divorced Ghaziyya, Umm Sharīk. He intended to divorce Sawda, but she asked him not to, so he kept her and she gave her day with him to ʿĀʾisha. The Prophet ﷺ intended to part from some of his wives, but they said, “Messenger of God, do not part from us. Give us of yourself and your property whatever you wish.” Then God, Almighty and Glorious, revealed: “You may defer whichever of them you will and take to yourself whichever you will.” [Qurʾān 33:51] He deferred Sawda, Ṣafiyya, Juwayriyya, Umm Ḥabība, and Maymūna. He would allot them of himself and his property whatever he wished. Those whom he kept close were ʿĀʾisha, Ḥafṣa, Zaynab, and Umm Salama.

He married (Khawla) bt. al-Hudhayl b. Hubayra b. Qubayṣa b. al-Ḥārith b. Ḥirfa b. Thaʿlaba b. Bakr b. Ḥubayb b. ʿAmr b. Ghanm b. Taghlib. Her mother was bt. Khalīfa b. Faḍāla b. Zayd b. Imruʾ al-Qays b. al-Khazraj al-Kalbī, the sister of Diḥya al-Kalbī. She died on the journey.

He married (Sharāf), the sister of Diḥya. Her mother was Laylā bt. Khālid b. Jābir b. Ḥāritha b. al-ʿUbayd, of Kalb. She died.

He married (al-ʿĀliya) bt. Ẓabyān b. ʿAmr b. ʿAwf b. ʿAbd b. Abī Bakr b. Kilāb. She remained with him as long as God willed; then he divorced her.

He married (Sanā) bt. al-Ṣalt b. Ḥabīb b. Ḥāritha b. Hilāl b. Ḥarām b. Simāk b. ʿAwf al-Sulamī. She was the paternal aunt of ʿAbd Allāh b. Khāzim al-Sulamī. She died before he reached her.

When the Prophet ﷺ took Banū Qurayẓa captive, he sent Saʿd b. Zayd al-Ashhalī with women from among them to Najd to sell them. The Prophet ﷺ kept (Rayḥāna) bt. Shamʿūn b. Zayd b. Khunāfa, of Banū ʿAmr b. Qurayẓa. She had been married to a paternal cousin of hers called ʿAbd al-Ḥakam. The Prophet ﷺ had invited her to accept Islam, but she insisted on remaining Jewish. This distressed him; he withdrew from her and was troubled. While he was sitting with his Companions, he heard the sound of two sandals. He said, “That is Thaʿlaba b. Saʿya al-Quraẓī, bringing me the good news that Rayḥāna has accepted Islam.” And so it was.

He ﷺ offered to marry her and veil her. She said to him, “Rather, leave me in your possession.” She remained in his possession until he died ﷺ. Her dwelling was in al-ʿĀliya.

He married (Asmāʾ) bt. al-Nuʿmān b. al-Aswad b. al-Ḥārith b. Shurāḥīl b. Kinda b. al-Jawn. She was among the most beautiful of his wives and the most like them. When the Prophet ﷺ began marrying women from distant tribes, ʿĀʾisha said, “He has turned his attention to women from distant tribes. They are likely to turn his face away from us.” He had asked for her hand when Kinda came to him as a delegation. When his wives saw her, they envied her and said to her, “If you want to win favor with him, seek refuge in God from him when he comes to you.” She did so. He turned his face away from her and said:

“Have you sought refuge in God? Go back to your family.” He left, with anger visible on his face.

Al-Ashʿath b. Qays said to him, “May God not grieve you, Messenger of God.

“Shall I marry you to a woman who is no less beautiful or noble in lineage?” He said, “Who is she?” He replied, “My sister (Qayla).” He said, “I have married her.” Al-Ashʿath set out for Ḥaḍramawt, prepared her and brought her along. But when he had departed from Yemen, news reached him of the Prophet’s death ﷺ so he took her back. ʿIkrima b. Abī Jahl married her. Al-Muhājir b. Abī Umayya al-Makhzūmī then married Asmāʾ bt. al-Nuʿmān b. al-Aswad.

The Prophet ﷺ had appointed him governor of Yemen. When the Prophet ﷺ died, al-Muhājir was in Yemen. After him, Qays b. Makshūḥ al-Murādī married Asmāʾ. She was the last woman the Prophet ﷺ married.

He married (the woman of al-Jawn), a Kinda woman, who was not Asmāʾ bt. al-Nuʿmān. The Prophet ﷺ sent Abū Usayd al-Sāʿidī, who had fought at Badr, and he brought her. Ḥafṣa said to ʿĀʾisha, or ʿĀʾisha to Ḥafṣa, “You apply henna to her, and I will comb her hair.” They did so, and one of them said to her, “When the Prophet ﷺ comes in to you, he likes a woman to say to him, ‘I seek refuge in God from you.’” When he came in to her, she said that. He put his sleeve over his face, covering himself, and said, “You have sought refuge with one who grants refuge,” three times. Then he left and said, “Abū Usayd, take her back to her family, and give her two rāziq garments—that is, two pieces of coarse cloth.” They say that she died of grief.

Then he married (Sanā) bt. Sufyān b. ʿAwf b. Kaʿb b. Abī Bakr b. Kilāb.

The Prophet ﷺ sent Abū Usayd al-Anṣārī to ask for a woman of Banū ʿĀmir, called (ʿAmra) bt. Yazīd b. ʿUbayd b. Ruʾās b. Kilāb, in marriage for him.

He married her. When he heard that she had a white skin condition, he divorced her.

Laylā bt. al-Khaṭīm b. ʿAdī b. ʿAmr came to him ﷺ. He was al-Nabīt b. Mālik b. al-Aws b. Ḥāritha. She was the sister of the poet Qays b. al-Khaṭīm.

He ﷺ had turned his back to the sun when she struck him on the shoulder while he was unaware. He said, “Who is this? May the Black One eat him!” He often said this.

She said, “I am the daughter of the feeder of birds and the challenger of the wind. I am Laylā bt. al-Khaṭīm.

“I have come to offer myself to you, that you may marry me.” He said, “I have done so.” She returned to her people and said, “The Messenger of God has married me.” They said, “What a bad thing you have done! You are a woman among co-wives, and the Messenger of God has other wives. You will be jealous over him, and he will pray against you. Ask him to release you from your commitment.” She did so, and he released her. While she was in one of the gardens of Medina, a wolf sprang upon her and ate part of her, in accordance with what he ﷺ had said, and she died.

The Prophet ﷺ took (Ṣafiyya) bt. Bashāma al-ʿAnbariyya captive during the expedition of ʿUyayna b. Ḥiṣn al-Fazārī. He offered her the choice of marrying him or being returned to her husband.

She chose her husband, and he sent her back. During this expedition was revealed: “Indeed, most of those who call you from behind the apartments lack understanding.” [Qurʾān 49:4]

He asked for (Ḍubāʿa) bt. ʿĀmir b. Qurṭ b. Salama b. Qushayr b. Kaʿb b. Rabīʿa b. ʿĀmir b. Ṣaʿṣaʿa in marriage. She had been married to Hawdha b. ʿAlī al-Ḥanafī, who died, leaving her a great deal of property. ʿAbd Allāh b. Judʿān married her, but he had no children. She asked him for a divorce, and he divorced her. Hishām b. al-Mughīra then married her.

She bore him Salama, who was among the best of the Muslims. She was one of the most beautiful women of the Arabs. The Messenger of God ﷺ heard of her beauty and asked her son Salama for her hand.

He said, “I will consult her.” He went to her and told her the news. She said, “You must consult me about the Messenger of God ﷺ? Go back and marry me to him.” Salama returned, but the Prophet ﷺ had heard that she was elderly. Salama went back to him, and the Prophet refrained from pursuing the matter; it did not go ahead.

Among those he did not marry was (Umm Hāniʾ), who was Hind bt. Abī Ṭālib. The Prophet had asked for her hand from Abū Ṭālib in the pre-Islamic period, and Hubayra b. Abī Wahb b. [ʿAmr b.] ʿĀʾidh b. ʿImrān b. Makhzūm had also asked for her hand. Abū Ṭālib married her to Hubayra.

He said to him, “Uncle, you married her to Hubayra and left me?” He replied:

“My nephew, we have entered into marriage ties with them, and a noble man returns a noble man’s favor.”

She bore Hubayra Hāniʾ, Yūsuf, and Jaʿda. Then she became Muslim, and Islam separated them. When Islam came, the Prophet asked her to marry him. She said:

“I loved you in the pre-Islamic period, so how could I not love you in Islam? But I am a woman with children, and I would dislike causing you trouble.” The Prophet ﷺ said, “The best women who have ridden camels are the women of Quraysh: the most tender toward a child in its youth, and the most careful of a husband’s property.”

Al-Muqawqis, ruler of Alexandria, gave the Prophet ﷺ Māriya the Copt and her sister. The Prophet ﷺ took (Māriya) as his concubine, and she bore him Ibrāhīm. He gave her sister to Ḥassān b. Thābit al-Anṣārī, and she bore him ʿAbd al-Raḥmān.

When he died, the Prophet ﷺ left nine wives: ʿĀʾisha, Ḥafṣa, Sawda, Umm Ḥabība, Umm Salama, Zaynab, Juwayriyya, Maymūna, and Ṣafiyya. Those of them from Quraysh were ʿĀʾisha, Ḥafṣa, Umm Ḥabība, Umm Salama, and Sawda. The first of them to join him ﷺ in death were Zaynab bt. Jaḥsh al-Asadiyya and Ṣafiyya bt. Ḥuyayy.

Then Sawda died toward the end of the time of ʿUmar b. al-Khaṭṭāb ﵀. Then Ḥafṣa died at the beginning of the time when allegiance was sworn to Muʿāwiya. Umm Ḥabība died a year before Muʿāwiya’s death. ʿĀʾisha died during Muʿāwiya’s time. Juwayriyya died during Muʿāwiya’s time as well. Umm Salama died during the time of Yazīd b. Muʿāwiya. She was the last of them to die. The Messenger of God ﷺ allotted ʿĀʾisha two days:

her own day and the day of Sawda, which Sawda had given her. He allotted his other wives one day each.

The brothers-in-law of the Messenger of God ﷺ

His brother-in-law through Khadīja ﵂ was al-Rabīʿ b. ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā b. ʿAbd Shams. Hāla bt. Khuwaylid had been married to him and bore him Abū al-ʿĀṣ b. al-Rabīʿ. She was Khadīja’s full sister. Abū al-ʿĀṣ married Zaynab, the daughter of the Messenger of God ﷺ ﵂ and she bore him ʿAlī and Umāma. ʿAlī b. Abī Ṭālib ﵁ married Umāma after Fāṭima ﵂.

Another brother-in-law of his ﷺ was Rabīʿa b. ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā b. ʿAbd Shams.

This was because he married Hāla after his brother.

Another brother-in-law of his was Qaṭan b. Wahb b. ʿAmr b. Ḥabīb b. Saʿd b. Mālik al-Muṣṭaliqī, through Hāla. She bore him ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā b. Qaṭan, whom the Messenger of God ﷺ likened to ʿAmr b. Luḥayy. ʿAmr was the first to alter the religion of Ismāʿīl ﵇.

Another brother-in-law of his was Wahb b. ʿAbd b. Jābir b. ʿUbayd b. Mālik al-Thaqafī, also through Hāla. She bore him Qaṭan b. Wahb.

His brother-in-law through Khālida bt. Khuwaylid, Khadīja’s sister, was ʿIlāj b. Abī Salama b. ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā b. Ghīra.

His brother-in-law through Ruqayqa bt. Khuwaylid was ʿAbd Allāh b. Bajjād b. al-Ḥārith b. Ḥāritha b. Saʿd b. Taym b. Murra b. Kaʿb. She bore him Amat Allāh bt. ʿAbd Allāh b. Bajjād.

His brother-in-law through ʿĀʾisha was al-Zubayr b. al-ʿAwwām b. Khuwaylid. Asmāʾ bt. Abī Bakr ﵀ was married to al-Zubayr. She was ʿĀʾisha’s paternal half-sister. She bore him ʿAbd Allāh, ʿUrwa, al-Mundhir, ʿĀṣim, Umm Ḥasan, and ʿĀʾisha, the children of al-Zubayr.

Ṭalḥa b. ʿUbayd Allāh b. ʿUthmān al-Taymī was his brother-in-law once through Umm Kulthūm bt. Abī Bakr; she bore Ṭalḥa Zakariyyā and ʿĀʾisha. Another time, through Ḥamna bt. Jaḥsh. And another time, through Qarība bt. Abī Umayya, the sister of Umm Salama.

Thus Ṭalḥa was his brother-in-law three times.

His brother-in-law ﷺ was ʿAbd al-Raḥmān b. ʿAbd Allāh b. Abī Rabīʿa b.

Al-Mughīra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. He had left behind Umm Kulthūm bint Abī Bakr, who bore him Ibrāhīm, ʿUthmān, Mūsā, and a daughter.

Among her former husbands was ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd [ibn] al-Ḥārith ibn Zuhra. Sawda bint Zamʿa had been his wife; she was the sister of Umm Ḥabība bint Zamʿa.

She had also previously been married to him through Zaynab bint Jaḥsh. Umm Ḥabība bint Jaḥsh had been his wife, but she bore him no children.

She had been married to Ḥuwayṭib ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Abī Qays, of the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy. Umm Kulthūm, Sawda’s sister from both her father and mother, had been his wife. She bore him ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥuwayṭib.

Among her former husbands, through Ḥafṣa bint ʿUmar, was ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd ibn al-Khaṭṭāb. Fāṭima bint ʿUmar, Ḥafṣa’s paternal sister, had been his wife.

She bore him ʿAbd Allāh and a daughter.

She had been married to Ibrāhīm ibn Nuʿaym al-Naḥḥām al-ʿAdawī. Ruqayya bint ʿUmar, Ḥafṣa’s paternal sister, had been his wife. Her mother was Umm Kulthūm bint ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁.

She had been married to ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Surāqa ibn al-Muʿtamir ibn Anas ibn Adhāh ibn Riyāḥ ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qurṭ ibn Rizāḥ. Zaynab bint ʿUmar, Ḥafṣa’s paternal sister, had been his wife. After him, Muʿammar ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy ibn Salūl, of the Banū al-Ḥublā, married her. She bore him ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd Allāh.

Among her former husbands, through Umm Salama, may God have mercy on her, was Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān ibn Ḥarb ibn Umayya. Ṣaghīra Qurayba bint Abī Umayya ibn al-Mughīra, Umm Salama’s paternal sister, had been his wife. She bore him no children.

She had been married to Zamʿa ibn al-Aswad ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā.

Kabīra Qurayba bint Abī Umayya, Umm Salama’s paternal sister, had been his wife.

She bore him ʿAbd Allāh, Wahb, and Yazīd. He was killed at al-Ṭāʾif and left no descendants.

Al-Ḥārith ibn Zamʿa was killed at Badr as an unbeliever.

She had been married to ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Abī Bakr al-Ṣiddīq. After Muʿāwiya, he married Ṣaghīra Qurayba. She bore him ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān.

ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵀ had Ṣaghīra Qurayba as his wife.

Islam separated the two of them.

She had been married to Munabbih ibn al-Ḥajjāj al-Sahmī. A daughter of Abī Umayya, whose name is not given, had been his wife.

Ṭalḥa had married her through Qurayba bint Abī Umayya.

She had been married to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿīd ibn al-Ḥakam. A daughter of Abī Umayya, whose name is not given, had been his wife.

She had been married to Ṣuhayb ibn Sinān al-Namrī, the client of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān.

Rīṭa bint Abī Umayya had been his wife. It is also said that she was the daughter of Abī Rabīʿa ibn al-Mughīra.

Among her former husbands, through Zaynab bint Jaḥsh, was Muṣʿab al-Khayr ibn ʿUmayr ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn ʿAbd al-Dār. Ḥamna bint Jaḥsh ibn Riʾāb, Zaynab’s sister, had been his wife. She bore him Zaynab, daughter of Muṣʿab.

Zaynab married ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Umayya ibn al-Mughīra, and bore him Muḥammad, Muṣʿab, and Qurayba. Qurayba married ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Hishām, and bore him Ḥafṣ …

Then, after Muṣʿab ibn ʿUmayr, Ṭalḥa married Ḥamna bint Jaḥsh. ibn ʿUbayd Allāh, and she bore him Muḥammad al-Sajjād and ʿImrān, the sons of Ṭalḥa.

Among her former husbands, through Umm Ḥabība bint Abī Sufyān, was al-Ḥārith ibn Nawfal ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. Hind bint Abī Sufyān had been his wife. She bore him ʿAbd Allāh al-Faqīh, who was also known as Babbah ibn al-Ḥārith; Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥārith the Elder; Rabīʿa; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān; Ramla; Umm al-Zubayr; Ḍarība; and another woman, daughters and sons of al-Ḥārith ibn Nawfal.

She had been married to Muḥammad ibn Abī Ḥudhayfa ibn ʿUtba ibn Rabīʿa. Ramla bint Abī Sufyān had been his wife. He was killed at al-Yamāma while she was still his wife. She was Abū Sufyān’s daughter and Umm Ḥabība’s sister.

After him, Saʿīd ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān married Ramla. She bore him Muḥammad.

Then ʿAmr al-Ashdaq ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayya also married Ramla. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān killed him. She bore him no children.

Among her former husbands was ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Umayya al-Aṣghar ibn ʿAbd Shams. After al-Sāʾib ibn Abī Ḥubaysh—whose name was Uhayb ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn Asad—he married Juwayriyya bint Abī Sufyān. Neither she nor al-Sāʾib bore him children.

She had been married to Ṣafwān ibn Umayya ibn Khalaf ibn Wahb ibn Ḥudhāfa ibn Jumah.

Umayma bint Abī Sufyān, Umm Ḥabība’s sister from both her father and mother, had been his wife.

She bore him ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ṣafwān. After Ḥuwayṭib ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Abī Qays ibn ʿAbd Wudd ibn Naṣr ibn Mālik ibn Ḥisl ibn ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, he married her. She bore him Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥuwayṭib.

Among her former husbands was also ʿIyāḍ ibn Ghanm ibn Jābir ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿĀmir ibn ʿUmayra ibn Wadīʿa ibn al-Ḥārith. Umm al-Ḥakam bint Abī Sufyān, Umm Ḥabība’s paternal sister, had been his wife. Her mother was Hind bint ʿUtba. Islam separated the two of them. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Rabīʿa ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥubayb ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Mālik ibn Ḥuṭayṭ al-Thaqafī married her. She bore him ʿAbd al-Raḥmān. Her son was attributed to her; he was called ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Umm al-Ḥakam. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUthmān was killed at al-Ṭāʾif as an unbeliever.

She had been married to Saʿīd ibn al-Akhnaṣ. Abū Saʿīd al-Sukkarī said: “This Saʿīd is the one of whom the Prophet ﷺ said, ‘May God drive him away!’ For he hated Quraysh.” He was the ally of the Banū Zuhra, and was Saʿīd ibn al-Akhnaṣ ibn Sharīq ibn Wahb ibn ʿIlāj al-Thaqafī. Ṣakhra bint Abī Sufyān had been his wife. She bore him children, including Abū Bakr ibn Saʿīd ibn al-Akhnaṣ, who used to transmit reports from his maternal aunt, Umm Ḥabība.

She had also been married to ʿUrwa ibn Masʿūd ibn ʿĀmir ibn Muʿattib al-Thaqafī, who is reported to have said of the Messenger of God ﷺ: “He is like the companion of Yā Sīn.” He had called from the roof of his house in al-Ṭāʾif, giving the call to prayer or proclaiming the oneness of God, when a man from the people of al-Ṭāʾif shot and killed him. Maymūna bint Abī Sufyān had been his wife. She bore him Dāwūd.

After ʿUrwa, al-Mughīra ibn Shuʿba al-Thaqafī married her.

Among her former husbands was ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muʿāwiya al-ʿAbdī. After Ḥuwayṭib ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, he married Umayma bint Abī Sufyān.

Among the former husbands of Maymūna bint al-Ḥārith was Ḥamza ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ﵀. Salmā bint ʿUmays, Maymūna’s maternal sister, had been his wife. She was Hind bint ʿAwf ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥamāṭa ibn Jurash, of Ḥimyar. The daughters of ʿUmays who shared the same mother were: Maymūna, wife of the Prophet ﷺ; Umm al-Faḍl Lubāba, mother of the sons of al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ﵁—these two were daughters of al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥazn al-Hilālī; Lubāba the Younger, daughter of al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥazn; and al-Quṣaymāʾ, daughter of al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥazn. These were Maymūna’s sisters from both her father and mother.

ʿAzza bint al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥazn was also their sister from both their father and mother. Their maternal sister was Zaynab bint Khuzayma ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Hilālī. Maymūna’s maternal sisters were Salmā, Asmāʾ, and Salāma, daughters of ʿUmays. Salmā bore Ḥamza ﵀ a daughter named Amat Allāh, who was Ummāma. The Messenger of God ﷺ married her to Salama ibn Abī Salama. He used to say, “Have I repaid Salama?”—meaning that he had repaid him by giving him in marriage the daughter of Ḥamza after Salama had given him his mother, Umm Salama, in marriage.

Among the former husbands of al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ﵁

was Kabīra Lubāba.

She bore him al-Faḍl, “the one who rode behind” (the Prophet ﷺ had him ride behind him during the Farewell Pilgrimage); al-Ḥibr ʿAbd Allāh; al-Jawād ʿUbayd Allāh; and Maʿbad. Maʿbad died a martyr in Africa; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān died a martyr in Syria; and Qutham died a martyr in Samarkand. The children also included Umm Ḥabīb. The sons of al-ʿAbbās ﵁ had no brothers more noble than they, nor whose graves were farther apart. Maʿbad died in Africa, ʿAbd al-Raḥmān in Syria, ʿAbd Allāh at al-Ṭāʾif, ʿUbayd Allāh in Medina, Qutham in Samarkand, and al-Faḍl in Syria.

Among his former husbands were Jaʿfar and ʿAlī, the sons of Abū Ṭālib, and Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq ﵃. Asmāʾ bint ʿUmays had been Jaʿfar’s wife. She emigrated with him to Abyssinia and bore him ʿAbd Allāh, ʿAwn, and Muḥammad. Jaʿfar was martyred at Muʾta ﵀. Abū Bakr ﵀ then married Asmāʾ. She bore him Muḥammad, who was killed in Egypt: he was put inside a donkey and burned. Then ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁ married her. She bore him Yaḥyā and ʿAwn.

Among her former husbands was Shaddād ibn Usāma ibn ʿAmr (who was “the guide”: he used to light a fire so guests could find their way to him) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jābir ibn Barr ibn ʿAtwāra ibn ʿĀmir ibn Layth ibn Bakr ibn ʿAbd Manāt ibn Kināna. After Ḥamza ﵀ he married Salmā bint ʿUmays. She bore him ʿAbd Allāh and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān.

She had also been married to al-Ṭufayl ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Muṭṭalib. Zaynab bint Khuzayma had been his wife.

She had been married to al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm.

Ṣughrā Lubāba had been his wife. She bore him Khālid ibn al-Walīd, “the Sword of God.”

She had been married to Ubayy ibn Khalaf ibn Wahb ibn Ḥudhāfa ibn Jumah.

The Messenger of God ﷺ killed him with his own hand on the day of Uḥud. Al-ʿAṣmāʾ bint al-Ḥārith had been his wife. She bore him Abū Ubayy and other siblings.

She had also been married to Ziyād ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mālik ibn Bujayr al-Hilālī. ʿAzza bint al-Ḥārith had been his wife.

She had also been married to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Kaʿb ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Kuʿayb ibn Munabbih ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Munabbih ibn al-Aws, of Khathʿam. Salāma bint ʿUmays had been his wife. She bore him Umayya, who married ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵀. She bore him Ṣāliḥ the Younger, Asmāʾ, and Lubāba, the children of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar. No woman among the Arabs is known to have had more noble in-laws than Hind bint ʿAwf, the mother of Maymūna and her sisters.

She had also been married to Ḥassān ibn Thābit ibn al-Mundhir ibn Ḥarām al-Anṣārī, the poet. Ḥassān was among those who accused ʿĀʾisha of adultery and falsely attributed her to Ṣafwān ibn al-Muʿaṭṭal al-Sulamī. He was devout and impotent, and had never uncovered a woman. He was martyred at al-Yamāma. When God, mighty and exalted, declared ʿĀʾisha innocent and revealed Qurʾānic verses affirming her innocence, the Prophet ﷺ had Ḥassān flogged among those whom he flogged. Ṣafwān sprang upon Ḥassān, struck him once with his sword, and said:

“Take the sword’s fly from you, for IAm a youth who, when reviled, is no poet.”

The Banū al-Najjār seized Ṣafwān, bound him, and brought him to the Prophet ﷺ.

He asked Ḥassān to forgo the blow from Ṣafwān, and Ḥassān forgave him. As compensation, the Prophet ﷺ gave him Shīrīn the Copt, the sister of Māriya. She bore him ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥassān.

Among these former husbands were ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf al-Zuhrī, Ṭalḥa ibn ʿUbayd Allāh al-Taymī, and Ḥuwayṭib ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, of the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy. All were her former husbands twice, except Ṭalḥa, who was her former husband three times.

The Campaigns of the Prophet ﷺ

The first took place in the year two. The Messenger of God ﷺ set out on campaign after several nights of Ṣafar had passed. He made a truce with the Banū Ḍamra, wrote an agreement with them, reached Waddān and al-Abwāʾ, then returned without encountering fighting, at the beginning of Rabīʿ al-Awwal.

In that year he set out on Monday, when three nights of Rabīʿ al-Ākhir had passed, and reached Buwāṭ, three stages from Medina on the road to Syria.

Then he returned on Monday, when ten nights of Rabīʿ al-Ākhir remained, without encountering fighting.

In that year was the campaign of Dhāt al-ʿUshayra, at the beginning of Jumādā al-Ūlā. There he made a truce with the Banū Mudlij and their allies among the Banū Ḍamra, and wrote an agreement with them. He returned with eight nights of Jumādā al-Ākhira remaining, without encountering fighting.

Then the First Campaign of Badr. He set out for it on Monday, twelve nights having passed of Jumādā al-Ākhirah, and returned with a few days of the month remaining. He encountered no resistance.

Then Yanbuʿ. He set out on Thursday, two nights having passed of Shaʿbān, and reached Yanbuʿ. He returned without encountering resistance.

Then he set out on Tuesday, fourteen nights having passed of Shaʿbān, and reached Ṣafwān. He made a treaty of mutual alliance with Banū Ghifār, and likewise with Banū Aslam. He encountered no resistance.

Then Badr, which was the Great Onset. He set out on Wednesday, eight days having passed of the month of Ramaḍān. He prevailed and inflicted heavy losses on Quraysh, killing and taking captives. He returned victorious and laden with spoils on Wednesday, with eight days of Ramaḍān remaining.

Then he set out for Qarkarat al-Kudr on Friday, the first of Shawwāl, and returned with ten days of the month remaining. He drove off camels and their herdsmen and encountered no resistance.

Then the Campaign of al-Sawīq. Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb had come to al-Madīnah, so the Prophet set out on Sunday, with seven days of Dhū al-Ḥijjah remaining. Abū Sufyān and his companions fled, leaving their provisions behind, which the Muslims took. Quraysh said, “Muḥammad’s companions only go out to drink sawīq.” He returned with eight days of Dhū al-Ḥijjah remaining, without having encountered resistance.

Then the year three. In it, the Messenger of God expelled Banū al-Faṭyūn without fighting. They joined the people of Syria.

Then, in the wake of Muḥarram, he set out for Banū Anmār ibn Bughayḍ at Dhū Amr and took spoils. There he allotted three camels to each cavalryman and one camel to each foot soldier. He returned when five days of Ṣafar had passed, without encountering resistance.

He besieged Banū Qaynuqāʿ on Sunday, when seven days of Ṣafar had passed. He expelled them and granted their lives to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy ibn Salūl, for they were his allies. He returned in Ṣafar without encountering resistance.

Then the Campaign of Buḥrān. He set out at the beginning of Rabīʿ al-Ākhir and reached Buḥrān, in the region of al-Farʿ. He returned without encountering resistance.

Then the Campaign of Uḥud. He set out on Friday, fourteen nights having passed of Shawwāl. God honored with martyrdom those of his Companions whom He honored.

Then the Campaign of Ḥamrāʾ al-Asad. He set out for it on Sunday, with fourteen days of Shawwāl remaining, and returned without encountering resistance.

Then the Campaign against Banū al-Naḍīr, in the year four. He set out against them on Tuesday, twelve nights having passed of Rabīʿ al-Awwal, and besieged them for twenty-three days, until he expelled them. He returned when five days of Rabīʿ al-Ākhir had passed.

And the Campaign of Dhāt al-Riqāʿ. He set out for it on Monday, when ten days of Jumādā al-Ūlā had passed, and returned on Wednesday of that month without encountering resistance.

And the Campaign of Badr al-Mawʿid as well. He set out for it on Thursday, at the beginning of Shaʿbān, and returned on Wednesday, with ten days of the month remaining, without encountering resistance.

Then the Day of the Trench. He set out for it on Thursday, when ten days of Shawwāl had passed, and its affair came to an end on Saturday, one night having passed of Dhū al-Qaʿdah.

Then the Campaign against Banū Qurayẓah. He set out for it on the day on which the affair of the Trench ended and besieged them for twenty-five days. He returned on Monday, four days having passed of Dhū al-Ḥijjah.

Then the year five. In it, the Prophet prepared for the Campaign of Dūmat al-Jandal. Arab merchants had complained to him of the oppression of Ukaydir ibn ʿAbd al-Malik al-Sakūnī. The Prophet set out at the beginning of Muḥarram, on Monday. When Ukaydir learned of his approach, he fled and abandoned the market. The Prophet returned from the road in the early part of Ṣafar, without encountering resistance.

And the Campaign against Banū Liḥyān of Hudhayl. He set out for it on Tuesday, the first of Jumādā al-Ūlā, seeking vengeance for Khubayb ibn ʿAmr and his companions. They took refuge on the mountain peaks. He attacked a group of them at a watering place of theirs called al-Kudr.

God—mighty and exalted—is the one who routed them, and the Prophet took their property as spoils. He continued from there until he reached ʿUsfān. From there he sent cavalry to al-Hadrah, which is six miles from Mecca. A Quraysh caravan eluded him, so he returned without encountering resistance.

And the Campaign of Dhū Qard, which is three days’ journey from al-Madīnah. He returned without encountering resistance.

Then the year six. In it was the Campaign of al-Muraysīʿ, a watering place belonging to Banū al-Muṣṭaliq of Khuzāʿah. He set out for it on Saturday, the first of Shaʿbān, took spoils, and departed in Shaʿbān.

Then the Campaign of al-Ḥudaybiyyah. He set out on Thursday, the first of Dhū al-Qaʿdah. The truce was concluded during it. He returned at the end of Dhū al-Ḥijjah, or after five days of Muḥarram had passed.

Then the year seven. In it, he set out in Muḥarram for Khaybar.

He besieged them for a little over ten days, then departed from there for the villages of ʿArabiyyah, without encountering resistance.

He returned at the beginning of Rabīʿ al-Ākhir.

/ Then the Compensatory ʿUmrah. He set out for it on Monday, when six days of Dhū al-Qaʿdah had passed, and Mecca was vacated for him for three days.

Then the year eight. The Campaign of the Conquest of Mecca took place in the month of Ramaḍān.

In it was the Campaign of Ḥunayn, in the middle of Shawwāl.

In it was the Campaign of al-Ṭāʾif, which he conquered.

And in it was the ʿUmrah of al-Jiʿrānah, between Ḥunayn and al-Ṭāʾif.

Then the year nine. In it was the Campaign of Tabūk, which was the Army of Hardship. He set out for it on Monday, the first of Rajab, and returned at the end of Shawwāl. These, then, are his campaigns ﷺ.

Mention of the Expeditions and Armies of the Messenger of God ﷺ

He commissioned ʿUbaydah ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Muṭṭalib to lead an expedition at the beginning of Rabīʿ al-Awwal. He reached the mountain pass of al-Murrah and encountered ʿIkrimah ibn Abī Jahl.

There was no fighting between them. He returned at the end of the month.

He commissioned Ḥamzah ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ﵀—in the middle of that month, to the coast. He returned at the end of the month, without encountering resistance.

He commissioned Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ at the beginning of Rajab to al-Kharrār. He returned without encountering resistance.

He commissioned ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaḥsh ibn Riʾāb al-Asadī in the last ten days of Rajab to Nakhlah. He encountered ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥaḍramī, who was coming from al-Ṭāʾif, bound for Mecca. He killed him and took what he had with him as spoils. The Messenger of God took one fifth of the spoils before the Qurʾān prescribed a fifth of the spoils. Then the Qurʾān was revealed concerning this, confirming his action. This was the first fayʾ that God—mighty and exalted—bestowed upon the Prophet. Concerning it was revealed:

“They ask you about fighting in the sacred month” (2:217), to the end of the verse. / He returned on the first day of Shaʿbān.

In it, the Prophet sent Ghālib ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Laythī on Sunday, when ten days of Shawwāl had passed. They encountered Banū Sulaym and killed men among them. They returned on Saturday, with fourteen days of Shawwāl remaining, laden with spoils.

In the year three, he sent Muḥammad ibn Maslamah and Sulakān ibn Salāmah to Kaʿb ibn al-Ashraf the Jew, and they killed him.

In it, the Prophet sent Abū Salamah ibn ʿAbd al-Asad al-Makhzūmī to Quṭn. ʿUrwah ibn Masʿūd al-Fazārī was martyred there.

In the middle of Rajab, he sent ʿAbd Allāh ibn Unays to Salām ibn Abī al-Ḥuqayq the Jew, and he killed him.

In it, with five days of Rajab remaining, he sent Zayd ibn Ḥārithah to al-Qarad. He returned without encountering resistance.

In it, at the end of Shawwāl, he sent Marthad ibn Kinān al-Ghanawī, the ally of Ḥamzah, to al-Rajīʿ. He encountered Banū Liḥyān. Marthad was martyred; he was their leader.

ʿĀṣim ibn Thābit ibn Abī al-Aqlaḥ was also martyred. A swarm of wasps protected him, so the polytheists could not get to his head. When night fell, a flood came and carried him away. Khubayb and Ibn al-Dathinnah were captured, and they crucified them at al-Tanʿīm.

In the year four, al-Mundhir ibn ʿAmr al-Sāʿidī set out with thirty men—four of them Emigrants and twenty-six Helpers—to Biʾr Maʿūnah. They were all killed except ʿAmr ibn Umayyah al-Ḍamrī, whom ʿĀmir ibn al-Ṭufayl released when he identified himself as belonging to Kinānah. This was when ten days of Ṣafar remained. Jabbār ibn Sulmā ibn Mālik ibn Jaʿfar ibn Kilāb killed ʿĀmir ibn Fuhayrah, the freedman of Abū Bakr ﵀. He thrust his spear through him. ʿĀmir slipped off the spear, and Jabbār saw him rise until he entered the sky. His body was not found among the slain.

/ In it, the Prophet sent Abū ʿUbaydah ibn al-Jarrāḥ to the coast.

He returned without encountering resistance.

In it, Zayd ibn Ḥārithah raided al-Jamuḥ, in the territory of Banū Sulaym. He returned without encountering resistance.

In it, he sent Abū ʿUbaydah ibn al-Jarrāḥ to Dhāt al-Qaṣṣah. He returned without encountering resistance.

Then the year five. In it, the Prophet dispatched ʿAbd Allāh ibn Unays al-Juhanī to Nabīḥ al-Hudhalī, and he killed him at ʿUranah.

In it, the Prophet sent ʿAmr ibn Umayyah al-Ḍamrī and Salamah ibn Aslam to assassinate Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb. The latter got wind of them, and they fled.

In it, he sent ʿAbd Allāh ibn Rawāḥah to kill Usayr ibn Zārim the Jew. He killed him and his companions.

In it, he sent Ghālib ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Laythī to al-Kudayd, against Banū al-Jalūkh. He returned without encountering resistance.

In it, he sent Zayd ibn Ḥārithah with an army to Wādī al-Qurā. He returned.

He sent him again to Umm Qirfah al-Fazārīyah and killed her. He took her daughter Hind captive, and the Prophet gave her to Salamah ibn al-Akwaʿ or to Salamah ibn Salāmah ibn Waqsh. In it, he dispatched ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb against al-Qārah. They took refuge from him in the mountains.

He rose against them with those who were with him and began hurling stones at them with both hands; he was left-handed and right-handed5

In it, he sent Bilāl ibn al-Ḥārith al-Muzanī to Banū Mālik ibn Kinānah.

They got wind of him. He found nothing in their dwellings except a horse, which he brought to the Prophet.

In it, he sent Bishr ibn Suwayd al-Juhanī to Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Kinānah.

They took refuge from him in a thicket, so he set it ablaze around them. When they returned, the Prophet said to them, “How badly you acted.”

In it, he sent Abū ʿUbaydah ibn al-Jarrāḥ with an army against Asad and Ṭayyiʾ.

He returned without encountering resistance.

In it, he sent ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb with an army to Turbah. He returned without encountering resistance.

The year six. In it, he sent Bashīr ibn Saʿd toward Fadak. He and his companions were killed.

In it, he sent Ghālib ibn ʿAbd Allāh toward Fadak. He caught up with those who had killed Bashīr and his companions.

In it, he sent Kaʿb ibn ʿUmayr al-Ghifārī to Dhāt Aṭlāḥ.

They were all martyred.

In it, he sent ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf against Kalb, as commander of an expedition. He ordered him…

that he might marry the daughter of its chief. So he married the daughter of al-Aṣbagh.

In it, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Rawāḥah raided Khaybar in a detachment, but its people fortified themselves.

He raided them a dozen or so times and captured most of their livestock.

In the year seven, the Prophet ﷺ sent Muḥayyisah ibn Masʿūd at the head of a detachment to Fadak. They accepted the terms of a treaty.

In it, he dispatched Zayd ibn Ḥārithah at the head of an army against Judhām. They had waylaid Diḥyah ibn Khalīfah al-Kalbī as he was returning from Caesar with the Prophet’s letter ﷺ. Zayd raided them and seized some of their property. Then the notables of the tribe came out to meet him with goodwill and honor. He accepted their offer, and some of them responded to his call.

In it was the affair of Fadak. Its people feared the Prophet ﷺ because of what he had done to Banū Qurayẓah, and so they moved to Khaybar. Fadak was among what God bestowed upon His Messenger ﷺ without fighting or the driving of horses. He treated its people as he treated the people of Khaybar, on the basis of half [the produce], and he could expel them whenever he wished.

In it was the expedition of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ al-Sahmī against Dhāt al-Salāsil. Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, and Abū ʿUbaydah ibn al-Jarrāḥ were with him in his army. He requested reinforcements, so the Prophet ﷺ reinforced him with an army that included Abū Bakr and ʿUmar. The army’s commander was Abū ʿUbaydah ibn al-Jarrāḥ. Abū Saʿīd said: Abū Bakr and ʿUmar ﵏ complained to the Prophet ﷺ about ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ. He said to them, “No one shall command you after me.” This confirms the caliphate of Abū Bakr and ʿUmar ﵏.

In it, the Prophet ﷺ sent Abū al-ʿAwjāʾ al-Sulamī at the head of a detachment against Banū Sulaym. He was martyred.

In it, he sent ʿUkāshah ibn Miḥṣan at the head of a detachment to al-Ghamrah. He returned without encountering resistance.

In it, he dispatched Zayd ibn Ḥārithah at the head of an army to al-Ṭaraf. He returned without encountering resistance.

In it, he sent ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Ḥudūd al-Aslamī at the head of a detachment to Aḍam, with Abū Qatādah and Muḥallim ibn Jaththāmah. Muḥallim killed a man from Ashjaʿ called ʿĀmir ibn al-Aḍbaṭ, after the man had greeted them with the greeting of Islam, because of something that had passed between them in the pre-Islamic period. The Prophet ﷺ was informed of this and said, “Did you first grant him safety, then kill him?”

Then Almighty God revealed a verse prohibiting haste: “Do not say to one who offers you peace, ‘You are not a believer’” (4:94) to the end of the verse. Muḥallim said, “He only said it with his tongue to protect himself; it was not firmly rooted in his heart.” The Prophet ﷺ said to him, “Why did you not search his heart, so you could know whether it was firmly rooted there or not?” The Prophet ﷺ prayed against him. Seven or eight [days] had not passed before he died. He was buried, but the earth cast him out. They buried him again, and it cast him out.

This was reported to the Prophet ﷺ, who said, “It does indeed close over someone worse than him. But God / intended to show you a lesson concerning the crime among you.” In it, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Ḥadrad was sent with two men to al-Ghābah, eight miles from Medina, when word reached him that Rifāʿah ibn Qays al-Jushamī intended to gather Qays to fight the Prophet ﷺ. They lay in wait for him. Ibn Abī Ḥadrad shot him and killed him. He brought his head to the Prophet ﷺ.

In the year eight, the Prophet ﷺ sent Zayd ibn Ḥārithah to Muʾtah. With him were Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Rawāḥah. Zayd was commander of the army; if he was killed, Jaʿfar was to command, and if he was killed, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Rawāḥah.

All three were killed. Khālid ibn al-Walīd then took the standard, although the Messenger of God ﷺ had not appointed him among those named. He killed Ibn Zāfilah al-Balawī and routed his army. That was in Jumādā.

In it, Khālid ibn al-Walīd was sent against Banū Jadhīmah of Kinānah. He surprised them at al-Ghumayṣāʾ and killed them in the most atrocious manner. The Prophet ﷺ sent ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁, who paid compensation for every life and everything taken from them, down to a dog’s drinking bowl.

In it, Khālid ibn al-Walīd was sent to Nakhlah. He demolished the shrine of al-ʿUzzā and cut down its trees.

In it, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mughaffal was sent at the head of a detachment to Awṭās. He gained booty and returned safely.

In it, Abū ʿĀmir al-Ashʿarī was sent and launched a raid against Hawāzin. He was struck in the knee and died. Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī took the standard after him. He charged at the killer of his uncle, Salamah ibn Samādīr, and killed him.

In it, al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām was sent with a detachment in pursuit of Mālik ibn ʿAwf al-Naṣrī, the leader of Qays on the day of Ḥunayn. He captured him with the help of Abū Qatādah.

Bilāl ibn al-Ḥārith al-Muzanī was dispatched against a group that had split away. They returned without encountering resistance.

/ In the year nine, the Prophet ﷺ sent ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁ at the head of an army to Yemen. He returned without encountering resistance.

In it, Usāmah ibn Zayd was sent at the head of an army to al-Dārūm in the land of Palestine. He gained booty and returned safely.

In it, ʿUyaynah ibn Ḥiṣn ibn Ḥudhayfah ibn Badr al-Fazārī was sent against Banū al-ʿAnbar. He took captives and killed people, then brought the captives to the Messenger of God ﷺ, who freed half and ransomed the other half.

In it, ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁ was sent to Yemen to collect their alms and take their poll tax.

Khālid ibn al-Walīd was dispatched against Ukaydir ibn ʿAbd al-Malik at the head of a detachment to Dūmat al-Jandal, and killed him. This did not happen in this year, but rather after this.

In the year ten, Usāmah ibn Zayd was sent at the head of an army to Muʾtah to raid it. The Prophet ﷺ passed away, so Abū Bakr ﵀ dispatched that army.

The commanders appointed by the Messenger of God ﷺ

ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁ was his commander in Medina during the expedition to Tabūk, when he said to him, “Are you not content to be to me as Aaron was to Moses?” Al-Muhājir ibn Abī Umayyah al-Makhzūmī was his commander over Kindah and al-Ṣadaf.

Ziyād ibn Labīd al-Bayāḍī was commander over Ḥaḍramawt and its alms. Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī was commander over Zabīd, Rimaʿ, ʿAdan, and the coast. Muʿādh ibn Jabal was commander over al-Jund. ʿAdī ibn Ḥātim was commander over the alms of Ṭayyiʾ and Asad. Mālik ibn Nuwayrah al-Yarbūʿī was commander over the alms of Banū Ḥanẓalah. Al-Zibirqān ibn Badr and Qays ibn ʿĀṣim were commanders over the alms of Saʿd Tamīm. ʿAttāb ibn Asīd ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ was appointed over Mecca. Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb was appointed over / Najrān. Yazīd ibn Abī Sufyān was commander over Taymāʾ. Khālid ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah was commander over Ṣanʿāʾ. The Prophet ﷺ passed away while Khālid was in office. His brother ʿAmr ibn Saʿīd was commander over Wādī al-Qurā, and their brother al-Ḥakam ibn Saʿīd was commander over the villages of ʿArabiyyah. Their brother Abān ibn Saʿīd was commander over al-Khaṭṭ in Baḥrayn.

Al-Walīd ibn ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayṭ was commander over Banū al-Muṣṭaliq. Al-ʿAlāʾ ibn al-Ḥaḍramī, the ally of Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ, was commander over al-Qaṭīf in Baḥrayn.

Muʿayqīb ibn Abī Fāṭimah, an ally of Banū Umayyah, was in charge of the spoils. Abū Ruhm al-Ghifārī was his commander in Medina when he set out for al-Ḥudaybiyyah. Subayʿ ibn ʿUrfuṭah al-Ghifārī was his commander in Medina when he raided Khaybar; it is also said that Abū Ruhm al-Ghifārī was Kulthūm ibn al-Ḥuṣayn. Abū Ruhm was also his commander in Medina during the expedition of the Conquest.

His commander over the pilgrimage season, when the people were polytheists, was ʿAttāb ibn Asīd. ʿAttāb stationed the Muslims at their appointed positions, while the polytheists remained apart at their own. Abū Bakr ﵀ was his commander over the pilgrimage season in the year nine, when some of the people were polytheists.

Abū Bakr stationed the Muslims at their appointed positions.

His commander over the delegation of Thaqīf to lead their prayers was ʿUthmān ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Bishr al-Thaqafī. He then appointed him over al-Ṭāʾif, and God—glorified and mighty—is the one who took him while he was in office. Abū Bakr ﵀ retained him until he died. ʿUmar retained him for two years of his caliphate. ʿUthmān wrote to ʿUmar seeking his permission to go on a campaign. ʿUmar said to him, “As for me, I will not appoint anyone over you. But appoint as your deputy whomever you wish.” He appointed Yaʿlā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Rabīʿah.

ʿUmar ﵀ appointed him over Baḥrayn and ʿUmān. He sent his brother al-Ḥakam ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ to Baḥrayn as his deputy. He raided the people of ʿUmān and Baḥrayn, with his brother al-Ḥakam leading the vanguard among ʿAbd al-Qays. He encamped at Tawwaj and fought / Sahark al-Adharjī.

His commander over the shares of the spoils on the day of Badr was Muḥammiyah ibn Juzʾ ibn ʿAbd Yaghūth ibn ʿUrayj ibn ʿAmr ibn Zubayd, an ally of Banū Jumah.

Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq ﵀ was the Prophet’s successor over his community.

The clients of the Messenger of God ﷺ

Zayd ibn Ḥārithah ibn Sharāḥīl al-Kalbī. He had been taken captive, and the Prophet ﷺ bought him before his prophetic mission, freed him, and adopted him. His beloved son was Usāmah ibn Zayd. His mother was Umm Ayman, whose name was Barakah, a client of the Messenger of God ﷺ.

Abū Rāfiʿ; Abū Kabshah; Abū Lubābah; Abū Luqayṭ;

Abū Hind; Abū Ayman; Abū Muwayhibah; Ruwayfiʿ;

Safīnah; Thawbān; Ṣāliḥ, who was Shaqrān; Anasah.

Abū Bakrah; and Wardān, both men from al-Ṭāʾif who came to him ﷺ on the day of the siege of al-Ṭāʾif. He also had a servant called Anjashah and a slave woman called Rawḍah.

The Story of Abū Kabshah

Quraysh used to attribute the Prophet ﷺ to Abū Kabshah, saying, “The son of Abū Kabshah said so,” and, “The son of Abū Kabshah did so.” Wahb ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah, Abū Āminah’s father, was kunya-named Abū Kabshah.

ʿAmr ibn Zayd ibn Labīd al-Najjārī was kunya-named Abū Kabshah. He was the grandfather of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, the father of his mother Salmā. Wajz ibn Ghālib ibn ʿĀmir ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAmr ibn Buʾayy ibn Malkān ibn Afṣā ibn Ḥārithah was kunya-named Abū Kabshah. He was the Prophet’s maternal ancestor: the mother of Wahb ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah, Qaylah, was the daughter of Wajz ibn Ghālib. Al-Ḥārith, known as Ghabshān, ibn ʿAmr ibn Buʾayy ibn Malkān was kunya-named Abū Kabshah. He worshipped al-Shiʿrā.

Al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā / ibn Rifāʿah ibn Mallān, the brother of Banū Saʿd ibn Bakr ibn Hawāzin, who fostered the Messenger of God ﷺ, was kunya-named Abū Kabshah.

His wife was Ḥalīmah daughter of al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Shajnah ibn Jābir ibn Nāṣirah, the wet nurse of the Messenger of God ﷺ.

Those named Muḥammad because they had heard that a prophet would be sent among the Arabs whose name would be Muḥammad, and so God gave the prophethood to Muḥammad ﷺ

Muḥammad ibn Sufyān ibn Mujāshiʿ. Sufyān had gone to Syria and stayed with a monk. The monk was impressed by his eloquence and intelligence. The monk asked him about his lineage.

He gave his lineage to Muḍar. The monk said to him, “A prophet will indeed be sent among the Arabs whose name will be Muḥammad.” Sufyān therefore named his son Muḥammad. Muḥammad ibn Barr ibn ʿAtwārah ibn ʿĀmir ibn Layth ibn Bakr ibn ʿAbd Manāh ibn Kinānah. Muḥammad ibn Khuzāʿī ibn ʿAlqamah ibn Muḥārib ibn Murrah ibn Hilāl ibn Fālīj ibn Dhakwān al-Sulamī. He was in the army of Abraha with the elephant. Muḥammad ibn Ḥimrān ibn Mālik al-Juʿfī.

Muḥammad ibn ʿUqbah ibn Uḥayḥah ibn al-Jallāḥ al-Awsī, from Banū Jaḥjabā.

Muḥammad ibn Maslamah al-Anṣārī, from Banū Ḥārithah. Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥarmāz ibn Mālik ibn ʿAmr ibn Tamīm.

Among those of the prophets ﵈—who were created circumcised and were not circumcised nor in need of circumcision:

Adam; Seth; Noah; Shem; Hūd; Ṣāliḥ; Lot; Joseph;

Moses; Shuʿayb; Solomon; Zechariah; Jesus; Shuʿayb ibn Dhī Mihdam;

Ḥanẓala ibn Ṣafwān, prophet of the people of al-Rass; / and Muḥammad—God bless him and them all.

Abū Saʿīd said: Ibn Ḥabīb said: Ibn al-Kalbī reported from Abū Muḥammad al-Murhabī, from a shaykh of Dhū al-Kalāʿ, who said: I heard Kaʿb al-Aḥbār say:

“I found in some of our books that Adam was found circumcised, and likewise eleven prophets were found circumcised: Seth, Enoch, Noah, his son Shem, Lot, Joseph, Moses, Solomon, Zechariah, Jesus, and Muḥammad—God bless him and them.”
