He said: Ibn al-Kalbī mentioned that Furwa ibn Saʿīd ibn ʿAfīf told me: I heard Mālik ibn Murāra al-Rahāʾī relate: “The reason for my conversion to Islam was that I heard a Jewish shaykh from Ḥimyar, who later became Muslim and had been granted a long life, say during his lifetime: ‘Six prophets were found circumcised: Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Shuʿayb, [and] the prophet of the people of Ḥaḍūr, the prophet of al-Rass, and the Muḍarī, the Seal of the Prophets—God bless him and them all.’”

Judges of the Arabs

The first of them was al-Afʿā ibn al-Ḥuṣayn ibn Ghanm ibn Rahm ibn al-Ḥārith al-Jurhumī, who judged between the sons of Nizār ibn Maʿadd concerning their inheritance: Muḍar, Rabīʿa, Iyād, and Anmār. His residence was Najrān in Yemen. Among his descendants were al-Sayyid and al-ʿĀqib, the two bishops of Najrān who sought to enter into mutual imprecation with the Messenger of God ﷺ.

The Judges from Quraysh, then from Banū Hāshim

(ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib) ibn Hāshim;

(al-Zubayr) ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib;

(Abū Ṭālib) ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib.

Among Banū Umayya: (Ḥarb) ibn Umayya; (Abū Sufyān) ibn Ḥarb.

Among Banū Makhzūm: (al-ʿAdl), who was al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm.

Among Banū Zuhra: (al-ʿAlāʾ) ibn Jāriya ibn Sumayr ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Salama ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Ghayra al-Thaqafī, an ally of Banū Zuhra.

/ Among Banū Sahm: (Qays) ibn ʿAdī ibn Saʿd ibn Sahm. The poet says of him:

As though, in nobility, he were Qays ibn ʿAdī

And (al-ʿĀṣ) ibn Wāʾil; and (Hāshim) ibn Saʿīd ibn Sahm.

Among Banū ʿAdī: (Nufayl) ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Riyāḥ ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qurṭ ibn Razāḥ ibn ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb.

Among Kināna: (Ṣafwān) ibn Umayya ibn Muḥarrith ibn Khaml ibn Shaqq ibn Ruqba ibn Mukhdaḥ ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Thaʿlaba ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Mālik ibn Kināna.

And (Salmā) ibn Nawfal ibn Muʿāwiya ibn ʿUrwa ibn Ṣakhr ibn Yaʿmar ibn Nufātha ibn ʿAdī ibn al-Dayl. The poet says of him:

Peoples hold sway, though they are no lords; the lord renowned is Salmā ibn Nawfal.

And (al-Shaddākh), who was Yaʿmar ibn ʿAwf ibn Kaʿb ibn ʿĀmir ibn Layth ibn Bakr. He was called Shaddākh because he settled blood claims between Quraysh and Khuzāʿa.

Quraysh had fought Khuzāʿa and wanted to expel them from Mecca.

The two parties agreed to accept Yaʿmar’s arbitration. He judged between them and made their blood claims equal, ruling that Khuzāʿa should not be expelled from Mecca.

Among Banū Asad ibn Khuzayma: (Suwayd) ibn Rabīʿa ibn Ḥuḏār ibn Murra ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Saʿd ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Dūdān ibn Asad.

Among Banū Tamīm: (Rabīʿa) ibn Mukhāshin ibn Muʿāwiya ibn Sharīf ibn Jarwa ibn Usayyid ibn ʿAmr ibn Tamīm. He would sit on a wooden throne in a wooden pavilion, and so he was called “Possessor of the Wooden Platforms.” Al-Aswad ibn Yaʿfur says of him:

I surely know, beyond what you have told me, that the path is the path of the Possessor of the Wooden Platforms.

/ His father, (Mukhāshin), had also been a judge before him. And (Aktham) ibn Ṣayfī ibn Riyāḥ ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Mukhāshin; (Ḍamra) ibn Ḍamra ibn Jābir ibn Quṭn ibn Nahshal ibn Dārim; (Ḥājib) ibn Zurāra ibn ʿAdus ibn Zayd ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Dārim; and (al-Aqraʿ) ibn Ḥābis ibn ʿIqāl ibn Muḥammad ibn Sufyān ibn Mujāshiʿ ibn Dārim.

Among Qays: (ʿĀmir) ibn al-Ẓarb ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAbbād ibn Yashkur ibn ʿAdwān—who was al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAmr ibn Qays; and (Harm) ibn Quṭba ibn Sayyār ibn ʿAmr, who was al-ʿAshrāʾ ibn Jābir ibn ʿUqayl ibn Hilāl ibn Sumayy ibn Māzin ibn Fazāra. To him were referred the rival claims of ʿĀmir ibn al-Ṭufayl and ʿAlqama ibn ʿUlātha. And (Sinān) ibn Abī Ḥāritha ibn Murra ibn Nashba ibn Ghayẓ ibn Murra ibn ʿAwf ibn Saʿd ibn Dhubyān; and (Ghaylān) ibn Salama ibn Muʿattib ibn Mālik ibn Kaʿb ibn ʿAmr ibn Saʿd ibn ʿAwf ibn Thaqīf, who was Qusayy. During the days of the pilgrimage season, he would sit: one day judging between people, one day reciting his poetry, and one day they would gaze upon his face.

Among Rabīʿa ibn Nizār: (Bishr) ibn ʿAmr ibn Mirthid ibn Saʿd ibn Mālik ibn Ḍubayʿa ibn Qays ibn Thaʿlaba; (al-Ḥārith) ibn ʿAbbād ibn Ḍubayʿa ibn Qays ibn Thaʿlaba; (Imruʾ al-Qays) ibn Abān ibn Kaʿb ibn Zuhayr ibn Jusham ibn Bakr ibn Ḥubayb ibn ʿAmr ibn Ghanm ibn Taghlib ibn Wāʾil; (ʿĀmir) al-Ḍaḥḥiyān ibn Saʿd ibn al-Khazraj ibn Taym Allāh ibn al-Nimr ibn Qāsiṭ. He would sit for people at midmorning. And (al-Juʿayd) ibn Ṣabra ibn Ḍamra ibn al-Dayl ibn Shann ibn Afṣā ibn ʿAbd al-Qays ibn Afṣā; and (Qays) ibn Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh Dhū al-Judayn ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Hammām ibn Murra ibn Dhuhl ibn Shaybān.

/ Among Iyād: (Wakiʿ) ibn Salama ibn Zahr ibn Iyād, who was the builder of the palace at Ḥazwara in Mecca. They have said many things about him: some said he was a soothsayer, and others said he was one of the truthful ones. Among his sayings was: “A nursing woman, a weaning woman, one who deposits, one who uproots, severance, calamity, the maintaining of kinship ties, and good speech: your Lord claims that He will surely reward good with recompense and evil with punishment.” When death came upon him, he gathered Iyād and then said:

“Listen to my counsel: speech is two words. The matter comes after explanation. Follow whoever is rightly guided, and reject whoever goes astray. Every sheep is tethered by its own leg.”

He was the first to say this phrase, and it became a proverb. And (Quss) ibn Sāʿida al-Iyādī ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAdī ibn Mālik ibn Abd Kān ibn al-Nimr ibn Wāʾila ibn al-Ṭamathān ibn ʿAwḏ Manāt ibn Yaqdum ibn Afṣā. And (Ḥanẓala) ibn Nahd ibn Zayd ibn Layth ibn Sūd ibn Aslam ibn al-Ḥāf ibn Quḍāʿa. The early poet says of Ḥanẓala:

Ḥanẓala ibn Nahd

The finest young man among Maʿadd

Among Yemen: (ʿAmr) ibn Ḥumama ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Rāfiʿ ibn Saʿd ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Luʾayy ibn ʿĀmir ibn Ghānim ibn Duhmān ibn Munahhib ibn Daws.

Provisioners for the Caravan

(al-Aswad) ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā;

(Musāfir) ibn Abī ʿAmr ibn Umayya;

(Abū Umayya) ibn al-Mughīra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm; and (Zamʿa) ibn al-Aswad ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn Asad.

Generous Men of the Jāhiliyya

Among Quraysh: (Hāshim) ibn ʿAbd Manāf; and (Umayya) ibn ʿAbd Shams.

/ Among Banū Taym ibn Murra: (Gold-Drinker), who was ʿUthmān ibn ʿAmr ibn Kaʿb ibn Saʿd ibn Taym ibn Murra. His father was (al-Sayyāl), who was ʿAmr ibn Kaʿb. And (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Judʿān ibn ʿAmr al-Sayyāl. When ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān grew old, his kinsmen confined him. Whenever he gave anyone something, they would go after the recipient and take it from him. So when a petitioner asked him, he would say:

“Stay close to me when I sit, for I will slap you. Be satisfied with nothing but a slap from me, or ransom your slap with a generous ransom that pleases you…”

…or ransom your slap with a generous ransom that pleases you.” Ibn Qays al-Ruqayyāt says of him:

The one who, if he points at you to slap you, makes a slap followed by a gift and a grant.

They related that Umayya ibn Abī al-Ṣalt came to ʿAbd Allāh, who had two singing girls with him. When he saw them, he said, “Good morning, Abū Zuhayr!” Then he began to recite:

“Should I mention my need, or has your modesty sufficed me? Modesty is your nature.

A friend, whom neither morning nor evening changes from fine conduct.

Your land is every virtue built by Banū Taym, and you are its sky.

If a man praises you one day, praise alone suffices him without further appeal.

You vie with the wind in generosity and glory when winter drives the dog into its lair.”

He said to him, “Take whichever one you wish by the hand.” He took one of them. Then he went out to the assemblies of Quraysh, and they said to him, “Umayya! You went to our elder and chief, who had two slave girls to entertain him, and you took one of them from him.” Umayya felt ashamed of this and went back. When ʿAbd Allāh saw him, he said to him, “Wait, until I tell you what brought you back. You passed by Quraysh, and they said such and such to you. I pledge to God that you shall take the other one too, for one of them is only fitting with the other.” He took them both and went out, saying:

Your gift adorns a man when you favor him with abundance; not every gift is an adornment.

It is no shame for a man to humble himself before you, though some requests are shameful.

Among Banū Makhzūm: (Hishām) ibn al-Mughīra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. Quraysh took his death as a date by which to reckon time. Buhayr ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿĀmir ibn Salama ibn Qushayr says of him:

The valley of Mecca has become desolate, as though Hishām were no longer on earth.

His two sons were (Abū Jahl) and (al-Ḥārith), sons of Hishām.

Aḥmad ibn Muḥammad ibn Isḥāq al-Musayyibī mentioned that my father told me, from a respected shaykh among our companions, who told me: al-Waqqāṣī told me, from al-Zuhrī, from Abū Ḥayya, from Abū Dharr ﵀—who said: I came to Mecca to perform the lesser pilgrimage and said, “Is there no one to host me?” They said, “Indeed, there are many, and the nearest of them is al-Ḥārith ibn Hishām.” He said: I came to his door and said, “Is there no food for a guest?” His maidservant said to me, “Indeed.” She brought out for me some raisins in her hand. I said, “Why did you not put them on a platter?” Then I realized that I was a guest.

She said, “Come in.” I went in, and there was al-Ḥārith on a chair, with bowls before him containing bread and meat, and hides bearing raisins. He said, “Help yourself.” So I ate.

Then he said, “This is for you.” I stayed three days, then returned to Medina and told the Prophet God bless him—about it. He said, “He is indeed a noble son of a noble. I wish he would become Muslim!”

Al-Kalbī reported in his chain of transmission, from two men of Banū Sulaym, brothers, who said: “One year we entered Mecca to perform the lesser pilgrimage, but found neither trade nor hospitality there. While we were in that state, we saw some people walking purposefully. We asked, ‘Where are these people going?’ We were told:

‘They are going for food.’ We joined their group and came to a house. We entered it, and there we saw a dark-complexioned, squinting man, seated on a couch and wearing a black robe, with bowls full of bread and meat. We sat down and ate. I became full before my brother, and said to him, ‘How much are you going to eat? Aren’t you full?’ The man seated on the couch said, ‘Eat; food is made to be eaten.’

“When we had finished, we left by a door different from the one by which we had entered. There we found camels standing. We asked, ‘What are these camels?’ We were told, ‘They are for the food you saw.’ We asked about the man, the host, and found that he was Abū Jahl ibn Hishām.”

Among Banū Jumah: (Khalaf) ibn Wahb ibn Ḥudhāfa ibn Jumah;

and (Ubayy) ibn Khalaf. The Messenger of God ﷺ—killed him with his own hand on the day of Uḥud;

and (Umayya) ibn Khalaf ibn Wahb ibn Ḥudhāfa; (Ṣafwān) ibn Umayya ibn Khalaf; (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Ṣafwān; and (ʿAmr) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣafwān ibn Umayya. He was deeply rooted in generosity. He was a generous man, son of a generous man, son of— A generous man, son of a generous man, son of a generous man. ʿAmr, ʿAbd Allāh, and Ṣafwān lived to see Islam.

Among Banū Kinānah: (Salmā) ibn Nawfal ibn Muʿāwiyah ibn ʿUrwah ibn Ṣakhr ibn Yaʿmur ibn Nufāthah ibn ʿAdī ibn al-Dayl.

Among Banū Tamīm: (Mālik) ibn Ḥanẓalah, who was called “Ghuraf” for his generosity.

Al-Aswad ibn Yaʿfur said:

Among the family of Ghuraf, if I sought solace,I would find among them a model for the one who counts.

And (al-Qaʿqāʿ) ibn Maʿbad ibn Zurarah ibn ʿAdas ibn Zayd, who was “the torrent of the Euphrates.”

Al-Farazdaq said:

They were strengthened by Ḥājib and the two sons of ʿIqāl,and by al-Qaʿqāʿ, the torrent of the Euphrates.

And (Ṣaʿṣaʿah) ibn Nājiyah ibn ʿIqāl ibn Muḥammad ibn Sufyān ibn Mujāshiʿ.

He was the one who brought the buried-alive girls back to life. When God—mighty and sublime is He—sent His Prophet ﷺ Ṣaʿṣaʿah had with him one hundred slave girls, and four more whom he had taken from their fathers lest they be buried alive.

He has many reports. Al-Farazdaq said of him:

My grandfather is the one who prevented the burying-alive of girlsand revived the buried-alive child, so that he was not buried.

And (Ghālib) ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿah ibn Nājiyah. He was the one who staked his wealth in his contest of drinking bouts with Suḥaym ibn Wuthayl al-Riyāḥī. The Kalb wagered against him, as they did against Ṭalḥah ibn Qays ibn ʿĀṣim and ʿAlī al-Salīl ibn Masʿūd ibn Qays ibn Masʿūd al-Shaybānī, as to which of them was the most generous. Ghālib proved the most generous of them. The others took the stake wagered against Ghālib. Ghālib ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿah lived to see Islam.

Among Banū Ḍabbah ibn Udd: (Juwayn) ibn Ẓahr. He provided sixty allotments of one quarter each.

He distributed a thousand she-camels, and his cup was in his hand before he drank from it. Al-ʿAlāʾ ibn Qurṭah said of him:

Juwayn came to us, from6,with sixty quarter-allotments and a thousand branded camels.

He distributed a herd, his cup in his right hand,and came with plunder like young palm shoots wrapped up.

And (al-Ḥurr) ibn Manīʿ, brother of Banū Dhuhl ibn Mālik ibn Bakr ibn Saʿd ibn Ḍabbah.

In a single day he gave away one hundred milk-camels, and gave their offspring to people other than those who had delivered them. Then, when they had become pregnant and had weaned their young the following year, he placed their coverings upon them. He slaughtered them and distributed their coverings. Ibn Faswah said of him:

By your life, since al-Ḥurr’s hair turned gray,he has been like the dawn, growing only brighter.

No man, however generous, has any meritover al-Ḥurr, unless Ibn Manīʿ sins.

A man who had run short of the wealth of Ibn Shaʿthah received it,though he had no close kinship and no intercessor.

Have you not seen that al-Ḥurr was not harmedby three hundred, who all shouted as they came together?

He lived to see Islam.

Among al-Ribāb, of Banū ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd Manāh ibn Udd: (al-Ḥārith) ibn Murrah ibn Jashm ibn ʿAwf. In a single morning he gave away a thousand she-camels.

Among Qays: (Haram) ibn Sinān ibn Abī Ḥārithah ibn Murrah ibn Nashbah ibn Ghayẓ ibn Murrah ibn ʿAwf ibn Saʿd ibn Dhubyān. The poets have said much about his generosity and made him a byword for it.

Among Rabīʿah ibn Nizār: (Yaqẓān) ibn Zayd ibn Arqam al-Ḥanafī. Because of his generosity, he was called “the rival of the wind.”

Among Banū Shaybān: (al-Khaṣīb), whose name was ʿĀmir ibn ʿAmr ibn Abī Rabīʿah ibn Dhuhl ibn Shaybān. He was called “al-Khaṣīb” because of his generosity. And (Qays) ibn Masʿūd ibn Qays ibn Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Hammām ibn Murrah. He had one hundred camels set aside for guests. Whenever their number decreased, he replenished it. Al-Shammākh said of him:

Give them their milk, just as you gavethe milk-camels of Banū Qays ibn Masʿūd.

And (Hawdhah) ibn Murrah al-Shaybānī. He swore that he would provide food for as long as the north wind blew.

Rifāʿ ibn al-Lajlāj said of him:

Among us is the one who made his camp at al-Buḥayrah in winter,and fed the people of Syria without keeping count.

It is said that his hearth’s ash remained at al-Buḥayrah.

Among Iyād: (Kaʿb) ibn Māmmah ibn ʿAmr ibn Thaʿlabah ibn Salūl ibn Kinānah ibn Shabābah ibn Saʿd ibn al-Dayl ibn Ushayb ibn Burd ibn Afṣā ibn Daʿmī. He has a number of reports. Among them is that he travelled with a party of Rabīʿah, Muḍar, Quḍāʿah, and Yemen. Their water ran out, and they began drinking it by the measure of a pebble.

When they stopped and divided the water, a man of Namir ibn Qāsiṭ saw that Kaʿb had taken his share. Kaʿb said, “Give your Namirī brother a drink for the morning.” The party then set out, and Kaʿb did not drink, for he had given his share to the Namirī. When they stopped, they divided the water again.

The Namirī looked at Kaʿb and gave him his own share as well. Kaʿb was so overcome by thirst that he could not rise. They said to him, “Kaʿb, you are near the water; get up.”

He could not. So they left him under a covering from the beasts and birds, and he died. Al-Farazdaq said of him:

I was like Kaʿb, except that my deathwas delayed from its appointed day at al-Ḍajāʿim.

When Kaʿb said, “Have you had your fill, Ibn Qāsiṭ?”he would say to him, “Give me more, you who moisten the throats!”

His father, Māmmah ibn ʿAmr, said:

Kaʿb came down to the water. Then he was told,“Kaʿb, go to the water, for you are its water-drawer”—but he did not go.

No man of the common folk have I ever given wineto drink while thirsty, with water, when its wine-jar had grown cold,

except Kaʿb, son of Māmmah. Then it was his turn to bedriven from death, though he was a free woman, a burning ember.

Among Yemen: (Ḥātim) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd ibn al-Ḥashraj ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn ʿAdī ibn Akhzam ibn Hazūmah ibn Rabīʿah ibn Jarwal ibn Thaʿl ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Ghawth ibn Ṭayyiʾ. And (Aws) ibn Ḥārithah ibn Lām, brother of Banū Jadīlah, of Ṭayyiʾ. Their reports are too numerous to count. And through them and through Kaʿb ibn Māmmah, a proverbial comparison is made. Ḥarīr7 said in praise of ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz:

Neither Kaʿb ibn Māmmah nor Ibn Saʿdīwas more generous than you, O ʿUmar, the generous one.

The Generous Men of Islam

Banū Hāshim: (ʿUbayd Allāh) ibn al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. He has reports about his generosity. Among them is that a money-changer set out before the people with nine thousand dinars.

His creditors pressed him, and he asked them to give him a respite so that he might conduct business and collect his debts.

They said, “No guarantor will satisfy us unless ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās guarantees you.” He came to his door and asked permission to enter. He went in, and his creditors entered with him. ʿUbayd Allāh was sitting there making a rope for a flock of sheep in front of him; the sheep were drinking. The money-changer told him his story. ʿUbayd Allāh said to his creditors, “Bring me your documents.” They brought them, and he tore them up, saying, “They are my responsibility.” He paid off nine thousand dinars on the man’s behalf, though there had been no prior business dealings or bond of kinship between them. And (al-Saffāḥ), who was ʿAbd Allāh al-Aṣghar ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās. And (Muḥammad) ibn Jaʿfar ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās. And (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib. He has many remarkable reports. Among them is that a man came to him while his riding camel had been brought forward for him to mount. The man said, “I am a man stranded without support.” ʿAbd Allāh gave him the camel, with all that was on it. The servant got up to take the sword from its scabbard. ʿAbd Allāh shouted at him, “Leave it, you fool!” Then he said to the man:

“My friend, do not be cheated out of the sword, for I bought it for a thousand dinars.”

Among the reports is that a woman came to him and said, “May your maternal aunt ransom you! This is a chicken. I raised it as a young hen and fattened it. I saw no one more deserving of it than you.” He took it from her and ordered that she be given a thousand dirhams. She said, “May God preserve you and help you in what you do.” He said, “Give her another thousand.” She said, “May God preserve your noble self.” He said, “Give her another thousand.”

She said, “May God grant me and all people the joy of your continued life.” He said, “Give her another thousand.”

She said, “May God make me a ransom for this kindly soul.” He said, “Give her another thousand.” She said, “That is enough, you spendthrift!” He said to her, “By God, if you had kept going, I would have kept going.” Among the reports is that a client of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ ibn al-Aswad al-ʿAdawī came to his master ʿAbd Allāh in Medina. One day they were discussing the generosity of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar. The client said, “We hear stories of ʿAbd Allāh’s generosity that surpass anything we have heard of anyone else.”

His master ʿAbd Allāh said to him, “Believe everything you hear about him; he is even more generous than that.” The client said, “By God, I would love to see some evidence of it.”

Ibn Muṭīʿ said to him, “Bring me a sheet of paper.” The client brought one. Ibn Muṭīʿ wrote on it a written acknowledgment of a debt owed to a certain man, naming the client, for three hundred dinars due immediately. He gave him the paper and said, “Go to him, greet him, and say: ‘This is an acknowledgment of a debt you owe me, Abū Jaʿfar!’ If he asks you how much it is, tell him the amount.” The client did so. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar said to him, “Is this money owed to you by us?” He replied, “Yes.”

ʿAbd Allāh said, “Boy, take him by the hand and give him the money written on his paper.” The client took the dinars.

Ibn Jaʿfar returned the paper to him. The client went back to Ibn Muṭīʿ, told him what had happened, and said, “I have never seen a man like this.” Ibn Muṭīʿ said, “Keep the dinars with you and spend none of them. Leave it ten days, then go to him again with the paper and say: ‘This is an acknowledgment of a debt you owe me.’” The client said to him, “I’m afraid he’ll recognize me and I’ll be exposed.” Ibn Muṭīʿ said, “Do as I tell you.” He went to Ibn Jaʿfar and said those words. Ibn Jaʿfar did no more than say, “Boy, give him what will suffice him.” The client took another three hundred dinars.

They returned the paper to him and did not take it. He came back to Ibn Muṭīʿ, greatly astonished. Ibn Muṭīʿ said, “Keep the money with you, leave it for a month, then go back to him.” He did so. Once again Ibn Jaʿfar did no more than say:

“Boy, give him what he says.”

The dinars now amounted to nine hundred. Ibn Muṭīʿ said to him, “Bring me all the money now.” The client brought it to him, and Ibn Muṭīʿ took it and rode to Ibn Jaʿfar with that client. He said to him, “Abū Jaʿfar, fear God and look to your own interests.” Ibn Jaʿfar said, “What is it?” Ibn Muṭīʿ said, “This client of mine came to you with a document claiming that you owed him three hundred dinars, although there had never been any transaction between you. You gave him the money and did not take his document. He came to you with it again and again, and you gave him nine hundred dinars.” Ibn Jaʿfar said, “Are you suggesting that I do not know what I own and what I owe?” Ibn Muṭīʿ said, “That is what I suspect.” Ibn Jaʿfar said, “Do not suppose what you have supposed. I know every dirham that is mine and every dirham that I owe. I knew that what your client claimed was false. But I had to choose between saying, ‘You owe me nothing,’ and his saying, ‘Yes, you do,’ with someone listening and my being left between one who believes me and one who disbelieves me; or else giving him what he claimed. Giving it to him seemed easier for me and preferable in my eyes.” Ibn Muṭīʿ said to him, “As for me, I advise you to fear God and look to your own interests.” Then he said, “Boy, bring what you have with you.” He handed it to Ibn Jaʿfar. Ibn Jaʿfar said, “What is this?” Ibn Muṭīʿ said, “This is the money my client took from you.” Ibn Jaʿfar said, “May God forgive you! By God, nothing that has left my possession will ever return to it. Let the man keep it as lawful and wholesome wealth.”

His reports are too numerous to recount. And (Ṭalḥat al-Khayr), namely Ṭalḥah ibn al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵀. His mother was [Umm] Isḥāq, daughter of Ṭalḥah ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUthmān. He was a generous provider of food and much praised. He left no descendants. And (ʿUbayd Allāh) ibn Abī Bakrah, client of the Messenger of God ﷺ.

Banū Umayyah: (Saʿīd) ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah.

Every day he slaughtered a camel to feed the people. And (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn ʿĀmir ibn Kurayz ibn Rabīʿah ibn Ḥabīb ibn ʿAbd Shams. He had the watering places of ʿArafāt and the market of Baṣrah, which he bought with his own money and gave to its people, so they paid no land tax on it. And (Khālid) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khālid ibn Asīd ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah ibn ʿAbd Shams.

He was generous and much praised.

Among Banū Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā: (Ḥamzah) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām ibn Khuwaylid ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā. He was [generous] and much praised.

Among Banū Zuhrah: (Ṭalḥat al-Nadā) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd [ibn] al-Ḥārith ibn Zuhrah. A man would come to him with a request, and he would seat him Then another would come and he would seat him, then a third would come. When they had reached the number of his garments, he went inside, threw his cloak to the first, and his shirt to the second, then said, “Hand me a garment so I can cover myself with it.” Then he threw his waist-wrapper to the third.

From Banū Taym: (Ṭalḥa) ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAmr ibn Kaʿb ibn Saʿd ibn Taym ibn Murra. He was Ṭalḥa the Good. A man came to him and asked him for something, invoking the kinship between them. He said to him: “This is my walled garden at such-and-such a place. I have been offered six hundred thousand dirhams for it. The money will come to me this evening. If you wish, the money is yours; if you wish, the garden is yours.” He was one of the members of the Consultative Council. Also (Yaʿqūb) ibn Ṭalḥa ibn ʿUbayd Allāh, and (ʿUmar) ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Maʿmar al-Taymī. There are reports about his generosity, including this one: Abū Ḥazāba al-Tamīmī had a slave-girl called Basbāsa, and he was passionately attached to her. Need forced him to sell her. ʿUmar ibn ʿUbayd Allāh bought her from him for a great sum. When he had received the money, the slave-girl went to enter. He caught hold of her garment and then said:

“Do you remember a need for Basbāsa today,

which came as anguish from the need of one who remembers?

Had the passage of time not kept me from you,

nothing but death would have separated us, so forgive me.”

“I take upon myself an aching grief at your parting,

with which I commune to a heart long given to thought.

Peace be upon you: there will be no visit between us,

nor union, unless Ibn Maʿmar so wills.”

Ibn Maʿmar said, “Then I do so will. She is yours, and so is her price.” He had bought her from him for one hundred thousand dirhams. Also (Ṭalḥa) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Abī Bakr al-Ṣiddīq ﵀. He was Ṭalḥa of the Dirhams, and was very generous. Al-Ḥazīn al-Kinānī said of him:

“If, Ṭalḥa, you gave me

a sturdy she-camel that makes the stones seem light,

your help was no use to me once,

or twice, but many times.

Your father was the one who confirmed the Chosen One,

and journeyed with the Chosen One wherever he journeyed.

Your mother is a fair-skinned Taymī woman;

when people are counted, she is among the finest.”

And from Banū Makhzūm: (al-Azraq), that is, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd Shams ibn al-Mughīra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. He was generous and much praised. Ibn al-Zubayr appointed him governor of Yemen and then dismissed him; he gave generously both while governor and after his dismissal. Abū Duhbal al-Jumaḥī said of him:

“He gave as an emir and as one deposed; yet his virtues were not stripped from him— they enveloped him, and he did not cast them off.”

And (al-Ḥakam) ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn Ḥanṭab ibn ʿUbayd al-Makhzūmī. He was generous and much praised. And (al-Mughīra) al-Aʿwar ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Hishām al-Makhzūmī. In Kūfa there were generous men who provided food, but he outdid them, until they withdrew and left him to do so alone. Among them were (ʿĪsā) ibn Mūsā ibn Ṭalḥa ibn ʿUbayd Allāh al-Taymī; (ʿAbd al-Malik) ibn Bishr ibn Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam; (Khālid) ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿUqba ibn Abī Muʿayṭ; and (the Banū ʿUmāra) ibn ʿUqba ibn Abī Muʿayṭ—all generous men who provided food. Also (Muḥammad) ibn Ḥāṭib ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Maʿmar ibn Ḥabīb al-Jumaḥī, who was generous; and (Ṣukhayr) ibn Abī al-Jahm ibn Ḥudhayfa ibn Ghānim al-ʿAdawī, who was generous. Al-Mughīra al-Aʿwar spread out leather mats in Kūfa and piled ḥays on them, so that riders and those on foot could eat from it. Al-Aqayshir al-Asadī said of him:

“The sea has come to you, overflowing over Quraysh,

al-Mughīrī; now Ibn Bishr has slipped away,

and the young goat—the young goat of Taym—has slipped away too,

when he saw that what he gave was but a little.

The fleeces of ʿUqba have healed me,

as have the kin of al-Ḥāṭib and the kin of Ṣakhr.

So do not be misled by their fine appearance,

nor by a saddle on dun horses and tigers.”

And from Banū Fihr: (Nahshal) ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Wahb al-Fihrī. He was generous.

And from Banū Tamīm: (Muḥammad) ibn ʿUmayr ibn ʿAṭārid ibn Ḥājib ibn Zurāra. In a single morning at Ādharbayjān, he mounted a thousand men on a thousand horses; they had fled to him from Bakr ibn Wāʾil. And (ʿAttāb) ibn Warqāʾ al-Riyāḥī.

He was generous and much praised.

And from Qays: (Asmāʾ) ibn Khārija ibn Ḥiṣn ibn Ḥudhayfa ibn Badr ibn ʿAmr al-Fazārī. He was generous and much praised. He was the one who compensated a Bedouin forty thousand dirhams for his dog.

And from Rabīʿa: (ʿIkrima al-Fayyāḍ) ibn Ribʿī ibn ʿUmayr ibn Ṣubayḥ ibn Lāy ibn Muʾāla ibn ʿĀmir ibn Mālik ibn Taym Allāh ibn Thaʿlaba ibn ʿUkāba.

And (al-Ḥārith) ibn Murra al-ʿAbdī distributed a thousand head of livestock in one day and mounted men on five hundred horses. And (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Sawwār ibn Hammām al-ʿAbdī.

He was at the frontier of Sind, with four thousand men. No other fire was ever lit alongside his. One night he looked and saw a man cooking. He asked about the fire. They said:

“It is for a man whose wife gave birth this night; he is making her khabīṣ.” He ordered his steward to serve the people khabīṣ along with their food.

And from Yemen: (Qays) ibn Saʿd ibn ʿUbāda ibn Dulaym ibn Ḥāritha ibn Ḥazma ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Ṭarīf ibn al-Khazraj ibn Sāʿida. Qays and seven of his forefathers, back to Ṭarīf, were generous men. Each generous man provided food. There are reports about Qays, including this one: He fell ill and thought his brothers were late in visiting him. He was told, “They are ashamed to come to you because of the debts they owe you.” He ordered a herald to proclaim, “Whoever owes Qays a debt is released from it.” His visitors became so numerous that they broke his staircase. And (ʿArāba) ibn Aws ibn Qayẓī al-Awsī. Al-Shammākh passed by camels carrying raisins and said to him, “Give me some of these raisins.” He replied, “Take charge of the whole caravan.” Al-Shammākh said, “Are you mocking my father?” He said, “Take it, as I tell you.” So he took the camels and all they carried. ʿArāba became poor and lost his sight. A man came to him and said, “I am stranded: I have neither a mount nor provisions.” ʿArāba was leaning on two black slaves as he made for the mosque. He said to the man, “Alas! By God, you have come to me when I am impoverished, and I own nothing on the face of the earth but these two slaves. Take them: sell one for the price of a mount, and the other for the price of your provisions.” The petitioner said, “By God, I would not strip you of your wings.” ʿArāba said, “They are free if you do not accept them. If you wish, take them; if you wish, set them free.” And (Ṭalḥa) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khalaf al-Khuzāʿī, known as Ṭalḥa of the Ṭalḥas. He was among the foremost in generosity. And (ʿAdī) ibn Ḥātim al-Ṭāʾī. ʿAmr ibn Ḥurayth asked him for his daughter’s hand. He said, “I will marry her to you on my terms.” ʿAmr feared he might press him hard in setting the terms.

So he held back and consulted others. He was told, “Marry her on his terms; he is a noble man.” He went to him, and accepted his terms. ʿAdī praised and glorified God, then said, “I have married her to you according to the Sunna: for four hundred and eighty dirhams.” ʿAmr ibn Ḥurayth sent him, as a gift for his daughter, forty thousand and a bundle of garments. ʿAdī divided them among those sitting with him and outfitted his daughter from his own means. A notable man of Kūfa once borrowed ʿAdī’s cooking pots for a wedding feast. ʿAdī slaughtered camels and filled them, then had them carried to the borrower in a dawhūq, full. He said, “This is how we lend our pots.” And (Khālid) ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Qasrī, governor of Iraq. There are many reports about his generosity. And (Qubayṣa) ibn al-Muhallab ibn Abī Ṣufra al-ʿAtakī.

The intercalators of the months were from Kināna, and were called the Qalāmis; one of them was called a Qalammus. They were the Arabs’ jurists and those who gave them religious rulings. The Arabs would sometimes live by their swords and spears, so observance of the three consecutive sacred months became difficult for them. One of these Qalāmis would therefore stand on the Days of Sacrifice at the Ḥijr and give them a ruling. No one would ask anyone else about anything. One man would stand at the door of the Kaʿba, and another would stand in the Ḥijr; each would say, “I am the one who is not to be blamed or censured, and whose decree is not to be overturned.” If a group came wanting to raid during Muḥarram, they would ask him to postpone Muḥarram. He would calculate for them and say, “This year, Ṣafar is the first [month].” He would make this calculation in a way that did not cause the year to revolve. They did not follow the lunar sightings and did not know what that was. They would depart on that basis: Muḥarram was postponed and Ṣafar brought forward. Thus Muḥarram was made lawful one year and sacred the next. The first to intercalate was (Ḥudhayfa) ibn ʿAbd ibn Nahm ibn ʿAdī ibn ʿĀmir ibn Thaʿlaba ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Mālik ibn Kināna.

Then his son (Qalaʿ) ibn Ḥudhayfa; then (ʿAbbād) ibn Qalaʿ; then (Qalaʿ) ibn ʿAbbād ibn Qalaʿ; then (Umayya) ibn Qalaʿ; then (ʿAwf) ibn Umayya ibn Qalaʿ; then (Junāda) ibn ʿAwf ibn Umayya ibn Qalaʿ, who continued into the Islamic period.

Those who incited [against the Prophet] from Quraysh

(Abū Lahab), whose name was ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. And (al-Ḥakam) ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ, the banished one. And (ʿUqba) ibn Abī Muʿayṭ ibn Abī ʿAmr ibn Umayya. The Messenger of God ﷺ beheaded him in captivity on his return from Badr.

He said, “Muḥammad! Am I the only one among Quraysh to be killed?” ʿUmar said, “A cup has clinked that is not one of them.”

He said, “Then who will care for the children?” The Prophet ﷺ said, “The Fire.” And (ʿAdī) ibn Ḥamrāʾ al-Thaqafī. He and Abū Lahab were neighbors of the Prophet ﷺ. And (ʿAmr) ibn al-Ṭulaṭila al-Khuzāʿī.

None of them became Muslim except al-Ḥakam, though his religious commitment was suspect.

Those of Quraysh who mocked [the Prophet] and died in various ways as unbelievers

Among them were (al-ʿĀṣ) ibn Wāʾil al-Sahmī; (al-Ḥārith) ibn Qays ibn ʿAdī al-Kaʿbī, who was devoted to idols. Whenever he passed a stone that he liked better than the one he already had, he would take it and throw away the one he had. It was concerning him that this was revealed: ‘Have you seen the one who has taken his own desire as his god?’ (Qurʾān 45:23). And (al-Aswad) ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā.

And (al-Walīd) ibn al-Mughīra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. And (al-Aswad) ibn ʿAbd Yaghūth ibn Wahb ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhra.

Those who divided [the mountain passes]

They were seventeen men from Quraysh who divided up the mountain passes of Mecca.

Whenever they attended the pilgrimage season, they would turn people away from the Messenger of God ﷺ.

It was concerning them that this was revealed: “As We sent down upon those who divided [the revelation into parts].” (Qurʾān 15:90). According to what Hishām ibn al-Kalbī related from his father, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, they were as follows: (From Banū ʿAbd Shams) three men: Ḥanẓala ibn Abī Sufyān, and ʿUtba and Shayba, the sons of Rabīʿa ibn ʿAbd Shams.

(From Banū Makhzūm) seven men: Abū Jahl, al-ʿĀṣ, Abū Qays ibn al-Walīd, Qays ibn al-Fākih, Zuhayr ibn Abī Umayya, al-Aswad ibn ʿAbd al-Asad, and Ṣayfī ibn al-Sāʾib.

(From Banū ʿAbd al-Dār) one man: al-Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Kalada.

(From Banū Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā) two men: Abū al-Bakhtarī ibn Hāshim and Zamʿa ibn al-Aswad.

(From Banū Sahm) two men: Munabbih and Nubayh, the sons of al-Ḥajjāj.

(From Banū Jumah) two men: Umayya ibn Khalaf, And Aws ibn Muʿayr, the brother of Abū Maḥdhūrah. They were two of the most eminent men of Banū Jumḥ. Abū al-Mundhir Hishām related, from his father, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās ﵁—concerning the words of God, Glorious and Mighty: “When it was said to them, ‘What has your Lord sent down?’ they said, ‘Good’” (Qurʾān 16:30): “This was revealed concerning ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd and his companions, whom the Messenger of God ﷺ—sent to answer the muqassimūn at the mountain passes of Mecca. With each of the polytheists was a Muslim who refuted what the polytheist said.”

Quraysh’s heretics

(Abū Sufyān) ibn Ḥarb; he embraced Islam.

(ʿUqbah) ibn Abī Muʿayṭ; he was beheaded in captivity.

(Ubayy) ibn Khalaf al-Jumaḥī; the Messenger of God ﷺ—killed him with his own hand on the day of Uḥud.

(Al-Naḍr) ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Kildah, of Banū ʿAbd al-Dār; the Messenger of God ﷺ—beheaded him in captivity.

(Munabbih) and (Nabīh), the two sons of al-Ḥajjāj al-Sahmī; they were killed at Badr. And (al-ʿĀṣ) ibn Wāʾil al-Sahmī.

And (al-Walīd) ibn al-Mughīrah al-Makhzūmī. They learned heresy from the Christians of al-Ḥīrah.

None of them embraced Islam except Abū Sufyān.

Those who provided food for the war at Badr

(Abū Jahl), namely ʿAmr ibn Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah, slaughtered ten camels.

And (Umayyah) ibn Khalaf al-Jumaḥī slaughtered nine camels. (Suhayl) ibn ʿAmr, of Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, slaughtered ten. (Shaybah) ibn Rabīʿah ibn ʿAbd Shams slaughtered nine. (ʿUtbah) ibn Rabīʿah ibn ʿAbd Shams slaughtered ten. (Munabbih) and (Nabīh), the two sons of al-Ḥajjāj al-Sahmī, slaughtered ten. (Al-ʿAbbās) ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib slaughtered ten. Muḥammad ibn ʿUmar al-Muzanī related that Quraysh overturned al-ʿAbbās’s cooking pots and did not eat their contents, because they knew he was inclined toward the Messenger of God ﷺ.

And (Abū al-Bukhtarī), al-ʿĀṣ ibn Hishām ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Asad, slaughtered ten. They were all killed at Badr. Al-ʿAbbās and Suhayl embraced Islam and became prominent Muslims.

The men of the īlāf

They were Qurayshites through whom God raised Quraysh up and sustained its poor. The īlāf were pacts: Hāshim, ʿAbd Shams, al-Muṭṭalib, and Nawfal, the sons of ʿAbd Manāf.

Hāshim’s trade journey was to Syria. He died at Ghazzah. Hāshim was the first to make the two journeys. Concerning him, al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥanash al-Sulamī, his maternal half-brother, said:

My brother Hāshim was no ordinary brother;

By God, Hāshim was no less than a lion.

Goodness was in his garment, in the grave’s hollow;

He took up the pact and came to the one who stayed behind.

Maṭrūd al-Khuzāʿī said:

The generous man died in Syria on the day he lay down, just as

Hāshim perished at Ghazzah—may he not be far away!

May the Lord of the courtyard never be far away! We visit him

As a sick man is visited, giving generously among the visitors.

His bowls overflow for whoever comes to him,

And his aid is given with tongue and hand.

ʿAbd Shams’s trade journey was to Abyssinia, and he died in Mecca. Al-Muṭṭalib’s trade journey was to Yemen, and he died at a place called Radmān. Nawfal’s trade journey was to Iraq, and he died at a place called Salmān. Each of these men was the chief of those who went out with him to trade in his direction. They had secured pacts for them from kings and the notables of the tribes. These were the lords of Quraysh and the ones who sustained them.

Maṭrūd ibn Kaʿb al-Khuzāʿī said:

The raiders and their sons

Are the best of fathers and mothers;

Among the white ones, each is a lord,

Sons of lords, descended from lords.

A grave at Radmān, a grave at Sal-

mān, and a grave near Ghazzah;

And a dead man who died near al-Ḥa-

jūn, east of the mountain passes.

He also said:

O man whose journey has turned him away,

Why not alight among the family of ʿAbd Manāf?

May your mother lose you! If you came to them,

They would shield you from hunger and wandering.

They are the ones who secure pacts from its horizons,

And who travel on the journey of the īlāf.

They face the wind each evening

Until the sun sets in al-Rajjāf.

The notables of Quraysh

In the pre-Islamic era, nobility and leadership among Quraysh belonged to Banū Quṣayy.

No one disputed their claim, nor did anyone boast over them. The people continued to submit to them, and they continued to be made leaders.

Quraysh had six distinctions, all held by Banū Quṣayy to the exclusion of the rest of Quraysh: the custodianship of the Kaʿbah, the provision of water, the provision of food for pilgrims, the council, the banner, and leadership. When Ḥarb ibn Umayyah died—Ḥarb had been the leader after al-Muṭṭalib—leadership and nobility became dispersed among Banū ʿAbd Manāf. Among Banū Hāshim were al-Zubayr, Abū Ṭālib, Ḥamzah, and al-ʿAbbās, the sons of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. Among Banū Umayyah was Abū Uḥayḥah, namely Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah, “the Turbaned One”: no one in Mecca would wear a turban of the same color as his, out of deference to him. This is what the poet meant:

A young woman whose father is among them, the Turbaned One,

And Marwān—few are his equals.

Among Banū al-Muṭṭalib was ʿAbd Yazīd ibn Hāshim ibn al-Muṭṭalib. This ʿAbd Yazīd was “pure, with no impurity in him.” Among Banū Nawfal was al-Muṭʿim ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal.

Among Banū Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā were Khuwaylid ibn Asad and ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith ibn Asad.

Quraysh’s distinctions in Islam were three: prophethood, the caliphate, and consultation.

Two of these belonged exclusively to Banū ʿAbd Manāf; in the third, consultation, they shared with Zuhrah, Taym, ʿAdī, and Asad. After the two shaykhs ﵃—the caliphate remained with Banū ʿAbd Manāf to the exclusion of Quraysh.

The tribes of the Perfumed Ones among Quraysh

Banū ʿAbd Manāf ibn Quṣayy; Banū Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Quṣayy; Banū Zuhrah ibn Kilāb; Banū Taym ibn Murrah ibn Kaʿb; and Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr ibn Mālik ibn al-Naḍr ibn Kinānah. They were a single hand in mutual support.

The tribes of the Confederates among Quraysh, the Blood-Lickers

ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy; Sahm and Jumḥ, the two sons of ʿAmr ibn Huṣayṣ ibn Kaʿb ibn Luʾayy; Makhzūm ibn Yaqaẓah ibn [Murrah ibn Kaʿb] ibn Luʾayy; and ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb ibn Luʾayy ibn Ghālib. They were called the Perfumed Ones and the Confederates because, when the descendants of Quṣayy increased, they wanted to take what was in the hands of Banū ʿAbd al-Dār. Quṣayy had assigned to ʿAbd al-Dār the custodianship of the Kaʿbah, the council, the provision of water, the provision of food for pilgrims, and the banner. Banū ʿAbd al-Dār refused to relinquish these things to them. So Quraysh split into factions.

ʿĀtikah daughter of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib brought out a bowl containing perfume. The tribes in the faction of Banū ʿAbd Manāf dipped their hands in the perfume and swore an alliance, and so they were called the Perfumed Ones.

The others slaughtered a camel and dipped their hands in its blood. A man from Banū ʿAdī licked some of that blood; they licked it and swore an alliance, and so they were called the Confederates.

Then they prepared for battle. Envoys went between them until they reached an agreement: Banū ʿAbd Manāf would have the provision of water and food for pilgrims, and Banū ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy the banner and custodianship of the Kaʿbah.

ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Qays al-Ruqayyāt said:

It lies between ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy,

When it is invoked, and ʿAbd Manāf;

Their forefathers are among the Perfumed Ones,

Then the Confederates’ chiefs prevailed.

The tribes of the Ḥilf al-Fuḍūl

Banū Hāshim; their allies, Banū al-Muṭṭalib; Banū Zuhrah; and Banū Taym.

Some scholars said that Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr were among them, but this was not agreed upon.

The cause of this alliance was that al-Zubayr ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān, and the leaders of these tribes assembled and swore an alliance: they would not allow anyone in Mecca to wrong another without supporting the wronged against the wrongdoer and securing his due for him.

The lowland tribes of Quraysh

Banū ʿAbd Manāf; Banū ʿAbd al-Dār; Banū ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā; Banū ʿAbd ibn Quṣayy ibn Kilāb; Banū Zuhrah ibn Kilāb; Banū Taym ibn Murrah;

Banū Makhzūm ibn Yaqaẓah ibn Murrah; Banū Sahm and Banū Jumḥ, the two sons of ʿAmr ibn Huṣayṣ ibn Kaʿb; Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb; and Banū Ḥisl ibn ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy; Banū Hilāl ibn Uhayb ibn Ḍabbah ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr; and Banū Hilāl ibn Mālik ibn Ḍabbah ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr.

They were called “lowlanders” because they entered the lowlands with Quṣayy.

The highland tribes of Quraysh

Banū Muʿayṣ ibn ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, and Taym al-Adram ibn Ghālib ibn Fihr.

Muḥārib and al-Ḥārith, the two sons of Fihr—except for Banū Hilāl ibn Uhayb ibn Ḍabbah and Banū Hilāl ibn Mālik ibn Ḍabbah, who were lowlanders.

The rest of Quraysh’s tribes were neither lowlanders nor highlanders. They were:

(Sāmah) ibn Luʾayy, who settled in ʿUmān; his descendants there are allies of the Azd of ʿUmān.

(Al-Ḥārith) ibn Luʾayy, who settled in al-Yamāmah; they are among Banū Hazān, of ʿAnazah ibn Asad ibn Rabīʿah ibn Nizār. Al-Ḥārith is Jusham. Jarīr said of them:

Sons of Jusham! You are not of Hazān, so trace yourselves

To the branch of the hills, from Luʾayy ibn Ghālib.

Do not give your daughters in marriage to the family of Ḍawr,

Nor to Shakīs; what a wretched tribe of strangers!

(Khuzaymah) ibn Luʾayy, who settled in the Jazīrah among Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Hammām ibn Murrah ibn Dhuhl ibn Shaybān. And (Saʿd) ibn Luʾayy, and (Banū ʿAwf) ibn Luʾayy, who settled among Ghaṭafān. It is said that ʿAwf ibn Saʿd ibn Dhubyān ibn Bughayḍ was this ʿAwf. Al-Ḥārith ibn Ẓālim al-Murrī, claiming Qurayshite ancestry on this account, said:

I raised the spear when they said, “Quraysh!” … and I likened the bearing and the tents.

My people are not Thaʿlabah ibn Saʿd,

Nor are they necks of Fazārah al-Shuʿrā.

The descendants of Saʿd ibn Luʾayy are called “Banānah.” The descendants of Khuzaymah ibn Luʾayy are called “the protectress of Quraysh.” The poet said:

The misguided Tujībī struck a blow

That returned Banānah to the sons of Shaybān.

The man of the ʿĀʾidhī tribe awaits a like blow,

For what is hidden has become as though it had happened.

When ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān became caliph, he affiliated these tribes with Quraysh.

The leaders of Quraysh in the War of al-Fijār

For (Banū Hāshim): al-Zubayr ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. For (Banū ʿAbd Shams):

Ḥarb ibn Umayyah. For (Banū al-Muṭṭalib): ʿAbd Yazīd ibn Hāshim ibn al-Muṭṭalib, “the pure, with no impurity in him.” His mother was al-Shifāʾ daughter of Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf.

For (Banū Nawfal): al-Muṭʿim ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal. For (Banū ʿAbd al-Dār): ʿIkrimah ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn ʿAbd al-Dār. For (Banū Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā): Khuwaylid ibn Asad and ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith ibn Asad. For (Banū Zuhrah): Makhramah ibn Nawfal ibn Uhayb ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah. For (Banū Taym ibn Murrah): ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān ibn ʿAmr. For (Banū Makhzūm): Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah. For (Banū Sahm): al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil. For (Banū Jumḥ): Umayyah ibn Khalaf. For (Banū ʿAdī): Zayd ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl. For (Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy): ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Shams, Abū “Suhayl the One-Eyed”. For (Banū Muḥārib ibn Fihr): Ḍirār ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ibn Mirdās. For (Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Fihr): ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Jarrāḥ, the father of Abū ʿUbaydah ibn al-Jarrāḥ. For (Banū Bakr ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Kinānah): Balʿāʾ ibn Qays al-Kinānī. And for (the Aḥābīsh): al-Ḥulays ibn Yazīd, from The Banū al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Kinānah. All of these were allied with one another.

Those who held authority over them were Ḥarb, Ibn Jadʿān, and Hishām. The chief responsibility for the affair among ʿAbd Manāf and Quṣayy rested with Ḥarb.

Names of those who rejected the worship of idols

Before the Prophet’s mission ﷺ and sought the religion of Abraham ﵇:

ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā became a Christian and died a Christian.

Waraqah ibn Nawfal ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā became a Christian, grew firmly committed to Christianity, read the scriptures, and died in that faith. Zayd ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Riyāḥ al-ʿAdawī did not become a Christian or a Jew. He shunned idols, carrion, blood, and animals sacrificed to idols, and forbade the burying alive of infant girls. He said, “I worship the Lord of the sky.” He openly reproached his people for what they were doing. He used to say, “O God, if I knew which direction was dearest to You, I would prostrate myself toward it. But I do not know.” Then he would prostrate himself on the palms of his hands. Zayd was the first to reproach Quraysh for their worship of idols. Then he set out seeking the religion of Abraham ﵇ and traveled throughout the land of Syria until he came to al-Balqāʾ. A learned monk there said to him, “The time is close at hand for a prophet to appear in your land, calling to the religion of Abraham.” On account of the monk’s words, he set off swiftly toward Mecca. When he was midway through the land of Judhām, they attacked him and killed him ﵁.

ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Jaḥsh ibn Riʾāb al-Asadī, then al-Ghanmī, an ally of the Banū Umayyah, became a Muslim and emigrated to Abyssinia. There he saw the Christians and was impressed by Christianity, so he became a Christian and died in that faith.

Naming those of the men of Badr who used to visit Ṣafiyyah bint ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib and were unmarriageable to her

Muḥammad, the Prophet ﷺ her nephew; al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām, her son; Ḥamzah ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, her brother; ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, her nephew; and ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān, her sister’s daughter’s son. His mother was Arwā bint Kurayz ibn Rabīʿah ibn Ḥabīb ibn ʿAbd Shams. Her mother was al-Bayḍāʾ, Umm Ḥakīm bint ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, who was the twin of ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. She was “chaste, never speaking improperly, and industrious, needing no instruction.” Abū Salamah ibn ʿAbd al-Asad ibn Hilāl al-Makhzūmī, her sister’s son, her sister’s son. His mother was Barrah bint ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. His maternal brother was Abū Sabrah ibn Abī Rahm ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Qays ibn ʿAbd Wadd, the brother of ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaḥsh ibn Riʾāb al-Asadī was also her sister’s son. His mother was Umaymah bint ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib.

Ṭulayb ibn ʿUmayr ibn Wahb ibn Quṣayy was her sister’s son. His mother was Arwā bint ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. Rabīʿah ibn Aktham al-Asadī was an ally of the Banū ʿAbd Shams. Before she married al-ʿAwwām ibn Khuwaylid, Ṣafiyyah had been married to al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥarb ibn Umayyah, and bore him Ṣafiyy. She then married this Rabīʿah ibn Aktham and bore him a daughter. Qudāmah ibn Maẓʿūn al-Jumaḥī was also among them. One day he was with al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām when al-Zubayr received the news that a daughter had been born to him. Qudāmah said to him, “Marry her to me.”

He married her to him. He was asked, “Are you marrying a very young girl?” He replied, “If I die, she is the most entitled to inherit from me; and if I live, she is al-Zubayr’s daughter.” Thus there were eleven men.

The drinking companions among Quraysh

ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim was a drinking companion of Ḥarb ibn Umayyah, until the two referred their rivalry to Nufayl ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, the grandfather of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb. Nufayl said to Ḥarb, “Abū ʿAmr, are you setting yourself against a man who is taller than you, greater of head, more handsome, less scruffy, has more children, and is more generous in giving?” When ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib won the contest, the two parted. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib died before the Fijār War between them and Hawāzin. He was one hundred and twenty years old.

Ḥarb then became a drinking companion of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān al-Taymī. Ḥamzah ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was a drinking companion of ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Sāʾib al-Makhzūmī. Both became Muslims. Abū Uḥayḥah Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah was a drinking companion of al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. Maʿmar ibn Ḥabīb ibn Wahb ibn Ḥudhāfah ibn Jumah was a drinking companion of Umayyah ibn Khalaf ibn Wahb ibn Ḥudhāfah. He was killed as an unbeliever on the day of Badr. ʿUqbah ibn Abī Muʿayṭ ibn Abī ʿAmr ibn Umayyah was a drinking companion of Ubayy ibn Khalaf. The Messenger of God ﷺ had ʿUqbah executed among the prisoners of Badr. Ubayy ibn Khalaf was killed in single combat on the day of Uḥud.

Al-Aswad ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn Asad was a drinking companion of al-Aswad ibn ʿAbd Yaghūth al-Zuhrī. The two were “the two al-Aswads.” They were among the most esteemed of Quraysh in the pre-Islamic era, and used to circumambulate the House with two swords apiece. They were among those who mocked the Messenger of God ﷺ. A man of Quraysh said this elegy for them:

When a matter is shrouded in confusion,

the two al-Aswads dispel the gloom from us.

Noble men, both have perished,

leaving Baṭḥāʾ and the two mountains desolate.

Abū Ṭālib ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was a drinking companion of Musāfir ibn Abī ʿAmr ibn Umayyah. Musāfir died, and Abū Ṭālib then became a drinking companion of ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Wadd ibn Naḍr ibn Mālik ibn Ḥisl ibn ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy. ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁ killed ʿAmr in single combat on the day of the Trench. At that time, ʿAmr was one hundred and forty years old.

ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah ibn ʿAbd Shams was a drinking companion of Muṭʿim ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf. ʿUtbah was killed as an unbeliever on the day of Badr. Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb was a drinking companion of al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ﵁. Al-Fākih ibn al-Mughīrah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm was a drinking companion of ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Zuhrah, the father of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf. Zayd ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā was a drinking companion of Waraqah ibn Nawfal ibn Asad. Waraqah died, and Zayd set out for Syria, where the Lakhm and Judhām killed him.

Shaybah ibn Rabīʿah ibn ʿAbd Shams was a drinking companion of ʿUthmān ibn al-Ḥuwayrith ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā. They both became Christians. Shaybah was killed as an unbeliever on the day of Badr.

Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah was a drinking companion of al-ʿĀṣ ibn Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah. They were called “the two fools of Quraysh.” ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁ killed al-ʿĀṣ ibn Saʿīd, and ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵀ killed al-ʿĀṣ ibn Hishām on the day of Badr.

Abū Lahab, ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, was a drinking companion of al-Ḥārith ibn ʿĀmir ibn Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Quṣayy. He was killed on the day of Badr. Al-Walīd ibn ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah was a drinking companion of al-ʿĀṣ ibn Munabbih ibn al-Ḥajjāj al-Sahmī. ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁ killed them both on the day of Badr.

Ḍirār ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ibn Mirdās al-Fihrī, the poet, was a drinking companion of Hubayrah ibn Abī Wahb al-Makhzūmī. Abū Jahl ibn Hishām, whose name was ʿAmr ibn Hishām, was a drinking companion of al-Ḥakam ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah. This al-Ḥakam was “the banished one.”

Al-Ḥārith ibn Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah was a drinking companion of Ḥakīm ibn Ḥizām ibn Khuwaylid ibn Asad. This Ḥakīm was born inside the Kaʿbah. His mother had entered the Kaʿbah while pregnant with him, and labor pains seized her there, so she gave birth to him inside it. They both became Muslims.

Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil ibn Hishām ibn Saʿīd ibn Sahm was a drinking companion of Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm, the father of Abū Jahl.

Nabīh ibn al-Ḥajjāj ibn ʿĀmir al-Sahmī was a drinking companion of al-Naḍr ibn al-Ḥārith, one of the Banū ʿAbd al-Dār. Nabīh was killed as an unbeliever on the day of Badr, and al-Naḍr was executed among the prisoners of Badr.

ʿUmārah ibn al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīrah was a drinking companion of Ḥanẓalah ibn Abī Sufyān ibn Ḥarb. ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁ killed Ḥanẓalah as an unbeliever on the day of Badr.

Al-Zubayr ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib, who was one of the young men of Quraysh, was a drinking companion of Mālik ibn ʿUmaylah ibn al-Sabbāq ibn ʿAbd al-Dār. Al-Arqam ibn Naḍlah ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf, also one of the young men of Quraysh, was a drinking companion of Suwayd ibn Hirmī ibn ʿĀmir al-Jumaḥī. Suwayd was the first to set up couches and serve milk and honey in Mecca. He left no descendants.

Al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥarb ibn Umayyah was a drinking companion of al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib.

When he died, al-ʿAwwām ibn Khuwaylid ibn Asad became his drinking companion. Al-Ḥārith ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā was a drinking companion of ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd al-Dār.

Abū al-Bukhtarī, al-ʿĀṣ ibn Hāshim ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Asad, was a drinking companion of Ṭalḥah ibn Abī Ṭalḥah ibn ʿAbd al-Dār. Al-Mujadhdhar ibn Dhiyād al-Balawī, an ally of the Aws, killed him on the day of Badr. ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁ killed Ṭalḥah on the day of Uḥud.

Munabbih ibn al-Ḥajjāj al-Sahmī was a drinking companion of Ṭuʿaymah ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf. Ṭuʿaymah was killed on the day of Badr, slain by Ḥamzah and ʿAlī ﵄. Abū Qays al-Anṣārī killed Munabbih.

Abū Sufyān ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was a drinking companion of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil al-Sahmī.

Abū Umayyah ibn al-Mughīrah al-Makhzūmī, the father of Umm Salamah, the wife of the Prophet ﷺ was called “Provision for the Traveler.” He was a drinking companion of Abū Wadāʿah ibn Ḍubayrah ibn Saʿīd ibn Saʿd ibn Sahm. The two of them used to serve honey in Mecca after Suwayd ibn Hirmī.

Abū Qays ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah was a drinking companion of Sufyān ibn Umayyah ibn ʿAbd Shams. Abū al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah was a drinking companion of Qays ibn ʿAdī ibn Saʿd ibn Sahm. The poet says of Qays:

In his house, in his house the drinking companion is received;

in nobility, he is like Qays ibn ʿAdī.

He would go to the wine seller with a whip in his hand. The seller would offer him his wine; if it was good, otherwise he would say, “Your wine is excellent,” then strike him on the head with his whip and leave.

The tribes of the ḥums among the Arabs

All of Quraysh; Khuzāʿah, because they settled in Mecca and lived alongside Quraysh;

and all those among the Arabs descended from Quraysh, and all those from the Arab tribes who settled in Mecca.

Among those descended from Quraysh were Kilāb, Kaʿb, ʿĀmir, and Kalb—the sons of Rabīʿah ibn ʿĀmir ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿah. Their mother was Majd bint Taym ibn Ghālib ibn Fihr.

Labīd ibn Rabīʿah referred to her in his words:

May my people, the sons of Majd, be given drink, and may I give drink

to Numayr and the tribes of Hilāl.

Also among them were the Banū al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Kinānah and Mudlij ibn Murrah ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Kinānah, because they settled around Mecca; and ʿĀmir ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Kinānah;

Mālik and Malkān, the sons of Kinānah; Thaqīf; ʿAdwān; Yarbuʿ ibn Ḥanẓalah; Māzin ibn Mālik ibn ʿAmr ibn Tamīm; and their mother Jandalah bint Fihr ibn Mālik ibn al-Naḍr. It is said that all the Banū ʿĀmir were ḥums because their brothers among the Banū Rabīʿah ibn ʿĀmir were ḥums.

Also ʿAllāf, that is, Rabbān ibn Ḥulwān ibn ʿImrān ibn al-Ḥāf ibn Quḍāʿah; and Janāb ibn Hubl ibn ʿAbd Allāh, of Kalb. His mother was Āminah bint Rabīʿah ibn ʿĀmir ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿah, whose mother was Majd bint Taym al-Adram ibn Ghālib ibn Fihr.

The tribes of the ḥillah among the Arabs

Tamīm ibn Murrah, all except Yarbuʿ, Māzin, Ḍabbah, Ḥumays, and Ẓāʿinah.

Ghath ibn Murrah; all of Qays ʿAylān except Thaqīf; and ʿAdwān;

ʿĀmir ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿah; all of Rabīʿah ibn Nizār; all of Quḍāʿah except ʿAllāf and Janāb; the Anṣār; Khathʿam; Bajīlah; Bakr ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Kinānah; Hudhayl ibn Mudrikah; Asad; Ṭayyiʾ; and Bāriq.

The tribes of the ṭuls

These were the rest of the people of Yemen, the people of Ḥaḍramawt, ʿAkk, ʿAjīb, and Iyād ibn Nizār.

The ḥums had imposed strict practices upon themselves in their religion. When they performed their rites, they did not melt fat, cook dried curds, or store milk. They did not separate a nursing mother from her nursling until it had become accustomed to being weaned. They did not cut hair or nails, and during pilgrimage they did not build with hair, camel hair, wool, or cotton. They ate no meat and touched no oil. They wore only new clothes, and circumambulated the House only in their shoes and garments. They did not touch the mosque with their feet, out of reverence for its precinct; nor did they enter houses through their doors. They did not go out to ʿArafāt, saying:

“We are the people of God.” They remained at Muzdalifah until they had completed their rites. After leaving Muzdalifah, they circumambulated at al-Ṣafā and al-Marwah. They stayed in red leather tents among their camel-litters.

The ḥillah forbade hunting during the rites, but not outside the Sacred Precinct. During the rites they shared generously with one another: a wealthy person would give away his property, or most of it, during his pilgrimage, and their poor would melt fat. They would cut from the wool, camel hair, and hair only what they needed. They wore only the clothes in which they had performed the rites; during the rites they wore no new clothes, entered neither a house’s nor a dwelling’s door, and had no shade shelter them as long as they remained in consecration. They applied oil and ate meat, and they were at their most prosperous during the days of their rites. When they entered Mecca after their departure, they gave in charity every saddlecloth and every garment they had. Then they hired garments from the ḥums, out of reverence for the Kaʿbah, so that they would circumambulate it only in new clothes. They did not put shoes between themselves and the Kaʿbah, so that their feet would not touch it directly. If they could find no clothes, they circumambulated naked. Each man of the ḥillah had a ḥaramī from among the ḥums, from whom he took clothes.

Anyone who could find no garment circumambulated naked. The ḥillah hired garments for the circumambulation when they returned to the House because, when they set out as pilgrims, they considered it unlawful to buy or sell anything until they had reached their homes—except meat.

The Messenger of God ﷺ had a ḥaramī, ʿIyāḍ ibn Ḥimār al-Mujāshiʿī. Whenever he came to Mecca, he circumambulated in the clothes of the Messenger of God ﷺ.

The ṭuls were between the ḥillah and the ḥums: in their state of consecration, they did as the ḥillah did, and in their clothing and entry into the House, they did as the ḥums did.

They did not uncover themselves around the Kaʿbah, borrow clothes, or enter houses except through their doors. They did not bury their daughters alive. They stood with the ḥillah and observed the same practices.

Leaders of the Arabs

After ʿĀmir ibn al-Ẓarb ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿIyādh ibn Yashkur ibn ʿAdwān, at their seasonal gatherings and as judges at ʿUkāẓ, came the Banū Tamīm.

When they had finished, they gave away every sandal and every garment they had. Then they hired garments from the Ḥums, out of reverence for the Kaʿba, so that they would circumambulate it only in new garments. They would not put anything between themselves and the Kaʿba; they approached it directly with their feet. If they could not find garments, they circumambulated naked. Each man of the Ḥilla had a Ḥums associate who would lend him his garments.

Anyone who could not find a garment circumambulated naked. The Ḥilla hired garments for the circumambulation on their return to the House because, when they set out as pilgrims, they considered it unlawful to buy or sell anything until they reached their homes—except meat.

The Messenger of God ﷺ—had as his Ḥums associate ʿIyāḍ ibn Ḥimār al-Mujāshiʿī. Whenever he came to Mecca, he circumambulated in the garments of the Messenger of God ﷺ.

The Ṭuls stood between the Ḥilla and the Ḥums: in their state of consecration they did what the Ḥilla did, and in their garments and their entry into the House they did what the Ḥums did.

They did not go naked around the Kaʿba, nor borrow garments. They entered houses through their doors, did not bury their daughters alive, stood with the Ḥilla, and performed the same rites.

Leaders of the Arabs

After ʿĀmir ibn al-Ẓarb ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿIyāḍ ibn Yashkur ibn ʿAdwān, in their seasons and as their judges at ʿUkāẓ, were the Banū Tamīm.

Their custodians in their religion and trustees of their qibla were Quraysh.

Those who gave legal opinions to them in their religion were the Banū Mālik ibn Kināna.

The man who held both the seasonal authority and the judgment at ʿUkāẓ was from the Banū Tamīm. This authority was held in turn by all their clans. Sometimes two men held the two offices, one for ʿUkāẓ and one for the season. The first to hold both the season and the judgment was Saʿd ibn Zayd Manāt ibn Tamīm. Then Ḥanẓala ibn Zayd Manāt ibn Tamīm held them. Then Dhuʾayb ibn Kaʿb ibn ʿAmr ibn Tamīm.

Then Māzin ibn Mālik ibn ʿAmr ibn Tamīm; then Thaʿlaba ibn Yarbuʿ ibn Ḥanẓala ibn Mālik ibn Zayd Manāt; then Muʿāwiya ibn Sharīf ibn Jarwa ibn Usayyid ibn ʿAmr ibn Tamīm; then al-Aḍbaṭ ibn Qurayʿ ibn ʿAwf ibn Kaʿb ibn Saʿd ibn Zayd Manāt; then Ṣulṣul ibn Aws ibn Mukhāshin ibn Muʿāwiya ibn Sharīf ibn Jarwa ibn Usayyid; then Sufyān ibn Mujāshiʿ ibn Dārim ibn Mālik ibn Ḥanẓala. Sufyān was the last Tamīmī to hold both the season and the judgment at ʿUkāẓ. When Sufyān died, the offices were separated, and neither the season nor the judgment was again held by one man among them until Islam came. Muḥammad ibn Sufyān ibn Mujāshiʿ then judged at ʿUkāẓ, and the office became their inheritance. The last to judge among them who lived to see Islam was al-Aqraʿ ibn Ḥābis ibn ʿIqāl ibn Muḥammad ibn Sufyān. After Ṣulṣul ibn Aws, al-ʿAllāq ibn Shihāb ibn Laʾī, of the Banū ʿAwāfa ibn Saʿd ibn Zayd Manāt, gave the permission to depart from the season. The last to lead them in the departure was Karb ibn Ṣafwān ibn Janāb ibn Shijna ibn ʿAṭārd ibn ʿAwf ibn Kaʿb ibn Saʿd ibn Zayd Manāt. Aws ibn Maghrāʾ al-Qurayʿī said of him:

They would not leave their station at the ritual gathering until it was said: “Depart, family of Ṣafwān!”

Names of those whom Abū Bakr ﵀—freed from among those tortured for God

Bilāl ibn Rabāḥ, the muezzin of the Messenger of God ﷺ—who fought at Badr.

ʿĀmir ibn Fuhayra, who fought at Badr. He was martyred at the Well of Maʿūna, but his body was not found among the slain. They related that Jabbār ibn Sulmā ibn Mālik ibn Jaʿfar ibn Kilāb said to ʿAmr ibn Umayya al-Ḍamrī, who was with those martyred that day and whom they spared when he declared his affiliation with Muḍar: “I struck one of your men with my spear, then saw no one there. I looked at him until he disappeared into the sky.” He said to him:

“That was ʿĀmir ibn Fuhayra, the client of Abū Bakr.”

Al-Nahdiyya and her daughter; and Zinnīra, a slave woman belonging to the Banū ʿAmr ibn Muʾammal al-ʿAdawī. They say that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb used to beat her to make her renounce Islam, until he grew tired. When he tired, he would say to her: “I ask your pardon. I have not stopped because I was sparing you or out of mercy for you; I have simply grown exhausted.” ʿUbayṣ and Umm ʿUbayṣ are mentioned by al-ʿĀʾishī.

The astute men of the Arabs

Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān; Ziyād, son of his father; ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ; Qays ibn Saʿd ibn ʿUbāda al-Anṣārī; al-Mughīra ibn Shuʿba al-Thaqafī; and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Budayl ibn Warqāʾ al-Khuzāʿī.

The women who wished for the death of the Messenger of God ﷺ—and their story

When the Messenger of God ﷺ—died, a man of Kalb from the Banū ʿĀmir ibn ʿAwf, of the Banū al-Jallāḥ, called Jahbal, carried the news of his death to Ḥaḍramawt. They were a household that had long lived in Ḥaḍramawt, in a village called Raḥba.

There were six women in Ḥaḍramawt, from Kinda and Ḥaḍramawt, who wished for the death of the Messenger of God ﷺ. They dyed their hands with henna and beat tambourines.

The dissolute women of Ḥaḍramawt went out to them and did as they had done. The women who joined the six numbered more than twenty. They lived scattered through the villages of Ḥaḍramawt, at Tarīm, Mashṭa, al-Nujayr, Tanʿa, Shabwa, and Dhamār. Among them were al-ʿAmarrada daughter of Maʿdīkarib and Hunayda daughter of Abū Shamir. These two were of noble families. Among those who joined them were al-Tayḥāʾ al-Ḥaḍramiyya, the mother of Sayf ibn Maʿdīkarib; Umm Shurāḥīl daughter of ʿUfayr, the grandmother of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Hārūn of al-Arḥūb; Ḥabra daughter of Shurayḥ, of al-Arḥūb; and Farīḍa, the grandmother of Abū al-Julayḥ of Ḥaḍramawt.

Malika daughter of Amānāt ibn Qays ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Shaybān ibn al-ʿĀtik was of Kinda. Asmāʾ daughter of Yazīd ibn Qays was of the Banū Wahb of Kinda.

/ Malika daughter of Qays ibn Shurāḥīl was a woman of Kinda. Her brother was killed on the day of al-Nujayr.

The daughter of al-Awdaḥ ibn Abī Karib was a woman of Kinda. Her brother was killed on the day of al-Nujayr.

A woman from Tanʿa, a noblewoman whose name was not given. And Hirr daughter of Yāman the Jewess, who was proverbial for fornication, so that people would say, “More adulterous than Hirr.” She had a blacksmith brother called Muwriq. And Umm Maʿdān.

Imruʾ al-Qays ibn ʿĀbis al-Kindī wrote to Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq ﵀. At that time, al-Muhājir ibn Abī Umayya was his governor over Kinda and al-Ṣadaf, and Ziyād ibn Labīd of the Banū Bayāḍa was his governor over Ḥaḍramawt. The Messenger of God ﷺ—had appointed them:

The dissolute women rejoiced on the day Jahbal announced the death of Aḥmad, the rightly guided Prophet.

May ﷺ who rests in a place where he was laid to rest, now dwelling in Yathrib.

O rider! If you pass by, convey my message to Abū Bakr, Aḥmad’s successor.

Do not spare adulteresses, black-crested, who claim that Muḥammad is not lost.

Quench my burning anguish by cutting off their hands: it is like embers between my sides, still uncooled.

A man of Tanʿa, a nobleman called Shaddād ibn Mālik ibn Ḍamj, also wrote to Abū Bakr ﵀:

Convey to Abū Bakr, when you reach him, that the adulteresses have sought every course.

They showed delight at the Prophet’s death and dyed their hands with al-ʿullām.

So cut off their hands, may you be guided, with a sword like lightning flashing through the folds of a cloud.

When their letters reached Abū Bakr, he said, “May God reward our brother of Kinda and our brother of Ḥaḍramawt well on behalf of Islam.” Then he wrote to al-Muhājir ibn Abī Umayya: “In the name of God, Most Gracious, Most Merciful. From Abū Bakr to al-Muhājir ibn Abī “Umayya. To proceed: The two righteous servants, Imruʾ al-Qays ibn ʿĀbis al-Kindī and Shaddād ibn Mālik al-Ḥaḍramī, who held fast to their religion when most of their people turned away from it—may [God?] reward them for that with the reward of the righteous, and bring the others to the deaths of the wrongdoers—have written to me. They claim that there are women among the people of Yemen who wished for the death of the Messenger of God ﷺ—and that Kinda’s singing girls and Ḥaḍramawt’s adulteresses joined them. They dyed their hands, displayed their charms, and beat tambourines, in defiance of God and in contempt for His right and the right of His Messenger ﷺ. When this letter of mine reaches you, march against them with your cavalry and infantry, until you cut off their hands. If someone prevents you or stands between you and them, give him due warning by presenting the case against him, and make him understand the enormity of the sin and aggression into which he has entered. If he turns back, accept it from him. If he refuses, then oppose him openly and on equal terms. ‘Indeed, God does not guide the plot of the treacherous.’ (12:52) By my life! I do not think that any man—indeed, I am certain that there is one—has made their worst deed seem fair to them and prevented you from cutting off their hands for even a mosquito’s wing’s worth of Muhammad’s religion ﷺ. By God, son of Abī Umayya! In assigning this matter to you instead of undertaking it myself, I do so because I wish you to earn the great reward “and abundant recompense. Know that this is an honor God has brought your way, if He carries it out by your hand. May God protect us and you through piety, and make the Hereafter better for us and for you than this world.”

When al-Muhājir read the letter, he assembled his cavalry and infantry, then marched against them.

/ Men of Kinda and Ḥaḍramawt stood between him and the women, so he gave them due warning. They refused anything but to fight him. Then most of them withdrew from him. He fought them, defeated them, seized the women, and cut off their hands. Most of the women died. Some migrated to Kūfa. A man of Ḥaḍramawt mocked a man whose mother’s hand had been cut off that day, saying:

Your old woman’s hand was cut off at Tarīm, as Umm Sayf’s was cut off at Mashṭa.

As for Hirr daughter of Yāman, a man called al-Azʿar, a hired servant of Abū Saʿr al-Adhmarī, had illicit relations with her. She bore him Ḥabīb. Ḥabīb then had relations with Daʿjāʾ, a woman of mixed ancestry who was a slave of the family of Salḵab, and she bore him Buḥayr. Buḥayr migrated to Kūfa and adopted a lineage in Ḥaḍramawt. Sharīk ibn Shaddād al-Tanʿī satirized him:

Neither Abū Bakr nor my father cut off my mother’s hand: I am a base-born, ignoble scion, an attached offshoot,

a hired servant of the family of al-Adhmarī, a eunuch; he is thought to have a bladder ailment from severe urination.

Nor did Hirr daughter of Yāman give birth to me, nor was Muwriq of the decorated garments my maternal uncle;

nor did Daʿjāʾ give birth to my maternal uncle or my father, nor have I any attachment to Ḥām, son of Noah.

Your limit with me, Buḥayr, is a blow that will make the folds of your belly swell.

A man descended from Hirr, when he claims descent, and from Daʿjāʾ, deserves to be humiliated and driven away.

The Arab most deeply rooted in being killed

ʿUmāra ibn Ḥamza ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām ibn Khuwaylid ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā. ʿUmāra and Ḥamza were killed at Qudayd: the Ibāḍīs killed them in the days of Marwān ibn Muḥammad ibn Marwān. Al-Ḥajjāj killed ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr at Mecca and crucified him head down. ʿAmr ibn Jurmūz al-Saʿdī killed al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām at Wādī al-Sibāʿ, as he was returning from the battle on the day of the Camel. The Banū Kināna killed al-ʿAwwām ibn Khuwaylid. The Banū Kaʿb ibn ʿAmr of Khuzāʿa killed Khuwaylid.

Four men from the people of Baṣra

None of them died before seeing one hundred people among his children and grandchildren:

Anas ibn Mālik al-Anṣārī; Abū Bakra, the client of the Messenger of God ﷺ; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿUmayr al-Laythī; Khalīfa ibn Bū al-Saʿdī.

The Arab guides whose guiding skill reached its limit

Duʿaymiṣ al-Raml al-ʿAbdī, who reached Wabār. No one else reached it. The reason he went there was that he would visit a splendid stallion, as white as paper, and put it to his camels. When they had been impregnated, he would leave. He bred thirty-four she-camels, white as paper. He mounted one of them. The stallion came to his camels as usual, and when it left, the young she-camels followed it. He mounted the one he had chosen and followed them. He filled ostrich eggs with water and buried them in the sand. He kept following the camel until he came down into a land more abundant than any other in palm trees and splendid camels. A voice called out:

“You have no claim to the young she-camels: they are from our camel. We have given you your mount in exchange.”

He returned, and began digging up the eggs and drinking the water, until he reached his people.

Wabār was the dwelling place of the Banū Umaym ibn Lawdh ibn Iram ibn Sām ibn Noah. They perished, and the jinn took possession of it.

/ ʿAbd Allāh ibn Urayqiṭ al-ʿAdawī, an ally of al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil al-Sahmī, was the guide of the Messenger of God ﷺ—on the night of the Hijra.

Rāfiʿ ibn ʿUmayra al-Ṭāʾī was the guide of Khālid ibn al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra from al-Yamāma to Syria, when Abū Bakr ﵀—wrote to him ordering him to proceed to Syria. He made the camels thirsty and muzzled them. They would slit open their bellies, water the horses with the fluid from their stomachs, drink it, and eat their meat. The poet said of him:

God bless Rāfiʿ! What did he see? He traversed wilderness from Qarāqir to Suwayy.

Five, whenever the craven whispered to her, she wept,

And (Khālid) ibn Dithār al-Fazārī was the guide of Banū Fazārah against Kalb on the Day of the Daughters of Qayn. Kalb had raided them, so Banū Fazārah raided them at the Daughters of Qayn.

That was when the people rallied around ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān. And (ʿAbd al-Jabbār) ibn Yazīd ibn Rabīʿah al-Kalbī was the guide of Banū al-Muhallab ibn Abī Ṣufrah. Al-Ḥajjāj had imprisoned them at Laʿlaʿ. They made a beard over the beard of a baker who used to come in to them, and thus fooled the guards until they escaped from prison. They joined Sulaymān ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, and al-Walīd interceded for them, securing their pardon. Ibn Rabīʿah was the one who said:

A people who once were kings, I guided them

To Taymāʾ, where no star’s light appears,

Nor moon, save a thin one, like a bracelet

A goldsmith has bent, gilding the bracelet.

And (al-Aṣaydif) ibn Ṣulayʿ ibn Abī ʿAmr ibn Qays al-Ṭāʾī was knowledgeable about the land and served as a guide.

Raiders of the Jāhiliyyah

(Abū al-Kharīf ʿUbayd) ibn Nashbah al-Murrī, (al-Ḥārith) ibn Ẓālim al-Murrī, (al-Barrāḍ) ibn Qays al-Kinānī, (Taʾabbaṭa Sharran) al-Fahmī, (Murrah) ibn Khulayf al-Fahmī, (Ḥanẓalah) ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAmr ibn Fātik al-Asadī, (ʿAmr) ibn Kulthūm al-Taghlibī, (Thumāmah) ibn al-Mustanīr al-Sulamī, then al-Ẓafrī, and (Muʿāwiyah) ibn al-Ḥārith al-Jushamī.

As for (Abū al-Kharīf), he was ʿUbayd ibn Nashbah ibn Murrah ibn Ghayẓ ibn Murrah ibn ʿAwf ibn Saʿd ibn Dhubyān. He was the one who taught al-Ḥārith ibn Ẓālim how to raid by stealth.

Al-Ḥārith came to him and said, “Teach me how to raid by stealth.” He replied, “When you resolve to do it, do it.” Al-Ḥārith asked him again, and he gave him his original answer. Al-Ḥārith asked him a third time, so Abū al-Kharīf charged at him with his sword, intending to strike him. Al-Ḥārith said, “What is this?” He replied, “This is raiding by stealth: I resolved to strike you.”

As for (al-Ḥārith) ibn Ẓālim ibn Judhaymah ibn Yarbuʿ ibn Ghayẓ ibn Murrah ibn ʿAwf, proverbial sayings about raiding by stealth and keeping one’s word are associated with him. As for his raiding by stealth, he killed Khālid ibn Jaʿfar ibn Kilāb while under the protection of al-Aswad ibn al-Mundhir, the king, and he killed the son of al-Nuʿmān ibn al-Mundhir. As for his killing of Khālid, Khālid had killed Zuhayr ibn Judhaymah al-ʿAbsī, who was the chief of Ghaṭafān on the Day of al-Nafrāwāt. After that, al-Ḥārith ibn Ẓālim went to al-Aswad ibn al-Mundhir. He found Khālid ibn Jaʿfar ibn Kilāb with him, staying at ʿĀqil. He invited them both to dates, which were brought on a leather mat.

Khālid ibn Jaʿfar said to al-Aswad, “May you never be cursed! Who is this?” He replied, “This is al-Ḥārith ibn Ẓālim, the chief of his people.” Khālid said, “Ḥārith, I think I have done you a good turn.”

“What is that?” he asked. Khālid replied, “I killed your uncle, the noblest of your people, Zuhayr ibn Judhaymah, and left you as their chief.” Al-Ḥārith said to him, “I shall repay you for your good turn.”

Al-Ḥārith kept moving his hand through the dates, oblivious. Khālid said to him,

“Why do you keep moving your hand through the dates?” Al-Aswad said, “Take the dates away.” They took them away.

Al-Ḥārith got up and left. Al-Aswad said to Khālid, “Why did you provoke this dog when you are my guest?” Khālid replied, “He is one of my servants. Even if I were asleep, he would not wake me.” When evening came, al-Aswad sent al-Ḥārith a large vessel of wine to keep him occupied with a singing girl.
