She said, “The king says to you, ‘Drink this.’” He took it, raised it toward his mouth, then poured it down between his belly and his clothes. With him was a young attendant from Banū Muḥārib ibn Khaṣafah, called Kharrāsh. Al-Ḥārith began gnawing the middle of his saddle in grief. The Muḥāribī said to him, “You are bent on doing something that will bring disaster.” Khālid ibn Jaʿfar returned to his tent. ʿUrwah al-Raḥḥāl ibn ʿUtbah ibn Jaʿfar ibn Kilāb reproached him for provoking al-Ḥārith. Then the two of them fastened the tent flap and went to sleep.

When the people had quieted down, al-Ḥārith ordered his Muḥāribī attendant to go ahead to a place he had arranged to meet him, taking al-Ḥārith’s riding camel with him. He said, “If the morning star rises and I have not come to you, go wherever you wish.” Al-Ḥārith set off until he reached Khālid’s tent. He tore open its flap, killed Khālid, and left.

As for his keeping his word: a man of Banū ʿAmr ibn Saʿd ibn Zayd Manāt ibn Tamīm, called ʿIyāḍ ibn Dayhath, had brought his camels to water and found al-Ḥārith ibn Ẓālim’s herdsmen there. ʿIyāḍ lowered his bucket to draw water for himself and his camels, but his rope was too short. He borrowed one of the ropes belonging to al-Ḥārith’s herdsmen and watered his camels. In the morning, some of al-Nuʿmān’s retainers came upon him and seized his camels and his family. He called out, “Ḥār! My neighbor, help!” Al-Ḥārith said to him, “Woe to you! When were you ever my neighbor?” He replied, “I tied my rope to your herdsman’s rope and watered my camels; then they were seized with that water in their bellies.” Al-Ḥārith said, “That is what being a neighbor means!”

He rode until he reached al-Nuʿmān and said, “May you never be cursed! You have taken the women and property of my neighbor, and I am his neighbor.” Al-Nuʿmān said to him, “Will you not mend what you have torn in your own hide?”—meaning that al-Ḥārith had killed Khālid while under the protection of al-Aswad, al-Nuʿmān’s brother. Then al-Nuʿmān threatened al-Ḥārith severely. Al-Ḥārith went away, and al-Nuʿmān regretted letting him go. He sought him, but he had escaped him. Al-Nuʿmān restored ʿIyāḍ’s family and property to him. Al-Nuʿmān had a son being nursed by Sinān ibn Abī Ḥārithah. Salmā, daughter of Ẓālim, was married to Sinān. Al-Ḥārith came to his sister, ostensibly on Sinān’s behalf, until she handed him al-Nuʿmān’s son. He cut the boy’s throat and fled to Mecca, where he sought the protection of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān.

As for the raid by stealth of (al-Barrāḍ), whose name was Rāfiʿ ibn Qays, he had entered into an alliance with Banū Sahm of Quraysh. He attacked a man from Hudhayl and killed him. The men of Hudhayl came to Banū Sahm demanding blood for their slain man. Banū Sahm said, “We have cast him off and disavowed responsibility for his crimes.” Hudhayl said, “Who will attest to this?” Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil said, “I cast him off as one casts off a dog.” The Hudhaylī men fell silent, seeing no way to pursue their claim.

He went to Ḥarb ibn Umayyah and entered into an alliance with him. He attacked a man from Khuzāʿah and killed him, then fled to Yemen. He remained there a year. Then he returned and approached Mecca. There he found the Hudhaylī and Khuzāʿī men seeking him, after he had been cast off. He said, “I have no one better to turn to than al-Nuʿmān ibn al-Mundhir.” He joined him and found the Arab delegations at al-Ḥīrah in his presence. He stayed, seeking permission to approach along with them. He did not get an audience, however, and his stay grew long; he was treated harshly.

Al-Nuʿmān had a caravan of valuables that he sent each year to trade at the ʿUkāẓ fair. Al-Nuʿmān went out and sat among the people at al-Ḥīrah. The caravans and trade caravans of al-Nuʿmān, when they reached Tihāmah on their way to the fair, had not been attacked—until al-Nuʿmān killed the brother of Balʿāʾ ibn Qays al-Kinānī.

Balʿāʾ began intercepting al-Nuʿmān’s caravans of valuables and plundering them. He did this twice. Al-Nuʿmān became afraid for his caravan of valuables. So he said one day, “Who will protect this caravan?” Al-Barrāḍ said, “I will protect it for you.” ʿUrwah al-Raḥḥāl ibn ʿUtbah ibn Jaʿfar ibn Kilāb said, “You will protect it against the people of al-Shayḥ and al-Qayṣūm? You are only a cast-off dog. By God, you are far too8 for that. But I, my king, will protect it for you against both tribes.” Al-Barrāḍ said, “Will you protect it across Tihāmah?” Al-Nuʿmān paid no heed to al-Barrāḍ’s words and scorned him.

He handed the caravan of valuables over to ʿUrwah al-Raḥḥāl. ʿUrwah set out with the caravan, and al-Barrāḍ followed behind him. When they were partway along the road, al-Barrāḍ brought out the divining arrows to cast lots.

ʿUrwah said to him, “What are you doing?” He replied, “I am seeking a favorable omen about killing you.” ʿUrwah laughed and thought nothing of it. When ʿUrwah reached his people, just beyond al-Jurayb, at a watering place called “Awārah”, he unloaded the caravan of valuables, turned the riding camels out to graze, and entered his tent to sleep. Al-Barrāḍ attacked him and killed him. Then he went on until he reached Khaybar. The War of al-Fijār between Kinānah and Qays came about because of him.

As for (Taʾabbaṭa Sharran), whose name was Thābit ibn Jābir ibn Sufyān, he was brave. He used to raid on foot, and horses could not keep up with him. He feared nothing.

He had many extraordinary stories about his raids. Whenever he resolved to do something, he did it. Once he went to a mountain in the land of Banū Liḥyān of Hudhayl to gather honey. He used to go there every year. The mountain stood alone. He came to it and climbed it. They had set a watch for him. A group of his companions were with him; they let down a rope, and Taʾabbaṭa climbed by it until he reached the cave where the honey was. They lowered waterskins to him. The Hudhaylī men were watching, and when they saw him safely settled in place, they rushed at his companions. The companions scattered, leaving him in the cave. The men gathered at the cave and called to him. He stuck his head out.

They said, “Climb up.” He said, “On what terms should I climb up?” They said, “Climb up, and we will decide what to do with you.” He said, “If, when I climb up, you promise not to kill me and to accept a small ransom from me, I will climb up.” They said, “You have no right to set terms for us. Climb up.” He said, “If I climb up, will you eat the honey I gathered?” They said, “Yes.” He said,

“By God, you will not both kill me and eat my honey.” He began pouring the honey from the waterskins out of the cave mouth onto a smooth rock beneath. The honey flowed down as they watched him in amazement and laughed. When he had finished and the honey had run down and away, he took a waterskin, tied it against his chest, and then descended into the honey. He kept slipping through it until he landed on the ground. He was three miles away from them. Then he set off and returned to his people.

As for (Murrah) ibn Khulayf al-Fahmī, he was one of the devils of the Arabs. He often raided and killed. Once he raided the Azd and took shelter on a forbidding mountain of theirs, hoping for an opportunity to attack. A slave woman of theirs came to a spring at the foot of the mountain to draw water into a waterskin. Murrah did not notice her. He was sitting on a rock, eating dates.

He dropped a date pit, and it fell onto the slave woman, who picked it up. When she saw that it was moist, she became suspicious. She looked up to see whether a bird had dropped it, but saw no bird. Murrah peered over the edge and saw her, but she did not see him. She filled her waterskin and went away. When she came out into the open, he saw her and feared she might have seen him. He watched her closely and thought to himself, “If she goes on without looking back, then she has not seen me.” When she had gone a short distance, she looked back. He knew she had seen him and would warn the people about him. He climbed down and set off quickly. The slave woman reached her people and told them what had happened. They took their weapons and followed the route she had described until they found his tracks. Night fell and darkness came on, and they lost the way. In the morning they found his tracks again and saw that he had left the path, so they followed him.

He continued until he reached al-Sikk, where he found no way out and no path. He climbed until he came to the open drop. He stopped, knowing that the men were on his trail, until they reached him.

When they saw him, they rushed at him. He said, “There is no harm in your waiting a little. If I fight you, I am likely to kill one of you. I will cast lots with the arrows I have with me. One says I should surrender to you; if that arrow comes up, I will surrender.

“Another says I should leap. If that arrow comes up, I will leap and spare you the trouble of killing me. The last says I should fight you—that is the course you are hurrying toward. There is no harm in delaying it a little.” They said, “Very well. Do what you want.” He stood there, and they held back. He brought out his arrows and cast lots. The arrow to leap came up.

They said, “What is it?” He said, “Too soon, too soon! It is a great drop, and I will not do it until I have made my case.” Then he cast lots a second time, and the arrow to leap came up. They said,

“What is it?” He said, “The arrow to leap came up, and I will do it.” They said,

“Then do it.” He sprang and began winding his clothes around himself. When he had finished, he took his shield and placed his forehead on the center of it. Then he plunged headfirst. They came up and stood where he had been. They watched him fall until he disappeared into the darkness. Then, suddenly, he was back among them. When he drew level with them, or nearly so, he slowed. Then he turned back down again.

He continued like that until he came down gently to the ground. The wind kept tossing him about. He went off to his people. Kaʿb Ḥadhar, the brother of Taʾabbaṭa, said:

By God, that day of mine and Murrah’s day,

When he wrapped his clothes about himself for death;

The wind tossed him on the shield, as it tosses

A sand-grouse in al-Ḥisāʾ into a net;

The wind lowered him, then lifted him up,

Until he reached the earth with a light fall.

And the day of truth, in a winding gorge,

When the road’s face was blocked to any traveler;

As though I were a bustard caught by

ʿAmr’s hunter, its wing a ring;

If I meet you, I will make your wounds worse,

In the throat, between the uvula and palate;

Or I will die, with death driving hard,

For in my breast there is a thorn against you.

As for Ḥanẓalah ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAmr ibn Fātik ibn al-Qalīb ibn ʿAmr ibn Asad, he used to move among the Arab tribes on account of his crimes, and he was well accompanied. The Arabs had come to know of his predatory violence. He was one of the handsomest of men, so he used to veil his face for fear that men would abduct him. During one of his wanderings, he alighted among the Banū ʿAbd Manāt ibn Bakr ibn Saʿd ibn Ḍabbah. With him was a female cousin, who had young children, and he had great herds of camels with a herdsman tending them. The Banū Ḍabbah said: “Ḥanẓalah is the most treacherous and wicked of men. If anyone were to attack him, it would be his own people, the sons of his father. He has never dwelt beside a people without bringing calamity upon them. Let us divide his property, kill him, and give his wife to so-and-so, for he has no wife.” They agreed on this plan. They spent the day casting lots for his property, until he grew angry with his wife and struck her. Then he went out in the morning to attend to his business. One of her female neighbors came and found her weeping. She asked, “Why are you crying?” She replied, “That wicked man beats me and treats me badly.” The neighbor said to her, “Do not cry. They are only waiting for the sacred months to end so that they can kill him. Only four or five nights remain. They will give you to so-and-so, the best of them in lineage, noblest in standing, and handsomest in appearance. These are the men casting lots for his property.” Ḥanẓalah’s wife showed by her manner that the news the neighbor had told her pleased her. When Ḥanẓalah returned to her that day, she told him the story. He rose and went to one of his camels, slaughtered it, and distributed its meat among the houses, except for the head, which he cooked. By evening it was done. He dug a deep well in his house, laid a mat over it, and placed a cushion beside it. When night had fallen, he sent his little daughter to them, one man at a time, and no one knew that his companion had been summoned. She would say—for in the Jāhiliyyah, when someone was summoned, he would ask, “For what?”—and his daughter would go to each man, who would ask her, “For what?” She would reply, “At your service.” He would come, thinking that her father was granting him a special favor. Ḥanẓalah girded on his sword. Each man who came, he would catch off guard while he sat for a while, cut off his head, and throw him into that well. He did this to seven of their elders.

One old man remained, blind. His son used to milk a she-camel for him. Ḥanẓalah sent for the old man as the last of those he summoned. When the son came, Ḥanẓalah leapt upon him and killed him. Ḥanẓalah left his house standing, his dog tied up and howling, and one of his camels’ young left hamstrung and bleating. He drove off his camels and his family and escaped. When the old man’s son was late, the old man kept saying, “He has not come.” They went looking for their companions, came to Ḥanẓalah’s house, found the men dead, and discovered that he had already gone. He has many tales from his wanderings.

As for ʿAmr ibn Kulthūm, among his acts of violence was the affair of Muḍriṭ al-Ḥijārah, namely ʿAmr ibn al-Mundhir ibn Māʾ al-Samāʾ al-Lakhmī, whose mother was Hind bt. al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAmr al-Maqṣūr ibn Ḥujr Ākil al-Murār al-Kindī. He was a powerful king. One day he said to his companions, “Do you know of any Arab in my kingdom who would disdain having his mother serve my mother?” They said, “Yes: ʿAmr ibn Kulthūm. His mother is Laylā bt. Muhalhil ibn Rabīʿah; her uncle is Kulayb ibn Wāʾil; her husband is Kulthūm; and her son is ʿAmr.” ʿAmr kept silent about what was in his mind. Then he sent to ʿAmr ibn Kulthūm, inviting him to visit and asking him to bring his mother Laylā to visit his mother Hind.

ʿAmr ibn Kulthūm arrived with the horsemen of Taghlib, and his mother Laylā was with him. He encamped on the bank of the Euphrates. When ʿAmr ibn Hind heard of his arrival, he ordered his pavilion to be set up between al-Ḥīrah and the Euphrates. He sent for the leading men of his kingdom and prepared a feast for them. Then he summoned the people and had food brought to them at the entrance of the pavilion.

He, ʿAmr ibn Kulthūm, and a select group of people were inside the pavilion. His mother Hind had a tent in one part of it, and Laylā, the mother of ʿAmr ibn Kulthūm, was with her in the tent.

ʿAmr ibn Hind had said to his mother, “When the people have finished eating and only the delicacies remain, send your servants away from you. Then, when I call for the delicacies, bring Laylā forward and have her hand you things, one after another.” Hind did as he had told her. When he called for the delicacies, Hind said to Laylā, “Hand me that dish.” Laylā replied:

“Let the one who needs it rise and get it herself.”

Hind said, “Hand it to me,” and pressed her.

Laylā said, “What humiliation! O people of Taghlib!” ʿAmr ibn Kulthūm heard her; his face flushed with anger while the people were drinking. He looked around, recognized the trouble, had heard his mother say “O people of Taghlib,” and saw ʿAmr ibn Hind’s sword hanging unsheathed in the pavilion. He struck ʿAmr ibn Hind on the head with it and killed him. Then he went out and cried, “O people of Taghlib!” They plundered his property and horses, took the women captive, and made for al-Jazīrah. Of this says Afanūn ibn Ṣarīm al-Taghlibī:

By your life, ʿAmr ibn Hind was no man of sound judgmentwhen he summoned Laylā to serve his mother.

Then Ibn Kulthūm rose to the sword, unsheathed,and seized one of his drinking companions by the throat.

As for Maʿbad ibn ʿUṣm ibn al-Nuʿmān al-Taghlibī, his father, Abū Ḥanash ʿUṣm ibn al-Nuʿmān, had been a neighbor of the king Sharaḥbīl ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAmr al-Maqṣūr ibn Ākil al-Murār al-Kindī. Sharaḥbīl made him his deputy. Abū Ḥanash stipulated to Sharaḥbīl that he not admit his son Maʿbad among his deputies or drinking companions. Sharaḥbīl said, “Why do you ask this of me, when people desire such a thing?” He replied, “Because he is a man who has never submitted to humiliation.”

Sharaḥbīl granted him this. Some time passed. Then Sharaḥbīl went hunting and caught a wild ass. A herdsman appeared before him. Sharaḥbīl said, “Bring us this herdsman’s fire-sticks.” A man went to him and borrowed his fire-sticks, and the herdsman lent them. They9 kindled a fire, roasted the meat, and drank. Sharaḥbīl asked, “Who is this herdsman?” He was told, “Maʿbad ibn Abī Ḥanash.” Sharaḥbīl said, “Maʿbad ibn Abī Ḥanash! Call him so that we may entertain him. His father claimed that he had never submitted to humiliation.” They summoned him. He came and ate. Then Sharaḥbīl signaled to one of his companions to provoke him. One of them said, “May the king live forever! I once exchanged words with a man of Banū Taghlib and struck his eye.” Maʿbad asked, “Did you give him his due?” “No,” he replied. “Did he forgive you?” “No.” Maʿbad struck him in the eye and said, “This is for that, and the aggressor is the more unjust!” This became a proverb.

Sharaḥbīl said, “A man of Taghlib spoke in my presence.” Maʿbad said to him:

“May his god aid the king! Let him speak well of Banū Taghlib or keep silent.”

Sharaḥbīl said to him, “And you would silence me?” Then he spoke. “A man spoke in my presence, and I disliked what he said, so I raised my bow and struck him on the head with it, splitting it open.” Maʿbad said, “May his god aid the king! Did you give him his due?” “No,” he replied. “Did he forgive you?” “No.” Maʿbad raised his bow and struck Sharaḥbīl on the head with it. Sharaḥbīl fell unconscious. Sharaḥbīl’s clansmen leapt upon Maʿbad and killed him. Sharaḥbīl came to and asked about Maʿbad. They said, “We killed him.”

He condemned them for it, saying, “By God, we did not keep faith with his father, and the man did not kill me. Why did you not wait for me to decide what to do with him?” He sent for Abū Ḥanash, told him what had happened, and said, “This is his blood-price.” Abū Ḥanash refused to accept it. Sharaḥbīl doubled it for him, but he refused. Sharaḥbīl said, “Since a king killed him, I will pay you the blood-price of a king.” Abū Ḥanash said, “I will never take payment for him.” Sharaḥbīl said to him, “By God, I do not fear you or your people, but I do fear your tongue.”

Abū Ḥanash said:

/ As for the satire you fear,you shall hear neither ill nor good.

I honor myself and keep clear of you,though one day in battle I may leave you sorely wounded.10

I shall repay you for what your hands have done;there is no respite for one who seeks our blood.

A neighbor is like a guest, inevitably:one day he departs, though he may stay a long while.

Sharaḥbīl set watchers over him and said, “If you see him tanning water-skins, he intends to go to his people.” Abū Ḥanash learned of this. He kept his camels thirsty for three watering intervals, one after another. Then, at the next watering interval, he drove them away when the people had dispersed from the watering places.

He passed by his family, took them with him, cut the lips off whichever of his finest camels he wished, and made for his people across the open country. This is the watering interval that the Arabs call “the watering interval of Abū Ḥanash.” When the Day of al-Kulāb came, Abū Ḥanash killed Sharaḥbīl.

As for Thumāmah ibn al-Mustanīr al-Sulamī, then al-Ẓafrī, and Muʿāwiyah ibn al-Ḥārith al-Jushamī, Thumāmah was the shrewdest of the Arabs, the most resolute, and the bravest. He used to raid on foot and would stipulate to anyone who raided with him: “I will not disobey you when you give me an order, and you must not disobey me when I give you one.” No one who raided with him ever returned without being killed, so he continued raiding alone. Then Muʿāwiyah ibn al-Ḥārith al-Jushamī, one of the Banū Jusham ibn Muʿāwiyah, set out until he came to Thumāmah at his home. He said, “I want to raid with you.”

Thumāmah said, “My raiding is indeed a gamble, and raiding with me is hard. Would you prefer something better?” He asked, “What is that?” Thumāmah said, “I will give you a portion of my property to make you independent, and you can return to your family.” He replied, “That is what I fled from. I want to go out raiding with you.” Thumāmah said, “Anyone who goes out with me must not disobey me in anything I command, even if it brings his death; and I will not disobey him in anything, even if it brings my death.” He said, “That is agreed.” They set out on foot until they entered the territory of Bajīlah.

One morning, in some part of their land, they entered a cave to spend the evening there and then set out. While they were in that cave, they saw a man standing on a mountain peak, calling for help. One said to his companion, “Do you think he has learned where we are?” People kept gathering around him until many had assembled. The two watched and listened. When the man was satisfied with those who had gathered, he said, “Listen! I have tracked two men who crossed the valley last night. I suspect their tracks belong to Thumāmah ibn al-Mustanīr and a companion of his.”

“Beware of them, for your lives and your livestock. Do not let livestock out to pasture unless people accompany it. Let no man go out alone until you are sure that this wicked man has left your territory.” Then he departed, and the people dispersed. Thumāmah said to his companion, “Have you ever seen a man with keener sight or more prudent counsel than this one?” He replied, “I have seen no one like him.” Thumāmah said, “Then go and bring him to me.” He asked, “What is this? How am I to bring him to you? By God, there is no one among the Arabs like him.” Thumāmah said, “That is the point. You must bring him to me. If you had first ordered me to bring him to you, I would have brought him—or died trying.” Muʿāwiyah came out of the cave and took his sword. When he was nearly in sight of the encampment, he buried his sword behind a rise that lay between him and the encampment. Then he walked on, making his way through the houses and asking for the house of their chief. They pointed it out to him. He reached the man who had spoken of himself, and sat down beside him. He said, “I am a man of Juhaynah of Quḍāʿah. With me are my aged father, my elderly mother, and my sister. We have property with us, and we wish to settle as your neighbors. Grant us protection.” The man said, “I have granted you protection.” Muʿāwiyah said, “Then give me a pledge of security from you.” The man brought him dates, and he ate some. Then Muʿāwiyah said, “My father will not believe me, and will not be reassured by what I tell him. Would you come with me to him, so that he may see you?” The man said, “I will.”

He set out with him. When they were out of sight behind the rise, Muʿāwiyah drew his sword11 and said, “By God, I will strike you on the head with this, or you will go where I take you.” He led him out and drove him along until he brought him to the cave. He said, “Go in.” The man entered, and Muʿāwiyah entered with him. He sat him beside Thumāmah and said, “There you are.” The Bajīlī said, “And you are Thumāmah?” He replied, “Yes, I am Thumāmah.” The man said, “I see that I warned people about you, yet I have fallen into your hands!” Thumāmah said, “Ransom yourself.” He replied, “Yes: seventy she-camels for you both.” Thumāmah said, “Give us a pledge that you will gather them for us until we pass by you on our way back. And give us safe conduct through your territory as we leave, then return to you.” The man said, “You have my pledge before God.” They went out and continued on their way. They acquired property, then returned. Passing by the Bajīlī, they took seventy she-camels from him and drove them off together.

Ḥarāḍah ibn al-Ḥārith al-Jushamī, the brother of Muʿāwiyah ibn al-Ḥārith who was with Thumāmah, came from his people to stay with Thumāmah’s family. He asked them about his brother. They said, “He went out with Thumāmah. By God, no one who goes out with him returns alive.” He waited until evening, then attacked Thumāmah’s brother, killed him, and went away.

Thumāmah and his companion drove their spoils onward. When they had entered the territory of Banū Sulaym, Thumāmah said to Muʿāwiyah, “You are safe now. Drive the camels at an easy pace.” Thumāmah mounted one of the camels and went ahead to his family. When they saw him, they began to weep. He asked, “What is wrong with you?” They said, “The brother of the Jushamī who is with you killed your brother.” They told him the story.

Thumāmah said, “Be quiet, and let no one leave the encampment.” He turned back until he came upon his Jushamī companion among the camels, just as the man was about to kill him. When the Jushamī saw him, he said, “Stay where you are. Do not come closer. What is the matter? What brought you back?” Thumāmah said, “I reached the encampment and found them safe. I feared some rash man of the encampment might act against you, so I came back to stay with you.” The Jushamī said, “No, by God! That is not what brought you back. You left me with one look and returned with another. God knows that you would only draw near me for a serious matter, which I recognize.” Thumāmah said, “It is no lie: your brother killed my brother.” The Jushamī said, “So you came to kill me. Give me a pledge and come close, so that you may settle with me and I with you.” They pledged one another, then drew close. Under safe conduct, they divided the camels, on the condition that the Jushamī leave their territory and return to his homeland.

After they had divided them, Thumāmah said to him, “Do you see the she-camel in your herd that the stallion mounted yesterday? You are safe from me until she gives birth.” The Jushamī drove his camels until he reached his brother and reproached him for what he had done. They stayed until, when he saw that the she-camel was about to give birth, he said to his brother, “Ḥarāḍah, by God, Thumāmah is watching that she-camel at this very moment, in my view. There was a pledge of safe conduct between us until she gave birth, and Thumāmah has been waiting for the she-camel to give birth.”

Thumāmah and one of his brothers went out and descended into the valley nearby, where they watched. When evening came, the Jushamī and his brother kept watch in turns. Muʿāwiyah fell asleep early in the night while Ḥarāḍah kept watch over him. Ḥarāḍah did not stay awake long before he too fell asleep. This was under Thumāmah’s watchful eye. Thumāmah said to his brother, “Stay where you are.” Thumāmah approached slowly until he passed Ḥarāḍah, who was asleep. He entered the tent and struck Muʿāwiyah with the sword in the middle of his body. Then he quickly returned to his brother and said, “Mount; I have killed the man.” When the sword struck him, Muʿāwiyah said to his brother Ḥarāḍah, “Run into the valley and leave me.” He ran out. Muʿāwiyah took his sword and went near the entrance of the tent, hoping that Thumāmah would return. When Thumāmah’s brother saw Ḥarāḍah running into the valley, he said, “By God, I do not think you killed the man. I see his brother running into the valley, wanting to call for help. You have wounded the man.

“Turn back against him. If you have not killed him, you have disabled him, so finish him off.” Thumāmah turned back. Muʿāwiyah was sitting, at the end of his strength, with his sword beside him, hoping Thumāmah would return. When Thumāmah entered the tent, Muʿāwiyah struck him and felled him. Thumāmah said, “Your brother is a sleeper, and my brother is a disgrace. Spare my brother, and tell your people to bury you and me in one grave.” Concerning this, Durayd ibn al-Ṣimmah al-Jushamī said:

By your life, I do not grieve for Ḥarāḍ, his mother’s son,standing at half the full measure of protection.

The night’s war prolonged beyond what he expected,so he slept. And here is one who sleeps, secure from sudden attack.

Ḥirāḍa kept watch over him. But Ḥirāḍa had not been there long before he fell asleep. That was within sight of Thumāma. Thumāma said to his brother, “Stay where you are.” Thumāma approached, walking slowly, until he passed Ḥirāḍa, who was asleep. He entered the tent and struck Muʿāwiya across the waist with his sword. Then he quickly returned to his brother and said, “Mount up; I have killed the man.”

When Muʿāwiya was struck by the sword, he said to his brother Ḥirāḍa, “Run into the valley and leave me.” He ran out. Muʿāwiya took his sword, went to the entrance of the tent, and hoped that Thumāma would come back a second time. When Thumāma’s brother saw Ḥirāḍa running through the valley, he said, “By God, I do not think you killed the man. I see his brother running through the valley, wanting to call for help. I think you only wounded him.

“Go back at him! If you have not killed him, you have at least disabled him; finish him off.” Thumāma turned back. Muʿāwiya was sitting there, barely alive, with his sword, hoping Thumāma would return. When Thumāma entered the tent, Muʿāwiya struck him and felled him. Thumāma said:

“Your brother is a light sleeper, and my brother is a heavy sleeper. Spare my brother, and tell your people to bury you and me in one grave.”

Durayd ibn al-Ṣimmah al-Jushamī said of this:

By your life, I do not grieve for Ḥirāḍ, his mother’s son, standing watch over half the allotment of protection.

The night’s battle lasted longer than he expected; he slept, while this man, safe from treachery, lay sleeping.

What a sorry course you took, and how poorly you tended it, while Thumāma watched over it, crouched upon his sword.

The lion crept toward him, stealthily shadowing him, with a keen-edged blade in his hand.

His adversary’s return gave the sword’s edge its chance; he quickened his step, while the other stood there.

He returned to his loyal friend in the tent, and blamed him; the loyal friend in the tent was the one to blame.

He said to him, “Go back and satisfy your anger; do not act on a mere suspicion, nor let prudence blame you.”

When he made out his figure, he struck him, with a blow whose force the resolve did not fail.

So the two died, struck down by heedlessness. For one who goes to meet death, no amulets are strung.

The Slayers of Islam

(Qurrān) ibn Yasār al-Faqʿasī.

(ʿUqayba) ibn Hubayra, brother of Banū Naṣr ibn Qāʿin.

(Mālik) ibn al-Rayb al-Māzinī.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Khāzim al-Sulamī.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Sabra al-Ḥarashī.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn al-Ḥajjāj al-Dhubyānī, then al-Taghlibī. (Al-Qattāl), namely ʿUbāda ibn al-Muḥabbab ibn al-Miḍraḥī al-Kilābī.

(ʿUbayd Allāh) ibn Ziyād ibn Ẓabyān, brother of Banū Taym Allāt ibn Thaʿlaba ibn ʿUkāba.

(ʿAmr) ibn Abjar ibn ʿAbbād, also brother of Banū Taym Allāt.

(ʿUbayd Allāh) ibn al-Ḥurr al-Juʿfī.

As for (Qurrān) ibn Yasār ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Jaḥwān ibn Faqʿas, he had a young bondwoman from his people named Laylā. He was among his camels.

On a hot day he went out into the wilderness with her. He found gazelles in a cave, slaughtered them, and threw away what was inside their bellies. He came to his people’s encampment one night and found boys playing. One of them said, “Whose eye is this?” Another said, “Laylā’s eye.” She was playing with them. He went to his house but did not find Laylā there. He had with him a waterskin containing milk, and some of the gazelles had begun to spoil. He set them down, then lay down in his house. Before long, Laylā came and entered through the back of the house.

She smelled the meat and said, “Ugh! How foul this smell is!” Qurrān thought she meant him. He said, “Laylā, which is harder on a woman: having her nose cut off, or having her heel tendon severed?” She tried to deceive him about her nose and said, “No, having her heel tendon severed, because she would become lame. If her nose were cut off, she could wear a face veil over it, and no one would see it or notice.”

She slipped out through the back of the house. He followed her with a knife and caught up with her, cutting her heel tendon. She dragged herself to the house of her maternal uncle, al-Shimirdal, which was next to her own, and appealed to him against Qurrān. The people came out to drive Qurrān away. He went up to a water seep on a mountain belonging to his people, called Uḥāmir. He stayed by that seep, drinking its water and watching al-Shimirdal’s camels, a hundred milch she-camels, until they set out for water. At dusk they headed toward the mountain. He came down and met them at sunset in a valley in their territory called al-Dahs. He came upon the herdsman milking one of the she-camels so that he could break his fast with its milk, for he was fasting. The herdsman did not notice until Qurrān struck the she-camel’s two heel tendons and hamstrung it. The herdsman’s milk vessel spilled, though he was fasting.

The herdsman cursed him, saying, “May God not reward you with good,” not realizing what Qurrān was doing. Qurrān charged at the herdsman and killed him. Then he mutilated him, cut off his genitals, and put them in his mouth. When the camels saw the flash of the sword, they stampeded a little. He hid the sword from them and soothed them with it until they quieted down. The hamstrung she-camel was called al-Kināz.

The place where he hamstrung her was therefore called the Fissure of al-Kināz. He then approached the camels, which had gone up to another fissure. He went to a she-camel called al-Mughriba and hamstrung her.

That fissure was called the Fissure of al-Mughriba. Then the camels turned toward a wide fissure, and he turned them into it for a little while. Then he hamstrung one of them called al-Jazlāʾ. That fissure was called the Fissure of al-Jazlāʾ. He continued driving them little by little, soothing them whenever they stampeded and hiding the sword from them, until he had hamstrung them all together. Five young she-camels remained. They were at the front of the herd, the fringes of the yearling she-camels. He drove them through the night until he brought them to a ravine in Uḥāmir called Laḥyā Jamal. He shot three of them with arrows, and stuck arrows from his quiver into the other two. The two broke away from him and withdrew into the ravine. He went out to drive them.

One of them made for the camels’ customary watering place. He went after the other alone and hamstrung her at a fissure called the Fissure of the Daughter of al-Raqbāʾ. This fissure was fifteen miles from where he had hamstrung the camels. The remaining young she-camel came out, and at dawn the yearling camels were standing by their mothers’ heads, bleating; the camels had been hamstrung, and some had died. When the yearlings caught the smell of blood and saw the slaughtered camels, they ran toward the water. The herdsman followed them. They were a hundred yearlings, their flanks, heads, and necks smeared with their mothers’ blood. They reached al-Shimirdal. The Banū Aʿyā came running to the camels, slaughtered those still alive, and divided their meat among themselves. Qurrān went up to Abān al-Abyaḍ, a mountain between Banū Asad and Qays, abundant in mountain gazelles, various kinds of wild animals, and water. He began following the wild animals, shooting and killing them, eating their meat and drinking from that water. While he was there, one day a leopard appeared before him. It confronted him as if it meant to attack him.

He pulled out his arrows and showed them to it. The leopard spread its claws and showed them to him. Qurrān drew his sword; the leopard bared its fangs. Then Qurrān sheathed his sword. The leopard turned away and left. Qurrān went on, and the leopard followed him. When Qurrān looked at it, the leopard turned back. Qurrān continued, and the leopard followed in his tracks. Qurrān glanced back at it, and the leopard turned away.

Qurrān realized that it was beckoning him. Qurrān was hungry that day, having failed to catch any game earlier. He followed the leopard, which walked ahead of him. He found a mountain gazelle whose neck the leopard had broken. Qurrān slaughtered it while the leopard watched from nearby. Qurrān then struck a fire, roasted it, cut it up, held pieces over the fire, and tossed them to the leopard.

Then he cut up the rest into strips. They continued like this: whenever Qurrān hunted something, he fed some of it to the leopard.

And whenever the leopard killed something, it led Qurrān to it. They had a pool to which they went for water. If the leopard arrived first, Qurrān held back until the leopard had drunk its fill and wallowed in the water. Then it came out and went off, and Qurrān drank. If Qurrān arrived first, the leopard held back until Qurrān had drunk and bathed. Qurrān said of this; he had a brother in Syria named al-Jawn ibn Yasār. He described the leopard:

“My two companions, let there be no dispute over the right to draw water between us: our watering turn belongs to whichever of us comes first.

The mountain gazelles have undertaken to provide our food; each of us gets his share, cut into strips and roasted.

I have a companion in the cave, a long-eared companion, my brother al-Jawn—except that he does not make excuses.

Each of us would be an enemy if he saw a vulnerable point in the other; in enmity, each of us is composed.

Whenever we meet, our words are silence, and our glances are like dark-gray spearheads.”

Al-Shimirdal sought redress against the Banū Faqʿas from ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān. He claimed that the young herdsman was a free man, that his camels had been hamstrung, and that their owner had been killed in the Sacred Precinct. ʿUthmān dealt harshly with them.

They paid two hundred in blood money for the owner, and compensated al-Shimirdal with two hundred. Qurrān said:

For Laylā’s visitors from among you, Banū Barthan, I advance toward danger more than Sulayk of the raiders.

You visit her, but I do not visit your women; alas for the sons of slave women who gather firewood!

Every evening they walk in linen to Laylā’s house, like the walk of the maces.

You have lied, by the House of God! You will not be safe from me, while you are rivals and suitors for Laylā.

He said this about hamstringing the camels:

With every Hibir in al-Qinān and Ṣāra, dried meat—Umm ʿĀmir has fed you well.

Her sons repaid her with kindness and rallied to her, with a she-camel and her foal, or a huge barren one.

He said this when he cut Laylā’s heel tendon:

I told them, “Find Laylā a staff to support her, or a lance of lote wood.”

I parted from Laylā in anger and left her, as Laylā was distressed by scarcity and by the hamstringing.

One of the Banū Faqʿas said:

I have never seen anyone hamstrung like Laylā, nor anyone, like Qurrān, more devoted and justly balanced.

As for (ʿUqayba) ibn Hubayra ibn Rabīʿa ibn Judhayma ibn Mālik ibn Naṣr ibn Qāʿin, he was bold and resolute. He had committed many acts of treachery and shown contempt for authority. Ibn al-Kalbī related, on the authority of Abū Sahl, from his father, that ʿUqayba had a little daughter or stepdaughter. He had a cousin named Tamīm ibn al-Akhtam, who had a little daughter. She and ʿUqayba’s daughter were playing together, and Tamīm’s daughter broke one of ʿUqayba’s daughter’s front teeth.

Tamīm went and gathered the notables of Banū Asad, then came to ʿUqayba and said, “Cousin, what you see has happened. Here is my daughter; break one of her teeth. Or take one of my own, if you wish.

“Or, if you wish, forgive her. She is still a little girl; she has not yet lost her baby teeth, and the tooth will grow back.” The people said, “The man has treated you fairly.” He replied, “By God, I shall kill him.” They repeated what they had said to him, and he repeated his answer. They told Tamīm, “Go,” thinking that ʿUqayba was joking. Tamīm knew he would do it, because of his murderous treachery and wickedness. Tamīm spent a year on his guard against him. One evening, while fasting, he prayed in his people’s mosque, then entered his house and forgot to shut the door. ʿUqayba came in with a sword and struck him until he killed him.

The people cried out. ʿUqayba was seized and taken to Muṣʿab ibn al-Zubayr. Muṣʿab questioned him, but he did not deny killing Tamīm. Tamīm had a son named ʿAnbasa, a young man. Muṣʿab paid a blood price to Manẓūr, another to Muḥammad ibn ʿUmayr, and another to Tamīm’s people. Tamīm’s daughter said:

ʿUqayba, may your hands never prevail! Was there no way to secure your right without killing Tamīm?

ʿUqayba, had you warned him, you would have found him like a sword whose stroke is gentler than its keen edge.

A stain will follow you among the tribe, and you will surely be killed for it, disgraced.

ʿUqayba said this when he killed him:

He fell dead, his anus gaping,12 where we met, like a torn young camel.13

Abū Samāl was given a hundred thousand dirhams. ʿAnbasa grew eager to collect the blood money, so one of Tamīm’s daughters went out bareheaded, saying:

If ʿUqayba is killed, O people, we bring joy to some, and give birth to illness.

If ʿUqayba is spared, O people, we shall be ʿUqba’s servants or his slave women.

May God curse the one who strips us bare, while ʿUqba remains safe forever, a cloak.

When the people heard what she said—and they had been inclined toward reconciliation—her words angered them, and they turned away from reconciliation. Muṣʿab handed him over to them. That day Muṣʿab sat in the mosque courtyard, and the people gathered. When ʿUqaybah became certain he would be killed, he said to Tamīm’s daughter: “By God, I struck your father a blow, and saw the Pleiades in his arse.” She said: “By God, you will be struck a blow, and I shall see the Daughters of the Bier in your backside.” [Then ʿUqaybah] turned to the people and said: “People!” Those who were standing sat down, and those walking hurried over.

When they had gathered, he said: “Be quiet. By God, I did not kill my cousin when I killed him because he had failed to give me my due and more; rather, I saw the Commander of the Faithful, ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁, in this very place where the emir is now, and Tamīm appeared before him from the side of the mosque. ʿAlī looked at him and said, ‘Whoever would like to see a chunk of the chunks of Hell, let him look at that man’—and he pointed to Tamīm—‘may God have mercy on his killer.’ So I killed him.” The people said: “May God have mercy on you.” And he was killed.

As for ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khāzim al-Sulamī, he was among the bravest and most resolute and powerful of men. He gained control of Khurāsān. Muḥammad ibn Ḥabīb said: Ibn al-Aʿrābī told me this account: The king of the Turks sent envoys to him, informing him that he would raid Khurāsān, or else accept a fixed tribute from him.

The envoys came to him on a day of intense cold. Ibn Khāzim ordered his vaulted chamber heated, and it was stoked until it glowed white. He seated the envoys on felt mats in the snow, with fur garments on them, while he sat among them in a thin robe. The envoys began to shiver with cold. When he knew that the vaulted chamber had been cleared of what was in it and cleaned, he said to the envoys:

“I expect the cold has harmed you. Come with us into the house.” As they approached the house, its heat met them, and they shrank back from entering. He went inside and sat at the head of the room.

The envoys returned to their master and told him that they had come from a devil whom neither snow nor fire affected. He then refrained from raiding.

Muḥammad ibn Ḥabīb said: I heard Ibn al-Aʿrābī recount that al-Muhallab was asked, “Who is the toughest of men?” He replied, “The man with the dappled mule,” meaning ʿAbbād ibn al-Ḥuṣayn al-Ḥabaṭī. He was asked, “What about Ibn Khāzim?” He said, “You asked me about the toughest man, and I told you. Had you asked me about the toughest of humankind and jinn, I would have said: ʿAbd Allāh and Muṣʿab, the sons of al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khāzim.”

As for ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sabrah al-Ḥarashī, he had several audacious exploits. Among them was this: There was a woman of Qays in one of the towns of Syria. A young man of Arabized descent made advances to her, courting her openly and seeking her in secret. The people passed by on their way to collect their stipends. The woman sent her maidservant to the caravanserai and said, “See whether there is anyone of Qays in the caravanserai.” The maidservant asked, “Is there a man of Qays here?”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sabrah said to her, “What do you need?” She replied, “My mistress is a woman of Qays, and she wants you.” He went with her to the woman, who told him about the young man. He said to her, “God willing, I will deal with him for you. Let him know that you have placed your affairs in my hands.” She did so. The young man entered the house with him, and ʿAbd Allāh struck him with his sword until he killed him. He said to the maidservant, “Dig with me.”

She dug. When she had finished digging, he leapt upon the maidservant, killed her, threw her into the pit with the young man, and buried her. The woman cried out. He said, “Be quiet. If you do not keep quiet, we shall all perish.” He left, went to his companions, took their travel money, and sent it to the woman, saying, “Buy a servant with this—one who does not know what is in your house.”

The money was seventy dinars. Another account says that this woman was from Banū Sulaym and was renowned for her beauty. The governor of the village where she lived had demanded her favors and pressed her persistently. She did not feel safe from him, so she sent to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sabrah, who was at Dābiq. He told her, “Receive him and invite him to stay.” She did so. ʿAbd Allāh hid in her house, until he did to the man what he did. He said:

She came to me at evening; I cannot blame her for coming,veiled from view, with a far-off, well-meaning man.

To protect her from a vile, squalid man,and in God and a cousin there is one who lifts oppression.

When I had put an end to her fear, her tearscame pouring forth, in vain, from her eyes there.

She wept for a slave girl of hers who had perished,though I killed her only to conceal the secrets.

I said to her: “Do not grieve; if we go safely,then, without fail, the matter will be made known.”

I relieved you of fear, and the Lord of the Throne does not fail;there is a reward in patience when calamities befall.

Here are seventy aws for you in her place;I think you will find a useful servant among them.

So let him be far away in death; but she will not be far away,for she was joined with him in the grave—what was destined has come to pass.

There was a perfumer named Fīrūz at the Bridge of Manbij. It reached ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sabrah that he had pinched the hand of a woman of Qays. She cried out, “ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sabrah!”

At that time ʿAbd Allāh was in Armenia. When the story and the woman’s words reached him, he said, “At your service!” He came rushing down until he reached the Bridge of Manbij, killed the perfumer, and returned to Armenia. More than one person was arrested over the perfumer’s death, until they learned that ʿAbd Allāh had killed him.

ʿAbd Allāh was imprisoned in Armenia. He said:

For Fīrūz, death lies in wait,whether the sea or the lion takes him;

or a scorpion, or a thorn lodged in his throat,or a snake with speckled scales along its back;

or two men of the Jarmuq, muttering to him in their tongue—their nearest lands are Ḥiṣnān or Bilād;

or a man who conceals his anger, whose enmityno one knows, nor what seethes in his breast.

The people gathered at Dābiq, preparing for a raid against the Byzantines. Ibn Sabrah sat down with an Iraqi cloth merchant to buy some garments from him. The merchant mentioned Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, and ʿUthmān, and spoke ill of them. Ibn Sabrah rebuked him, saying, “Attend to your own business and leave what does not concern you.” The man did not stop speaking. ʿAbd Allāh took a sword from the sword-polishers and killed him with it.

He mounted his horse and fled to his family. He said:

The vile man’s mother was shamed,and he saw me strike with a resolute blow.

We came to him seeking no quarrel, but he provoked usagainst him, heedless of the dreaded calamities.

He wrongfully attacked ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffānand spoke ill of Abū Bakr, though he was no truth-teller.

He spoke ill of Abū Ḥafṣ. What an outrage!The crushing calamities have brought it to pass.

I said to him, “Stop! Woe to you—do not persist!”But my gentle warning did not turn the fool aside.

In my anger, I raised over him a keen-edged sword,its blade swift to cut through the parting of the hair.

I left him in the marketplace, falling face-firstbefore the champions of Islam at Marj Dābiq.

As for al-Qattāl al-Kilābī—whose name was ʿUbādah ibn Muḥibb ibn al-Muḍraḥī—a female slave belonging to his paternal uncle angered him, and he killed her. His uncle claimed that the slave girl had been pregnant.

His uncle said:

“Give my two sons back to me, you fatherless lot!As for the mother of my sons, I care nothing for her.”

When al-Qattāl saw this, he exhumed the slave girl from the grave. He brought men from the mine—meaning the gold mine—then cut open her belly and extracted her womb. He said, “Do you see a child?” Then he put her back and said:

I am the one who pulled her out,then called upon strapping young men.

They bore witness, and they refuted what was said.

Al-Qattāl also attacked a paternal cousin of his, named Ziyād, and killed him. He was brought to Medina and imprisoned there for some time. A Qurashī named Ismāʿīl ibn Habbār ibn al-Aswad ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn Asad was in charge of the prison. He used to denounce him before the emir, saying:

“Al-Qattāl sings and says:

Whenever I wish, noble women of Quraysh and ʿĀmirsing to me upon the back of a high couch.”

The emir sent for al-Qattāl and said to him, “Are you the one who said what has reached me?” He replied, “I did not say that. What I said was:

The shackles sing to me, and the emir’s foreign loutsdrive me to prison, speaking gibberish.”

Muṣʿab ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf said to al-Qattāl, “Would you have the courage, if I gave you a sword and saddled a mount for you, to kill Ibn Habbār and then escape on the mount?” He said, “Yes.” He gave him a sword and saddled a mount for him. Al-Qattāl waited until he had performed the evening prayer, then said to Ibn Habbār, “Take me out so I can pray in the open.” He took him out. Al-Qattāl prayed, with his sword concealed under his cloak. When he finished, he took his sword and struck Ibn Habbār with it, shoved him into the prison, shut the door on him, went out, and mounted the beast, turning it toward his own land. Then he said:

I left Ibn Habbār lying slain behind me,while Shābah lay ahead, the place I seek.

With the sword of a man whose name I shall never reveal,even if my soul is beset by cares.

Then he reached ʿAmāyah and said:

Is there a refuge at Ṣāḥat al-ʿAmqāʾ, or at ʿAmāyah,or at al-Udamā, from the dread of death?

He would stay there with Ḥabīb ibn Jabbār ibn Salmā ibn Mālik: by day he would be there, and at night he would go down to Ḥabīb and stay with him. Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam said, “Whoever among my slaves guides me to al-Qattāl will be free. Whoever is a free man will receive such-and-such.”

He offered a generous reward. A man from Banū al-ʿAjlān went out and came to Marwān, telling him where al-Qattāl was staying with Ḥabīb ibn Jabbār. Marwān sent a force to him.

When they came to Ḥabīb, he took his daughter out of the bridal litter, put al-Qattāl inside it, dressed him in her clothes, and drew the curtain. When the men saw the woman, they were embarrassed. Ḥabīb said, “It is not right for you to enter upon my womenfolk and my household.” The men hesitated and withdrew. Al-Qattāl said:

Has it reached the young men of my people that,when the battle grew fierce, I took the name Zaynab,

drew my cloak over the growth of my beard,and showed the men my henna-stained fingertips?

As for Mālik ibn al-Rayb ibn Ḥawṭ ibn Qurṭ ibn Ḥisl ibn Rabīʿah ibn Kābiyah ibn Ḥirqūṣ ibn Māzin, he was a highwayman who waylaid travelers. A rajaz poet says of him:

May God save you from al-Qaṣīm,the valley of Falj and Banū Tamīm,

from Ghuwayth, who opens the packs,and from Abū Ḥardabah the sinner, Mālik and his poisoned sword.

A man from the Anṣār, from among the people of Medina, had been appointed governor over ʿAmr ibn Ḥanẓalah. He arrived and seized Mālik and Abū Ḥardabah. He sent Abū Ḥardabah away but stayed behind with the men among whom Mālik was held. He ordered a slave of his to lead Mālik along. Mālik caught the Anṣārī’s slave off guard, seized the sword he was carrying, and struck him until he killed him. Then he charged the Anṣārī, killed him, and fled until he reached Bahrain.

From there he crossed over to Fārs. He said:

Why do you say the sword weighs down my shoulder,when it drives me among men laid low?

Were it not for the sword’s blade, which leads meby its strap, I would be a stout-fingered, harsh man.

As for ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Ḥurr ibn ʿAmr ibn Khālid ibn al-Mujammaʿ ibn Mālik, his encounters with Ibn Ziyād, Muṣʿab, and Ibn al-Ashtar were many.

He used to plunder the revenues of al-Sawād, and he was generous. Ibn al-Ḥurr had settled at al-Anbār while raiding Narsī, the dihqān of al-Anbār, whom he later took into his service. Narsī informed him that an Abyssinian named al-Ghadāf was waylaying travelers between ʿĀnah and al-Anbār and entering al-Anbār by day. No one dared stop him for fear of him. When— A man from among the Anṣār, one of the people of Medina, had been appointed over ʿAmr ibn Ḥanẓala. He arrived and seized Mālik and Abū Ḥardaba. He sent Abū Ḥardaba away, but stayed behind with the group among whom Mālik was. He ordered a servant of his to drive Mālik along. Mālik caught the Anṣārī’s servant off guard, while the sword was on him. He snatched it from him and struck him until he killed him. Then he attacked the Anṣārī and killed him, and fled until he reached al-Baḥrayn.

Then he crossed from there to Fārs. He said:

Why do you say the sword weighs down my shoulderwhen it drives me among men felled in battle?

Were it not for the sword’s keen edge, which keeps leading me,with its strap, I would be a thick-fingered, hard-hearted lout.

As for ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Ḥurr ibn ʿAmr ibn Khālid ibn al-Mujammaʿ ibn Mālik, he had many engagements with Ibn Ziyād, with Muṣʿab, and with Ibn al-Ashtar.

He used to plunder the lands of al-Sawād, and he was generous. Ibn al-Ḥurr had settled at al-Anbār, and during his raids against Narsī, the governor of al-Anbār—whom he later appointed to the post—Narsī informed him that a black man called al-Ghadāf was waylaying travelers between ʿĀna and al-Anbār and entering al-Anbār by day. No one dared stop him for fear of him. When a woman pleased him, he seized her husband and bound him, then went to her and satisfied his desire with her while her husband watched. Jealousy and indignation seized ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Ḥurr, and he asked the man to describe al-Ghadāf’s whereabouts. The man described it to him. He rode alone and made his companions swear that none of them would follow him. Then he set out toward the place. Al-Ghadāf had come into view. Ibn al-Ḥurr recognized him from the description. He approached, riding as if he stood tall upon his horse, large in stature. He wore a red turban. ʿUbayd Allāh rode alongside him until they descended into a valley. The black man said to him, “Where have you come from, horseman?” He replied, “From al-Anbār.” The man said, “I have heard that Ibn al-Ḥurr has settled there. Where do you think he is headed?” He replied, “He is after you. I am ʿUbayd Allāh. Beware, you dog!” Then he charged him and thrust at him, knocking him down. The black man leapt up, seized his horse’s bridle, and fled up the valley. Ibn al-Ḥurr pursued him and caught him before he could mount his horse. He struck his leg and severed it. The black man seized his leg and hurled it at Ibn al-Ḥurr. It struck his head. They say Ibn al-Ḥurr said, “He nearly knocked me down with the force of his throw.” Then he looked at him, his eyes blazing. He walked over and killed him, and took his horse and weapons. Then he returned to al-Anbār. When its people saw him, they cried, “God is greatest!” Of this Ibn al-Ḥurr says:

I saw a man in a desolate valley,like a lion when he attacks a hero,

A mighty quarry: had you seen his statureamong men, you would have likened him to a camel.

I rode alongside him for a while; I did not fear him,save that I kept glancing around me, lest I see a thicket.

I hurled him down among rivers and valleys;none but I knows what he did.

He is called al-Ghadāf, and his crest had tilted:By God, al-Ghadāf met his appointed term!

Those who wore turbans in Mecca for fear that women would be captivated by their beauty

(Ḥanẓala) ibn ʿUthmān [ibn ʿAmr] ibn Fātik ibn al-Qulayb ibn ʿAmr ibn Asad ibn Khuzayma. Al-Ḥaḍr also used to veil himself. (Al-Zibriqān), namely Ḥuṣayn ibn Badr, one of the Banū Bahdala ibn ʿAwf ibn Kaʿb ibn Saʿd ibn Zayd Manāt ibn Tamīm.

(Subayʿ) al-Ṭahawī.

(Aʿfar) al-Yarbūʿī. (Barjad), namely Qays ibn Ḥassān ibn ʿAmr ibn Mirthad, one of the Banū Qays ibn Thaʿlaba.

(Zayd al-Khayl) ibn Muhalhil al-Ṭāʾī.

(ʿAmr) ibn Ḥamma ibn [al-Ḥārith ibn] Rāfiʿ al-Dawsī.

(Qays) ibn Salama ibn Sharāḥīl ibn Aṣhab al-Juʿfī.

(Jarīr) ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Bajalī. (Dhū al-Kalāʿ)

Sumayfaʿ ibn Nākūr al-Ḥimyarī. (Qays) ibn al-Khaṭīm al-Awsī. (Imruʾ al-Qays) ibn Ḥujr al-Kindī.

Those who rode a large horse, with their big toes dragging on the ground

(Jadhīma) ibn ʿAlqama ibn Firās, “the spear’s target,” al-Kinānī.

Rabīʿa ibn ʿĀmir ibn Jadhīma ibn ʿAlqama ibn Firās used to walk alongside a woman riding in a litter and kiss her; he was therefore called “Kisser of Women in Litters.” Also (Zayd al-Khayl) ibn Muhalhil al-Ṭāʾī.

(Abū Zubayd), Ḥarmala ibn al-Nuʿmān al-Ṭāʾī.

(ʿAdī) ibn Ḥātim ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Ṭāʾī.

(Qays) ibn Saʿd ibn ʿUbāda al-Anṣārī. His father was (Saʿd) ibn ʿUbāda.

(Saʿd) ibn Muʿādh al-Anṣārī.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Ubayy ibn Salūl, the hypocrite, one of the Banū al-Ḥubla.

(Bashīr) ibn Saʿd, of the Banū al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj.

(Jabala) ibn al-Ayham al-Ghassānī.

(Ḥamal) ibn Mirdās al-Nakhaʿī.

(Mālik) al-Ashtar ibn al-Ḥārith al-Nakhaʿī.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn al-Ḥuṣayn Dhī al-Ghuṣṣa al-Ḥārithī.

(ʿĀmir) ibn al-Ṭufayl al-Jaʿfarī.

(Qays) ibn Salama ibn Sharāḥīl ibn Aṣhab al-Juʿfī. The stallion would become jealous of him because of his size: when it saw him among the camels, it grew jealous of him.

The raḍafāt of the Arabs

Shaybān. Taghlib. Iyād. Bahraʾ.

The jamarāt of the Arabs

(Ḍabba) ibn Add.

(ʿAbs) ibn Baghiḍ.

(Al-Ḥārith) ibn Kaʿb.

(Yarbūʿ) ibn Ḥanẓala.

The skulls of the Arabs

(Kalb) ibn Wabara ibn Taghlib ibn Ḥulwān ibn ʿImrān ibn al-Ḥāf ibn Quḍāʿa.

(Ṭayyiʾ) ibn Udad.

(Ḥanẓala) ibn Mālik ibn Zayd Manāt ibn Tamīm.

(ʿĀmir) ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿa ibn Muʿāwiya ibn Bakr ibn Hawāzin.

The hearthstones of the Arabs

(Sulaym and Hawāzin), sons of Manṣūr ibn ʿIkrima ibn Khaṣafa ibn Qays ʿAylān ibn Muḍar: one hearthstone. (Ghatafān): one hearthstone.

(Aʿṣur and Muḥārib) ibn Khaṣafa: one hearthstone.

The Ḍubayʿāt

All of them belong to Rabīʿa. (Ḍubayʿa) ibn Qays ibn Thaʿlaba is the most distinguished among them.

(Ḍubayʿa) Aḍjam ibn Rabīʿa ibn Nizār.

(Ḍubayʿa) ibn ʿAjl ibn Lujaym.

The Rabāʾiʿ

Among Tamīm: (Rabīʿa al-Jūʿ) ibn Mālik ibn Zayd Manāt ibn Tamīm.

(Rabīʿa) ibn Ḥanẓala ibn Mālik ibn Zayd ibn Tamīm.

(Rabīʿa) ibn Mālik ibn Ḥanẓala ibn Mālik ibn Zayd Manāt ibn Tamīm. Each of them is the other’s paternal uncle.

(Rabīʿa) ibn Kaʿb ibn Saʿd ibn Zayd Manāt ibn Tamīm; they are the Ḥubāq.

The Aqāriʿ among the Arabs

(Qurayʿ) ibn ʿAwf ibn Kaʿb ibn Saʿd ibn Zayd Manāt ibn Tamīm.

(Qurayʿ) ibn Muʿāwiya ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Jadhīma ibn ʿAwf.

(Qurayʿ) ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Numayr.

The eloquent men of Islam

(Abān) ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān.

(Abān) ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ.

(ʿAbd al-Malik) ibn ʿUmayr al-Laythī.

(Abū al-Aswad) al-Duʾalī.

(Muḥammad) ibn Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ. / (Al-Ḥasan) al-Baṣrī.

(Qabīṣa) ibn Jābir al-Asadī.

Those who gave a ruling in the pre-Islamic period that accorded with the ruling of Islam, and those who performed an act in the pre-Islamic period that God made a practice in Islam

The first instance of this is that Quraysh did not stand at ʿArafāt and did not leave Muzdalifa.

Quṣayy ibn Kilāb built the landmark at Muzdalifa. A fire was lit there to guide those standing at ʿArafāt when they left ʿArafāt for Muzdalifa. God made it a landmark in Islam and commanded people to stand there and pray.

ʿĀmir ibn al-Ẓarb al-ʿAdwānī gave a ruling concerning the hermaphrodite, and the ruling of Islam followed it. In the pre-Islamic period, Dhurb ibn Ḥawṭ ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Ḥāritha ibn Ḥayy al-Ṭāʾī also gave a ruling concerning the hermaphrodite like that of ʿĀmir ibn al-Ẓarb. Of him, Adham ibn Abī al-Zaʿrāʾ al-Ṭāʾī says in Islam:

One of us gave a ruling, and it accordedwith the practice of Islam in the pre-Islamic age.

The Arabs were unanimous in bequeathing inheritance to sons and not daughters.

Dhū al-Majāṣid, namely ʿĀmir ibn Jashm ibn Ghanm ibn Ḥabīb ibn Kaʿb ibn Yashkur, bequeathed his property to his children in the pre-Islamic period, with the male’s share equal to that of two females. This accorded with the ruling of Islam.

Those who forbade wine, drunkenness, and divining arrows in the pre-Islamic period

(ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib) ibn Hāshim.

(Shayba) ibn Rabīʿa ibn ʿAbd Shams, who was killed at Badr.

(ʿUthmān) ibn ʿAffān.

(Waraqa) ibn Nawfal ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā.

(Al-Walīd) ibn al-Mughīra, who beat his son Hishām for drinking it.

(Al-ʿAbbās) ibn Mirdās al-Sulamī, who said, “I will not drink a drink / that makes me the chief of my people in the morning and their fool by evening.”

(Qays) ibn ʿĀṣim al-Saʿdī.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Judʿān. He became drunk and began grappling with the moon. When he was told of this in the morning, he forbade wine.

(Miqyas) ibn Qays ibn ʿAdī al-Sahmī. He became drunk and began drawing with his urine, saying, “An ostrich or a camel.” When he sobered up and was told of this, he forbade wine.

(ʿĀmir) ibn al-Ẓarb al-ʿAdwānī.

(Ṣafwān) ibn Umayya ibn Muḥarrith al-Kinānī.

(ʿAfīf) ibn Maʿdī Karib al-Kindī.

(Al-Aslūm) al-Yāmī, of Hamdān.

(Abū Umayya) ibn al-Mughīra and (al-Ḥārith) ibn ʿUbayd, both al-Makhzūmīs.

(Zayd) ibn ʿAmr ibn Nufayl al-ʿAdawī.

(ʿĀmir) ibn Ḥudhaym al-Jumaḥī.

(Abū Dharr) al-Ghifārī.

(Yazīd) ibn Jaʿūna al-Laythī.

(Abū Wāqid) al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAwf al-Kinānī.

(ʿAmr) ibn ʿAbasa al-Sulamī.

(Quss) ibn Sāʿida al-Iyādī.

(ʿUbayd) ibn al-Abraṣ al-Asadī.

(Zuhayr) ibn Abī Sulmā al-Muzanī. And the two al-Nābighas: al-Dhubyānī and al-Jaʿdī.

(Ḥanẓala) the monk, son of Abū ʿĀmir al-Ghasīl, the one washed by the angels.

(Qabīṣa) ibn Iyās ibn Abī Ghafr ibn al-Nuʿmān ibn Ḥayya, the brother of the Banū Hiny ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Ghawth ibn Ṭayyiʾ. In another section, however, it is Iyās ibn Qabīṣa ibn Abī Ghafr who is cited as the one who said:

Whatever the calamity of death may bring,I see the tormented moon as a youth:

It appears small, then its light growsand its form, until at last it is full.

It draws near, its light and radiance fading;it dwindles until it disappears from sight.

So too a man grows, then wanes,and is renewed in his wake after he has passed.

Some have composed poetry on this subject. Qays ibn ʿĀṣim became drunk and pinched his daughter’s fold of flesh. When he was told of this, he forbade wine. He said:

I saw that wine has its benefits, yet in itare traits that corrupt a noble man.

By God, I shall not drink it as long as I live,nor ever invite a boon companion to it.

For wine disgraces those who drink itand brings upon them a grievous calamity.

When its intoxication goes round, it rises up,and visions make the wise man foolish.

ʿAfīf ibn Maʿdī Karib al-Kindī said this; it was on account of it that he was called “ʿAfīf” (“Chaste”), for his name was Sharāḥīl:

She said to me, “Come, let us indulge in youthful folly.”I replied, “I have refrained from what you know.”

I bid farewell to the divining arrows, though in the course of time I had become14

I have forbidden wine to myself, untilI am buried in the depths of a grave.

ʿĀmir ibn al-Ẓarb al-ʿAdwānī said:

If I drink wine, I drink it for its pleasure;if I give it up, I am its hater and reviler.

Were it not for pleasure and the young men, I would not see it,nor would it see me, except from the brink of the grave.

It demands from a young man what he does not possess,and steals the people’s reason and wealth.

It bequeaths rancor to people without any grievance,and brings shame upon a man of valor and sound judgment.

I swore by God to pour it and drink ituntil the dust of the grave scatters my limbs.

Al-Aslūm al-Yāmī, who was among those who forbade fornication and wine in the pre-Islamic period, said:

Ḥātim al-Ṭāʾī said, concerning the prohibition of wine and debauchery:

I hope that I shall die without having gainedany worldly pleasure through debauchery or wine.

Before Islam, the Arabs had six virtues: Islam abolished three of them and strengthened the other three:

Among them were the (custodianship) and the (provision of water).

Among them was the fact that Quraysh would feed the ṣabā wind for as long as it blew. When it subsided, they would stop.

Among them was the (dome) of ʿAwf ibn Abī ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf ibn / Muḥallim ibn Dhuhl ibn Shaybān: no hungry person would enter it without being fed, nor anyone afraid without being made safe.

Among them was the (palm grove) of Rabīʿah ibn al-Aswad al-Yashkurī, which he had made over to the wayfarer. His grave is there. At the Farewell Pilgrimage, when the Messenger of God ﷺ set aside every blood claim and privilege of the Jāhiliyyah except the custodianship and the provision of water, the son of Rabīʿah ibn al-Aswad stood up and said: “Messenger of God! My father had dedicated a palm grove of his to wayfarers. Is this a privilege for him? Then uphold it.” The Prophet ﷺ ordered him to uphold it. His descendants harvest those palms in groups of three and two: one palm has a single trunk and three tops. They harvest it twice a year. A man of Bakr ibn Wāʾil said:

Come to the arbiters of Bakr ibn Wāʾil,and be not like the ignorant, ever wavering.

The Banū Khālid, of the family of Shaybān, in the heights,or the noble ones among the descendants of ʿAmr ibn Murthid,

or the Yashkurīs, whose deeds are noble,descendants of the host’s benefactor from the family of al-Aswad.

This is what Muʿāwiyah referred to when he said: “I wish the owner of Rabīʿah ibn Aswad’s palm grove were in my place as caliph.”

Among them was (a privilege among Bajīlah), which belonged to the Banū Afṣā ibn Nadhīr ibn Qasr ibn ʿAbqar. Whenever a stranger came to stay with them, they would take charge of his property and entrust it to a man whose honesty they approved. They would not burden him with their own property while he remained among them. When he departed, they would return his property and travel with him. If he died, they would pay the blood price for him. If he was killed, they would seek vengeance for his blood. If he survived, they would escort him to a place of safety. ʿAmr ibn al-Khathārim says of this:

Whoever is a stranger, I ama guide for his estrangement among Afṣā.

They help the rich in his wealth,and enrich the poor among their neighbours.

A man who married into the families of four caliphs

ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān: al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik married his daughter ʿAbdah bint ʿAbd Allāh. Sulaymān ibn ʿAbd al-Malik married ʿĀʾishah bint ʿAbd Allāh. Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik married Umm Saʿīd bint ʿAbd Allāh. Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik married Ruqayyah bint ʿAbd Allāh.

A caliph greeted by his uncle, his father’s uncle, and his grandfather’s uncle

Hārūn al-Rashīd: when the caliphate came to him, he was greeted by Sulaymān ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Manṣūr, his uncle; al-ʿAbbās ibn Muḥammad, his father’s uncle; and ʿAbd al-Ṣamad ibn ʿAlī, his grandfather’s uncle. ʿAbd al-Ṣamad was al-ʿAbbās’s uncle; al-ʿAbbās was Sulaymān’s uncle; Sulaymān, was Hārūn’s uncle.

Seven members of his family, all sons of caliphs, greeted Jaʿfar al-Mutawakkil:

Muḥammad, son of al-Wāthiq; Aḥmad, son of al-Muʿtaṣim; Mūsā, son of al-Maʾmūn; ʿAbd Allāh, son of Muḥammad al-Amīn; Abū Aḥmad, son of al-Rashīd; al-ʿAbbās, son of Mūsā al-Hādī; and Manṣūr, son of al-Mahdī.

The Arabs most deeply rooted in treachery

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Muḥammad ibn al-Ashʿath ibn Qays ibn Maʿdīkarib ibn Muʿāwiyah ibn Jabalā: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān was appointed governor of Sijistān by al-Ḥajjāj ibn Yūsuf.

He betrayed him, and died a traitor and a rebel. His father, Muḥammad, had betrayed the people of Ṭabaristān. ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Marjānah had appointed him governor of it. He made peace with them and concluded a treaty with them. Then he betrayed them and raided them. They ambushed him in the mountain passes, killed his son Abū Bakr, and disgraced him. Al-Ashʿath / betrayed the Banū al-Ḥārith ibn Kaʿb. There had been a treaty and peace between them.

He raided them, and they captured him. He ransomed himself for two hundred she-camels, paid one hundred, and did not pay the rest until Islam came. Islam abolished what had been in the Jāhiliyyah. Al-Ashʿath also betrayed and apostatized from Islam. There was a truce between Qays ibn Maʿdīkarib, al-Ashʿath’s father, and Murād, lasting until a fixed term. On the last day of the term, he raided them treacherously. That day was Friday. They said to him, “The term still has today left.” He was a Jew. He said, “It is not lawful for me to fight tomorrow.” He fought them, and they killed him and routed his army. Maʿdīkarib had made a peace treaty with Mahrah, then raided them in breach of the treaty. They killed him, cut open his belly, filled it with pebbles, and kept saying to him: “Eat your fill! May you never be full, son of the whores of Ḍarīyah!”

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān was nicknamed “The Camel’s Fart.” He was sent to fight the Azāriqah. Al-Muhallab said to him, “My nephew! Dig a trench around yourself, for I do not feel safe from a night raid against you.” He did not do so. He said, “They”—meaning the Azāriqah—“are less of a concern to me than the Camel’s Fart.” They raided him by night and utterly destroyed his army. He fled in rout, not turning back for anything. The poet said:

You left our sons with their throats bleeding, and came fleeing, O Camel’s Fart!

He was the man who betrayed Muslim ibn ʿAqīl ibn Abī Ṭālib. When the people deserted Muslim in Kūfah, he took refuge at night with a woman. She was a client of al-Ashʿath. She gave him supper, made his bed, and placed a lamp in his room. Her son came home drunk and saw her tending to that room. / He asked her about it. She told him that Muslim was inside, that he would leave in the morning, and that the people of Kūfah had abandoned him. In the morning, the son went to ʿAbd al-Raḥmān and told him. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān went to his father, who was with Ibn Marjānah, and whispered in his ear. Muḥammad ibn al-Ashʿath got up and informed Ibn Marjānah. He said to him, “Bring him to me.” He went to him and said, “You have nothing to fear from him; I am your protector.” Then he took the sword from Muslim’s hand. When he brought him to Ibn Marjānah, Ibn Marjānah killed him.

The commanders of a thousand from Muḍar. A man was not called a commander of a thousand until he led a thousand.

Al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Quṣayy led the Banū ʿAbd Manāf and their allies from the Aḥābīsh (namely, the Banū al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāt ibn Kinānah, ʿAḍl, al-Dayth of the Banū al-Hūn ibn Khuzaymah, al-Muṣṭaliq, and al-Ḥayāʾ of Khuzāʿah) on the day of Dhāt Nakīf, in the war against the Banū Layth ibn Bakr ibn ʿAbd Manāt ibn Kinānah.

Balʿāʾ ibn Qays al-Kinānī led the Banū ʿAbd Manāt on the day of Dhāt Nakīf, the day of al-Mushallal, and the day of al-Fijār.

Abū Sufyān Ṣakhr ibn Ḥarb led Quraysh and Kinānah on the day of Uḥud and the day of the Trench, in the war against the Messenger of God ﷺ.

ʿĀmir ibn al-Ẓarb al-ʿAdwānī led all of Rabīʿah, Muḍar, and Quḍāʿah on the day of al-Baydāʾ, against Yemen, when it had joined Madhḥij.

Mālik ibn ʿAwf al-Naṣrī led all of Hawāzin on the day of Ḥunayn, in the war against the Messenger of God ﷺ.

ʿAwf ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿĀmir ibn Judhaymah, the brother of the Banū Naṣr ibn Quʿayn, led the Banū Asad ibn Khuzaymah on the day of ʿUkāẓ; Quraysh were not at ʿUkāẓ.

Rabīʿah ibn Ḥidhār al-Asadī led the Banū Asad on the day of the Euphrates, for ʿAdī [a] ibn the sister of al-Ḥārith ibn Abī Shamir al-Ghassānī.

Zarārah ibn ʿAdas ibn Zayd ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Dārim led Tamīm and others on the day of Shuwayḥiṭ, against ʿUdhrah ibn Saʿd Hudhaym.

Laqīṭ ibn Zarārah led all of Tamīm, except the Banū Saʿd ibn Zayd Manāt, against the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿah on the day of Jabalā. He was killed that day.

Al-Aqraʿ ibn Ḥābis ibn ʿIqāl ibn Muḥammad ibn Sufyān ibn Mujāshiʿ led all of Ḥanẓalah on the first day of al-Kulāb.

Al-Nuʿmān ibn Mujāshiʿ led the Banū Dārim and their allies on the day of al-Ṣafrāʾ.

Al-Namir ibn Ḥammān ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Kaʿb ibn Saʿd and al-Aḍbaṭ ibn Qurayʿ ibn ʿAwf ibn Kaʿb ibn Saʿd led all of Saʿd, against Ḥimyar and its allied groups, on the day of Ṣanʿāʾ.

Muḥallim ibn Suwayṭ al-Ḍabbī, the brother of the Banū Ṣabāḥ, led all of al-Ribāb.

He was the first commander: the first to travel through the land of Muḍar as a leader. He raided Iraq and the territory of Kisrā, reaching al-ʿUdhyayb. The camels then grew wary of the water’s gurgling.

One of the Ḍabbīs said:

They halted at the pools of al-ʿUdhyayb, thoughcamels had never halted at the pools of al-ʿUdhyayb.

They fear the water’s gurgling, while it offers themthe sun’s glare; if only what conceals it would pass away.

Qays ibn ʿĀṣim al-Saʿdī, on the day of Labākh and Thītal—which was the second day of al-Kulāb—commanded all of the Banū Saʿd.

Zuhayr ibn Judhaymah al-ʿAbsī led all of Ghaṭafān.

ʿAmr ibn Juʾayyah ibn Lawdhān al-Fazārī led all of Ghaṭafān on the day of al-Ukhnān, against the Banū Bakr ibn Wāʾil.

Badr ibn ʿAmr led Ghaṭafān against the Banū Asad. Khālid ibn al-Abbaḥ al-Asadī killed him.

Ḥudhayfah ibn Badr led the Banū Fazārah and Murrah on the day of al-Nisār and the day of al-Jifār, and in the Dāḥis war, until he was killed in it on the day of al-Habāʾah.

ʿUyaynah ibn Ḥiṣn ibn Ḥudhayfah led Ghaṭafān against the Banū Taghlib on the day of al-Sājsī. He was “the fool whom they obey.” His son Saʿīd ibn ʿUyaynah was the most deeply rooted of people in command. He was the one who raided Kalb on the day of the Daughters of Qayn, accompanied by Ḥalḥalah ibn Ashyam. They killed and exceeded the bounds in killing. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān exacted retaliation from them.

Khālid ibn Jaʿfar ibn Kilāb led Hawāzin after he killed Zuhayr ibn Judhaymah al-ʿAbsī on the day of al-Nafrāwāt.

Al-Aḥwaṣ ibn Jaʿfar led all the Banū ʿĀmir and their allies from Bajīlah and Bāriq on the day of Shuʿb Jabalā. It was one of the greatest of the Arab days.

The commanders of a thousand from Rabīʿah

Rabīʿah ibn Murrah ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Zuhayr al-Taghlibī led Muḍar, Rabīʿah, and Quḍāʿah on the day of al-Sullān, against the people of Yemen.

His son, Kulayb ibn Rabīʿah—Kulayb Wāʾil—led Rabīʿah, Muḍar, and Quḍāʿah on the day of Khazāzī, against Yemen.

Al-Hudhayl ibn Hubayrah al-Thaʿlabī, the poet, son of Qubayṣah ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥabīb ibn Ḥarfah ibn Thaʿlabah ibn Bakr. Al-Ḥawfazan—that is, al-Ḥārith ibn Shurayk ibn ʿAmr ibn Qays al-Shaybānī—was one who ranged far afield. Bisṭām ibn Qays ibn Masʿūd ibn Qays ibn Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh Dhū al-Jaddayn. Al-Ḥārith ibn Waʿlah al-Dhuhlī. Abjar ibn Jābir al-ʿIjlī. Qays ibn Ḥassān ibn ʿAmr ibn Murthid, the brother of the Banū Qays ibn Thaʿlabah. Qatādah ibn Maslamah al-Ḥanafī.

Athāl ibn Ḥujr ibn al-Nuʿmān ibn Maslamah al-Ḥanafī. Al-Hudhayl ibn ʿImrān al-Taghlibī. The Banū Māzin ibn Mālik ibn ʿAmr ibn Tamīm killed him on the day of al-Ṣalīb.

The commanders of a thousand from Quḍāʿah

Among the Sulayḥ ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥāf ibn Quḍāʿah were Diyād ibn Habbūlah and Dāwūd al-Lathq ibn Habālah.

Among Kalb ibn Wabrah ibn Taghlib ibn Ḥulwān ibn ʿImrān ibn al-Ḥāf ibn Quḍāʿah was Zuhayr ibn Janāb ibn Hibal.

Among ʿUdhrah ibn Saʿd Hudhaym ibn Zayd ibn Layth ibn Sūd ibn Aslam ibn al-Ḥāf b. Quḍāʿah: (Rizāḥ) b. Rabīʿah b. Ḥarām b. Ḍannah b. ʿAbd b. Kabīr b. ʿUdhrah. He was the maternal brother of Quṣayy b. Kilāb. All of Quḍāʿah rallied behind Rizāḥ. He was the one who strengthened Quṣayy’s position against Khuzāʿah and Banū Bakr b. ʿAbd Manāt b. Kinānah.

In Kalb as well: (ʿUmayrah) b. Aws b. Thaʿlabah b. ʿAwf b. Kaʿb b. Dhuhl. ʿUmayrah was called “the king.”

Among Bahraʾ: (al-Ashal) b. ʿAmr al-Thaʿīl.

Those Who Led Large Forces from Yemen

(Karz) b. ʿAbd Allāh b. ʿĀmir b. ʿAbd Allāh b. ʿĀmir b. ʿAbd Allāh b. ʿAbd Shams b. Ghamghamah, of Bajīlah.

And among Madhḥij: (ʿAbd Yaghūth) b. Waqqāṣ b. Ṣalāʾah al-Ḥārithī.

The Taym killed him at the Second Battle of al-Kulāb.

And (al-Ashʿath) b. Qays b. Maʿdīkarib [b. Muʿāwiyah] b. Jiblah al-Kindī. Al-Ashʿath, his father Qays, and his grandfather Maʿdīkarib all led forces. And (Kabsh) b. Hāniʾ al-Kindī. And (al-Qashʿam) b. Yazīd al-Kindī.

And (Shurāḥīl) b. Aṣhab al-Juʿfī, whom the Banū Jaʿdah killed.

And (Dahr) b. al-Ḥaddāʾ b. Dhuhl al-Juʿfī. The Banū ʿUqayl killed him.

And (Yazīd) b. Anas b. al-Dayyān al-Ḥārithī. And (Dhū al-Ghuṣṣah), that is, al-Ḥuṣayn b. Shaddād / b. Qinān al-Ḥārithī. And (Mukharrim) b. Ḥazn b. Yazīd al-Ḥārithī. And (al-ʿUbāb), that is, Rabīʿ b. Duhy b. Kaʿb b. Rabīʿah al-Ḥārithī. He raided the Sawād in the days of Kisrā, and his cavalry got bogged down in the Euphrates.

And (Ḥujr) b. Yazīd b. Salamah al-Kindī. And (Qays) b. Salamah al-Kindī. Khālid b. Jaʿfar b. Kilāb captured him on the Day of al-Ḥawmān.

And (al-Zuwayr), that is, Ibn ʿAnjad, whose name was ʿAlqamah b. Salamah b. Mālik al-Kindī.

And (Ḥassān) b. ʿAmr b. al-Jawn al-Kindī. And (Muʿāwiyah) b. Sharāḥīl b. Akhḍar al-Kindī.

And (Ḥudayj) b. Jafnah b. Qutayrah al-Sakūnī.

And (Hubayrah) b. al-Mikshūḥ b. ʿAbd Yaghūth al-Murādī. And (Farwah) b. Misk al-Murādī.

As for Ḥimyar, it had the Tubbaʿs and kingship, and their standing was too great for them to be counted among those who led large forces. A man was not counted among them unless he led a thousand.

Those Who Held Grants from Wāʾil

They were nobles to whom kings assigned estates. As for Muḍar, they were free of royal rule and owed no allegiance to kings, except for some of Tamīm who lived in al-Yamāmah and its surrounding districts. Those who held grants were: (Qays) b. Masʿūd b. Qays b. Khālid b. ʿAbd Allāh Dhū al-Jadayyn b. ʿAmr b. al-Ḥārith b. Hammām b. Murrah b. Dhuhl b. Shaybān. Kisrā granted him al-Ubullah and eighty of its villages.

And (Yazīd) b. Mushhir b. Aṣram b. Thaʿlabah b. Asʿad b. Hammām b. Murrah b. Dhuhl b. Shaybān. And (al-Ḥārith) b. Waʿlah b. al-Mujālid b. Yathribī b. al-Zabbān b. al-Ḥārith b. Mālik b. Dhuhl b. Thaʿlabah b. ʿUkābah.
