Those around whom Hawāzin rallied—and Hawāzin as a whole rallied in the jāhiliyyah only around these four men from Banū Jaʿfar b. Kilāb are: (Khālid) b. Jaʿfar b. Kilāb, after he killed Zuhayr b.

Judhaymah b. Rawāḥah; and (ʿUrwah al-Raḥḥāl) b. ʿUtaybah b. Jaʿfar;

and (al-Aḥwaṣ) b. Jaʿfar; and (ʿĀmir) b. Mālik b. Jaʿfar b. Kilāb.

Those Who Attained Leadership over an Arab Tribe

All of (Muḍar) rallied in al-Baṣrah around (al-Aḥnaf) b. Qays al-Saʿdī on the day Masʿūd b. ʿAmr al-ʿAtakī was killed.

All of (Tamīm) in Khurāsān rallied around (al-Ḥuraysh) b. Hilāl.

(Muḍar, Rabīʿah, and Yemen) in Khurāsān rallied around (Wakīʿ) b. Ḥassān b. Abī Sūd al-Ghadānī on the day Qutaybah b. Muslim b. ʿAmr al-Bāhilī was killed.

In the jāhiliyyah all of (Bakr b. Wāʾil) rallied around (Jahbal) b. Thaʿlabah al-Yashkurī, around (ʿAmr) b. Shaybān b. Dhuhl, around (ʿAmr) b. Qays al-Aṣamm on the Day of al-Zuwayrayn, around (al-Kalḥ) on the day he was killed at Dhāt al-Ramram, around (Bishr) b. ʿAmr b. Masʿūd on the Day of al-Shayyaṭayn, and around (Hammām) b. Murrah in the War of al-Basūs, and around (al-Ḥārith) b. ʿAbbād on the Day of Qiḍḍah.

(Muḍar, Rabīʿah, and Yemen) rallied around (ʿAbd Allāh) b. al-Jārūd at Rustaqābādh, on the day he betrayed and fought al-Ḥajjāj b. Yūsuf. He was killed that day.

(Muḍar, Rabīʿah, and Yemen) in Khurāsān rallied around (Shaybān) b. Salamah al-Sadūsī, together with the Khārijites who followed him. Naṣr b. Sayyār, governor of Khurāsān, was besieged in Marw for three years.

All of (Qays of al-Jazīrah) in Islam rallied around (Zufar) b. al-Ḥārith b. ʿAbd ʿAmr b. Muʿāz—with a zāy—b. Yazīd b. ʿAmr b. Khuwailid.

(Taghlab, al-Nimr, and their affiliated groups) rallied around (Ḥanẓalah) b. Hawbar and (Shuʿayb) b. Mulayk.

(Quḍāʿah) in Syria rallied around (Ḥumayd) b. Ḥurayth b. Baḥdal al-Kalbī.

(Taghlab) rallied on the Day of al-Ḥashshāk, after the killing of Ḥanẓalah b. Hawbar, around (Marrār) b. ʿAlqamah al-Zahrānī.

Tribes Whose Numbers Do Not Increase

Ibn al-Kalbī said: (Banū Nāshim) b. Afṣā b. Duʿmī b. Jadīlah b. Asad b. Rabīʿah b. Nizār: four in number; whenever a child was born among them, one of them died. (Banū Fuhayr) b. Kaʿb b. Judhaymah b. ʿAbd al-Qays: ten men; their number did not increase. (Banū ʿAmr) b. Jashm b. Bakr b. Ḥabīb b. ʿAmr b. Ghanm b. Taghlab: a small number of households. They are called Banū al-Qaṣmāʾ, and are known by this name. (Banū ʿAbd Allāh) b. Afṣā b. Jadīlah: four in number; three of them in al-Jazīrah are known as Banū al-Ward, and one is in Jurjān. (Banū Ḥumays) b. Nāshirāh b. Naṣr b. Sawāʾah b. Saʿd b. Mālik b. Thaʿlabah b. Dūdān b. Asad: twelve men. (Banū Jassās) b. ʿAmr b. Khuwayyah b. Lawdhān, of Banū Fazārah: four or three men. (Kulayb) b. ʿAdī b. Janāb b. Habal: four men. Banū Kulayb are known as Banū al-Raḥḥāl. (Banū Ḥumays) b. Add b. Ṭābikhah: sixty men; their number did not increase. (Banū Ḥabīb) b. Kaʿb b. Rabīʿah b. ʿĀmir b. Ṣaʿṣaʿah: seven or eight men; their number did not increase. (Banū Shaqrah), of Tamīm, three men; their number did not increase. (Banū Muslim) b. Shakl, among Kalb: fifty men.

(Banū … b. Aws) b. Zayd al-Lāt b. Rufaīdah: a small number of households.

(Banū al-Ḥārith) b. Zayd al-Lāt b. Rufaīdah: thirty men.

Genealogical Proximity

ʿAbd al-Ṣamad b. ʿAlī b. ʿAbd Allāh b. al-ʿAbbās b. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib performed the pilgrimage in the year 156. Yazīd b. Muʿāwiyah b. Abī Sufyān performed the pilgrimage in the year 50 AH. They were equal in the number of generations separating them from ʿAbd Manāf.

The number of generations between ʿAbd al-Ṣamad and Khālid b. ʿAbd Allāh Dhū al-Jadayyn b. ʿAmr b. al-Ḥārith b. Hammām b. Murrah b. Dhuhl b. Shaybān b. Thaʿlabah b. ʿUkābah, up to Nizār, was equal.

The number of generations between ʿAbd al-Ṣamad b. ʿAlī and Saʿīd b. Zayd b. ʿAmr b. Nufayl, up to Kaʿb b. Luʾayy, was equal.

ʿAbd Allāh b. al-Ḥārith b. Nawfal b. al-Ḥārith b. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib was born in the year eight after the Hijrah, during the time of the Messenger of God ﷺ. ʿAbd al-Ṣamad b. ʿAlī was born in the year 109. They were equal in genealogical proximity.

ʿUmar b. al-Khaṭṭāb ﵀, ʿAlī b. ʿAbd Allāh b. al-ʿAbbās ﵁—and the poet Ṭarafah b. al-ʿAbd were equal in genealogical proximity to Nizār b. Maʿadd.

ʿAlī b. ʿAbd Allāh b. al-ʿAbbās was the counterpart of ʿAttāb b. Asīd b. Abī al-ʿĀṣ b. Umayyah as far back as ʿAbd Manāf. ʿAbd al-Ṣamad b. ʿAlī died in the year 185, and lived until, in his time, there was a man seven generations removed from ʿAlī b. ʿAbd Allāh: Jaʿfar b. al-Faḍl b. al-ʿAbbās b. Mūsā b. ʿĪsā b. Mūsā b. Muḥammad b. ʿAlī.

The number of generations between Fāṭimah, daughter of the Messenger of God ﷺ and be pleased with her—and Ismāʿīl b. Muḥammad b. Saʿd b. Abī Waqqāṣ, up to Kilāb b. Murrah, was equal. Sufyān b. ʿUyaynah transmitted from Ismāʿīl b. Muḥammad.

Names of the Chamberlains Who Attended the Messenger of God and the Caliphs after Him

The usher of the Messenger of God ﷺ—was (Anasah), his client.

The chamberlain of Abū Bakr ﵀—was (Shudayd), his client. The chamberlain of ʿUmar ﵀—was (Yarfāʾ), his client. The chamberlain of ʿUthmān ﵀—was (Ḥumrān), his client.

The chamberlain of ʿAlī b. Abī Ṭālib ﵁—was (Qanbar), his client.

The chamberlain of Muʿāwiyah was (Abū Ayyūb), his client. The chamberlain of Yazīd b. Muʿāwiyah was (Ṣafwān), his client. The chamberlain of Marwān b. al-Ḥakam was (Abū al-Minhāl al-Aswad), his client. The chamberlain of ʿAbd al-Malik b. Marwān was (Abū Yūsuf), his client. The chamberlain of al-Walīd b. ʿAbd al-Malik was (Khālid), his client. The chamberlain of Sulaymān b. ʿAbd al-Malik was (Abū ʿUbayd), his client. The chamberlain of ʿUmar b. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz was (Ḥubaysh), his client. The chamberlain of Yazīd b. ʿAbd al-Malik was (Saʿīd), his client.

The chamberlain of Hishām was (Ghālib), his client. The chamberlain of al-Walīd b. Yazīd was (Qaṭarī), his client. The chamberlain of Yazīd b. al-Walīd was (Qaṭan), his client. The chamberlain of Ibrāhīm b. al-Walīd was (Qaṭan), the chamberlain of Yazīd b. al-Walīd. The chamberlain of Marwān b. Muḥammad was (Saqlāb), his client.

The chamberlain of Abū al-ʿAbbās ʿAbd Allāh b. Muḥammad b. ʿAlī was (Abū Ghassān) Ṣāliḥ b. al-Haytham, his client. The chamberlain of al-Manṣūr was (ʿĪsā) b. Rawḍah.

Rawḍah was his mother, and his father was Najīḥ, his client. Abū al-Khaṣīb, his client, was with him. When Ibn Rawḍah died, Abū al-Khaṣīb took his place. Al-Rabīʿ, a client of the family of Abū Farwah, used to serve with him. When Abū al-Khaṣīb died, al-Rabīʿ took his place as chamberlain. This same al-Rabīʿ was the chamberlain of al-Mahdī and the chamberlain of al-Hādī. The chamberlain of al-Rashīd was (Bishr) b. Maymūn, his client. (Muḥammad) b. Khālid b. Barmak served with him. Then came (al-Faḍl) b. al-Rabīʿ. / The chamberlain of al-Amīn was (al-ʿAbbās) b. al-Faḍl b. al-Rabīʿ. The chamberlain of al-Maʾmūn was (ʿAbd al-Ḥamīd) b. Shabīb b. Ḥumayd b. Qaḥṭabah al-Ṭāʾī. Then (Muḥammad and ʿAlī), sons of Ṣāliḥ, served as his clients. Then came (Ismāʿīl) b. Muḥammad b. Ṣāliḥ, with (Muḥammad) b. Ḥammād b. Danqash. (al-Ḥasan) b. Abī Saʿīd was in charge of the general chamberlaincy.

The chamberlain of al-Muʿtaṣim was (Waṣīf) the Turk, his client, with (Muḥammad) b. Ḥammād b. Danqash. This same Waṣīf was the chamberlain of al-Wāthiq, with (Ibn Ḥammād) serving alongside him until Ibn Ḥammād died. Then (Muḥammad) b. ʿĀṣim served with Waṣīf. This same Waṣīf was the chamberlain of al-Mutawakkil, with (Muḥammad) b. ʿĀṣim. When Muḥammad b. ʿĀṣim died, al-Mutawakkil appointed (Yaʿqūb) Qawṣarah b. Ibrāhīm in Ibn ʿĀṣim’s place. When Yaʿqūb died, he appointed (al-Marzubān) b. al-Fayrazān in his place.

When al-Marzubān died, he appointed (Ibrāhīm) b. al-Ḥasan b. Sahl in his place. Then Ibrāhīm b. al-Ḥasan b. Sahl died, and (al-Ḥusayn) b. Ismāʿīl b. Muṣʿab was appointed chamberlain, with (ʿAttāb) b. ʿAttāb serving alongside him.

Those among the notable men whose eye was lost in war

(Abū Sufyān) Ṣakhr ibn Ḥarb: he lost his eye on the day of al-Ṭāʾif, while with the Messenger of God ﷺ.

(Hāshim) ibn ʿUtba ibn Abī Waqqāṣ: he lost his eye on the day of al-Yarmūk.

(Al-Ashʿath) ibn Qays: on the day of al-Yarmūk.

(Mālik) ibn al-Ḥārith al-Ashtar: on the day of al-Yarmūk.

(Al-Mughīra) ibn Shuʿba: on the day of al-Qādisiyya.

(Qubayṣa) ibn Dhuʾayb al-Khuzāʿī: on the day of al-Ḥarra.

(ʿAdī) ibn Ḥātim al-Ṭāʾī: on the day of the Camel, with ʿAlī ﵁.

(Ṭalḥat al-Ṭalaḥāt) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khalaf: at Samarqand.

(Al-Muhallab) ibn Abī Ṣufra: at Samarqand.

(Qaṭan) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥuṣayn: in Ādharbayjān.

(Qays) ibn Makshūḥ: on the day of al-Yarmūk.

(ʿAmr) ibn Maʿdīkarib: on the day of al-Yarmūk.

(Jarīr) ibn ʿAbd Allāh: at Hamadhān.

(ʿUtba) ibn Abī Sufyān: on the day of the Camel, with ʿĀʾisha.

(Mālik) ibn Musammaʿ: on the day of al-Jafr.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Yazīd ibn Kurz al-Qasrī: on the day of Marj Rāhiṭ, with Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam.

(Saʿīd) ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān: at Samarqand.

The first man born to two Hāshimites

He was the son of Abū Ṭālib ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. His mother was Fāṭima bt. Asad ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf.

The first man born successively to three Hāshimite women

ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Nawfal ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib: his mother was Khālida bt. Muʿattib ibn Abī Lahab. Her mother was ʿĀtika bt. Abī Sufyān ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. Her mother was Umm ʿAmr bt. al-Muqawwim ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib.

Two men whose paternal and maternal uncles were caliphs; no others like them are known in Islam

One was (ʿUthmān) ibn ʿAnbasa ibn Abī Sufyān ibn Ḥarb; the other was (Yaḥyā) ibn ʿUrwa ibn al-Zubayr. As for ʿUthmān, his mother was Zaynab bt. al-Zubayr, so his maternal uncle was ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr, and his paternal uncle was Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān. Yaḥyā ibn ʿUrwa ibn al-Zubayr’s mother was a daughter of al-Ḥakam ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ; thus his paternal uncle was ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr, and his maternal uncle was Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam.

Those Arabs who led the pilgrimage season; they were eight men

(Al-Mughīra) ibn Shuʿba al-Thaqafī, in the year 40.

(Al-Ḥajjāj) ibn Yūsuf al-Thaqafī, in the years 72, 73, and 74.

(Abū Bakr) ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥazm al-Anṣārī, in the year 96 and the year 100.

(ʿAbd al-Wāḥid) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Kaʿb ibn Qunayʿ al-Naṣrī, in the year 104.

(Yūsuf) ibn Muḥammad ibn Yūsuf ibn al-Ḥakam al-Thaqafī, in the year 125.

(Al-Walīd) ibn ʿUrwa ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAṭiyya, of Banū Saʿd ibn Bakr, in the year 131.

(Ziyād) ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Madān al-Ḥārithī, in the year 133.

(Yazīd) ibn Manṣūr al-Ḥimyarī, in the year 159.

The famous markets of the Arabs in the pre-Islamic period, and their transactions there

Among them was the market of (Dūmat al-Jandal), between Syria and the Ḥijāz.

It opened on the first day of the month of Rabīʿ I and continued until the middle of the month. Then it was extended and remained open until the beginning of the next month. After that, they dispersed and returned at the same time the following year.

Kalb and Jadīla of Ṭayyiʾ were its neighbors. Its rule was shared between Ukaydir al-ʿAbbādī, then al-Sakūnī, and Qunāfa al-Kalbī. When the ʿAbbādīs prevailed, Ukaydir took charge of it; when the Ghassānids prevailed, they put Qunāfa in charge. Their victory came about through the two kings contending: whichever king defeated the other by bringing forth what was laid against him left him and the market, and did there as he wished. No one could sell anything there without his permission, until the king had sold whatever he wished to sell, in addition to whatever he received from the market’s tithes.

Kalb had many qinn there in felt tents. They compelled their young women to engage in prostitution. They were the most numerous of the Arabs. The Arabs’ method of concluding sales there was to cast stones. This was because a group of men might gather around an item and bargain with its owner. Whichever of them was satisfied cast his stone. Sometimes a group would agree on the item, leaving them no choice but to share it, even though they disliked doing so. Sometimes they agreed and cast their stones together, when they had settled the matter among themselves; if they were in agreement, they would reduce the price for the seller of the item. Every merchant setting out from Yemen or the Ḥijāz would seek Quraysh’s protection for as long as he remained in the lands of Muḍar, because Muḍar did not interfere with Muḍar’s merchants, and no ally of a Muḍarī would provoke them. This was the arrangement among them. Thus Kalb did not molest them because of its alliance with Banū Tamīm, and Ṭayyiʾ likewise did not molest them because of its alliance with Banū Asad. Muḍar used to say:

Quraysh has lifted from us the reproach of the debtthat Ismāʿīl bequeathed to us.

When they took the road to Iraq, they sought the protection of Banū ʿAmr ibn Mirthad, of Banū Qays ibn Thaʿlaba, and all of Rabīʿa would grant them safe passage.

Then they moved on from there to (al-Mushaqqar) at Hajar, where its market opened on the first day of Jumādā II and continued until the end of the month. Persians would arrive there, crossing the sea with their merchandise. Then the market dispersed, and they returned at the same time the following year. ʿAbd al-Qays and Tamīm were its neighbors. Its rulers were from Banū Tamīm, from Banū ʿAbd Allāh ibn Zayd, the clan of al-Mundhir ibn Sāwā. The Persian kings appointed them over it: Banū Naṣr over al-Ḥīra and Banū al-Mustakbir over ʿUmān. They conducted themselves there and governed it as kings did at Dūmat al-Jandal. They collected tithes from them.

The merchants who went there sought Quraysh’s protection, because it was reached only by way of the lands of Muḍar. Their method of sale there was mulāmasa and hamhama. Mulāmasa was by gesture: one would gesture to another, and they would make their sale without speaking, until they agreed by gestures.

As for hamhama, it was so that neither party would swear falsely if the buyer claimed that he had changed his mind.

Then came the market of (Ṣuḥār) in ʿUmān. It opened on the first day of Rajab and continued for five nights. There, al-Julundī ibn al-Mustakbir collected tithes from them.

Then came the market of (Dabā), one of the Arabs’ two ports. Merchants from Sind, India, China, and the East and West came to it. Its market opened at the end of Rajab. Their method of sale there was bargaining. Al-Julundī ibn al-Mustakbir collected tithes from them there and at the market of Ṣuḥār, and dealt with them as kings did elsewhere.

Then came the market of (al-Shiḥr), in the land of Mahra. The market was held beneath the mountain on which the tomb of Hūd ﵇ stands. No tithes were collected there, because it was not royal territory. The merchants there sought the protection of Banū Muḥārib ibn Harb, of Mahra. It opened in the middle of Shaʿbān. Their method of sale there was the casting of stones.

Then came the market of (ʿAdan). It opened on the first day of Ramaḍān and continued for ten days. They sought no one’s protection there, because it was royal territory, subject to established authority. The Abnāʾ collected tithes from them there, but neither bought nor sold in their markets. The Abnāʾ were the sons of the Persians who conquered Yemen with Wahriz and killed the Abyssinians.

Then came the market of (Ṣanʿāʾ). It opened in the middle of Ramaḍān and continued until its end. The Abnāʾ collected tithes from them. Their method of sale there was al-jass, the touching of hands.

Then came the market of (al-Rābiya) and the market of (ʿUkāẓ). Al-Rābiya was in Ḥaḍramawt and ʿUkāẓ was in upper Najd, near ʿArafāt. No one could reach al-Rābiya without a pledge of protection, because it was not royal territory. Whoever prevailed there overcame his opponent. Quraysh sought the protection of Banū Ākil al-Murār there, while the rest of the people sought the protection of the family of Masrūq ibn Wāʾil, of Kinda. It was a place of honor for both families. Banū Ākil al-Murār held a position of precedence because of Quraysh’s superiority over the rest of the people.

Some people would go to it, and others to ʿUkāẓ. Both markets opened on the same day: the middle of Dhū al-Qaʿda, and continued until the end of the month. ʿUkāẓ was one of the greatest markets of the Arabs. Quraysh, Hawāzin, and groups from the various Arab tribes would camp there: Ghatafān, Aslam, and the Aḥābīsh (who were Banū al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Manāt ibn Kināna, ʿAḍal, al-Dayth, al-Ḥayā, and al-Muṣṭaliq). It opened in the middle of Dhū al-Qaʿda and continued until the end of the month.

No tithes were collected there, and no pledge of protection was needed. Their method of sale was sirār: when a sale was concluded and the merchant had an acquaintance there who would bid up the price but did not intend to buy, he would make him a partner in the profit.

Then came the market of (Dhū al-Majāz), near ʿUkāẓ. It opened on the first day of Dhū al-Ḥijja and continued until the Day of Tarwiya. Then they went to Minā.

Then (Naṭāt) at Khaybar and the market of (Ḥajar) in al-Yamāma opened on the Day of ʿĀshūrāʾ and continued until the end of Muḥarram.

The names of the twelve deputies of the Messenger of God ﷺ; they were twelve men

Three of them were from al-Aws ibn Ḥāritha: from Banū ʿAbd al-Ashhal, (Usayd) ibn Ḥuḍayr ibn Sammāk ibn ʿAtīk [ibn Rāfiʿ] ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Zayd ibn ʿAbd al-Ashhal ibn Jusham ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj ibn ʿAmr ibn Mālik ibn al-Aws ibn Ḥāritha; and (Abū al-Haytham) Mālik ibn Mālik ibn al-Tayyihān ibn Mālik ibn ʿAtīk [ibn Rāfiʿ] ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Zayd ibn ʿAbd al-Ashhal; and from Banū Salima, (Saʿd) ibn Khaythama ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Mālik ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Naḥāṭ ibn Kaʿb ibn Ḥāritha [ibn Ghanm] ibn Salima ibn Imruʾ al-Qays [ibn Thaʿlaba ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf] ibn Mālik ibn al-Aws ibn Ḥāritha.

Nine of them were from al-Khazraj: (Abū Umāma) Asʿad ibn Zurāra ibn ʿUdas ibn ʿUbayd ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Ghanm ibn Mālik ibn al-Najjār—namely, Taym Allāh ibn Thaʿlaba ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Khazraj ibn Ḥāritha; (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Rawāḥa [ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Imruʾ al-Qays] ibn ʿAmr ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Mālik al-Agharr ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Khazraj ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj ibn Ḥāritha; (Saʿd) ibn al-Rabīʿ ibn ʿAmr ibn Abī Zuhayr [ibn Mālik] ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Mālik al-Agharr ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Khazraj ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj ibn Ḥāritha; (Khārija) ibn Zayd ibn Abī Zuhayr ibn Mālik ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Mālik al-Agharr; (Saʿd) ibn ʿUbāda ibn Dulaym ibn Ḥāritha ibn Abī Ḥuzayma ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Ṭarīf ibn al-Khazraj ibn Sāʿida ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Khazraj [ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj] ibn Ḥāritha; and (al-Mundhir) ibn ʿAmr ibn Khunays ibn [Ḥāritha] ibn Lawdhān ibn ʿAbd Wudd ibn Zayd ibn Thaʿlaba ibn al-Khazraj ibn Sāʿida; (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn [ʿAmr ibn] Ḥarām [ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Ḥarām] ibn Kaʿb ibn Ghanm ibn [Kaʿb ibn] Salima ibn Saʿd ibn ʿAlī ibn Asad ibn Sārida ibn Yazīd ibn Jusham ibn al-Khazraj ibn Ḥāritha; (al-Barāʾ) ibn Maʿrūr ibn Ṣakhr ibn Khansāʾ ibn Sinān ibn ʿUbayd ibn ʿAdī ibn Ghanm ibn Kaʿb ibn Salima; (Rāfiʿ) ibn Mālik ibn al-ʿAjlān ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿĀmir ibn Zurayq [ibn ʿĀmir ibn Ruzaq] ibn ʿAbd Ḥāritha ibn Mālik ibn Ghaḍab ibn Jusham ibn al-Khazraj ibn Ḥāritha; and (ʿUbāda) ibn al-Ṣāmit ibn Qays ibn Uṣrum ibn Sālim ibn Mālik ibn Sālim ibn ʿAwf ibn al-Khazraj ibn Ḥāritha.

Al-Kalbī did not count Rāfiʿ ibn Mālik; in his place he counted Khārija ibn Zayd. Al-Ḥizāmī did not count Khārija, but did count Rāfiʿ. Among what supports al-Ḥizāmī’s account is the statement of Kaʿb ibn Mālik. When the Messenger of God ﷺ—came to Medina, Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb and Ubayy ibn Khalaf al-Jumaḥī wrote to the Anṣār:

Now then: no Arab tribe was more hateful to us as a people with whom a blood-feud might arise than you. You have taken one of our men, the noblest among us in station and the most deeply rooted among his people in lineage; you have given him refuge and protected him. This is a disgrace and a blemish upon you. Release him to us. If he proves good, we are the most entitled to him; but if he proves otherwise, we are the most entitled to take charge of him.

Kaʿb ibn Mālik replied to them with these verses on the day of Uḥud, naming the chiefs of the clans:

As for al-Barāʾ ibn Maʿrūr, he died in Medina before the Hijra. He was the first to face the qibla, and he died facing it. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr was killed at Uḥud. Asʿad ibn Zurāra was killed at Badr. Rāfiʿ was killed at Badr. Saʿd ibn ʿUbāda died at Ḥawrān during the caliphate of Abū Bakr ﵀. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Rawāḥa was killed at Muʾta. Al-Mundhir was killed at Biʾr Maʿūna. Saʿd ibn al-Rabīʿ was killed at Badr. ʿUbāda ibn al-Ṣāmit died in the time of Muʿāwiya. Khārija ibn Zayd was killed at Uḥud. Saʿd ibn Khaythama was killed at Badr. It is said that Abū al-Haytham ibn al-Tayyihān was killed with ʿAlī ﵁—on the day of Ṣiffīn. Usayd ibn Ḥuḍayr died during the caliphate of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵀—and Abū al-Haytham ibn al-Tayyihān died during the caliphate of ʿUmar ﵁. Kaʿb ibn Mālik said:

Tell Ubayy that his judgment was foolish,and that the morning of al-Shiʿb has come; doom is at hand.

God has denied what your soul promised you;He watches over the affairs of men, seeing and hearing.

Tell Abū Sufyān that a light has shone for usthrough Aḥmad, a radiance of God’s guidance.

So do not rejoice in a gathering for the cause you seek;rally and assemble all whom you can gather.

Know this: our breaking of the pledgesis forbidden to you by the company who gave their oath.

Al-Barāʾ and Ibn ʿAmr both forbade it,and Asʿad forbids it to you, as does Rāfiʿ.

Saʿd, the Sāʿidī, and al-Mundhir forbade it;if you attempt it, they will cut off your nose.

If you seek the pledge of Ibn al-Rabīʿ,he will not surrender it. Do not let hopes deceive you.

He will keep it, and al-Qawqalī, son of Ṣāmit,is beyond your reach, a vigorous youth.

Nor will Ibn Rawāḥa give it to you;the poison beneath him is deadly to any who break his protection.

Abū al-Haytham, too, is faithful to such a pledge,submissive in keeping the right he has granted.

And Ibn Ḥuḍayr is no prize for you, should you seek him;will you not desist from the folly of error?

Saʿd, the brother of ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf, isa sturdy defender of every matter you attempt.

Those are stars for you; if one of them is hidden,another rises over you, ill-omened, in the darkness of night.

A man who married four women, all named ʿĀtika

Abū Umayya ibn al-Mughīra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. He was married to (ʿĀtika bt. ʿĀmir), the one who turns back the camel-borne women, daughter of Rabīʿa [ibn Mālik] ibn Judhayma ibn ʿAlqama ibn Firās al-Kinānī. She bore Umm Salama, the wife of the Messenger of God ﷺ. He was also married to (ʿĀtika bt. ʿUtba) ibn Rabīʿa ibn ʿAbd Shams. She bore him Qurayba the Younger and others. (ʿĀtika bt. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib) bore ʿAbd Allāh, Zuhayr, and Qurayba the Elder. And (ʿĀtika), the paternal aunt or sister of Abū Ihāb al-Tamīmī, who was their ally, bore him al-Muhājir or Rabīʿa ibn Abī Umayya.

The first of the sons of the Emigrants to be named Muḥammad

The first was (Muḥammad) ibn Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib, then (Muḥammad) ibn Abī Ḥudhayfa ibn ʿUtba ibn Rabīʿa ibn ʿAbd Shams, then (Muḥammad) ibn Ṭalḥa ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUthmān al-Taymī, then (Muḥammad) ibn Abī Bakr al-Ṣiddīq.

Those among the Anṣār named Muḥammad

The first was (Muḥammad) ibn al-Jadd ibn Qays ibn Ṣakhr ibn Khansāʾ ibn Sinān ibn ʿUbayd ibn ʿAdī ibn Ghanm ibn Kaʿb ibn Salima ibn Saʿd ibn ʿAlī ibn Asad ibn Sārida ibn Yazīd ibn Jusham ibn al-Khazraj; then (Muḥammad) ibn Thābit ibn Qays ibn Shammās ibn Mālik ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Mālik ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Khazraj ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj ibn Ḥāritha; then (Muḥammad) ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥazm ibn Zayd ibn Lawdhān ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd ʿAwf ibn Ghanm ibn Mālik ibn al-Najjār; then (Muḥammad) ibn Anas ibn Faḍāla ibn ʿAdī ibn Ḥarām ibn al-Haytham ibn Ẓafar, who was Kaʿb ibn al-Khazraj ibn ʿAmr ibn Mālik ibn al-Aws. He was born in the year of the Farewell Pilgrimage.

The first child born after the Hijra among the sons of the Qurashī Emigrants

ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām ibn Khuwailid. He was born at the end of twenty months from the Hijra.

And among the Anṣār

Al-Nuʿmān ibn Bashīr ibn Saʿd ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Khilās ibn Zayd ibn Mālik ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Khazraj ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj. He was born in Rabīʿ al-Ākhir, at the end of fourteen months.

Names of the Saʿds who fought at Badr, among the Emigrants

(Saʿd) ibn Abī Waqqāṣ, whose name was Mālik ibn Uhayb ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhra. And (Saʿd), the client of Ḥāṭib ibn Abī Baltaʿa. It is said that he was Saʿd ibn Qays, from Kalb. And (Saʿd) ibn Khawla, the client of Abū Ruhm ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, from Banū Mālik ibn Ḥisl ibn Luʾayy. It is said that he was from Yemen.

And among the Anṣār

(Saʿd) ibn Muʿādh ibn al-Nuʿmān ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Zayd ibn ʿAbd al-Ashhal ibn Jusham ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj ibn ʿAmr ibn Mālik, from al-Aws.

(Saʿd) ibn Zayd ibn Mālik ibn ʿAbd ibn Kaʿb ibn ʿAbd al-Ashhal.

(Saʿd) ibn ʿUbayd ibn al-Nuʿmān ibn Qays ibn ʿAmr ibn Zayd ibn Umayya ibn Zayd ibn Mālik ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf ibn Mālik, from al-Aws.

(Saʿd) ibn al-Rabīʿ ibn ʿAmr ibn Abī Zuhayr ibn Mālik ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Mālik ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Khazraj ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj.

(Saʿd) ibn Khaythama ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Mālik ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Naḥḥāṭ ibn Kaʿb ibn Ḥāritha ibn Ghanm ibn al-Salam, from al-Aws.

(Saʿd) ibn ʿUthmān ibn Khalda ibn Mukhallad ibn ʿĀmir ibn Zurayq ibn ʿĀmir ibn Zurayq ibn ʿAbd Ḥāritha ibn Mālik ibn Ghaḍb ibn Jusham ibn al-Khazraj.

(Saʿd) ibn Yazīd ibn al-Fākih ibn Zayd ibn Khalda ibn ʿĀmir ibn Zurayq, or Asʿad.

(Saʿd) ibn ʿUbāda ibn Dulaym ibn Ḥāritha ibn Abī Khuzayma ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Ṭarīf ibn al-Khazraj ibn Sāʿida.

Those who witnessed Badr, among the first Qurashī Emigrants whose name was ʿAbd Allāh

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn ʿUthmān, Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq ﵀.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Jaḥsh ibn Riʾāb ibn Yaʿmar ibn Ṣabra ibn Murr ibn Kabīr ibn Ghanm ibn Dūdān ibn Asad ibn Khuzayma, an ally of Banū ʿAbd Shams.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Masʿūd ibn Ghāfil ibn Ḥabīb ibn Shamkh ibn Fār ibn Makhzūm ibn Ṣāhila ibn Kāhil ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Tamīm ibn Saʿd ibn Hudhayl, an ally of Banū Zuhra.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Surāqa ibn al-Muʿtamir ibn Anas ibn Adāʾ ibn Riyāḥ ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qurṭ ibn Razāḥ ibn ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Maẓʿūn ibn Ḥabīb ibn Wahb ibn Ḥudhāfa ibn Jumah.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Makh Rama ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Abī Qays ibn ʿAbd Wudd ibn Naṣr ibn Mālik ibn Ḥisl ibn ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Suhayl ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Shams ibn ʿAbd Wudd ibn Naṣr ibn Mālik ibn Ḥisl.

And among the Anṣār

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Suhayl ibn Zayd ibn ʿĀmir ibn ʿAmr ibn Jusham ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj ibn ʿAmr ibn Mālik ibn al-Aws.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Ṭāriq ibn ʿAmr, from Baliyy, an ally of Banū Ẓafar of al-Aws.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn— Jubayr ibn al-Nuʿmān [ibn Umayya] ibn al-Barrak, who was Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Thaʿlaba ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf ibn Mālik ibn al-Aws.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Salama ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Mālik ibn ʿAdī ibn al-ʿAjlān, from Baliyy, an ally of Banū ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf of al-Aws.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Rawāḥa ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn ʿAmr ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Mālik ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Khazraj ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn ʿAnbas, an ally of Banū al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn ʿUmayr ibn Ḥāritha ibn Thaʿlaba ibn al-Jallās ibn Umayya ibn Jadāra ibn ʿAwf ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn ʿUrfuṭa, an ally of Banū Jadāra.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Zayd ibn ʿAbd Rabbih ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Zayd ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj. He was the one to whom the call to prayer was shown in his sleep.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn al-Rabīʿ ibn Qays ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAbbād ibn al-Abjar ibn ʿAwf ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Khazama ibn Aṣram ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAmāra, from Baliyy, an ally of Banū Qawqal of al-Khazraj.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy ibn Mālik ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿUbayd ibn Mālik ibn Sālim ibn Ghanm ibn ʿAwf ibn al-Khazraj, known as Ibn Salūl.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥaram ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Ḥarām ibn Kaʿb ibn Ghanm ibn Kaʿb ibn Salima ibn Saʿd ibn ʿAlī ibn Asad ibn Sārida ibn Yazīd ibn Jusham ibn al-Khazraj.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn al-Jadd ibn Qays ibn Ṣakhr ibn Khansāʾ ibn Sinān ibn ʿUbayd ibn ʿAdī ibn Ghanm ibn Kaʿb ibn Salima.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn al-Ḥumayr, whose name was al-Ḥārith ibn Shana ibn Marīṭ ibn Murra ibn Naṣr ibn Dahmān ibn Subayʿ ibn Bakr ibn Ashjaʿ, an ally of Banū ʿUbayd ibn Salima.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn al-Nuʿmān ibn Baldama ibn Khunās ibn Sinān ibn ʿUbayd ibn ʿAdī ibn Ghanm ibn Kaʿb.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Kaʿb ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf ibn Mabdhūl ibn ʿAmr [ibn Ghanm] ibn Māzin ibn al-Najjār. He was the one who took the spoils at Badr.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn al-Nuʿmān ibn Sinān ibn ʿUbayd ibn ʿAdī ibn Ghanm ibn Kaʿb ibn Salima.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Qays ibn Khalda ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Sawād ibn Mālik ibn Ghanm ibn Mālik ibn al-Najjār, namely Taym Allāh ibn Thaʿlaba ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Khazraj.

Naming the Lamenters

They had asked the Messenger of God ﷺ—to provide mounts for them on the expedition to Tabūk, but he could find none for them:

(ʿUlbah) ibn Ṣayfī ibn ʿAmr ibn Zayd ibn Jusham ibn Ḥārithah ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj ibn ʿAmr ibn Mālik ibn al-Aws.

(Sālim) ibn ʿUmayr ibn Thābit ibn Kulafah ibn Thaʿlabah ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf ibn Mālik ibn al-Aws.

(Hurmā) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Rifāʿah ibn Najdah ibn Majdaʿah ibn ʿAdī ibn Numayr ibn Wāqif ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Mālik ibn al-Aws.

(ʿAbd al-Raḥmān) ibn Kaʿb ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf ibn Mabdhūl ibn ʿAmr ibn Ghanm ibn Māzin ibn al-Najjār; he was Abū Laylā.

(Salamah) ibn Ṣakhr ibn Salmān ibn Ḥārithah ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Zayd Manāt, from the descendants of Ḥabīb ibn ʿAbd Ḥārithah ibn Mālik ibn Ghaḍb ibn Jusham ibn al-Khazraj. They are reckoned among Banū Bayāḍah.

(ʿAmr) ibn ʿAnmah ibn ʿAdī ibn Sinān ibn Nābiʾ ibn ʿAmr ibn Suwād ibn Ghanm ibn Kaʿb ibn Salamah ibn Saʿd ibn ʿAlī ibn Asad ibn Sārdah ibn Tazīd ibn Jusham ibn al-Khazraj.

(ʿIrbaḍ) ibn Sāriyah, from Banū Sulaym ibn Manṣūr.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Mughaffal, from Muzaynah ibn Add. This Muzanī is also said to be called ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr.

Naming Those Who Killed the Jewish Kaʿb ibn al-Ashraf

(ʿAbbād) ibn Bishr ibn Waqsh ibn Zughabah ibn Zaʿūrāʾ ibn ʿAbd al-Ashhal ibn al-Aws.

(Abū Nāʾilah) Silkān ibn Salāmah ibn Waqsh.

(al-Ḥārith) ibn Aws ibn Muʿādh ibn al-Nuʿmān ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Zayd ibn ʿAbd al-Ashhal.

(Muḥammad) ibn Maslamah ibn Salamah ibn Khālid ibn ʿAdī ibn Majdaʿah ibn Ḥārithah ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj ibn ʿAmr ibn Mālik ibn al-Aws.

(Abū ʿAbs) ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Jabr ibn ʿAmr ibn Zayd ibn Jusham ibn Ḥārithah ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj, from al-Aws.

Those Who Killed Ibn Abī al-Ḥuqayq

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn ʿAtīk ibn Qays ibn al-Aswad ibn Murī ibn Kaʿb ibn Ghanm ibn Salamah ibn Saʿd ibn ʿAlī ibn Asad ibn Sārdah ibn Tazīd ibn Jusham ibn al-Khazraj.

(Masʿūd) ibn Sinān ibn Qays ibn al-Aswad ibn Murī ibn Kaʿb.

(Abū Qatādah) ibn Rabʿī ibn Baldamah ibn Khunās ibn Sinān ibn ʿUbayd ibn ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb ibn Ghanm ibn Kaʿb ibn Salamah.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Unays ibn Asʿad ibn Ḥarām ibn Ḥabīb, from the descendants of al-Bark ibn Wabrah, of Quḍāʿah, an ally of Banū Suwād, of Banū Salamah, of al-Khazraj.

(Khuzāʿī) ibn al-Aswad, their ally, from

The One Who Killed al-ʿAṣmāʾ bt. Marwān

She used to satirize the Muslims and cause them harm. She was the grandmother of Abū Mūsā al-Anṣārī, the man of the al-Ṣadafāt.

(ʿUmayr) ibn ʿAdī ibn Kharashah ibn Umayyah ibn ʿĀmir ibn Khaṭmah—which is ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jusham ibn Mālik ibn al-Aws. He is the one called “ʿUmayr the Reciter.”

He was blind.

Those Who Turned Back on the Day the Two Armies Met, Whom God Pardoned, Among the Quraysh Emigrants

(ʿUthmān) ibn ʿAffān ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah.

(Abū Ḥudhayfah) ibn ʿUtbah ibn Rabīʿah.

And Among the Anṣār

(Saʿd) ibn ʿUthmān ibn Khaldah ibn Mukhallad ibn ʿĀmir ibn Zurayq ibn ʿAbd Ḥārithah ibn Mālik ibn Ghaḍb ibn Jusham ibn al-Khazraj. His brother was (ʿUqbah) ibn ʿUthmān.

They all went to al-Jalʿab, and none of them entered Medina by its front approach15. They say they reached al-Khubayb. The place is one and the same, with a narrow valley between the two called al-Khubayb. It lies on the route of Dhū al-Qaṣṣah and the route of al-Ṭaraf, twenty-two miles from Medina.

Those Who Stayed Behind from Tabūk and Whom God Pardoned; They Were Deferred Until God’s Decree

(Murārah) ibn al-Rabīʿ ibn Zayd ibn ʿUbayd ibn Zayd ibn Mālik ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf ibn Mālik ibn al-Aws.

(Hilāl) ibn Umayyah ibn ʿĀmir ibn Qays ibn ʿAbd al-Aʿlam ibn ʿĀmir ibn Kaʿb ibn Wāqif, who was Sālim ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Mālik ibn al-Aws.

(Kaʿb) ibn Mālik ibn Abī Kaʿb ibn al-Qayn ibn Suwād ibn Ghanm ibn Salamah, of al-Khazraj.

He Appointed Over the People of Badr and Others

(Basbas) ibn ʿAmr ibn Thaʿlabah ibn Kharashah ibn ʿAmr ibn Saʿd ibn Dhibyān ibn Rushdān ibn Qays ibn Juhaynah, an ally of Banū Sāʿidah ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Khazraj.

(ʿAdī) ibn Abī al-Zaghabāʾ—whose name was Sinān ibn Subayʿ ibn Rabīʿ ibn Zimrah ibn Budayl ibn Saʿd ibn ʿAdī ibn Naṣr ibn Kāhil ibn Naṣr ibn Mālik ibn Ghāṭifān ibn Qays ibn Juhaynah—an ally of Banū Mālik ibn al-Najjār, of al-Khazraj.

And He Appointed Over the Companions at Uḥud

(Anas) ibn Faḍālah ibn ʿAdī ibn Ḥarām ibn al-Haytham ibn Ẓafar, who was Kaʿb ibn al-Khazraj ibn ʿAmr ibn Mālik ibn al-Aws, and his brother (Muways) ibn Faḍālah.

Naming Those Who Collected the Qurʾān in the Time of the Messenger of God ﷺ—They Were Six Men

(Saʿd) ibn ʿUbayd ibn al-Nuʿmān ibn Qays ibn ʿAmr ibn Zayd ibn Umayyah ibn Zayd ibn Mālik ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf ibn Mālik ibn al-Aws.

He was the first to collect the Qurʾān. And (Abū al-Dardāʾ) ʿUwaymir ibn Zayd ibn Qays ibn ʿĀʾishah ibn Umayyah ibn Mālik ibn ʿĀmir ibn ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Khazraj ibn al-Ḥārith. And (Muʿādh) ibn Jabal ibn ʿAmr ibn Aws ibn ʿĀʾidh ibn ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿUday ibn Saʿd ibn ʿAlī ibn Asad ibn Sārdah ibn Tazīd ibn Jusham ibn al-Khazraj. And (Abū Zayd) Thābit ibn Zayd ibn al-Nuʿmān ibn Mālik ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Mālik ibn Thaʿlabah ibn Kaʿb ibn al-Khazraj.

And (Ubayy) ibn Kaʿb ibn Qays ibn ʿUbayd ibn Zayd ibn Muʿāwiyah ibn ʿAmr ibn Mālik ibn al-Najjār. And (Zayd) ibn Thābit ibn al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Zayd ibn Lawdhān ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd ʿAwf ibn Ghanm ibn Mālik ibn al-Najjār.

Naming the Bearers of Good News Whom the Messenger of God ﷺ—Used to Send to Medina with News of Conquests and Safety

[The Expedition of Badr]

At the conquest of Badr: (Zayd) ibn Ḥārithah ibn Shurāḥīl ibn Kaʿb al-Kalbī, the client of the Messenger of God ﷺ—and (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Rawāḥah ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Thaʿlabah al-Anṣārī.

[The Expedition of Dhāt al-Riqāʿ]

During the expedition of Dhāt al-Riqāʿ: (Jaʿāl) ibn Surāqah, an ally of the Anṣār.

[The Expedition of al-Muraysīʿ]

During the expedition of al-Muraysīʿ: (Abū Namlah) Muʿādh ibn Zurarah al-Anṣārī, then al-Ẓafarī.

[The Expedition of Ḥunayn]

During the expedition of Ḥunayn: (Nuhayk) ibn Aws ibn Khuzaymah ibn ʿAdī al-Anṣārī, then al-Ashhalī.

[The Expedition of al-Ḥudaybiyah]

During the expedition of al-Ḥudaybiyah: (Salamah) ibn Aslam ibn Ḥuraysh al-Anṣārī al-Ashhalī.

Naming Those of the Clients Who Were at Badr

(Zayd) ibn Ḥārithah ibn Shurāḥīl al-Kalbī, the client of the Messenger of God may ﷺ. And (Abū Kabshah), his client ﷺ. And (Anasah), his client.

And (Ṣubayḥ), the client of Abū al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah. When Abū Salamah ibn ʿAbd al-Asad fell ill, he carried him on his camel. Then he took part in all the campaigns thereafter. And (Khabbāb), the client of ʿUtbah ibn Ghazwān, an ally of Banū Nawfal. And (Saʿd), the client of Ḥāṭib ibn Abī Baltaʿah. It is said that he was from Kalb. And (Khabbāb) ibn al-Aratt, the client of the daughter of Umm Anmār, the client of al-Akhnas ibn Sharīq al-Thaqafī. And (ʿĀmir) ibn Fuhayrah, the client of Abū Bakr. And (Bilāl) ibn Rabāḥ, the client of Abū Bakr ﵏. And (Ṣuhayb), the client of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Judʿān. He was from al-Nimr ibn Qāsiṭ. And (Muhjaʿ), the client of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb. It is said that he was from Yemen. And (Saʿd) ibn Khawlah, the client of Abū Ruhm. It is said that he was from Yemen. And (ʿUmayr) ibn ʿAwf, the client of Suhayl ibn ʿAmr.

It is said that he was from al-Azd.

And Among the Clients of the Anṣār

(Sālim), the client of the Anṣārī woman Bint Yaʿār, who is called the client of Abū Ḥudhayfah. (Tamīm), the client of Saʿd ibn Khaythamah, who is called the client of Banū Ghanm.

(Ḥabīb) ibn Aswad, the client of Banū Ḥarām. (Tamīm), the client of Khurāsh ibn al-Ṣimmah. It is said that he was from Yemen.

(al-Nuʿmān) ibn Sayyār, the client of Banū ʿUbayd. (ʿAntarah), the client of Sulaym ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥadīdah.

(ʿUṣaymah) ibn ʿAbd Allāh, the client of Banū Māzin.

It is said that he was from Ashjaʿ. (Abū al-Ḥamrāʾ), the client of al-Ḥārith ibn Rifāʿah.

A Man Whose Paternal Aunt and Maternal Aunt the Messenger of God Married

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Abī Bakr al-Ṣiddīq ﵀. His mother was Quraybah bt. Abī Umayyah, the sister of Umm Salamah; she was his maternal aunt. ʿĀʾishah bt. Abī Bakr was his paternal aunt.

Those Among the Companions of the Prophet ﷺ—Who Held the View of Mutʿah

(Khālid) ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Anṣārī. And (Zayd) ibn Thābit al-Anṣārī.

And (Salamah) ibn al-Akwaʿ al-Aslamī. And (ʿImrān) ibn al-Ḥuṣayn al-Khuzāʿī.

And (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ﵁.

Naming Those Among the Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ—Who Were with ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁—at al-Jamal and Ṣiffīn

(ʿAmmār) ibn Yāsir ﵀—was a veteran of Badr. He took part at al-Jamal and Ṣiffīn, and was killed at Ṣiffīn ﵀.

(Sahl) ibn Ḥunayf was a veteran of Badr. He took part at al-Jamal and Ṣiffīn. He was the one who said: “Suspect people according to your own judgment. By God, whenever we put our swords on our shoulders with the Messenger of God ﷺ—for some matter, it led us onward to a matter we recognized, except for this affair of ours.” He died at Kūfah.

(ʿUthmān) ibn Ḥunayf. He was the man whom ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵀—sent out, and who surveyed al-Sawād. ʿAlī ﵁—appointed him governor of al-Baṣrah. Ṭalḥah and al-Zubayr came to him, and they wrote an agreement of truce between them until ʿAlī ﵁—arrived. Then what happened at al-Jamal happened. ʿUthmān’s first campaign with the Prophet ﷺ—was at Uḥud. Thereafter, he took part with him ﷺ—in all the campaigns.

(Saʿd) ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Ṣimmah ibn ʿAtīk ibn Mabdhūl.

He accompanied the Prophet ﷺ. He was with ʿAlī ﵁—on the day of Ṣiffīn, and was killed that day. (Jāriyah) ibn Qudāmah ibn Zuhayr ibn al-Ḥuṣayn, one of Banū Saʿd ibn Zayd Manāt ibn Tamīm. Reports are transmitted from the Prophet ﷺ—on his authority. ʿAlī ﵁—sent him against Ibn al-Ḥaḍramī. Muʿāwiyah had sent Ibn al-Ḥaḍramī to al-Baṣrah. Jāriyah besieged him in the house of Sunbayl, then set the house ablaze around Ibn al-Ḥaḍramī and his companions. (Abū Masʿūd) al-Anṣārī. ʿAlī ﵁—appointed him as his deputy in Kūfah. His daughter was married to al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī ﵄. Then He dismissed him, and he returned to Medina. It is said that Abū Ayyūb Khālid ibn Zayd was with him at Ṣiffīn; this was related by al-Wāqidī. It is also related that Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī was at Ṣiffīn with ʿAlī ﵁ and then returned to Medina. Abū Umāma al-Ṣudayy ibn al-ʿAjlān al-Bāhilī. It is related from him that he said: “I was present at Ṣiffīn. They would not finish off a wounded man, pursue one who was fleeing, or strip a slain man.” Khuzayma ibn Thābit ibn al-Fākih ibn Thaʿlaba, of Banū Khaṭma. He was “the possessor of the two testimonies.” He carried the standard on the day of the conquest of Mecca. He was with ʿAlī ﵁ at the Battles of the Camel and Ṣiffīn, and was killed at Ṣiffīn. Hāshim ibn ʿUtba ibn Abī Waqqāṣ.

He accepted Islam on the day of the conquest. He was with ʿAlī ﵁ at the Battles of the Camel and Nahrawān. His eye was put out on the day of al-Yarmūk. He is the one who said:

A one-eyed man seeks a place for his family,

And he was killed on the day of Ṣiffīn. Sulaymān ibn Ṣurad al-Khuzāʿī. He used to be called Yasār, but the Messenger of God ﷺ named him Sulaymān. When the Messenger of God ﷺ died, he moved to Kūfa and settled there. He was with ʿAlī ﵁ at the Battles of the Camel and Ṣiffīn. Al-Ashʿath ibn Qays al-Kindī. He came to the Prophet ﷺ with seventy men of his people, and they accepted Islam. He was with ʿAlī ﵁ at the Battles of the Camel and Ṣiffīn. Qays ibn Saʿd ibn ʿUbāda. His father had entrusted him to the Prophet ﷺ to serve him. He remained with ʿAlī ﵁ in his campaigns.

He was in command of ʿAlī’s vanguard. Abū ʿAmra, whose name was Bashīr ibn ʿAmr ibn Miḥṣan ibn Madhbūl. His mother was Kabsha, the sister of Ḥassān ibn Thābit. He was killed with ʿAlī ﵁ at Ṣiffīn. Ḥujr ibn al-Adbar al-Kindī. He came to the Messenger of God ﷺ and was present at al-Qādisiyya and the Battles of the Camel and Ṣiffīn. Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān killed him at Marj ʿAdhrāʾ. It is said that Ḥujr was the first to proclaim the oneness of God, mighty and exalted, at Marj ʿAdhrāʾ when it was conquered: he entered it proclaiming God’s greatness. ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥamiq. He pledged allegiance to the Prophet ﷺ during the Farewell Pilgrimage. He had companionship with him thereafter. He was among those who marched against ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān ﵀ and was with ʿAlī ﵁ at the Battles of the Camel and Ṣiffīn. Ibn Umm al-Ḥakam killed him in al-Jazīra and sent his head to Muʿāwiya.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās. He was with ʿAlī ﵁ at the Battles of the Camel and Ṣiffīn.

ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās. The Prophet ﷺ died when he was twelve years old. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar ibn Abī Ṭālib. He remembered of the Prophet ﷺ that he entered upon his mother when Jaʿfar ﵀ was martyred and announced his death to her. Al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī. He remembered of the Prophet ﷺ that he took a date from the alms dates, removed it from his mouth, and put it back among the alms dates. ʿUmar ibn Abī Salama. The Messenger of God ﷺ died when he was nine years old. He was with ʿAlī ﵁ on the day of the Battle of the Camel. Jaʿda ibn Hubayra ibn Abī Wahb. His mother was Hind bint Abī Ṭālib.

He remained with ʿAlī ﵁ in his campaigns. ʿAlī sent him to Khurāsān.

And al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib.

Those who were present at Ṣiffīn with Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil al-Sahmī. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ. Zumal ibn Khushāf ibn Khudayj al-ʿUdhrī. He came to the Messenger of God ﷺ who wrote him a document and assigned him a standard. He was present at Ṣiffīn with that standard, on Muʿāwiya’s side. Busr ibn Abī Arṭāʾ, one of Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy. It is said that the Messenger of God ﷺ died while he was a small boy; he did not relate anything from him. It is also said, however, that he related from him that “a thief from the spoils was brought to him, and he did not cut off his hand.”

Ḥabīb ibn Maslama al-Fihrī. It is related that he came to the Prophet ﷺ while he was in Medina. His father caught up with him and said, “O Prophet of God, my hand and my foot!” He replied, “Take him back, for he is likely to perish.” He said: his father died that year. The Prophet ﷺ died when he was twenty-two years old. He had not taken part in any campaign with him.

He has traditions that he relates from the Prophet ﷺ. He was with Muʿāwiya at Ṣiffīn.

Muʿāwiya appointed him governor over Medina, and he died there. Al-Nuʿmān ibn Bashīr.

Nāʾila sent him with ʿUthmān’s shirt ﵀ to Muʿāwiya in Syria. He settled in Syria and Iraq; later, his descendants went to Medina and Baghdad. He was one of Muʿāwiya’s officials over Kūfa. Ṣuḥār ibn ʿAbbās al-ʿAbdī, of Banū Murra ibn Ẓafar ibn al-Dayl. He came to the Prophet ﷺ. Ṣuḥār was among those who sought vengeance for ʿUthmān. ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir al-Juhanī. He was a Companion of the Messenger of God ﷺ. When Abū Bakr called the people to Syria, he set out and was present at the conquests of Syria and Egypt. He settled in Egypt and built a house there. He was with Muʿāwiya at Ṣiffīn.

Khārija ibn Ḥudhāfa. He was ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ’s deputy in Egypt. It was him whom the Khārijite struck in Egypt, thinking he was ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ. Faḍāla ibn ʿUbayd. He was present at Uḥud, the Trench, and all the campaigns with the Messenger of God ﷺ.

Then he went to Syria and settled in Damascus. He was in charge of Muʿāwiya’s judiciary:

Al-Ṣimma ibn Qays. He has traditions from the Messenger of God ﷺ. He was in charge of Muʿāwiya’s police. Al-Ḥakam ibn ʿAmr al-Ghifārī. He was a Companion of the Prophet ﷺ until the Prophet died. He moved to Baṣra and built a house there. Ziyād ibn Abīhi appointed him governor of Khurāsān, and he remained governor there until he died in the time of Muʿāwiya. Samura ibn Jundab al-Fazārī. He was in charge of Ziyād ibn Abīhi’s police. Al-Mughīra ibn Shuʿba. He governed Kūfa for Muʿāwiya. Muʿāwiya ibn Khudayj al-Kindī. He fought Muḥammad ibn Abī Bakr al-Ṣiddīq ﵀ in Egypt. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Qays al-Fihrī. He has traditions from the Messenger of God ﷺ. He was in charge of Muʿāwiya’s police. ʿAdī ibn ʿUmayra ibn Farwa ibn Zurāra ibn al-Arqam ibn al-Nuʿmān ibn ʿAmr ibn Wahb ibn Rabīʿa ibn Muʿāwiya al-Akrimīn. Banū al-Arqam had a mosque in Kūfa. When ʿAlī ﵁ arrived in Kūfa, his followers began speaking ill of ʿUthmān. The Banū al-Arqam said, “We will not remain in a town where ʿUthmān is reviled.” They left for al-Jazīra and settled in al-Ruhāʾ. They were with Muʿāwiya at Ṣiffīn. That day, ʿAdī ibn ʿUmayra struck him on the hand. Abū al-Ghādiya was present with the Messenger of God ﷺ at al-Ḥudaybiyya. It is related that he fought ʿAmmār ibn Yāsir ﵁.

It is said that the man who killed him was Ḥuwayy ibn Mātiʿ al-Saksakī, the grandfather of Nūḥ ibn ʿAmr, who served Isḥāq ibn Ibrāhīm ibn Muṣʿab al-Khurāsānī. They report that five men shared in killing ʿAmmār ﵀ each claiming to have killed him; Abū al-Ghādiya was the one who severed his head. Abū Firās Muḥammad ibn Firās al-Sulamī told me, from Hishām, from his father, who said: I was at the gate of al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik when a large, ruddy old man came up. Written between his shoulders was: “Witness to the Conquest of Conquests.” They used to call the killing of ʿAmmār “the Conquest of Conquests.” He said to the doorkeeper, “Ask permission for me to enter, and say: ‘This is Abū al-Ghādiya, the killer of ʿAmmār.’” Al-Kalbī said: I took him by the hand and said to him, “By your father! You have made your opponent out to be a prize bull.”

Notable Blind Men

The prophet Shuʿayb, son of Yūbab ibn ʿAyfā ibn Madyan ibn Ibrāhīm ﵇. The prophet Isḥāq ﵇ son of Ibrāhīm ﵇. Zuhra ibn Kilāb ibn Murra ibn Kaʿb. ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim. Al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās. Umayya ibn ʿAbd Shams, who was one-eyed. Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb, who was one-eyed.

Al-Ḥakam ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ. Abū Quḥāfa. Makhrama ibn Nawfal al-Zuhrī. Saʿīd ibn Yarbuʿ al-Makhzūmī. Al-Fākih ibn al-Mughīra.

ʿAmr ibn Umm Maktūm. Al-Ḥārith ibn al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib.

Muṭʿim ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf. Abū Bishr ibn Muṭʿim ibn ʿAdī. Al-Walīd ibn ʿUqba ibn ʿUmāra ibn ʿUqba ibn Abī Muʿayṭ. He said, listing those of Banū ʿAbd Manāf who were veiled from sight:

By my life! Though blindness has befallen me,

Noble men have suffered the loss of their sight.

Umayya and his son were veiled from sight;

So too were Abū ʿAmr, Ṣakhr, and Hāshim.

Shayba, the best of men, was Hāshim’s son—

And who among us is safe from the hand of time?

Those are my forefathers; I have been struck by their affliction,

And I know that I shall follow the people.

Abū Bakr ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Hishām ibn al-Mughīra.

ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUtba ibn Masʿūd. ʿUbayd Allāh is the one who said to ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz and ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān:

Do not be surprised that you receive and speak,

For no people have been filled with anything worse than pride.

Touch the earth’s dust; from it you were created,

And in it lie the return and the journey to the gathering.

If I wished, I could speak of many things about you,

Openly, or say them in private.

If I neither command nor forbid you,

I would laugh at him until he enters in and grows bolder.

ʿArāba al-Awsī. ʿUtba ibn Masʿūd al-Hudhalī. Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Anṣārī. Abū Umāma al-Bāhilī. Abū Aḥmad ibn Jaḥsh ibn Riʾāb al-Asadī. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Arqam. Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Sulamī.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Awfā, whose name was ʿAlqama ibn Khālid. Al-Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib.

Ḥassān ibn Thābit. Kaʿb ibn Mālik. ʿItbān ibn Mālik. Qatāda ibn al-Nuʿmān al-Anṣārī. Abū Usayd al-Sāʿidī. ʿUmayr ibn ʿAdī al-Khaṭmī.

Qatāda ibn Diʿāma al-Sadūsī. Durayd ibn al-Ṣimma al-Jushamī. He was present at Ḥunayn [uncertain reading: the text gives “Ḥunayn” with a question mark], though blind, and was killed that day. Hārūn ibn Sulaymān ibn al-Manṣūr, Commander of the Faithful. Khuzayma ibn Khāzim al-Nahshalī. ʿAmr ibn Huddāb al-Māzinī.

Abū al-Jahm ibn Ḥudhayfa ibn Ghānim al-ʿAdawī. Mūsā ibn Mūsā al-Hādī.

ʿAbd al-Ṣamad ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās. A physician came in to see him and gave him hope of recovery. He said, “What harm is it to me, when I am the seventh of seven, all of whom have lost their sight?” By this he meant seven of his forefathers who had lost their sight.

Notable Lepers

Judhayma al-Abrash, king of Iraq among the Azd. He was called “the Radiant.” Yarbuʿ ibn Ḥanẓala ibn Mālik ibn Zayd Manāt ibn Tamīm. Aws ibn Ḥajar says of this:

The sons of al-Abras were your peers;

They outlasted the later and the earlier.

ʿAmr ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAdas ibn Zayd ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Dārim.

Ḍamra ibn Ḍamra ibn Jābir ibn Qaṭan ibn Nahshal ibn Dārim. Al-Abyaḍ ibn Mujāshiʿ ibn Dārim. Al-Abyaḍ ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Kinda. Qushayr ibn Kaʿb ibn Rabīʿa ibn ʿĀmir ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿa. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Hubayra al-Qushayrī. His son Ziyād was a notable in Khurāsān.

Durayd ibn al-Ṣimma, whose father al-Ṣimma was Muʿāwiya ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Muʿāwiya ibn Bakr ibn Hawāzin. Al-Rabīʿ ibn Ziyād ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sufyān ibn Nāshib ibn Hadm ibn ʿAwdh ibn Ghālib ibn Quṭayʿa ibn ʿAbs. Labīd ibn Rabīʿa has a rajaz poem about him, in the presence of al-Nuʿmān, in which he says:

Easy now, may you be spared reproach; do not eat with him:

His buttock is mottled with leprosy.

He puts his finger in it;

He pushes it in until he hides his knuckle,

As though seeking something to tempt him.

Al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥilliza al-Yashkurī, the poet. Jassās ibn Murra ibn Dhuhl ibn Shaybān ibn Thaʿlaba ibn ʿUkāba, the killer of Kulayb. ʿUmayra ibn Aws ibn ʿAwf ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Dhuhl ibn al-Khazraj ibn Zayd Allāh ibn Rufayda al-Kalbī. Al-Saffāḥ al-Taghlibī, namely Salama ibn Khālid ibn Kaʿb ibn Zuhayr ibn Taym. He once stood to speak in a war between Bakr and Taghlib, and passed wind. He said, “Every piebald horse breaks wind.” A piebald coat is a defect in horses; by this he meant that only a defective one does that.

Balʿāʾ ibn Qays ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yaʿmar al-Kinānī. He was asked, “What is this whiteness on you?” He replied, “A sword—God drew it bright.” Abū ʿAzza, namely ʿAmr ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmayr ibn Wahīb ibn Ḥudhāfa. Abū ʿAzza was a poet. He was captured at Badr, and the Messenger of God ﷺ released him after taking his pledge that he would neither satirize him nor make war against him. He captured him at Uḥud, and the Messenger of God ﷺ struck off his head.

Quraysh had expelled him from Mecca for fear of contagion. At night he would stay in the mountain ravines, and by day he would take shelter under the trees. He developed dropsy of the belly, so he took a dagger and thrust it into his stomach. The fluid drained away, his leprosy was cured, and he returned to Mecca. He said:

O God, Lord of Wāʾil and Nahd,and of she-camels and sleek horses,and of those who strive in the land of Najd,I have become Your servant and the son of a servant.You cured the leprosy on my skin,after I had pierced my belly.

(Anas) ibn Mālik al-Anṣārī.

(Muʿāwiya) ibn Ḥazn ibn Mawʾila ibn Muʿāwiya ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Mālik ibn Kaʿb al-Ḥārithī. He was called “the Marked One” because of his leprosy.

(Saʿd) ibn Ḥāritha ibn Lām al-Ṭāʾī. Ḥātim speaks of him and his brother Unayf ibn Ḥāritha:

And the son of al-Nujūd, when he goes out with his belly distended,the soot-dark cookpots, and the one with a spotted belly.

(ʿAmr) ibn Huddāb al-Māzinī.

(Muḥammad) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ismāʿīl ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar.

(Shimr) ibn Dhī al-Jawshan.

(Zurāra) ibn Qays ibn al-Ḥārith al-Nakhaʿī.

(Abān) ibn ʿUthmān / ibn ʿAffān.

(Al-Mughīra) ibn Ḥabnāʾ al-Tamīmī.

(Qaṭarī) ibn al-Fujāʾa al-Tamīmī, the Khārijite.

(Al-Ḍaḥḥāk) ibn Qays al-Fihrī, the man of Marj Rāhiṭ.

(Ayman) ibn Khuraym ibn Fātik al-Asadī.

The notable one-eyed men

(Abū Sufyān) ibn Ḥarb, at al-Ṭāʾif with the Prophet ﷺ. He later became blind.

(Umayya) ibn ʿAbd Shams. He later became blind.

(Al-Mughīra) ibn Shuʿba al-Thaqafī, on the day of al-Ḥudaybiya.

(Al-Ashʿath) ibn Qays al-Kindī, on the day of al-Yarmūk.

(ʿAdī) ibn Ḥātim al-Ṭāʾī, at the Battle of the Camel with ʿAlī ﵁.

(Hāshim) ibn ʿUtba ibn Abī Waqqāṣ, on the day of al-Yarmūk.

(Qabīṣa) ibn Dhuʾayb, on the Day of al-Ḥarra.

(Jarīr) ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Bajalī, at Hamadhān in the time of ʿUthmān.

(ʿUtba) ibn Abī Sufyān, at the Battle of the Camel with ʿĀʾisha.

(Saʿīd) ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān, at Samarqand.

(Ṭalḥa al-Ṭalḥāt) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khalaf al-Khuzāʿī, at Samarqand.

(Al-Muhallab) ibn Abī Ṣufra, at Samarqand with Saʿīd.

(Mālik) ibn Mismāʿ, at al-Jafra.

(Qaṭan) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥuṣayn al-Ḥārithī, in Ādharbayjān.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Yazīd al-Qasrī, on the day of Marj Rāhiṭ with Marwān.

(Al-Mukhtār) ibn Abī ʿUbayd al-Thaqafī.

ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Ziyād struck him with his whip, and his eye was lost. (Qays) ibn Makshūḥ al-Murādī, on the day of al-Yarmūk. (Mālik al-Ashtar) al-Nakhaʿī, on the day of al-Yarmūk.

(Al-Aḥnaf) ibn Qays al-Tamīmī, at Samarqand.

(ʿUbayd Allāh) ibn Abī ʿUqayl al-Thaqafī.

(Muslim) ibn ʿUqba al-Murrī, known as Musrif, at Ṣiffīn with Muʿāwiya.

The notable cross-eyed men

The notable, scant-bearded flat-nosed men

/ (Al-Ḥārith) ibn [Abī] Shamir al-Ghassānī. (Al-Mundhir) ibn al-Mundhir ibn al-Nuʿmān ibn Māʾ al-Samāʾ al-Lakhmī. (Qays) ibn Saʿd ibn ʿUbāda al-Anṣārī.

Sons of Christian women

(Al-Ḥārith) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Rabīʿa al-Makhzūmī. His mother was an Abyssinian woman named Ṣabḥāʾ.

(ʿUthmān) ibn ʿAnbasa ibn Abī Sufyān.

(Al-ʿAbbās) ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān.

(Khālid) ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Qasrī.

(ʿUbayd Allāh) ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Abī al-Aʿwar al-Sulamī.

(Yazīd) ibn Asīd al-Sulamī.

(Mālik) ibn Ḍabb al-Kalbī.

(Shaqīq) ibn Salama, Abū Wāʾil.

(Ḥanẓala) ibn Ṣafwān al-Kalbī. It is said that Ḥanẓala’s mother once went out to a church, accompanied by some maidservants. She passed Ḥanẓala, who was with some Bedouins of Kalb. One of them said, “By God, this Christian woman of yours is very fat! Is there no young man among you for her?” Ḥanẓala said to him, “Be civil, may God have mercy on you! She is the mother of one of your companions.”

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Abī ʿAmr ibn Ḥafṣ ibn al-Mughīra al-Makhzūmī.

Sons of Abyssinian women

(Naḍla) ibn Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf.

(Nufayl) ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Riyāḥ ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qurṭ ibn Razāḥ ibn ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb.

(ʿAmr) ibn Rabīʿa ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥubayyib ibn Judhayma, of Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy. Their mother was Ṣahāl, an Abyssinian woman who belonged to Hāshim ibn ʿAbd Manāf.

(Al-Khaṭṭāb) ibn Nufayl, whose mother was Ḥayya, who belonged to Jābir ibn Abī Ḥabīb al-Fahmī. They relate that Thābit ibn Qays ibn Shammās al-Anṣārī taunted ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, saying, “O son of a black woman!” Then God ﵎ revealed: “O you who believe! Let not one people ridicule another people: perhaps they may be better than them.”

(49:11) (ʿAmr) ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil al-Sahmī.

(Maʿmar) ibn ʿUthmān al-Taymī.

(Al-Ḥārith) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Rabīʿa al-Makhzūmī. His mother was Ṣabḥāʾ, an Abyssinian Christian woman.

(ʿUthmān) ibn al-Ḥuwayrith ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Quṣayy.

(Ṣafwān) ibn Umayya ibn Khalaf al-Jumaḥī.

(Hishām) ibn ʿUqba ibn Abī Muʿayṭ.

(Mālik) ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUthmān al-Umawī.

His mother was al-Ṣamʿāʾ. (Hammām) ibn Muṭarrif al-Taghlibī. (Yaʿlā) ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿUqba ibn Abī Muʿayṭ. The poet says of him:

It is as if, on the parting places of Yaʿlā’s head,black beetles had settled in the age of al-Biṭāḥ.

“In God’s name; then give me a boy,and name him Aflaḥ or Rabāḥ.”

(A son) of Hibat Allāh ibn Ibrāhīm ibn al-Mahdī. His mother was Rummār.

(Aḥmad) ibn Muḥammad ibn Ṣāliḥ al-Makhzūmī.

(Al-Akhnasī), namely [blank in original]; al-Arqam, namely [blank in original].

Customs established by the Jāhiliyya, some of which Islam retained and some of which it abolished

Among these was that they would divorce a woman three times. A man would say to his wife, “You are divorced once,” and he had the greatest right to her. If he divorced her twice, the same applied. If he divorced her three times, he had no claim on her. Al-Aʿshā said the following: he had married a woman, and her people wanted her back from him. They threatened to beat him if he did not divorce her, so he said:

O neighbor, part from me, for you are divorced;such are people’s affairs, coming and going.

They said to him, “Make it two.” He said:

Part from me, for separation is better than the stick,or a flash will keep above your head.

They said to him, “Make it three.” He said:

Part from me, chaste one, whose modesty is beyond reproach,beloved among us, and loving in return.

The first to divorce was Ismāʿīl ibn Ibrāhīm ﵉.

They would ask a woman’s father, brother, paternal uncle, or one of her paternal cousins for her hand. A suitable man would seek a suitable woman. If one of the two was superior to the other in lineage, a higher bride-price would be sought for him. If he was of mixed parentage, he would seek the hand of a woman of mixed parentage, and she would be married to a man like herself. When the suitor came to them, he would say, “Good morning.” Then he would say, “We are your equals and peers. If you marry her to us, we will have attained what we desire, and so will you. We will be grateful to you for the marriage tie. If you turn us away for a reason we understand, we will return excused.” If he was a close relative of hers or of her people, her father or brother, when she was brought to him, would say, “May you prosper, bear sons, and not daughters! May God make you a source of numbers, strength, and endurance. Be good in character and honor your husband. Let your perfume be water.” If she was married away from home He said to her: “May you not become prosperous, nor bear a son, for you bring distant kin near and bear enemies. Improve your character and make yourself dear to your husband’s kin, for they have an eye watching you and an ear listening. Let your perfume be water.”

They used to make the pilgrimage to the House, perform the lesser pilgrimage, circumambulate the House seven times, touch the Black Stone, and run between al-Ṣafā and al-Marwa.

On al-Ṣafā stood Isāf, and on al-Marwa Nāʾila. They were two idols, both from Jurhum. Isāf committed fornication with Nāʾila inside the Kaʿba, and they were transformed into two stones. They were placed on al-Ṣafā and al-Marwa so that people might take warning from them. But afterward they were worshipped. Abū Ṭālib said:

And circuits between the two Marwas and al-ṢafāAnd what images and apparitions there are upon them.

They used to recite talbiya, though some of them associated partners in their talbiya. Quraysh, whose rite was for Isāf, used to say: “At Your service, O God, at Your service; at Your service. You have no partner, except a partner who belongs to You. You own him and what he owns.”

Thereafter each tribe had its own talbiya.

The talbiya of those whose rite was for al-ʿUzzā was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service; at Your service, and joyfully obedient. How beloved we are to You!”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for al-Lāt was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service; at Your service. Our House is sufficient as a sanctuary: it is neither abandoned nor afflicted. Rather, it is of pure earth.

Its masters are among the righteous of creation.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Jahār was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service; at Your service. Make our sins requited, and guide us to the clearest beacon. Grant us enjoyment and prolong our stay with Jahār.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Suwāʿ was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service; at Your service. We have come back to You. Suwāʿ has been sought from You.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Shams was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service; at Your service, as long as our day endures, with its nightfall, its heat, and its cold. We shall neither choose anything nor bring it harm.

A pilgrimage to a Lord whose goodness is upright.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Muḥarriq was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service.

At Your service, a true pilgrimage, in devotion and servitude.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Wadd was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service; at Your service, as an apology to You.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Dhū al-Khalaṣa was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service; at Your service with what is most pleasing to You.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Munṭabiq was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service; at Your service.”

When ʿAkk reached Mecca, they used to send two black enslaved youths ahead of them. The two enslaved youths, stripped and naked, would walk on a litter.

They would do no more than say: “We are the two ravens of ʿAkk.” When the two youths called out thus, those of ʿAkk behind them would shout: “ʿAkk has come to You in submission; Your servants are the two Yamānīs, so that we may make the pilgrimage again, upon the swift riding camels.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Manāt was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service; at Your service. Were it not that Bakr is below You, people would honor You and forsake You. The pilgrims of ʿAthj have never ceased coming to You; we are on their tracks, below You.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Saʿīda was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service.

At Your service, at Your service. We have not come to you for amusement, nor seeking wealth. Rather, we have come to you for counsel.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Yaʿūq was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service; at Your service. Make evil hateful to us and make good beloved to us. Do not make us insolent, so that we exult, and do not burden us with a stumble.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Yaghūth was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service; at Your service. Make us beloved for what You possess, for we are Your servants and have come to You.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Nasr was: “Bīk, O God, at Your service; at Your service. We are servants, and we are all ready and at hand. You are the praised one among us. Return our dominion and our game.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Dhū al-Lubā was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service; at Your service. Lord, ward off Muḍar from us, and grant us safe passage on this journey. What is among them is surely a warning.

Suffice us, O God, against the lords of Hajar.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Marḥab was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service; at Your service, we are in Your presence. At Your service, make us beloved to You.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Dhurayḥ was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service; at Your service. We are all ungrateful, and all of us deny the blessing. So protect us from every lurking serpent.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Dhū al-Kaffayn was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service.

At Your service. The Jurhum are Your servants; other people are newcomers, while they are Your ancient folk. And we have the stronger claim to Your protection than they.”

The talbiya of those whose rite was for Hubal was: “At Your service, O God, at Your service. We are free of tribute.

You have forbidden us to the points of the spears. People envy us for our success.”

All these idols were in the lands of the Arabs, worshipped alongside God—mighty and majestic is He! There is no god but He. Al-ʿUzzā was a tree at Nakhlah, beside which stood an idol worshipped by Ghaṭafān. Its keepers were from Banū Ṣirma b. Murra. Quraysh held it in great reverence. Ghanī and Bāhila worshipped it along with them. The Messenger of God ﷺ sent Khālid b. al-Walīd, who cut down the tree, demolished the shrine, and broke the idol.

Al-Lāt belonged to Thaqīf at al-Ṭāʾif and was represented by a rock. They used to screen that shrine and make it resemble the Kaʿba. It had doorkeepers and a covering. They treated its valley as inviolable. The Messenger of God ﷺ sent Abū Sufyān b. Ḥarb and al-Mughīra b. Shuʿba, who demolished it. Its keepers were the family of Abū al-ʿĀṣ, of Banū Yasār b. Mālik of Thaqīf.

Jahār belonged to Hawāzin at ʿUkāẓ. Its keepers were the family of ʿAwf al-Naṣrī. Muḥārib shared it with them. It stood on the slope of Aṭḥal.

Suwāʿ was at Nuʿmān. Banū Kināna, Hudhayl, Muzayna, and ʿAmr b. Qays b. ʿAylān worshipped it. Its keepers were Banū Ṣāhila of Hudhayl.

Shams belonged to Banū Tamīm and had a shrine. All the tribes of Add worshipped it: Ḍabba, Tamīm, ʿAdī, ʿIkl, and Thawr. Its keepers were from Banū Aws b. Mukhāshin b. Muʿāwiya b. Sharīf b. Jarwa b. Usayyid b. ʿAmr b. Tamīm.

Hind b. Abī Hāla and Ṣafwān b. Usayyid b. al-Ḥalāḥil b. Aws b. Mukhāshin broke it.

Wadd belonged to Banū Wabra. Its site was at Dūmat al-Jandal. Its keepers were Banū al-Furāfiṣa b. al-Aḥwaṣ, of Kalb.

Al-Fals was in Najd and was worshipped by Ṭayyiʾ. It was near Fayd. Its keepers were Banū Būlān.

The Anṣār, Azd Shanuʾa, and others of the Azd worshipped Manāt. It was on the coast. Its keepers were the Ghaṭārīf of the Azd.

Saʿd Hudhaym and the rest of Quḍāʿa, except Banū Wabra, worshipped al-Saʿīda and Manāt.

The Azd also worshipped al-Saʿīda. Its keepers were Banū al-ʿAjlān.

Its site was at Uḥud.

Dhū al-Khalaṣa had a shrine and was worshipped by Bajīla, Khathʿam, al-Ḥārith b. Kaʿb, Jurm, Zubayd, al-Ghawth b. Murra b. Add, and Banū Hilāl b. ʿĀmir, who were its keepers. It was between Mecca and Yemen, at al-ʿAblāʾ, four stages from Mecca. Today it is a lime kiln, according to what I was told.

Yaghūth belonged to all of Madhḥij. It was at Anʿum. Ghuṭayf, of Murād, fought them over it until they fled with it to Najrān. They installed it with Banū al-Nār, of al-Ḍibāb, of Banū al-Ḥārith b. Kaʿb. They all gathered around it.

Yaʿūq belonged to Hamdān and Khawlān. It was at Arḥab.

Nasr belonged to Ḥimyar, who performed rites for it, revered it, and professed devotion to it. It was at Ghumdān,

the palace of the king of Yemen.

Dhū al-Lubā belonged to ʿAbd al-Qays at al-Mushaqqar. Its keepers were among them, from Banū ʿĀmir.

Al-Muḥarriq at Salmān belonged to Bakr b. Wāʾil and the rest of Rabīʿa. They had made one of its offspring for each clan of Rabīʿa. Among ʿAnaza was (Balj) b. al-Muḥarriq. Among ʿUmayra and Ghufayla was (ʿAmr) b. al-Muḥarriq. Its keepers were the family of al-Aswad al-ʿAjlī.

Dhurayḥ belonged to Kinda at al-Nujayr in Yemen, in the direction of Ḥaḍramawt.

Marḥab belonged to Ḥaḍramawt. Its keeper was Dhū Marḥab, and it was named after him.

Munṭabiq belonged to al-Sulaf, ʿAkk, and the Ashʿarīs. It was an idol of copper. They spoke to it from inside, and it uttered speech unlike anything ever heard. When the idols were broken, they found a sword inside it. The Messenger of God ﷺ chose it for himself, and it was named Mikhḏam.

Isāf and Nāʾila belonged to Quraysh and the Aḥābīsh.

Hubal belonged to Banū Bakr, Mālik, Malkān, and the rest of Banū Kināna.

Quraysh worshipped the patron idol of Banū Kināna, while Banū Kināna worshipped the patron idol of Quraysh.

The Arabs revered these idols, on which all were agreed and to which they gathered once each year. In every clan, moreover, there were things they never left behind; they would ask them, and receive answers from them.

Dhū al-Kaffayn belonged to Khuzāʿa and Daws. When ʿAmr b. Ḥamma al-Dawsī broke it, he said: “O Dhū al-Kaffayn, I am not of your ancient folk. Our birth is older than your birth.”

These were the principal idols of their tyrants, to which they turned after their pilgrimage. They would not return to their homes until they had passed by them, honored them, drawn near to them, and performed rites for them.

The Arabs used to stand at ʿArafāt and depart from there while the sun was still bright, coming to Muzdalifa. Quraysh did not leave Muzdalifa or stand at ʿArafāt. They used to say: “We do not venerate what lies beyond the Sanctuary as we venerate the Sanctuary.” Quṣayy built the sacred station and used to light lamps there, so that the people of ʿArafāt could find their way when they came to Muzdalifa.

God preserved it as a sacred station, and commanded that people stand there. Al-ʿĀmirī said of their standing there in the pre-Islamic period:

I swear by Him to whom Quraysh made pilgrimageAnd by the station of the pilgrims at Ilāl.

They used to offer sacrificial animals and throw the pebbles at the stone pillars. They revered the sacred months and treated them as inviolable, except Ṭayyiʾ and Khathʿam, who used to make them permissible.

They used to bathe after sexual impurity and wash their dead. Al- Afwah al-Awdī said:

Come, both of you, comfort me, and know that I am in peril;What I said will not save me: discord will not, nor caution;What I said will not save me whenThe joints of my limbs appear and my gaze grows fixed.

They brought cold water to wash me—

What a washing this is, to be followed by dust!

They cast his garments about him and grieved;They uttered ringing cries, and the people rose in commotion,Toward a pit to which he made his way with haste—That is the House of Truth; not wool and hair.

They leveled the earth over him, wet and dry.Indeed, everything besides that will be put to the test.

They used to shroud their dead and pray over them. Their prayer was this: the dead person would be carried on a bier, then his heir would stand and recall all his good qualities and praise him, then say: “May God’s mercy be upon you.” Then he would be buried. And God said— May God—exalted is He—say: “And pray for them; your prayer is a reassurance for them” (9:103). Prayer from created beings means supplication. Hence it is said in the tradition: “When someone is invited, let him respond. If he is not fasting, let him eat; but if he is fasting, let him pray”—that is, let him supplicate. Al-Aʿshā said:

A reddish wine, whose Jewish pourercircled it round,

then brought it forth, with its sealstill upon it.

The wind met it in its jar,and he prayed over its jar and marked it.

He also said:

My daughter says, as my departure draws near,“Lord, spare my father sickness and pain.”

May there be upon you the like of what you have prayed;close your eyes and sleep, for a person has a place to lie down.

A man of Kalb, from the pre-Islamic era, said to his father:

ʿAmr! If you die while I am alive,I shall offer many prayers for you.

I shall make half my wealth for Ibn Salmā,in my lifetime, if I live, and after my death.

A certain versifier said of the shroud:

Tubbaʿ and Dhū Yazan he destroyed,and Dhū Nuwās he destroyed, and Dhū Jadan.

His share of what it held and storedwas a wipe of camphor, a washing, and a shroud.

Most Arabs believed in resurrection. Of this al-Aʿshā says:

No one who builds a templeand sets up crosses within it,

who comes and goes from the King’s prayers,now bowing, now crying aloud,

is more devout than you in the reckoning,when the faces scatter dust.

They believed in the reckoning. Al-Akhnas ibn Shihāb al-Tamīmī, from the pre-Islamic era, said:

I witnessed the adversary on the day he defended himself,and took from him a decisive settlement.

And I knew that God requites His servanton the Day of Reckoning for the finest deeds.

When a man died, they would take the riding camel he had ridden and station it at his grave, with its head turned back toward its forelegs and its head wrapped in its halter. They would neither feed nor water it until it died, so that he might ride it when he came out of his grave.

They used to say that if this was not done, he would be raised on foot on the Day of Resurrection.

That she-camel on which this was done was called “al-baliyyah.” Abū Zubayd al-Ṭāʾī mentioned women stripped of their ornaments at a funeral, likening them to such camels:

Like al-baliyyāt, their heads in their halters,their cheeks, granting the hot winds, exposed.

Jarībah ibn Ashyam al-Faqʿasī said:

Saʿd! If I should die,I charge you—for the closest brother is the one who gives counsel—

do not leave your father stumbling on footat the Resurrection, falling headlong and pitching forward.

Carry your father on a worthy camel,and guard against sin; that is the sounder course.

Few among what I have amassed are mountsin the terror on which I may ride when they cry, “Ride!”

ʿAmr ibn Zayd al-Kalbī advised his son:

My son! When I leave you, equip mein the grave with a camel and a sturdy saddle,

so that I may ride it for the Resurrection when they cry, “Depart!”We shall all go together, packed close, to the gathering of the Gatherer.

Whoever does not arrive upon a long-striding she-camelwill be among the throng, pressed down or stumbling.

The Arabs did not bequeath anything of the inheritance to daughters, women, or children. They bequeathed only to one who had secured the spoils and fought on horseback.

The first in the pre-Islamic era to leave inheritances to daughters, giving a daughter one share and a son two shares, was Dhū al-Majāṣid al-Yashkurī, namely ʿĀmir ibn Jashm ibn Ḥabīb. A man of the Anṣār died before the inheritance verse was revealed. He was called Aws ibn Thābit, of Banū Khaṭmah, and left four daughters, who were quite unattractive. His paternal cousins took all his property. Aws’s wife came to the Prophet ﷺ—and said: “Messenger of God! Aws ibn Thābit has died, leaving good property. His two paternal cousins, Qatādah and ʿArfaṭah, came and took the property, giving nothing to his daughters. They are in my care, without food or drink, and I have nothing that can provide for them.”

She said: “They took the property and gave nothing to his daughters; they are in my care, without food or drink, and I have nothing in my hands to provide for them.” He ﷺ—said: “Return to your house until I see what God decrees concerning them.” Then God—exalted is He—revealed: “Men shall have a share of what parents and near relatives leave, and women shall have a share of what parents and near relatives leave” (4:7). He did not specify what the share was. He sent word to Qatādah and ʿArfaṭah: “Do not distribute any of the property, for a share has been revealed for the daughters of Aws. I will let you know what it is.” Then the inheritance verse was revealed: “God enjoins you concerning your children: for the male is the like of the share of two females” (4:11). The Messenger of God ﷺ—gave the daughters two-thirds and the wife one-eighth.

The Arabs did not marry their daughters, mothers, sisters, maternal aunts, or paternal aunts.

The Arabs did marry their fathers’ wives; this was the most abominable thing they did. A man who took over his father’s wife was called a “ḍayzan.” Aws ibn Ḥajar said:

Persian ways among you are no strange thing,for every one of you has been a ḍayzan to his father before him.

When a man died, his eldest son would rise and cast his garment over his father’s wife that is, his father’s other wife, not his own mother, as stated above—and thereby inherited the right to marry her. If he had no desire for her, one of his brothers would marry her with a new bride-price. Islam separated many men and women from their fathers’ wives.

Among them was Manẓūr ibn Zabbān ibn Sayyār ibn ʿAmr al-Fazārī, whose wife was Mulaykah bint Khārijah ibn Sinān al-Murrī. He took her after his father and she bore him Khawlah bint Manẓūr. Al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī ﵁—then married her; after al-Ḥasan, ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām took her in marriage. Manẓūr is the one who says:

Ah! I care not today what Time has done,if Mulaykah and wine have gone from me.

ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵀—separated the two of them.

Another was Tamīm ibn Abī ibn Muqbil. He was married to Dahmāʾ, his father’s wife. Islam separated the two of them.

He is the one who said:

Has any lover attained his desire from Dahmāʾin the pre-Islamic age without being stoned?

Muḥṣin ibn Abī Qays ibn al-Aslat had cast his garment over his father’s wife, Kubayshah bint Maʿn, of Banū Khaṭmah, and thereby inherited the right to marry her. Then he left her without consummating the marriage or providing for her. She came to the Messenger of God ﷺ and told him, saying: “He has left me without providing for me and will not set me free, so that I may return to my family.”

He ﷺ—said: “Return to your house; if God decrees anything concerning your case, I will inform you.” Then God—glorified and mighty is He—revealed: “And do not marry women whom your fathers married, except what has already passed” (4:22). He ordered him, and he set her free.

Women whose cases resembled that of Kubayshah and Muḥṣin came to him ﷺ. Their marriage rights had been inherited by men other than their husbands’ sons. Heirs used to inherit the right to marry women just as they inherited property. Then God—glorified and mighty is He—revealed: “O you who believe! It is not lawful for you to inherit women against their will, nor to constrain them …” (4:19). They then set the women free.

They used to marry two sisters at once. Abū Uḥayḥah, Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayyah, married two sisters, Ṣafiyyah and Hind, daughters of al-Mughīrah ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm. Qusayy—namely Thaqīf ibn Munabbih—married Āminah and Zaynab, daughters of ʿĀmir ibn al-Ẓarb, in one marriage. Hannām ibn Salamah al-ʿĀʾishī, brother of Banū Taym Allāt ibn Thaʿlabah ibn ʿUkābah, married two sisters.

They used to cut off the right hand of a thief and crucify a highway robber.

Al-Nuʿmān ibn al-Mundhir crucified a man from Banū ʿAbd Manāf ibn Dārim, of Tamīm, who was a highway robber. In the pre-Islamic era Quraysh cut off the hands of men for theft.

Among them were Wābiṣah ibn Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm, who stole in the pre-Islamic era and had his hand cut off. Hishām said: ʿAwf ibn ʿUbayd ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm also had his hand cut off. It was cut off several times; then he stole again and was stoned until he died. Also among them were al-Khayyār ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf; ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUthmān ibn ʿAmr ibn Kaʿb ibn Saʿd ibn Taym, whose hand was cut off for stealing camels; Mudrik ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿUbayd ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm; and Mulayḥ ibn Shurayḥ ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Asad and Muqays ibn Qays ibn ʿAdī al-Sahmī, who stole the ornaments of the Kaʿbah in the pre-Islamic era. Mālik ibn ʿUmaylah ibn al-Sabbāq ibn ʿAbd al-Dār said of this, addressing Ḥumayd ibn Zuhayr, who was Mulayḥ’s cousin:

Ḥumayd wished he had been a menstruating womanon the days Mulayḥ’s fingers were severed,

the days his hand was severed from his arm—then he could not approach a rival to contend with him.

He also said to Mudrik ibn ʿAwf, who was nicknamed “Darwās”:

We left Darwās of Makhzūm lying prostrate,because of what his nails and fingers had done.

He became easy prey for the beasts,his limbs and forearms streaming blood.

The Arabs, unlike all other nations, practiced ten things.
