Five were “of the head”: rinsing the mouth, sniffing water into the nose, using the toothstick, parting the hair, and trimming the moustache. Five were “of the body”: circumcision, shaving the pubic hair, plucking the armpit hair, clipping the nails, and cleaning oneself after relieving oneself. These were particular to the Arabs, to the exclusion of other nations. Al-Nābighah al-Jaʿdī said:

Kaʿb! Would you be interested in BanūKalb, now that the root of honor is gone?

Do you know? And you area people whose moustaches are trimmed for you.

Al-Nābighah al-Dhubyānī mentioned the toothstick:

She brightens with the two foremost feathers of a dove of the thicket,whose gums are made cool, their blackness rubbed with antimony.

Ṭarafah mentioned parting the hair:

He swung a gleaming white blade,polished, intending it for the parting in my hair.

They kept their covenants and did not eat carrion. Ḥārithah ibn Aws al-Kalbī, from the pre-Islamic era, said:

I will not eat carrion as long as I live,even if my poverty leaves me destitute.

I will not undo a firm covenantuntil the layers of the grave cover me.

When God—exalted is He—brought Islam, al-Furāt ibn Ḥayyān al-ʿIjlī came as a delegate to the Prophet may ﷺ—and asked him about covenants. He said: “Islam has only made them more binding.” Then God—exalted is He—revealed to His Prophet ﷺ: “Fulfil the covenants” (5:1).

Among the practices Islam abolished were marrying one’s father’s wives; marrying two sisters at once; an heir inheriting the right to marry his kinsman’s widow just as he inherited property; giving inheritances to people other than their rightful recipients, to the exclusion of the deceased’s children; giving inheritances to males and not females; tethering a man’s she-camel backward at his grave; the baḥīrah, the sāʾibah, the waṣīlah, the ḥām, divining by arrows, and maysir.

As for the baḥīrah, the sāʾibah, the waṣīlah, and the ḥām, the reason for these practices was that the people of the tents used to set aside meat from their wealth for their gods, while the people of settled dwellings used to set aside crops for them. One practice of the people of the tents was this: when a she-camel had given birth five times, they would attend to the fifth, provided it was not male, and slit its ear.

That was the baḥīrah. Sometimes a whole herd of baḥīrah would gather; no fleece would be shorn from them, God’s name would not be mentioned when they were ridden, and nothing would be loaded on them. Their milk was for men and not women.

As for the sāʾibah, a man would set some of his property free for it, whether an animal or a person, and it would remain forbidden forever. Its benefits were for men and not women.

As for the waṣīla, when a ewe had borne seven times, they would take the seventh offspring. If it was male, they slaughtered it; if female, they left it among the sheep.

If it bore a male and a female, they said, “She has joined her brother,” and both were forbidden. Their produce, and the milk of the female, belonged to men and not to women. This is what God—mighty and majestic—says: “They say, ‘What is in the wombs of these cattle is reserved exclusively for our males and forbidden to our wives. But if it is stillborn, they are partners in it.’” (6:139)

As for the ḥām, when a male camel had sired offspring whose own offspring had grown up, so that his son had become a grandfather, they said, “This one has protected his back. Leave him alone.” He was not to be made to carry a load or ridden, and was not to be denied water or pasture. When this animal, which they had dedicated to their gods, died, men and women shared in eating it.

As for the people of settled places who cultivated the land, whenever they plowed a field or planted a grove, they drew a line down its middle and divided it in two. They said, “What lies on this side of the line is for their gods; what it produces is for God.” If anything they had assigned to God fell into the portion they had assigned to their gods, they left it there. But if anything they had assigned to their gods fell into the portion they had assigned to God—blessed be His name—they returned it. Then they let water flow through the portion they had designated for their gods. If it broke through into the portion they had designated for God—mighty and majestic—they blocked it.

If they sent water through the portion they had designated for God and it broke through into the portion they had designated for their gods, they said:

“Leave it; it needs it.” Then God—glorious and mighty—is He—revealed: “They set aside for God a share of what He has brought forth of crops and livestock, saying, ‘This is for God,’ according to their claim, ‘and this is for our associates.’ But what is for their associates does not reach God, while what is for God reaches their associates. How evil is their judgment!” (6:136)

As for the divining arrows, when the Arabs had a dispute, a marriage, or some matter they wished to pursue but did not know what to do, and the matter was not clear to them, they took16 arrows marked “Do it,” “Do not do it,” “Yes,” “No,” “Good,” “Evil,” “Slow,” and “Fast.” As for disputes, the arrows used for them were white and unmarked. They would cast them; whoever drew his arrow had the right on his side. There were two arrows for residence and travel.

They would go to the custodian of the idols. The custodian would say, “O God, bring out whichever of the two is better for so-and-so.” The person accepted whatever came out. If they doubted a man’s lineage, they cast arrows marked “genuine” and “attached.” If the “genuine” arrow came out, they affiliated him with them, even if he was an adopted son. If the arrow marked “attached” came out, they disowned him, even if his lineage was genuine. These were the arrows of divination by lots.

As for maysir, the people would gather and jointly buy a camel, then divide it into ten portions. The two shoulders were each one portion.

The back was one portion. The two “sons of the forearm,” namely the two upper forelegs, were each one portion. The “son of the neck,” namely the withers, was one portion. The malḥāʾ, which is between the hump and the base of the tail, was one portion. The rump was one portion. The two thighs were each one portion. The ṭāṭif, the hump, the neck, and the belly were divided among the portions until they were equal.

The butcher’s share, consisting of the head and the forefeet, was given to the butcher. If a bone remained after the portions had been divided, and it could not properly be divided among them, it belonged to the butcher. If one of the participants in the game took it, he was reviled for it. That bone was called al-raym. The poet said:

“You were like the bone of al-raym, which the butcher knows nothow to assign when dividing the meat.”

The badʾ is the allotted share. The participants are the people who take part in maysir; they are the notables of the tribe. Then they bring the ḥarḍa, a man who practiced religious devotion among them and had never eaten meat purchased with money. They bring the arrows, which are eleven in number. Seven of them have a share if they win; if they lose, their holders owe an amount corresponding to the share they would have received had they won. The other four serve as weights for the arrows: if they win, they receive no share, and if they lose, they owe nothing.

As for the arrows that carry a share, the first is al-fadhdh, with one notch on its shaft. If it is drawn, its holder takes one share; if it loses, its holder owes the price of one share. Next is al-tawʾam: its holder gets two shares if it wins and owes the price of two shares if it loses. Next is al-ḍarīb: its holder gets three shares if it wins and owes three if it loses. Then al-ḥils: its holder gets four if it wins and owes [four if it loses. Then al-nāfis: its holder gets five if it wins and owes] five if it loses. Then al-misbāl: its holder gets six if it wins and owes six if it loses. Then al-muʿallā: its holder gets seven if it wins and owes seven if it loses. The names of the four arrows used to weight the lots are al-safīḥ, al-manīḥ, al-muḍāʿaf, and al-muṣaddar. All the arrows are brought, and each man has stated which of the seven he has chosen. The participants are only seven; there cannot be more than that. If they are short by one or two men, and the remaining men wish to take whatever arrows are left over, a man may take one or two arrows and receive their winnings if they win, or pay on their behalf if they lose. They allow this.

This is called “completion.” Al-Nābigha al-Dhubyānī said:

“I complete the shares of my participants and grant themgifts doubled, and clothe the great bowl with dark-red meat.”

The arrows are gathered and tied together in a piece of leather [called “the rabāba”]. Then the ḥarḍa takes a fold of cloth and wraps it around his right hand17 so that he cannot feel an arrow belonging to someone he favors and give him an unfair advantage in drawing it. Then a white cloth, called the mujwal, is brought and spread before the ḥarḍa. A man called the raqīb stands over him and hands the bundle of arrows to the ḥarḍa, who has turned his face away. The ḥarḍa takes it with his right hand and slips his left hand beneath the cloth, then stirs the arrows with his left hand.

When an arrow rises out, he takes it and hands it to the raqīb. If it is one of those with no share, it is returned to the bundle. If al-muʿallā comes out, its holder takes seven shares. If al-misbāl comes out after it, its holder takes the remaining three. Those who lose pay for three shares from another camel. / The same procedure is followed for those who win and those who lose.

Sometimes they slaughter several camels, and those who win pay nothing toward the price of the camels.

The payment is borne only by those who lose. It is not lawful for the losers to eat any of that meat. If a man’s arrow wins and they wish to cast his arrow again for a wager, they do so.

Among the customs instituted by Quraysh was the blood-oath. The reason for it was that Khudāsh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Qays ibn ʿAbd Wudd ibn Naṣr ibn Mālik ibn Ḥisl ibn ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy had gone as a merchant to Yemen, accompanied by ʿĀmir—or ʿAmr—ibn ʿAlqama ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Manāf, as companion and hired man. He was a young lad. When they were partway along the road, they met a caravan and asked them for a rope for one of their needs. ʿĀmir ibn ʿAlqama tossed them a rope that belonged to Khudāsh and that they had with them. They went off with it. Khudāsh said to ʿĀmir, “You gave them my rope without my permission?” They exchanged words. Khudāsh sprang upon him and struck him on the head with a staff he was holding. He split his head open. Some say the blow landed on his kidney. ʿĀmir fell ill from it, until he feared for his life.

He passed by one of the Arab encampments, gave them his lineage, and told them, “Khudāsh ibn ʿAbd Allāh struck me with this blow, and I think it will kill me. If I die and do not return to you, inform my people, Banū ʿAbd Manāf, of this and tell them what happened to me. If I live, I will pass by you and tell you.” Soon afterward, he died from it. Khudāsh arrived in Mecca and was asked about ʿĀmir. He said, “His appointed fate overtook him.”

They believed him and suspected nothing else, until the Arab pilgrims arrived for the season.

Those members of the encampment, to whom ʿĀmir had entrusted his message, came asking where the meeting place of Banū ʿAbd Manāf was. They were directed to them. They went to them / and told them what had happened to ʿĀmir. Khudāsh was circumambulating the House, unaware of what had happened. Men of Banū ʿAbd Manāf went to the portico of Zamzam and took poles from it. Then they went to Khudāsh and beat him with them. The people cried, “God, God, Banū ʿAbd Manāf!” Khudāsh said, “What have I done to you?” They said, “You killed our companion.” He said, “By God, I did not kill him.” When he said this, they stopped and dealt fairly with one another, and submitted the matter to the judgment of al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra, who was then the eldest of Quraysh. He ruled that Khudāsh should swear, together with fifty men of Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, that he was innocent of ʿĀmir’s blood; afterward they were to pay the blood money. Banū ʿAbd Manāf accepted this. When the men of Banū ʿĀmir came forward to swear at the Kaʿba, among them Ḥuwayṭib ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn Abī Qays, his mother came and took his hand, preventing him from entering the blood-oath. They took another man in his place. After they had sworn, not a single one of those who swore lived out the year.

Most of their estates thus passed to Ḥuwayṭib. He became the owner of more estates than anyone else in Mecca.

Abū Ṭālib said:

“Over the price of a rope, may you have no father, a blowwith a staff—ropes have come, and many ropes.

Come to the judgment of Ibn Ṣakhra;he will judge between us and then deal justly,

as he used to decide the matters that arose among us,taking the worthy course and settling the affair.”

Ṣakhra was the mother of al-Walīd ibn al-Mughīra. She was the daughter of al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd Allāh, of Qasr.

It is said that they accepted the judgment of Abū Sufyān ibn Ḥarb. In this version, the verse of Abū Ṭālib is reported as:

“Come to the judgment of Ibn Ḥarb;he will judge between us and then decide.”

Among their customs was that women had no waiting period to observe after divorce.

A woman whose husband had died / would remain after him for a year. Several children were born to such women on the beds of their husbands, from their former husbands. Among them was Saʿd ibn Zayd Manāt ibn Tamīm, who married al-Nāqimiyya while she was pregnant by Muʿāwiya ibn Bakr ibn Hawāzin. She gave birth, on Saʿd’s bed, to Ṣaʿṣaʿa. When Saʿd died, his sons denied him his inheritance. He joined his own people. This is what al-Mukhabbal says:

“As Saʿd said while his son was leading him:‘I have grown old; keep the rabbits away from me, Ṣaʿṣaʿa!’”

Shurayḥ ibn al-Aḥwaṣ said:

“Laqīṭ wishes for me to meet him—is your son Ṣaʿṣaʿa ibn Saʿd a disgrace to you?”

Among them was Rabīʿa ibn ʿĀṣim ibn Juzʾ ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿĀmir ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿUqayl. His mother was from Jaʿfī and had been married to al-Fughār al-Jaʿfī, who was Hubayra ibn al-Nuʿmān. He divorced her while she was pregnant with Rabīʿa. ʿĀṣim married her, and three months later she gave birth while in his bed. Al-Fughār disputed the child with him before ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵀ and ʿUmar ruled that Rabīʿa belonged to al-Fughār, on the basis of his mother’s statement that he was from Jaʿfī. He ruled— ʿAlī ﵁ ruled that he belonged to the ʿUqaylīs because he had been born in their bed. The ʿUqaylīs maintained that she gave birth ten months after ʿĀṣim married her, while the Jaʿfīs maintained that she gave birth in less than three months. The Jaʿfī said of him:

“ʿUmar ruled among you justly; what he ruledfor you will not be undone by the passing nights.

ʿAlī disagreed with him in your case, though he isa good guide and commander in what he established among you.

So return to Jaʿfī, whether few or many;otherwise, you are truffles on barren ground.”

/ Muslim ibn Rabīʿa answered him:

“If I am of Jaʿf ibn Qays, then we arethe chiefs of Qaḥṭān, the foremost son of ʿĀmir.

And if I am not of the clan of Jaʿf, then we aremen of great deeds among the ʿUqayl ibn ʿĀmir.”

He said: Today they are the leading men of Banū ʿUqayl. Among them was Muḥammad ibn ʿUmayr ibn ʿAṭārid ibn Ḥājib ibn Zurāra. In the early days of Islam, ʿUmayr had taken captive the mother of this Muḥammad, who was pregnant by Mālik ibn ʿAwf al-Naṣrī. She gave birth to Muḥammad in ʿUmayr’s bed, so he was affiliated with him. Jarīr ibn ʿAṭiyya said of him:

“We know who your father is, O Dārim;return to your own people, the Banū Duhmān.”

This was common among Quraysh and the Arabs. Were we to pursue it exhaustively, the account would grow long.

Among their customs was that they earned money by prostituting their slave women. Some of them had a banner set up in the marketplaces of the Arabs, and men would come to them and commit fornication.

Islam abolished this and did away with it among the things it abolished. These women had many children and descendants, well known.

In the Jāhiliyya, marriage to women took four forms. A woman would be asked for in marriage and then married. A woman would have a lover who visited her; if she bore a child, she would say, “He is so-and-so’s,” and the man would then marry her. A woman would have a banner and men would visit her; if two men came to her during one period of purity, she assigned the child to one of them. Such a woman was called al-muqassima. A man might have intercourse with a woman belonging to a group of people; they would buy her child, then, if he desired the woman, he would acknowledge the child as his, buy her, and take her as a wife.

Names of the notable mukātabūn of Baṣra and Kūfa

/ Among them was al-Jaʿd ibn Qays al-Hamdānī, whose well in Kūfa is known as “Biʾr al-Jaʿd.”

It is called “the Well of al-Jaʿd.” His father entered into a manumission contract for thirty thousand. Among them was (Rāshid), the father of Muḥammad ibn Rāshid, client of Bashīr ibn Jarīr al-Bajalī. He entered into a manumission contract for thirty thousand. Among them were (Ruzayn Abū Dāwūd, Sulaymān, and Hārūn), clients of the family of Abū Ḥāzim al-Bajaliyyīn, the clan of al-Naḍr ibn Ismāʿīl. He entered into a manumission contract for forty thousand. Among them was (ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz), husband of the mother of Shuʿbah ibn al-Ḥajjāj, the jurist.

ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz was a client of Banū Baḥtar. His father, Yasār, entered into a manumission contract for thirty thousand.

Among them was (Ziyād), a client of Thaqīf, father of Yazīd ibn Ziyād. They were in Azerbaijan. He entered into a manumission contract for thirty thousand. He was a merchant. All of these men were merchants except al-Jaʿd, for he received a stipend of two hundred. Among them was (Abū Saʿīd), client of Khālid ibn ʿUrfuṭah.

He entered into a manumission contract for fifty thousand, two slave girls, and two boys to be taken as concubines. Abū Saʿīd used to sell sheep. Among them was (Rāshid), client of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Budayl ibn Warqāʾ al-Khuzāʿī. He entered into a manumission contract for seventy thousand. He used to sell enslaved people. Among them was (Ashras) ibn Jubayr al-Nakhaʿī, ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān’s agent in Iraq. ʿAbd al-Malik assigned him a stipend of two thousand. His father, Jubayr, entered into a manumission contract for fifty thousand and ten enslaved people. He had wealth to supplement his stipend. Among them was (Abū Mirdās), the grandfather of Yazīd ibn al-Fayḍ ibn Abī Mirdās. Abū Mirdās entered into a manumission contract during the time of ʿUthmān with a man from Banū Murhibah for forty thousand, thirty she-camels, and their herdsman.

Among them was (Ṭahmān) al-Wabadhī, client of al-Walīd ibn ʿUqbah. Al-Walīd entered into a manumission contract for seventy thousand. He used to lend money and sell goods on credit until the stipend was paid. Among them was (Abū al-Ḍurays), client of Banū Tamīm. His father entered into a manumission contract for / fifty thousand. He used to sell sheep. Among them was (Bazwān), father of al-Fuḍayl ibn Bazwān, client of Banū al-Bakkāʾ. He entered into a manumission contract for fifty thousand. His son Fuḍayl was among the most distinguished clients of Kufa.

Among them was (Ghazwān), client of al-Barāʾ ibn Qubayṣah ibn Abī ʿAqīl al-Thaqafī. He entered into a manumission contract for fifty thousand and a number of young slaves. He lived in Jurjān. Among them was (Sarīʿ), client of ʿAmr ibn Ḥurayth. He entered into a manumission contract for thirty thousand. His son al-Walīd ibn Sarīʿ was ʿAbd al-Malik’s agent in Iraq. Among them was (al-Muhallab) ibn Ṭalḥah the scribe. His father entered into a manumission contract with a man from Jarm for thirty thousand.

He used to sell enslaved people. Among them was (Abū Dukayn), client of the Jamaliyyīn, from Murād.

He entered into a manumission contract for one hundred thousand. He used to sell goods on credit until the stipend was paid. He sold all manner of goods: perfume, enslaved people, and riding animals. Once, he lent Murād seven hundred thousand dirhams until the stipend was paid. Among them was (Sufyān), client of Qarazah ibn Kaʿb al-Anṣārī.

He entered into a manumission contract for seventy thousand. Qarazah remitted forty thousand of it and took the remaining thirty thousand. With it he bought enslaved people and freed them. Among them was (Yazīd), father of Abū Ṣādiq al-Azdī, from Ghāmid, who used to transmit from Rabīʿah ibn Nājidh, from ʿAlī ﵁. He entered into a manumission contract for twenty thousand. Among them was (the grandfather of Jabal) ibn Yazīd al-Azdī, from Banū Ẓubyān, the scribe. He entered into a manumission contract for forty thousand.

He was with al-Manṣūr, who attached him to Abū Ayyūb al-Khawzī. Among them was (Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān), grandfather of ʿUmar ibn Ṣāliḥ ibn Abī ʿAbd al-Raḥmān. He entered into a manumission contract for fifty thousand; he was the client of a man from Khathʿam. Among them was (Basṭām), client of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Miḥriz ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā ibn ʿAbd Shams ibn ʿAbd Manāf. He entered into a manumission contract for thirty thousand. Among them was (Ṭarkhān), grandfather of Muḥammad ibn Bishr ibn Ṭarkhān the merchant, client of the family of / Abū Ḥāzim al-Aḥmasiyyīn. He entered into a manumission contract for twenty thousand.

He was a butcher and sold cattle. These are the men of Kufa who entered into manumission contracts.

Names of the men of Baṣra who entered into manumission contracts

(Sīrīn). He had been sent as a gift to ʿUmar, who gave him to Abū Ṭalḥah al-Najjārī.

He gave him to Anas ibn Mālik. Anas entered into a manumission contract with him for forty thousand. The family of Anas, however, claim that ʿUmar gave him to Anas. Sīrīn wanted to enter into a manumission contract, but Anas refused.

He said to him, “What prevents you?” He replied, “I want you to die so I can inherit from you.” Sīrīn went to ʿUmar and said, “I wanted Anas to enter into a manumission contract with me, but he refused.” ʿUmar ordered him, and Anas entered into a manumission contract with him for forty thousand. (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn al-Ḥārith, Sīrīn’s sister’s son, was a Nabataean. His father, al-Ḥārith, had been among those taken captive and had entered into a manumission contract. Sīrīn bought him.

He was freed by Sīrīn’s purchase of him, in accordance with the saying of the Messenger of God ﷺ: “Whoever owns a blood relative is free.” Thus he was Sīrīn’s client. Among them was (Farūkh), client of al-Rabīʿ ibn Ziyād al-Ḥārithī. He entered into a manumission contract for one hundred thousand and paid fifty thousand. Then he brought it to al-Rabīʿ, who said, “Farūkh, you are free, and it is yours.” He was among those taken captive in Sijistān.

Al-Farazdaq said of him:

Abū Ḥāḍir! Why have your two cloaks becomea mantle and a waist-wrap on Farūkh’s daughter?

Abū Ḥāḍir! Whoever weighs a man knows his fornication,and whoever drinks from the spout wakes up drunk.

Abū Ḥāḍir was al-Asadī. Among them was (Fīrūz) Ḥuṣayn, who was among those taken captive at Zāliq.

He is the man after whom the Fīrūz Canal in Baṣra is named. His patron, Ḥuṣayn ibn al-Ḥurr al-ʿAnbarī, entered into a manumission contract with him for fifty thousand. Then he came to him and said, “I dislike being a contract slave.

“But I will buy my freedom from you with this sum.” He took it from him and freed him. He had gone out with ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Muḥammad ibn al-Ashʿath. On the Day of al-Zāwiyah, al-Ḥajjāj’s herald called out, “Whoever brings Fīrūz’s head will receive ten thousand.” Fīrūz called out, “Whoever brings me al-Ḥajjāj’s head will receive one hundred thousand.” Al-Ḥajjāj became alarmed for himself and no longer trusted his confidants. Later, al-Ḥajjāj captured Fīrūz and demanded that he show him his wealth. He replied, “I have property owed to me by people and deposits in their keeping. Take me out with my guards so I can collect them.” He took him out with his guards. Fīrūz called out, “People! Whoever knows me knows me; whoever does not, I am Fīrūz, client of Ḥuṣayn ibn al-Ḥurr al-ʿAnbarī.

“If anyone owes me a deposit or owes me money, then he is absolved of all of it, absolved completely.”

Then he returned to al-Ḥajjāj, who killed him. He had many stories. One was that two Arabs, who were cousins, boasted against one another. One of them had a mother who was a concubine, and the other, the son of an Arab woman, taunted him on account of his mother.

Fīrūz passed by them at that mountain pass. The son of the concubine said, “This is my maternal uncle,” pointing to Fīrūz. Fīrūz saw him point at him and asked, “What are you two doing?” The man replied,

“This is my cousin. He taunted me because his mother is Arab, and I boasted of you because my mother is Persian.”

Fīrūz ordered one of his attendants to bring him ten thousand dirhams and a bundle of Yemeni ʿaṣb cloth. The attendant brought them while Fīrūz stood there. He handed them to the man and said, “Nephew, tell your cousin’s maternal uncle to bring him something like this.” Among them was (Abū Nāfiʿ), who had been a slave of a son of ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿĀmir ibn Kurayz. His patron was miserly.

He asked to enter into a manumission contract. His patron said, “I will enter into a contract with you for one hundred thousand.” Abū Nāfiʿ went to a man from the family of Maʿmar and said, “I have money, and I dislike my patron.

“Buy me, and enter into a contract with me for whatever / you wish.” He bought him for ten thousand and entered into a manumission contract with him for fifty thousand. His clientage passed to him. Among them was (Abū al-Qāsim) al-Barrād, client of Banū al-Hujaym. He entered into a manumission contract with his patron for one hundred thousand dirhams and a large bowl every day. His wife said, “Abū al-Qāsim, we still have not been freed.” He asked, “How so?” She said,

“Because my daughters grind the spices for those Bedouins every day.” He said to Banū al-Hujaym, “Sons of al-Hujaym, sell me this bowl.” They replied, “We dislike being cut off from your house.” He said, “By God, that is what I want.” They asked, “What will you give us for it?” He said, “I will give you thirty thousand for it.” He was freed for a total of one hundred and thirty thousand. He was non-Arab in speech.

One of his sons said to another, “Why don’t you hire this old man a tutor to correct his speech?”

They approached Abū al-ʿArbaḍ al-Sulamī, who had been the tutor to the sons of Ziyād ibn Abīhi, and said to him, “This is no trouble for you. We will pay you one hundred dirhams each month and cover your family’s expenses, and you can sit with this old man. Whenever an objectionable word passes his lips, tell him and forbid him to say it. You will sit on a prayer mat, and the old man on another.” He agreed. Whenever he said some such thing, Abū al-ʿArbaḍ would say to him, “Abū al-Qāsim, if only you had said such-and-such.” Then a boy passed by carrying figs on a platter. The old man said, “Boy, how is clay sold?” Abū al-ʿArbaḍ fell silent. Then he said, “Donkeys are coming from the river carrying clay.” The old man said, “Boy, where did you bring these figs from?” Abū al-ʿArbaḍ said:

“By God, I have made them both speak correctly! What if you made the clay figs and the figs clay?” Abū al-Qāsim said, “Woe to you, Abū al-ʿArbaḍ! How clever you are!” He replied, “How clever you are! So-and-so’s mother is divorced three times if I sit with you again after today, ever.” Among them was (Sālim) al-Mantūf. His father entered into a manumission contract with his patron / from Banū Qays ibn Thaʿlabah for fifty thousand. Among them was (al-Qāsim) ibn Muslim, client of Banū Yashkur. His father entered into a manumission contract with his patron for thirty thousand. Al-Qāsim had a vigor, bearing, and command of transmitted reports unlike those of the sons of contract slaves. Among them was the jurist (Maymūn) ibn Mihrān. He entered into a manumission contract with his patrons from Banū Naṣr ibn Muʿāwiyah.

The Arabs’ men of fidelity

(Awfā) ibn Miṭar al-Māzinī. A man had been his neighbor, and the man had a wife with him. Qays, his brother, was taken with her, but could not reach her while her husband was there. He treacherously killed her husband. When Awfā heard of it, he killed his brother Qays in retaliation for his neighbor. He said:

Daughter of al-ʿUmrī! I have worn no garment of a fornicator,nor do I cloak myself in treachery.

I acted against Qays on account of his neighbor’s protection,to safeguard my honor; my honor is safeguarded.

(Al-Ḥārith) ibn ʿAbbād. An instance of his fidelity was that, on the Day of Qaḍḍah, he captured ʿAdī ibn Rabīʿah, the brother of Muhalhil, without knowing who he was. He said to him, “Show me ʿAdī.” ʿAdī said to him, “If I show him to you, will I be safe?” He gave him his word. ʿAdī then said, “I am ʿAdī.” So he released him. This is what al-Kumayt meant:

How could al-Samawʾal match such fidelity,when tribulations had brought his resolve to its limit,

on the day he bought honor at the price of bereavement?The grief-stricken man may indeed keep his pledge. nor Ibn Muḥallim, nor Abū Bujayr— their fidelity is a marvel indeed.

Abū Bujayr is al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbbād.

(Al-Samawʾal) ibn Ḥayyā ibn ʿĀdiyā al-Ghassānī. An instance of his fidelity was that when Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Ḥujr set out for Caesar, he entrusted some coats of mail to him.

When / Imruʾ al-Qays died, one of the kings of Syria raided him. He shut the fortress against the king.

The king seized a son of his who was outside the fortress and called to him, “Samawʾal! Hand over Imruʾ al-Qays’s coats of mail, or I will kill your son.” He consulted his womenfolk and household. They all advised him to hand over the coats of mail and save his son. Samawʾal appeared before the king and said, “I have no way to hand over the coats of mail. Do as you wish.”

The king slaughtered his son as Samawʾal watched, then withdrew. When the pilgrimage season came, Samawʾal carried the coats of mail and delivered them to Imruʾ al-Qays’s heirs. Al-Aʿshā said:

Neighbor of Ḥayyā, whoever came under his protectionfound him more faithful and generous than the neighbor of Ibn ʿAmmār.

He offered him two harsh choices, and he said to him,“Whatever you say, I will hear and obey.”

He hesitated only briefly, then said to him,“Slaughter your sacrifice; I will protect my neighbor.”

(ʿAwf) ibn Muḥallim al-Shaybānī. An instance of his fidelity was that Marwān ibn Zinbāʿ al-ʿAbsī had incurred the enmity of ʿAmr ibn Hind al-Lakhmī, who had resolved not to grant him safe-conduct until he placed his hand in his own. Marwān raided Bakr ibn Wāʾil, and a man from Taym Allāt ibn Thaʿlabah ibn ʿUkābah captured him. The man was not powerful. Marwān feared that ʿAmr ibn Hind would learn where he was and send someone to seize him. He heard the mother of his captor say to her son, “It is as though you have captured Marwān al-Quraẓ.” Marwān said to her, “What do you expect from Marwān?” She replied, “I expect a hundred camels from him.”

He said, “I am Marwān, and they are yours, on condition that you deliver me to ʿAwf ibn Muḥallim.” She said, “Who will guarantee that you keep your word?” He picked up a stick from the ground and said, “This will guarantee it for you.”

Her son carried him until he came to ʿAwf’s tent, but he did not find him there. / His daughter Khumāʿah granted Marwān protection.

ʿAmr ibn Hind learned where he was and sent for him. ʿAwf refused to hand him over unless he gave him safe-conduct. He said, “I have sworn an oath not to grant him safe-conduct until he places his hand in mine.”

ʿAwf said, “I will fulfill your oath by placing my hand between yours and his.”

ʿAmr said, “Agreed.” Marwān was given safe-conduct. ʿAmr ibn Hind said, “No free man in ʿAwf’s valley.” It became a proverb.

(Marwān) ibn Zanbāʿ. One instance of his fidelity was that he gave as a pledge, for one hundred camels, the stick he had taken from the ground, and he fulfilled the pledge with them.

And (ʿUmayr) ibn Sulmā al-Ḥanafī. One instance of his fidelity was that a man of Banū ʿĀmir ibn Kilāb had sought ʿUmayr ibn Sulmā’s protection, and the man had with him a beautiful woman.

Qarīn, ʿUmayr’s brother, used to converse with her until word of this reached her husband. He forbade her to do so.

She feared him and stopped. When Qarīn saw this, he leapt upon her husband and killed him, while ʿUmayr was away. The slain man’s brother went to the grave of Sulmā, sought refuge there, and said:

When you seek protection in al-Yamāmah, seek protectionfrom Zayd ibn Yarbuʿ and the family of Mujammaʿ.

You came to Sulmā and sought refuge at his grave,and the brother of al-Zamānah seeks refuge with al-Amnaʿ.

Qarīn! If you had seen my horsemenat ʿAmāyatayn, along the sides of Ḍalfāʿ,

you would have thought of fidelity; you would not have beena treacherous woman, with a finger stained by betrayal.

You would have known that when you took refuge with them,your hands would eagerly reach out to Āl and Ḥālim would strike.

ʿUmayr ibn Sulmā arrived and seized his brother. When this reached the leading men of Banū Ḥanīfah, they came to him and spoke with him. He said: “By God, I will not release him unless my protected neighbor pardons him.” They went to the slain man’s brother and offered him double the blood-money, but he refused. His mother, who was Qarīn’s mother, spoke to ʿUmayr.

He refused. Then he brought his brother out, crossed the valley of al-Yamāmah, tied him to a palm tree, and said: “Since you refuse either to pardon him or to accept the blood-money, wait until I cross the valley on my way back; then do with him as you will, but do not let me see you.” The man killed him. His mother said:

He makes excuses for which there is no excuse;whoever kills his brother has done wrong.

(Abū Ḥanbal) Jāriyah ibn Murr al-Ṭāʾī, then al-Thaʿlabī. One instance of his fidelity was that Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Ḥujr al-Kindī was the protected neighbor of ʿĀmir ibn Juwayn al-Ṭāʾī, then al-Jarmī. ʿĀmir kissed Imruʾ al-Qays’s wife. She informed him of this. He set out, intending to seek protection from Jāriyah ibn Mur, but did not find him; instead, he met his son. His son said to him:

“I will protect you from everyone except Abū Ḥanbal.” He accepted this and moved in with him. When Abū Ḥanbal arrived, he saw how much property Imruʾ al-Qays had. His son told him what he had promised regarding protection. He consulted his wives about taking the property. All of them advised him to do so, saying: “He has no claim of protection upon you.” Abū Ḥanbal went out until he reached the valley and called out: “Abū Ḥanbal is treacherous!” The echo from the mountain answered him with the same words. Then he called out: “Abū Ḥanbal is faithful!” The echo answered him with those words. He said: “This is better than that.” Then he went to his dwelling, milked a young ewe from his flock, drank its milk, rubbed his belly, and said: “Would I betray him when the milk of a young ewe has sufficed me?” He kept faith with Imruʾ al-Qays and said:

I have sworn that I will not betray over young she-camels,even if I were tested with mothers of yearlings.

For betrayal among peoples is a disgrace,and a free man is content with a leg.

Then he bound Imruʾ al-Qays to him by oath. Imruʾ al-Qays told him that ʿĀmir ibn Juwayn had kissed his wife. Abū Ḥanbal rode out with his household until he reached ʿĀmir ibn Juwayn’s dwelling, with Imruʾ al-Qays beside him. He said to him: “Kiss his wife as he kissed your wife.” He did so.

(al-Muʿallā) al-Ṭāʾī, one of the Banū Taym of Jadīlah, who today are called “Lamps of the Darkness.” Al-Mundhir was pursuing Imruʾ al-Qays … He sought refuge with al-Muʿallā, who granted him protection. Al-Muʿallā left to attend to one of his affairs. Al-Mundhir learned where Imruʾ al-Qays was and rode until he reached al-Muʿallā’s son. Al-Muʿallā’s son took Imruʾ al-Qays and brought him into a tent containing the women of his household, and denied that he was there. Al-Mundhir searched the dwellings of al-Muʿallā until he reached the tent where Imruʾ al-Qays was. He said to him: “The women of al-Muʿallā’s household are in there, and you will not reach them.”

He called out to his people, and they prevented him. Imruʾ al-Qays said:

When I alighted with al-Muʿallā,I alighted with the lofty heights of Shimām.

Neither the king of Iraq nor the king of Syriahas power over al-Muʿallā.

The sons of Taym, lamps of the darkness,have comforted the heart of Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Ḥujr.

(ʿUṣaymah) ibn Khālid ibn Sinān ibn Minqar. Al-Nuʿmān grew angry with Banū ʿĀmir ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿah, killed some of them, and scattered the rest. ʿUṣaymah gave them refuge and protection. Al-Nuʿmān sent him a message: “Send me my slaves.” He refused and called his people with their rallying cry, “Kawthar.” Al-Nuʿmān came, and ʿUṣaymah went out to meet him. He thrust his spear toward the bridge of the king’s horse’s nose and said to him: “Turn back, you farting king! If I wished to put it anywhere other than this place, I would have done so.” Then ʿUṣaymah clothed Banū ʿĀmir and escorted them to a place of safety. He said of this:

We protected the chiefs of ʿĀmir from al-Nuʿmānwith our swords in the dread-filled encounter.

Gently, may you never be cursed! Do not expect my pledge to fail;I have no desire to save myself at the expense of my protected neighbor.

(Badr) ibn Ḥamrāʾ al-Ḍabbī. A year of famine struck the people. The leading men of Banū Taym Allāh ibn Thaʿlabah—Mālik, ʿĀmir, and Ḥulayḥah—came to him and sought his protection. He remained faithful to them until they prospered and returned. Some of them sought the protection of Musāwir, who proceeded to violate their women.

Badr said:

I kept a fidelity such as people had never seenat Tiʿshār, when you yearned for the leading men.

Whoever is made an object of violationthrough his neighbor’s wife—I am a man who keeps away from my neighbor’s wife.

The Ṭalḥahs renowned for generosity

(Ṭalḥah) ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUthmān al-Taymī, the Companion of the Messenger of God ﷺ. He was Ṭalḥah al-Fayyāḍ and Ṭalḥah al-Khayr.

(Ṭalḥah) ibn ʿUmar ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Maʿmar al-Taymī. He was Ṭalḥah al-Jūd.

(Ṭalḥah) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Abī Bakr al-Ṣiddīq. He was Ṭalḥah al-Darāhim.

(Ṭalḥah) ibn al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib. He was Ṭalḥah al-Khubz. He left no descendants.

(Ṭalḥah) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAwf al-Zuhrī, the son of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf’s brother. He was Ṭalḥah al-Nadā.

(Ṭalḥah) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khalaf al-Khuzāʿī. He was Ṭalḥah al-Ṭalḥāt.

The names of the Companions of the Cave

Ibn al-Kalbī said that they were: Maksamlinā, Yamlīkhā, and Marṭūlis;

Dhūnuwānis, Diyūdunis, Sārabiyūnis, Kashfū Ṭadbiyūs,

and Baṭīnūsūs.

The name of the king from whom they fled was (Daqyānūs), and the name of the king in whose time they appeared was (Tīdīsūs). Their messenger was (Yamlīkhā). The names of the two judges to whom Yamlīkhā was brought were (Mārnūs) and (Asṭūs). The name of the dog was (Qiṭmīr); it was a greyhound. The name of the shepherd was (Dalis). The name of the city was (Ephesus). The name of the district from which they came was (Anūs). The name of the cave was (Anjilūs). They turned over twice a year, once in winter and once in summer. The entrance of the cave faced Banāt Naʿsh.

Names of those who entered Islam while each man had ten wives; all of them were from Thaqīf

(Masʿūd) ibn Muʿattib.

(Masʿūd) ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿUmayr.

(ʿUrwah) ibn Masʿūd.

(Sufyān) ibn ʿAbd Allāh.

(Ghaylān) ibn Salamah.

(Abū ʿAqīl) Masʿūd ibn ʿĀmir ibn Muʿattib. All of them were from Thaqīf. When they entered Islam, Ghaylān, Sufyān, and Abū ʿAqīl each divorced six wives and kept four each. ʿUrwah died a Muslim and had not been ordered to divorce some of his wives.

Names of the nine men who spread corruption in the land among the people of Ṣāliḥ ﵇—(Miṣdaʿ) ibn Dahr. (Qudār) ibn Sālif. (Huraym).

(Ṣuʾāb). (Dāb). (Riʾāb). (Daʿmī). (Harmā).

(Raʿīn) ibn ʿAmr.

Those who led the people in prayer during the siege of ʿUthmān ﵀

The people were led in prayer by (ʿUbayd Allāh) ibn ʿAdī ibn al-Khiyār ibn ʿAdī ibn Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf. The pilgrimage season was conducted, by order of ʿUthmān, by (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn ʿAbbās.

Names of the Lakhmid kings of al-Ḥīrah and others

The first of them was (ʿAmr) ibn ʿAdī ibn Naṣr ibn Rabīʿah ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Mālik ibn ʿAmr ibn Numārah ibn Lakhm. He was the first of the Arab kings to make al-Ḥīrah a residence. He ruled for one hundred and eighteen years. Then his son (Imruʾ al-Qays) al-Badīʾ, the First, ruled for one hundred and fourteen years. Then his son (ʿAmr) ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ruled for thirty years. Then (Aws) ibn Qallām ibn Baṭīnā ibn Jumayhir ibn Liḥyān—said also to be of Banū Liḥyān: al-Ḥārith ibn Kaʿb. He was one of the ʿAmālīq, of Banū Fārān ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAmālīq. Jaḥjabā ibn ʿAtīk, of Lakhm, rose against him and killed him. Aws’s reign lasted five years. Then (Imruʾ al-Qays) al-Badīʾ, who was Muḥarriq the First—the first to punish by fire—son of ʿAmr ibn Imruʾ al-Qays al-Badīʾ, ruled for twenty-five years. Then his son (al-Nuʿmān) ibn Imruʾ al-Qays al-Badīʾ ruled. He was the son of al-Shaqīqah, daughter of Abū Rabīʿah ibn Dhuhl ibn Shaybān. He was the horseman of Ḥalīmah. He was the one who commissioned the Khawarnaq, which he built for Bahrām Jūr ibn Yazdegerd. Bahrām Jūr had been raised in his care. He was the one who gave up his kingship and wandered the earth as a devotee. Al-Nuʿmān’s reign lasted thirty years, until he gave up his kingship. Then his son (al-Mundhir) ibn al-Nuʿmān ruled after him for forty-four years. Then his son (al-Aswad) ibn al-Mundhir ruled for twenty years. Then his brother (al-Mundhir) ibn al-Mundhir ruled for seven years. Then his nephew (al-Nuʿmān) ibn al-Aswad ruled for four years. Then (Abū Yaʿfur) ibn ʿAlqamah ibn Mālik ibn ʿAdī ibn al-Dhumayl ibn Thawr ibn Asas ibn Rabbī ibn Numārah ibn Lakhm ruled for three years. Then (al-Mundhir) ibn Imruʾ al-Qays, known as Dhū al-Qarnayn, ruled. His mother was Māʾ al-Samāʾ, namely Māwiyyah, daughter of ʿAwf ibn Jusham ibn Hilāl ibn Rabīʿah ibn Zayd Manāt, of al-Nimr ibn Qāsiṭ. He ruled for forty-nine years. Then (ʿAmr) ibn al-Mundhir ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ruled. ʿAmr was the Stone-Crusher. He ruled for sixteen years.

In the eighth year and eighth month of this ʿAmr’s reign, the Messenger of God ﷺ—was born. This was the Year of the Elephant. Then his brother (Qābūs) ibn al-Mundhir ruled for four years. Eight months into Qābūs’s reign, ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib ibn Hāshim died. Then (al-Suharb) the Persian ruled for one year. Then (al-Mundhir) ibn al-Mundhir ruled for four years. Then (Abū Qābūs) al-Nuʿmān ibn al-Mundhir ibn al-Mundhir ruled for twenty-two years. In the third year and eighth month of his reign, the Greater Fijār—the Fijār of al-Barāḍ—took place. This was when the Messenger of God ﷺ—had completed twenty years of age. Eighteen years and eight months into this al-Nuʿmān’s reign, the Kaʿbah was built. This was in the eleventh year of the reign of Khosrow Parvīz ibn Hurmuz. Then (Iyās) ibn Qabīṣah al-Ṭāʾī ruled, with al-Nakhīrajān alongside him, for nine years. One year and eight months into Iyās ibn Qabīṣah’s reign, God—mighty and exalted is He—sent Muḥammad ﷺ—as a messenger. This was in the sixteenth year of the reign of Parvīz ibn Khosrow. The Battle of Dhū Qār, in which the Arabs took their revenge upon the Persians, took place during Iyās ibn Qabīṣah’s reign, while the Messenger of God ﷺ—was in Mecca, before he emigrated. Then (Āzādhbah) the Persian was appointed ruler and reigned for seventeen years. The Messenger of God ﷺ—passed into the mercy and good pleasure of God when fifteen years and eight months of Āzādhbah’s rule had elapsed. Abū Bakr ﵀—assumed charge of the community. Then (al-Gharūr), namely al-Mundhir ibn al-Nuʿmān, ruled Ibn al-Mundhir, who was killed in Baḥrayn on the day of Juʾāthā. His reign, together with the reigns of others, lasted until Khālid ibn al-Walīd came to al-Ḥīrah: eight months. Al-Ḥīrah had been inhabited for five hundred and some thirty years, until al-Kūfah was founded and the Muslims settled there. All the kings of the house of Naṣr and those appointed in succession from among the ʿIbād and Persians numbered twenty. The total period of their rule was five hundred and twenty-two years and eight months.

The names of those who united the Persian kingdom after they had been divided into separate groups, and the number of years they ruled

First among them was Ardashīr ibn Bābakān, for fourteen years and ten months. Then Sābūr ibn Ardashīr ruled for thirty-two years. Then Hurmuz ibn Sābūr ibn Ardashīr ruled for one year and ten months. Then Bahrām ibn Sābūr ruled for seven years. Then his son Bahrām ibn Bahrām ruled for thirteen years. Then Narsī ibn Sābūr ruled for seven years. Then his son Hurmuz ibn Narsī ruled for thirteen years. Then his son Sābūr ibn Hurmuz ibn Narsī, Sābūr Dhū al-Aktāf, ruled for seventy-two years. Then his brother Ardashīr ibn Hurmuz ruled for four years. Then, after him, Ardashīr ibn Hurmuz’s son Sābūr ibn Ardashīr ruled. His father had died while his mother was pregnant with him, so the crown was placed on her belly. He ruled for thirty-two years. Then his son Bahrām ibn Sābūr ruled for eleven years. Then his son Yazdajird ibn Bahrām, who is called “Yazdajird the Harsh,” ruled for twelve years.

/ It is said that he was given this name because, when he became king, he said, “If a guilty person is brought before me, I will punish him with a punishment such as no one before me has inflicted.” People feared him, and so they were spared punishment by him. Then his son Bahrām ruled for sixteen years. Then his brother Fīrūz ibn Yazdajird ruled for twenty-seven years and one day. Then his brother Balāsh ibn Yazdajird ruled for fewer than three years. Then Qubādh ibn Fīrūz ruled for forty-three years. In his time, sectarian beliefs appeared, the Mazdakites emerged, and the Abyssinians gained control of the land of Yemen. Then his son Anūshirwān ibn Qubādh ruled for forty-nine years—or, according to another account, seven years—and seven months and some days. In the forty-second year of his reign, the Prophet ﷺ was born. Then Hurmuz [ibn] Anūshirwān ruled after him for twelve years. Then Kisrā Abrūwīz ibn Hurmuz ruled for thirty-eight years. At the beginning of the twentieth year of his reign, God, Glorious and Exalted, sent Muḥammad as a messenger. Then he emigrated to Medina in the thirty-third year of Kisrā’s reign. Then his son Shīrūyah rose against Kisrā Abrūwīz, killed him, and killed his two brothers. His reign lasted eight months. During his reign, plague struck the Persian nobles and grandees; they died, and Shīrūyah died of it as well. Then his son Ardashīr ibn Shīrūyah ruled. He was a boy, and ruled for twenty years. Shahrbarāz treacherously killed him. His reign fell in the year eleven of the Hijrah. Then Shahrbarāz ruled after him for thirty-eight days. Then his nephew, a son of Kisrā, called Kisrā ibn Qubādh ibn Hurmuz, ruled for ten months. Then he was killed. Then a man descended from Ardashīr, named Fīrūz, ruled for fifty days. They claim that Ādhar Mīdhukht had him killed by a plot. Then Ādhar Mīdhukht ruled for four months, / then died. Then a young son of Kisrā, called Farrukhzādh Khusraw, ruled for some months and days. Then he died. Altogether, eight persons ruled after Kisrā and before Yazdajird ibn Shahriyār ibn Kisrā became king; they ruled for four and a half years. Then Yazdajird ibn Shahriyār ibn Kisrā ruled. His reign began in the year eleven of the Hijrah, and he ruled for twenty years. He was killed at Marw during the caliphate of ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān ﵀. ʿUthmān’s governor of Khurāsān was ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿĀmir ibn Kurayz ibn Rabīʿah ibn Ḥabīb ibn ʿAbd Shams.

The names of the kings of Ḥimyar

According to Hishām ibn al-Kalbī, Qaḥṭān ruled for two hundred years. Then Yaʿrub ibn Qaḥṭān ruled for two hundred years. Then Yashjub ibn Yaʿrub. Then Sabaʾ, who was ʿAbd Shams. Then Ḥimyar ibn Sabaʾ. Then Naṣr ibn Sabaʾ. Then al-Ḥārith ibn Shaddād—also said to be ibn Shaddad—ibn al-Malṭāṭ ibn ʿAmr ibn Dhū al-Sinn ibn al-Ṣawwār ibn ʿAbd Shams ibn Wāʾil ibn al-Ghawth ibn Jīdān ibn Qaṭan ibn Zuhayr ibn ʿArīb ibn Ayman ibn al-Humaysʿ ibn Ḥimyar ibn Sabaʾ. Al-Ḥārith was al-Rāʾish. He was the first to be called Tubbaʿ. He was called “king of kings.” Then came Shaddād ibn ʿĀd and Shadīd ibn ʿĀd. Shadīd died, and Shaddād remained. He built Iram of the Pillars. Then al-Haddād ibn al-Rāʾish ruled briefly. Then Abrahah ibn al-Rāʾish ruled. He was Dhū al-Manār, because he was the first to build a beacon. Then his son al-ʿAbd ibn Abrahah Dhū al-Adhʿār. Then Afrīqis ibn Qays ibn Ṣayfī ibn Sabaʾ ibn Kaʿb ibn Zayd ibn Sahl ibn ʿAmr ibn Qays ibn Muʿāwiyah ibn Jusham ibn ʿAbd Shams ibn Wāʾil ibn al-Ghawth ibn Qaṭan ibn ʿArīb ibn al-Humaysʿ ibn Ḥimyar ruled. Africa was named after him. He reached Tangier and brought the Berbers there; their homelands had been in Palestine.

He reached the Valley of the Ants and found the ant18 carrying a horse and its rider. He was unable to cross, so he turned back, set up an idol there, and wrote on its breast in the Musnad script: “There is no route beyond this, nor any good to be sought. Let no one attempt the crossing, lest he perish.”

Then his brother Mālik ibn Qays ibn Ṣayfī, a descendant of Sabaʾ, ruled. He settled in Ṣanʿāʾ and Ghumdān, which had existed long before him. Then came al-Ḥārith ibn Mālik ibn Qays ibn Ṣayfī. Then Mayyās ibn Yaʿrub. Then ʿUrwah ibn Muʿāwiyah ibn Mayyās. Then Māʾ al-Samāʾ ibn ʿUrwah. Then Sharāḥbīl ibn Yaḥṣub.

Then al-Hamāl ibn al-Hamāl ibn al-Muthallam, from Jīham. Then al-Ṣaʿb ibn Qurayn ibn al-Hamāl. It is said that he is Dhū al-Qarnayn, whom God mentions in His Book. God knows best.

Labīd said:

Al-Ṣaʿb Dhū al-Qarnayn has become a corpse,

Lying in al-Ḥanw, in a grave forever still.

Then Zayd ibn Hamāl ruled; he is the one whom God, Glorious and Exalted, called Tubbaʿ. God knows best. Then Bārān Yahanʿim ruled. Then al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAmr ibn Yaʿfur ibn al-ʿAbd Dhū al-Adhʿār. He was called Nāshir Yanʿum. He campaigned and reached the Valley of Sand but found no crossing, so he stayed there. When the Sabbath day came, the sand observed the Sabbath—that is, it stopped. A man of his household, called ʿAmr ibn Yazīd ibn Yaʿfur, crossed with a number of his companions. They did not return. He set up an idol and wrote on its breast:

“This idol was made by the Ḥimyarite king Nāshir Yanʿum.”

ʿAlqamah ibn Yazīd, brother of the man who crossed the Valley of Sand, said of him:

O Nāshir, king of kings! You have undertaken a task

That towers above the great kings’ farthest journeys.

You brought us to a vile valley, its channel

Sand that we see piled up like mountains.

/ Then Zuhayr ibn ʿAbd Shams ruled. He used to19 the women of Ḥimyar, until Yalmaqah arose—she was Bilqīs, daughter of al-Yashraḥ ibn Dhū Jadan ibn Yashraḥ ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Qays ibn Ṣayfī. Her mother was Rawāḥah, daughter of al-Sikkin, king of the jinn. She was one of the wisest women. She killed Zuhayr and became queen.

When she sat, a thousand qēls stood above her head. She was the woman associated with the hoopoe.

There is a story about her killing Zuhayr. Sulaymān ibn Dāwūd ﵉, married her.

Then al-Ḥārith ibn Sharāḥbīl Dhū Jadan ruled. Then Dhū Rudāʿ Yahanʿim Dhū al-Malāḥī ruled. Malik Yakrub marched against him and killed him. Then Malik Yakrub ruled; he was the father of Mālik, of whom al-Aʿshā said:

Time betrayed Abū Mālik,

And what righteous man has it not betrayed?

Then Asʿad ruled. He was Tubbaʿ the Younger, Tubān ibn Malik Yakrub ibn Qays ibn Zayd ibn ʿAmr Hūdh and al-Adhʿār ibn Abrahah Dhū al-Manār ibn al-Rāʾish ibn Qays ibn Ṣayfī ibn Sabaʾ. He was Abū Karib. He was the one who brought the Jews from the land of the Ḥijāz to Yemen. Then his son Ḥassān, Dhū Maʿāhir, ruled. Then his brother ʿAmr ibn Tubbaʿ killed him at Furḍat Nuʿm, on the road to al-Raqqah.

He ruled after him. He was Mūthibān. Then Lakhniʿah Yanūf rose against him. He had no longstanding claim and was not of the royal house. He was Dhū Shanātir. He killed ʿAmr.

Dhū Shanātir ruled and was a wicked sodomite. Then Zuraʿah Dhū Nuwās rose against him, killed him, and ruled after him. Then he became a Jew, professed Judaism, and summoned the people to it. He accepted no one among them except on the condition that they become Jews or be killed. He took the name Yūsuf.

He was the man of the Trench. He dug trenches at Najrān and lit fires in them.

He summoned its people to Judaism. They followed an inherited remnant of the religion of Jesus ﵇. When they refused him, he cast them into the fire. He burned the Gospel and killed some twenty thousand of them by the sword, apart from those he burned in the fire and those of them whom he mutilated.

It was because of him that the Abyssinians came to Yemen and took control of it, on account of what he had done to the Christians. When Dhū Nuwās fought the Abyssinians and they routed his army, he rode his horse into the sea and drowned, fearing capture. That was the last anyone heard of him.

The kings of Kindah

When Tubbaʿ Abū Karib set out intending to go to Iraq, he stopped in the land of Maʿadd and appointed Ḥujr ibn ʿAmr, Ākil al-Murār, over them. He governed them well. They agreed that, if Ḥujr died, they would make his son king over them after him. He continued as king until he grew senile. His sons included ʿAmr and Muʿāwiyah, who was al-Jawn. When Ḥujr died, his son ʿAmr succeeded him. He was called al-Maqṣūr because his rule was confined to his father’s kingdom. Ḥujr’s son Muʿāwiyah al-Jawn ruled over al-Yamāmah.

When ʿAmr died, his son al-Ḥārith ruled after him. He was a powerful king, far-ranging in his campaigns. He often raided the Banū Naṣr, the kings of al-Ḥīrah. He had brought one of their kings under his authority. His rule reached Ṣurāt Jāmāsb, which is near Qaṣr Ibn Hubayrah. He remained thus until Imruʾ al-Qays ibn al-Mundhir ibn ʿAmr ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAdī ibn Naṣr died in al-Ḥīrah. His son al-Mundhir ibn Imruʾ al-Qays succeeded him. Al-Ḥārith rose against him, summoned the Arabs, and they pledged allegiance to him. Fīrūz ibn Yazdajird ibn Bahrām ibn Yazdajird ibn Sābūr was the one who had made Imruʾ al-Qays ibn al-Mundhir king. Fīrūz died, and Qubādh the Heretic, son of Fīrūz, ruled after him.

Al-Mundhir ibn Imruʾ al-Qays the Lakhmid invited him to adopt heresy, but he refused.

Al-Ḥārith accepted it, so Qubādh made him king and expelled al-Mundhir. When Qubādh died, Anūshirwān restored the kingdom to al-Mundhir. Qubādh had made an agreement with al-Ḥārith that al-Ḥārith would have the lands beyond al-Ṣurāt, as far as the land of the Arab tent-dwellers and townspeople. Al-Ḥārith had distributed his sons among Maʿadd: he made Ḥujr king over the Banū Asad ibn Khuzaymah. And he made Sharāḥbīl king over Tamīm and al-Ribāb. He made Salamah king over Bakr and Taghlib. He made Maʿdīkarib, who was Ghulafāʾ, king over Qays and Kinānah. When al-Ḥārith died, each of his sons secured his own kingdom, and their power grew strong. As for the Banū Asad, they killed their king Ḥujr, the father of the poet Imruʾ al-Qays. Sharāḥbīl and Salamah rose against one another and fought. Sharāḥbīl was killed by Abū Ḥanash ʿUṣm ibn al-Nuʿmān al-Taghlibī, who was with Salamah ibn al-Ḥārith. Salamah ibn al-Ḥārith was struck by paralysis and died.

Maʿdīkarib ibn al-Ḥārith became deranged with grief over his brother Sharāḥbīl. He wandered aimlessly and died. The kingdom of Kindah fell apart. Then ʿAmr Aqḥal ibn Abī Karib ibn Qays ibn Salamah ibn al-Ḥārith, the king, stood up and said, “People of Kindah! You have now found yourselves without a settled home. Your nobles are gone, and your kingdom has fallen apart.

“I do not feel secure about the Arabs’ intentions toward you. So join your people.” They set out and joined them in Ḥaḍramawt, where they have remained to this day.

The cause of the Ghassānids’ rule

The reason the Ghassānids came to rule with the Romans was that the Ḍajāʿim—namely the Banū Ḍajʿam ibn Ḥamāṭah ibn Saʿd ibn Sulayḥ ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥāf ibn Quḍāʿah—were kings in Syria before the Ghassānids arrived. Sulayḥ collected tribute for the Romans from anyone who settled in their territory, whether from Muḍar or elsewhere. Then the Ghassānids came with a great host, intending to go to Syria. until they came down upon them. Sulayḥ said to them, “Either you agree to pay the kharāj, or we will fight you.”

They refused, so Sulayḥ fought them, and they defeated Ghassān. The chief of Ghassān at that time was Thaʿlaba ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Mujālid ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAdī ibn ʿAmr ibn Māzin ibn al-Azd.

Ghassān agreed to pay them the kharāj. They collected from them, each year, one dinar, one and a half dinars, or two dinars per head, according to their means. They continued collecting from them for as long as God willed, until Jidhʿ ibn ʿAmr al-Ghassānī killed Sulayḥ’s tax collector, who was Sabīṭ ibn al-Mundhir ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf ibn Ḍajʿam ibn Ḥamāṭa. Sulayḥ called out its battle cry, and Ghassān called out its battle cry. They met at a place called al-Muḥaffaf. Ghassān routed them. The king of the Romans feared they might join the Persians against him.

He sent to Thaʿlaba, saying, “You are a people of great fighting strength and great numbers.

You have killed this tribe, which was the fiercest of the Arab tribes and the most numerous in men.

I shall put you in their place and draw up a treaty between us: if a large force of Arabs assails you, I shall send you forty thousand Roman fighters with their equipment. If a large force of Arabs assails us, you must provide twenty thousand fighters, on condition that you do not intervene between us and Persia.” Thaʿlaba accepted this, and the treaty was written between them.

He appointed Thaʿlaba king and crowned him. The king of the Romans was called Dīqiyūs.

Then Thaʿlaba died, and the Romans made his son al-Ḥārith ibn Thaʿlaba king. Al-Ḥārith died, and they made his son Jabalā king. Jabalā died, and they made his son al-Ḥārith king. He was the son of Māriya of the Two Earrings, daughter of al-Arqam ibn Thaʿlaba ibn ʿAmr ibn Jafna. It is also said that she was Māriya daughter of Ẓālim ibn Wahb ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Muʿāwiya ibn Thawr, of Kindah. After al-Ḥārith, al-Nuʿmān ibn al-Ḥārith ruled. Then al-Mundhir ibn al-Ḥārith; then al-Munaydhir ibn al-Ḥārith; then Jabalā ibn al-Ḥārith; then Abū Shammar ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Jabalā ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Thaʿlaba ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAmr ibn Jafna; then al-Ḥārith al-Aʿraj ibn Abī Shammar ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAdī ibn ʿAmr al-Ḥasḥās—who was Ḥāritha ibn Bakr ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAdī ibn ʿAmr ibn Māzin ibn al-Azd. He was not of Jafna, but his mother was Jafni. Their kingship remained among them until the last of them, Jabalā ibn al-Ayham ibn Jabalā ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Jabalā ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Thaʿlaba ibn ʿAmr ibn Jafna. His reign extended into the caliphate of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵀. He embraced Islam, then apostatized and returned to Roman territory. He has a story.

The Chiefs of Police of the Caliphs

The first to appoint a chief of police was ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān ﵀. His chief of police was ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qunfudh al-Taymī of Quraysh, over the ʿAdawī and the like.

No one walked before him carrying a spear, nor was there a body of police. The chief of police of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁ was Maʿqil ibn Qays al-Riyāḥī.

The chief of police of Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān was Yazīd ibn al-Ḥurr al-ʿAnsī. The chief of police of Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiya was also Yazīd ibn al-Ḥurr. When al-ʿAnsī died, Yazīd appointed Ḥumayd ibn Ḥurayth ibn Baḥdal al-Kalbī as his chief of police. The chief of police of Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam was Yaḥyā ibn Qays ibn Ḥāritha al-Ghassānī. The chief of police of ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān was ʿAbd Allāh ibn Hāniʾ al-Awdī. Then he dismissed him and appointed Yazīd ibn Abī Kabsha al-Saksakī in his place. Then he dismissed him and appointed Yazīd ibn Bishr al-Saksakī in his place.

He remained chief of police until he died, and Kʿab ibn Ḥāmid al-ʿAnsī was appointed in his place.

He was chief of police under ʿAbd al-Malik until ʿAbd al-Malik died. His son al-Walīd confirmed him in office.

Then al-Walīd sent him on a naval expedition and appointed Abū Nātal Riyāḥ ibn ʿAbda al-Ghassānī as chief of police. When Kʿab returned, he reinstated him as chief of police. Then he sent him on the summer campaign with al-ʿAbbās ibn al-Walīd and appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd al-Ḥakamī as chief of police.

Then Kʿab returned, and he reinstated him as chief of police. When al-Walīd died, Sulaymān confirmed him in office until Sulaymān died. ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz became caliph, dismissed him, and appointed Rūḥ ibn Yazīd ibn Bishr al-Saksakī. When Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik became caliph, he reinstated Kʿab ibn Ḥāmid al-ʿAnsī. When Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik became caliph, he confirmed Kʿab ibn Ḥāmid al-ʿAnsī as chief of police for more than ten years. Then Kʿab died, and Yazīd ibn Yaʿlā al-ʿAnsī was appointed in his place, and remained until Hishām died. Then al-Walīd ibn Yazīd became caliph and appointed one of the Banū Baḥdal, of Kalb, as chief of police. When Yazīd ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik took power, he appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿĀmir al-Yaḥṣubī as chief of police. When Ibrāhīm ibn al-Walīd took power, he confirmed him in office. When Marwān ibn Muḥammad took power, he appointed al-Kawthar ibn al-Aswad al-Ghanawī as chief of police.

When Abū al-ʿAbbās became caliph, he appointed ʿAbd al-Jabbār ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Azdī as his chief of police. When al-Manṣūr became caliph, he confirmed him in office. Then he dismissed him, appointed him governor of Khurāsān, and appointed ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, ʿAbd al-Jabbār’s brother, as chief of police. Then he dismissed him and appointed Mūsā ibn Kaʿb al-Tamīmī in his place, until Mūsā died. He appointed al-Musayyab ibn Zuhayr al-Ḍabbī over the ʿAdawī, and assigned the spear to Ḥamza ibn Mālik al-Khuzāʿī. Then he20 the spear to al-Musayyab ibn Zuhayr, until al-Manṣūr died. Then al-Mahdī became caliph and appointed— Naṣr ibn Mālik al-Khuzāʿī as his chief of police. Then Naṣr died, and Ḥamza ibn Mālik was appointed chief of police. Then he dismissed him and appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mālik in his place. He remained chief of police under him and under al-Hādī until al-Hādī died. Al-Rashīd became caliph and appointed al-Qāsim ibn Naṣr as his chief of police. Then he dismissed him and appointed Khuzayma ibn Khāzim. Then he dismissed him and appointed al-Musayyab ibn Zuhayr, then dismissed him. He reinstated Khuzayma ibn Khāzim. Then he dismissed him and appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mālik. Then he dismissed him and appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khāzim. Then he dismissed him and reinstated Khuzayma ibn Khāzim.

Then he dismissed him and reinstated ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mālik. Then he dismissed him and reinstated ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khāzim. Then he dismissed him and appointed ʿAlī ibn al-Ḥajjāj. Then he dismissed him and appointed Ibrāhīm ibn ʿUthmān ibn Nuhayk al-ʿUkkī. Then he killed him and appointed Wahb ibn Ibrāhīm in his place, naming him ʿUthmān ibn ʿUthmān. Then he dismissed him and appointed Ibn al-Shakhkhīr, who remained until al-Rashīd died. Then al-Amīn became caliph. His chief of police was Muḥammad ibn al-Musayyab ibn Zuhayr, and responsibility for the ʿAdawī lay with al-Sindī ibn Shāhik. Then he dismissed Muḥammad, appointed him governor of Ārminiya, and appointed Muḥammad ibn Ḥamza ibn Mālik in his place. Then he dismissed him and appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khāzim. Then al-Maʾmūn became caliph, and his chief of police was al-ʿAbbās ibn al-Musayyab. Then al-ʿAbbās died, and Abū Khālid al-Bāzayār was appointed in his place. Then Ṭāhir ibn al-Ḥusayn ibn Muṣʿab was appointed. Then he appointed him governor of Khurāsān.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṭāhir took his place. Then ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṭāhir was appointed governor of Syria, Egypt, and Khurāsān. His deputy over the police was Isḥāq ibn Ibrāhīm ibn Muṣʿab.

Al-Maʾmūn went out to Syria and the Jazīra, and his chief of police was Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm ibn Muṣʿab. Isḥāq ibn Ibrāhīm, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṭāhir’s deputy, was in Iraq until al-Maʾmūn died. Then al-Muʿtaṣim became caliph and confirmed Isḥāq ibn Ibrāhīm as ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṭāhir’s deputy over Madīnat al-Salām and the Sawād. Then ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṭāhir died, and Ṭāhir ibn ʿAbd Allāh was appointed in his place, with Isḥāq as his deputy in Madīnat al-Salām and the Sawād. Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm was chief of police at the military encampment until al-Muʿtaṣim died. Then al-Wāthiq became caliph, and his chief of police was Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm, deputy to Isḥāq. Then he dismissed Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm and appointed Muḥammad ibn Isḥāq ibn Ibrāhīm, his father’s deputy, in his place, until al-Wāthiq died. Then al-Mutawakkil became caliph and confirmed Isḥāq ibn Ibrāhīm as deputy to Ṭāhir ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṭāhir, and Muḥammad ibn Isḥāq as deputy to his father, until Isḥāq died. He appointed Muḥammad over his father’s provinces and added many more to them, until Muḥammad ibn Isḥāq died. He appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Isḥāq to Madīnat al-Salām and Ibrāhīm ibn Isḥāq as his chief of police at Sāmarrāʾ. Then Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṭāhir arrived, and he appointed him in place of Isḥāq ibn Ibrāhīm as chief of police, deputy to his brother— Ṭāhir ibn ʿAbd Allāh. The chief of police at the military encampment was Ibrāhīm ibn Isḥāq ibn Ibrāhīm ibn Muṣʿab, deputy to Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṭāhir.

Names of Eminent Secretaries

Zayd ibn Thābit al-Anṣārī used to write the revelation for the Messenger of God ﷺ. Zayd also served as a scribe for ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵀; he was learned in Qurʾānic recitation, the laws of inheritance, and jurisprudence. ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān was a scribe for Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq ﵏ and later became caliph. Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam was ʿUthmān’s scribe, and later became caliph.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān was secretary of the register in Madīna during the time of Muʿāwiya, and later became caliph. ʿAmr ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ was secretary of the register in Madīna. He sought the caliphate and was killed in pursuit of it. ʿUbayd ibn Aws al-Ghassānī, chief of the people of Syria, was a scribe for Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān. Saʿīd ibn Nimrān al-Hamdānī, chief of Hamdān, was a scribe for ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁; afterward, he held the judgeship of Kūfa for Ibn al-Zubayr. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khalaf al-Khuzāʿī, Abū Ṭalḥa al-Ṭalḥāt, was secretary of the register in Baṣra for ʿUmar and ʿUthmān ﵏ until he was killed alongside ʿĀʾisha on the Day of the Camel. Khārija ibn Zayd ibn Thābit was in charge of the register in Madīna before— ʿAbd al-Malik. Then ʿAmr ibn Saʿīd held the post after ʿAbd al-Malik. After ʿAmr ibn Saʿīd came ʿUthmān ibn ʿAnbasa ibn Abī Sufyān ibn Ḥarb. All of this took place in the time of Muʿāwiya. In the time of Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiya, Yazīd ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Zamʿa ibn al-Aswad ibn al-Muṭṭalib ibn Asad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā was in charge of the register in Madīna. Then came Ḥumayd ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf al-Zuhrī. Then ʿUthmān ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Arqam al-Zuhrī, during the rule of Ibn al-Zubayr. When the people united behind ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān in the year seventy-three, he appointed ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn al-Ḥārith ibn al-Ḥakam ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ. Then Ibrāhīm ibn Muḥammad ibn Ṭalḥa ibn ʿUbayd Allāh held the post after him. Then Ibn Khārija al-Anṣārī held it. He remained in the post until Hishām, who appointed one of the clients to it. Saʿīd ibn Jubayr was a scribe for ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUtba ibn Masʿūd, who was a judge.

After that, Saʿīd also served as a scribe for Abū Burda ibn Abī Mūsā al-Ashʿarī, who was a judge under al-Ḥajjāj ibn Yūsuf. Al-Ḥajjāj appointed him after Shurayḥ. Al-Ḥasan ibn Abī al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī was a scribe for al-Rabīʿ ibn Ziyād al-Ḥārithī in Khurāsān. Ziyād ibn Abīhi was a scribe for al-Mughīra ibn Shuʿba; then he wrote for Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī, then for ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿĀmir ibn Kurayz, then for ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib. Abū Jubayra al-Anṣārī was a scribe for ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb over the register of Kūfa.

ʿĀmir al-Shaʿbī was a scribe for ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ ibn al-Aswad al-ʿAdawī and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd al-Khaṭmī, both of them governors of Kūfa for ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr. Muḥammad ibn Sīrīn was a scribe for Anas ibn Mālik in Fārs. Qabīṣa ibn Dhuʾayb al-Khuzāʿī was a scribe for ʿAbd al-Malik over the chancery register. Qays ibn ʿAṭārid was a scribe for al-Walīd ibn ʿUqba ibn Abī Muʿayṭ in Kūfa. Qabīṣa ibn Jābir al-Asadī was a scribe for Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ in Kūfa. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Abzā was a scribe for Nāfiʿ ibn al-Ḥārith al-Khuzāʿī, the governor of Mecca for Abū Bakr and ʿUmar ﵏.

The Prolific Fools of Quraysh and Others

ʿAbd al-Dār ibn Quṣayy was prolific. ʿĀmir ibn Kurayz ibn Rabīʿa ibn Ḥabīb ibn ʿAbd Shams was prolific. Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ was prolific. Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Hishām ibn al-Mughīra, Abū Jahl’s brother, was prolific. Al-ʿĀṣ ibn Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ and al-ʿĀṣ ibn Hishām ibn al-Mughīra were called “the two fools of Quraysh.” Sahl ibn ʿAmr, one of the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy, was prolific.

Muḥammad ibn Ḥāṭib al-Jumaḥī. ʿAmr ibn Ḥurayth had no children.

ʿUtba ibn Abī Sufyān had no children. ʿAmr ibn Suhayl ibn ʿAmr had no children. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān had no children. Muʿāwiya ibn Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam was prolific. Bakkār ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān had no children.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Qays ibn Makhrama ibn al-Muṭṭalib—the Banū al-Muṭṭalib were called “the fools.” Al-Aḥwaṣ ibn Jaʿfar ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥurayth had no children.

The Fools of Thaqīf

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Umm al-Ḥakam, who was the son of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Rabīʿa. Al-Mughīra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī ʿAqīl. Al-Qāsim ibn Muḥammad ibn al-Ḥakam ibn Abī ʿAqīl.

The Fools of the Arabs

Mālik ibn Zayd Manāt ibn Tamīm was prolific. Bakr ibn Ḥabīb ibn ʿAmr ibn Ghanm ibn Taghlib was prolific. Kalb ibn Wabara was prolific.

Kilāb [the parentheses were accidentally omitted] ibn Rabīʿa ibn ʿĀmir ibn Ṣaʿṣaʿa was prolific. ʿAjl ibn Lujaym was prolific. ʿAtūd ibn ʿUnayn ibn Salāmān ibn Thaʿl ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Ghawth was prolific. ʿAdī ibn Junāb ibn Hubal al-Kalbī was prolific. ʿAmr ibn Juwayya ibn Lawdhān al-Fazārī was prolific. ʿUyayna ibn Ḥiṣn ibn Ḥudhayfa al-Fazārī was prolific. He was “the fool who was obeyed.” Aws ibn Jābir ibn Kaʿb ibn ʿUlaym ibn Junāb ibn Hubal was prolific. Bajjād ibn Qays ibn Masʿūd ibn— Qays ibn Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Dhī al-Jaddayn: he had children. Ḍirār ibn Miʿqal, the brother of Banū Jaḥḥāsh and father of the poet Abū al-Shammākh: he had children. Qubayṣa ibn al-Muhallab ibn Abī Ṣufra: he had children. Sulaymān ibn Nuʿmān ibn Qays ibn Maʿdīkarib al-Kindī: he had no children. Khudāsh ibn Zuhayr ibn Janāb al-Kalbī: he had no children.

(Banū Hāniʾ ibn Masʿūd) were all fools except Qubayṣa, who escaped their folly and was therefore called “Aflat.” They all had children. Among them were (ʿĀmir, Qays, Suwayd, and Jubayr); these were fools. Qubayṣa escaped, and was therefore called “Aflat.” Yazīd ibn Tharwān, known as “Habannaqa,” was the brother of Banū Qays ibn Thaʿlaba.

Foolish women

(Rayṭa) daughter of Saʿd ibn Taym ibn Murra ibn Kaʿb ibn Luʾayy: she is the one who “unraveled her yarn after it had been strongly spun” (16:92). And (al-Raqʿāʾ), whose name was Asmāʾ, of Balī, of Quḍāʿa. She was the mother of Kinda and Masrūq, sons of Ḥāritha ibn Lām al-Ṭāʾī.

And (Daghah) daughter of Miʿnaj, by whom the proverb is coined. She is “al-Jaʿrāʾ,” the name by which Banū al-ʿAnbar ibn ʿAmr ibn Tamīm are insulted. And (Umm ʿAmr), daughter of Junaydib ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥammah al-Dawsī. She was the mother of ʿAmr and Abān, sons of ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān. And (Rayṭa) daughter of ʿĀmir ibn Numayr used to mark the heads of her sons by shaving patches, so that she could tell her children from those of her co-wife. Her co-wife was Hind daughter of Hilāl ibn Sumayy, a Fazarī. Rayṭa’s children were Qurṭ, Qurayyiṭ, Qurayṭ, and ʿAmr, sons of ʿAbd ibn Abī Bakr. Her co-wife took her son ʿAmr, shaved the patch of hair from his head, and claimed him, prevailing over her. And (Umm Hishām), daughter of Hishām ibn Ismāʿīl ibn Hishām al-Makhzūmī.

The cause of the confusion of tongues

Ibn al-Kalbī said: Noah ﵇ boarded the Ark on the thirteenth day of Ramaḍān, and disembarked from it on the first day of Ramaḍān in the following year. Thus it was a year less thirteen days. The water remained after it had receded from the land of Judhām, which is Ḥismā, for forty years. The Ark was three hundred cubits long, fifty cubits wide, and thirty cubits deep. It rose six cubits out of the water. It was covered over and had three doors. The people were at the upper door, the livestock at the second, and the beasts of prey at the lowest. Noah carried aboard the Ark his sons Shem, Ham, and Japheth, their wives, and a total of eighty people who had embraced Islam. All creation drowned except for those eighty.

Noah, his wife, his three sons and their wives, and seventy-two people made up the eighty. The name of Noah’s son who drowned was Shālūmā; he was Canaan. The Arabs call him Yām. The names of their wives were: Shem’s wife (Maḥlath Baḥwā); Ham’s wife (Adhanfānshā); Japheth’s wife (Zazqath Nabāb); Noah’s wife (Wāʿilah); and Lot’s wife (Wāhilah).

The Ark came to rest at al-Jūdī, and they disembarked. They built eighty houses, and the place was called Sūq Thamānīn (“Market of Eighty”). When they grew numerous, they built Babylon, twelve parasangs by twelve parasangs. They multiplied there until they numbered one hundred thousand. Their king was Nimrod ibn Canaan ibn Sennacherib ibn Nimrod ibn Cush ibn Ham ibn Noah. He turned them away from Islam. They went to bed speaking Syriac, but awoke with no one among them able to understand another’s speech. Their tongues became confused. God made ʿĀd and ʿAbīl, sons of ʿAwṣ ibn Aram ibn Shem ibn Noah, understand Arabic. They were the first to speak Arabic, as were Thamūd and Jadīs, sons of Jāthir ibn Aram ibn Shem; ʿAmālīq; Ṭasm; and Umaym ibn [? Banū] Lūdh [ān ibn Aram] ibn Shem; and the descendants of Yaqṭan ibn ʿĀbir ibn Shālakh ibn Arfakhshadh ibn Shem ibn Noah. They divided into seventy-two languages: thirty-two belonged to the descendants of Shem, and the rest to the descendants of Ham and Japheth. Noah’s son, born after the Ark, whom they called Yūnāṭir, assigned them their banners. The descendants of ʿĀd set out and settled in al-Siḥr. There God destroyed them, at a valley called al-Mughīth. The descendants of ʿAbīl settled at the site of the city of the Prophet ﷺ.

The descendants of Thamūd settled at al-Ḥijr and its environs. Ṭasm and Jadīs settled in al-Yamāmah.

And Yāman, the descendants of Yaqṭan ibn ʿĀbir, settled in Yemen; it was then called Yemen because of their settling to the right of them. The ʿAmālīq settled at the site of Ṣanʿāʾ, before Ṣanʿāʾ existed. The descendants of Umaym settled in the land of Wabār, belonging to Umaym. Fāligh ibn ʿĀbir ibn Shālakh ibn Arfakhshadh divided the land among them. The ʿAmālīq then came and expelled the descendants of ʿAbīl from the city, settling them at the site of al-Juḥfah. They settled there, and a flood came by night and swept them away, casting them into the sea; hence the place was called al-Juḥfah. One of the descendants of ʿAbīl lamented his people:

O eye, weep copiously for ʿAbīl—willwhat has passed return through the welling of your tears?

They inhabited Yathrib, where there was nohairless one, no cry-maker, nor any with a hump.

They planted their palms by a flowing stream,then ringed the palms with thickets.

From (ʿĀd) came the prophet Hūd ﵇ son of ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Khulūd ibn ʿĀd.

From (Thamūd) came Ṣāliḥ ﵇ son of Āṣif ibn Kamāshiḥ ibn Arūm ibn Thamūd.

Names of the sons of Ishmael ﵇

Qaydar. Niyāwudh, who was Nabīth. Adhbul. Manshā. Mishmāʿah.

Dawmā. Māsh. Adhūr. Ṭaymā. Yaṭūr. Nabsh. Qayḍam.

Descendants of Isaac

Al-ʿĪṣ. And Jacob, who was Israel ﵇.

Names of the sons of Jacob

Shimʿūn. Rubīl. Judah. Lāwī. Yissākhar. Zabūlūn.

Dīnā—(their mother was Iliyah daughter of Aḥban ibn Batuʾīl ibn Nāḥūr)—and Jādh and Āshīr.

—(their mother was Bilhā, Rachel’s maidservant)—and Joseph and Benjamin—(their mother was Rachel, Leah’s sister)—and Dan and Naphtali—(their mother was Zilpah, Leah’s maidservant).

Among the descendants of Levi ibn Jacob were (Moses) and (Aaron), the two prophets ﵈, sons of ʿImrān ibn Qāhith ibn Lāwī ibn Jacob; and (Zechariah) ibn Bishway. His son (John) was among the descendants of Aaron ibn ʿImrān. And (Qārūn) ibn Yīṣhar ibn Qāhith ibn Lāwī. And (the Samaritan), whose name was Mīkhā ibn Raʿūwīl ibn Qāhith ibn Lāwī. Moses’ mother was (Yūkhābidh) daughter of Lāwī; she was his paternal aunt.

Among the descendants of Judah was (Solomon), son of David ibn Īshī ibn ʿŪbadh ibn Bāʿadh ibn Shalmūn ibn Naḥshūn ibn ʿAmmīnādhāb ibn Ārim ibn Ḥaḍrūn ibn Pharez ibn Judah.

Also among the descendants of Levi were (Qurayẓah, al-Naḍīr, and Hadal), sons of al-Naḥḥām ibn Yanḥūm ibn ʿAwf ibn Qays ibn Finḥās ibn al-ʿĀzar ibn al-Kāhin ibn Aaron ibn ʿImrān ibn Qāhith ibn Lāwī.

Among the descendants of the prophet Joseph ﵇ was (Elisha) ibn ʿAdī ibn Shūṭalaḥ ibn Ephraim ibn Joseph ibn Jacob. And (Joshua) ibn Nun ibn Ephraim ibn Joseph. He was Moses’ attendant.

Jonah ibn Mattā was among the descendants of Benjamin ibn Jacob.

Among the descendants of al-ʿĪṣ ibn Isaac were (Job) ibn Zāriḥ ibn Amūṣ ibn Līfazar ibn al-ʿĪṣ ibn Isaac; (al-Khiḍr), Khiḍrūn ibn ʿAmiyāʾīl ibn Fulān ibn al-ʿĪṣ; and (Elijah) ibn Tishbīn ibn al-ʿĀzar ibn al-Kāhin ibn Aaron.

The name of the believer among Pharaoh’s people was Ḥizbīl or Khuzbīl. He was the brother of Āsiyah, Pharaoh’s wife. Hishām said: Ḥizbīl was the husband of the woman who combed hair. Pharaoh had placed him in charge of half the people.

The magicians

The magicians numbered seventy-two men: seventy from the Children of Israel, and two men from Persia. The seventy embraced Islam and were crucified. The two Persians did not embrace Islam and escaped. The one about whom the following was revealed—“Recite to them the account of the one to whom We gave Our signs, but he cast them off” (7:175)— (7:175) was (Balaam) ibn Bāʿūrā ibn Satūm ibn Fawāsīm ibn Māb ibn Lot ibn Hārūn ibn Tāriḥ ibn Nāḥūr.

Among the descendants of Midian ibn Abraham was

(Shuʿayb) ibn Yūbab ibn ʿAyyafā ibn Midian. And (Mālik) ibn Dhaʿr ibn Yūbab ibn ʿAyyafā, who brought Joseph up from the well. The staff came to the man to whom Moses was married ﵇; Abraham ﵇ had given it to Midian, his son. It then came to Shuʿayb.

Moses married the daughter of (Yathrūn) ibn Raʿūwīl ibn Yūbab, the nephew of the prophet Shuʿayb ﷺ. Shuʿayb had given the staff to Yathrūn when he was dying. Yathrūn’s daughter mentioned it to Moses ﵇; he asked Yathrūn for it, and Yathrūn gave it to him. It was made from a tree of Paradise. Its length was the same as Moses’ height. The woman’s name was Ṣabbūrā.

The lineage of Mary, daughter of ʿImrān

She was Mary, daughter of ʿImrān ibn Mātān ibn al-ʿĀzar ibn Īlyūd ibn Ṣādiq ibn ʿĀzariyyūn ibn Īlyā ibn Abīwud ibn Zarbābīl ibn Shalthāthīl ibn Yūḥanā ibn Yāshiyā ibn Rāḥabʿām ibn Solomon ibn David ibn Īshī ibn ʿŪbadh; her lineage has already been given.

The lineage of Daniel
