Khubayya bt. Rabīʿa ibn ʿAbd Yālīl ibn Sālim ibn Mālik ibn Ḥuṭayṭ bore Masʿūd, Wahb, ʿĀmir, ʿAmr, Murra, and Muʿāwiya, the sons of Muʿattib ibn Mālik ibn Kaʿb ibn ʿAmr.

Ṣafiyya bt. al-Mughīra ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn Makhzūm bore Uḥayḥa, al-ʿĀṣī, al-Ḥakam—whom the Messenger of God ﷺ named ʿAbd Allāh and appointed to teach wisdom; he was martyred at Muʾta—and Saʿīd ibn Saʿīd, who was martyred at al-Ṭāʾif—and ʿAmr, who was martyred at Ajnādayn, the sons of Abī Uḥayḥa, Saʿīd ibn al-ʿĀṣ ibn Umayya.

Qulāba bt. al-Ḥārith ibn Kalda ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿIlāj bore Ḥabīb, Masʿūd, ʿAbd Yālīl, Rabīʿa, and Kināna, the sons of ʿAmr ibn ʿUmayr ibn ʿAwf ibn ʿUqda ibn Ghayra al-Thaqafī. They used to provide food at al-Riyāḥ.

Fāṭima bt. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Rabīʿa al-Thaqafiyya bore ʿUthmān, al-Ḥakam, Abū Umayya, and Abū ʿAmr, the sons of Abī al-ʿĀṣ ibn Bishr ibn Sufyān.

Tamāḍir bt. al-Sharīd al-Sulamiyya had [the sons] Qays, Mālik, al-Ḥārith, Warqāʾ, Wahb, and Shaʾs, sons of Zuhayr b. Judhayma b. Rawāḥa b. Rabīʿa b. Māzin al-ʿAbsī.

Ṣuḥār bt. Sufyān b. Ẓālim b. Fazāra bore Sayyār, the man with the bow, who pledged his bow as security for a thousand camels in the killing of al-Ḥārith b. Ẓālim, on behalf of al-Nuʿmān al-Akbar; ʿAwf, the bearer of their standard; Naḍla; Khālid; and al-Ḥārith, who left no descendants—the sons of ʿAmr b. Jābir b. ʿUqayl b. Hilāl b. Sumayy b. Māzin b. Fazāra.

A certain Asadiyya bore Ḥudhayfa, the lord of Maʿadd; ʿAwf; Rabīʿa; Mālik; Ḥaml; and al-Ḥārith, sons of Badr b. ʿAmr b. Juwayya b. Lawdhān b. ʿAdī b. Fazāra.

Nuḍayra bt. Marwān b. ʿUṣaym b. Mālik bore Ḥiṣn, Mālik, Muʿāwiya, Shurayk, and Ward, sons of Ḥudhayfa b. Badr.

Umm Qirfa bt. Rabīʿa b. Badr b. ʿAmr bore Qirfa, Kharasha, Ḥuṣayn, Qays, Arṭāʾa, Marthad, Muʿāwiya, Jābala, Ḥakma, ʿUbayd, and Shurayk, Zufar and Ḥiṣn, sons of Mālik b. Ḥudhayfa b. Badr. This Umm Qirfa was the most honored of the Arabs. Whenever a dispute arose among Ghatafān, she would send her veil, which they would hang between them, and they would make peace. Zayd b. Ḥāritha al-Kalbī, the client of the Messenger of God ﷺ, killed her.

Kabsha bt. ʿAbd Allāh b. Qunfudh b. Mālik al-Sulamiyya bore Muʿāwiya, Ṣakhr, Kurz, and Bishr, sons of ʿAmr b. al-Ḥārith b. al-Sharīd.

Hāla bt. Rabīʿa b. al-Ḥuraysh b. Kaʿb bore Zuhayr, Khālid, Mālik, ʿAmr, ʿĀmir, and Saʿd, sons of Rabīʿa b. ʿAmr b. ʿĀmir b. Rabīʿa b. ʿĀmir b. Ṣaʿṣaʿa. They were called “the defenders” on the Day of al-Masʿaṭ, when Khathʿam and al-Ḥārith b. Kaʿb were involved; it was the Day of Jubayl al-Ṣabāḥ.

Laylā bt. Zinbāʿ b. Aḥaymar b. Bahdala was the mother of Zarrāra, ʿAmr, Shurāḥīl, and Yathribī, sons of ʿAdas b. Zayd.

Sharāf bt. Bahdala b. ʿAwf was the mother of Muḥammad, Qurṭ, and Ḥawwāʾ, sons of Sufyān b. Mujāshiʿ.

Tamāḍir bt. ʿAṭṭārid b. ʿAwf b. Kaʿb was the mother of “the Stones”: Ṣakhr, Jandal, and Jarwal, sons of Nahshal b. Dārim.

Rabīʿa b. Nizār had four women, and there was not one among them whose brother, father, husband, and son had not attained chiefship and rabʿ [a status term; the precise meaning is uncertain].

Among them was Māriya bt. Shuraḥbīl b. ʿAmr b. Marthad, of the Banū Qays b. Thaʿlaba. Her husband was Ḥumrān b. ʿAbd ʿAmr b. Bishr b. Marthad; her son was Ghaḍbān b. Ḥumrān; and her brother was Wāʾil b. Shuraḥbīl. All of these became chiefs and attained rabʿ [a status term; the precise meaning is uncertain].

Qutayla bt. Ḥassān b. Bishr b. ʿAmr b. Marthad. Her husband was Saʿd b. Ḍubayʿa b. Shuraḥbīl b. ʿAmr b. Marthad; her son was Dulayḥ b. Saʿd; and her brother was ʿAmr b. Ḥassān. All of these became chiefs and attained rabʿ [a status term; the precise meaning is uncertain].

Hind bt. Marthad b. ʿAmr b. Marthad. Her husband was Shuraḥbīl b. Ḍubayʿa b. ʿAmr b. Marthad; her son was al-Ḥaṭm, that is, Shurayḥ b. Shuraḥbīl;

and her brother was ʿUbāda b. Marthad. All of these became chiefs and attained rabʿ [a status term; the precise meaning is uncertain].

/ Her sister Zaynab bt. Marthad b. ʿAmr. Her husband was ʿAlqama b. Masrūq b. Dhuhl b. ʿAmr b. Marthad; her son was Qays b. ʿAlqama;

and her brother was ʿUbāda b. Marthad. All of these became chiefs and attained rabʿ [a status term; the precise meaning is uncertain].

Names of the chiefs of the Children of Israel, who were twelve

From the tribe of Reuben: Shammūʿ b. Zakkūr. From the tribe of Simeon: Sharafūṭ b. Ḥūrī. From the tribe of Judah: Kūlab b. Yufannā.

From the tribe of Issachar: Yaghūʿil b. Yūsuf. From the tribe of Ephraim b. Joseph ﵇: Yūshaʿ b. Nūn. From the tribe of Benjamin: Yalṭā b. Rūfuwā. From the tribe of Zebulun: Kaddāʾīl b. Shūdhī. From the tribe of Manasseh b. Joseph: Kaddī b. Sūsī. From the tribe of Dan: ʿAmāʾīl b. Kamlī. From the tribe of Asher: Shattūr b. Mīkhāʾīl.

From the tribe of Naphtali: Yaḥyā b. Waqsī. From the tribe of Gad: Kuwāʾil b. Mūkhī.

The two believers among them were Joshua and Caleb. Moses prayed against the others ﵇, and they perished in the plague, accursed.

Names of the Apostles, who were twelve

Shimʿūn Miqrā Kayfā. Andrayūs. Yaʿqūb b. Zabday. Yūḥannā.

Fīlfūs. Bartalmā. Tūmā. Mattā. Makhāsā. Yaʿqūb b. Khalfī. Labī and Takanīrī.

Shimʿūn Fannānāyā. Yahūdhā Iskhar Yūṭā.

Eleven remained in the faith, while Judas Iscariot disbelieved.

The Jews hired him to guide them to the Messiah ﷺ for thirty silver mithqāls. He guided them to him. God—mighty and majestic—raised him up to Himself and cast his likeness upon Iṣṭafānūs. They seized him and crucified him. Their king was named Hīrūdus. Ibn al-Kalbī said: “The king was named Bīlāṭūs.”

Names of the chiefs of the ʿAbbāsids, descendants of ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib

Sulaymān b. Kathīr al-Khuzāʿī. Abū ʿAlī Mūsā b. Kaʿb al-Tamīmī. Abū ʿUyayna Mālik b. al-Haytham al-Khuzāʿī. Abū Naṣr al-Qāsim b. Mujāshiʿ al-Tamīmī. Lāhiz b. Qurayṭ al-Tamīmī. ʿĪsā b. Aʿyan al-Khuzāʿī. Abū al-Ḥakam ʿAmr b. Aʿyan al-Khuzāʿī. Abū Ḥamza Qaḥṭaba b. Shabīb al-Ṭāʾī. Abū ʿAbd al-Ḥamīd Sabl b. Ṭahmān al-Rabʿī, a Shaybānī.

ʿImrān b. Ismāʿīl, Abū al-Najm, client of the family of Abū Muʿayṭ. Khālid b. Ibrāhīm, Abū Dāwūd al-Dhuhlī. Ṭalḥa b. Ruzaq, client of Khuzāʿa.

Al-Aghlab b. Sālim.

The Nimrods, six men

The first: Nimrod b. Canaan b. Ham b. Noah. He was the one who ruled the world. He was Abraham’s contemporary ﵇.

The second: Nimrod b. Kush b. Canaan b. Ham b. Noah.

He was the one associated with the vultures. His mother was Fartan bt. Yārib b. al-Darmsīl b. Muḥawwil b. Akhnūkh, that is, Enoch ﷺ.

The third: Nimrod b. Māsh b. Aram b. Shem b. Noah.

The fourth: Nimrod b. Canaan b. Sennacherib b. Nimrod b. Kush b. Canaan b. Ham b. Noah. It is said that he was Abraham’s Nimrod ﵇.

The fifth: Nimrod b. Sārūʿ b. Arʿawā b. Faligh b. ʿĀbir b. Shālikh b. Arpachshad b. Shem b. Noah.

The sixth: Nimrod b. Canaan b. al-Muḍāḍ b. Qaḥṭān b. ʿAbīr b. Arpachshad b. Shem b. Noah.

The Pharaohs, three men

The first of them: Sinān b. al-Ashāl b. ʿAlwān b. al-ʿUbayd b. ʿArīj b. ʿAmālīq b. Yalmaʿ b. ʿĀbir b. Isliḥā b. Lūdh b. Shem b. Noah. He was known by the teknonym Abū al-ʿAbbās. He was the Pharaoh of Abraham.

/--- The second: al-Rayyān b. al-Walīd b. Layth b. Fārān b. ʿAmr b. ʿAmālīq b. Yalmaʿ. He was the Pharaoh of Joseph.

The third: al-Walīd b. Muṣʿab b. Abī Ahwan b. al-Halwāth b. Fārān b. ʿAmr b. ʿAmālīq b. Yalmaʿ. He was the Pharaoh of Moses. He said:

“The Pharaoh of Joseph was the grandfather of the Pharaoh of Moses, and his name was Barkhūz.”

Names of the corrupters in the land from the people of Lot

Lāhīdh. Qaṭanfar. Shukarkam. Dabal. Qashfā. Qashfatā.

Makkīkhā. Mallīkhā. Maslīkhā.

The cities of the people of Lot: Sadūmā. Ṣabūʿaym. Dādūmā.

ʿĀmūrā. It is also said: Ṣūbūrā.

Names of the hypocrites, thirty-six men

Among the Aws were Durayy b. al-Ḥārith; al-Jallās b. Suwayd b. al-Ṣāmit, who stayed behind at Tabūk; his brother al-Ḥārith b. Suwayd, who treacherously killed al-Mujadhdhar b. Dhiyād on the day of Uḥud. The Messenger of God ﷺ killed him. Also among them were Bajjād b. ʿUthmān b. ʿĀmir and ʿAbd Allāh b. Nabtal, who used to relay the conversations of the Messenger of God ﷺ.

Qays b. Zayd, who was killed on the day of Uḥud; Abū Ḥubayha b. al-Azʿar, who was among those who built the Mosque of Harm; Thaʿlaba b. Ḥāṭib and Muʿattib b. Qushayr, the two who pledged to God, “If He gives us of His bounty, we shall surely give alms and be among the righteous” (9:75). Muʿattib was the one who said on the day of the Confederates: “Muḥammad promises us the treasures of Chosroes and Caesar, while one of us cannot even feel secure that he can go relieve himself! ‘God and His Messenger promised us nothing but delusion’” (33:12). Also, Rāfiʿ b. Zayd. This Rāfiʿ, Muʿattib b. Qushayr, and a group of their people sought refuge in Islam and claimed to profess it.

Some men of their people, who were Muslims, summoned them to the Messenger of God ﷺ to resolve a dispute between them. But they summoned them instead to a soothsayer from among the judges of the Jāhiliyya. Concerning them was revealed: “Have you not seen those who claim to believe in what has been sent down to you and what was sent down before you, yet wish to seek judgment from the false deity?” (4:60). Also, Jāriya b. ʿAmr b. Mujammaʿ and his sons, Mujammaʿ, Yazīd, and Zayd, who were among those who built the Mosque of Harm; ʿAbbād b. Ḥunayf b. Wāhib, who was among those who built the Mosque of Harm; and Wadīʿa b. Thābit, who was among those who built the Mosque of Harm. Concerning him was revealed: “‘We were only conversing and playing!’ Say: ‘Was it God and His signs?’” (9:65)—the verse. Also, Khudhām b. Khālid, who provided the Mosque of Harm from his own house. It is said that it was his son Wadīʿa. Also, Rāfiʿ and Bashīr, sons of Ziyād; Qays b. Rifāʿa the poet, who used to go with al-Ḍaḥḥāk b. Khalīfa to a Jewish synagogue, where a lamp in the synagogue struck his eye; Murabbaʿ b. Qayẓī; and Aws b. Qayẓī, who said to the Messenger of God ﷺ on the day of the Trench: “Our houses are exposed” (33:13).

And Ḥāṭib b. Umayya b. Rāfiʿ b. Suwayd, to whom someone said, “Your son has been carried to the house.” The women began to weep for him. Someone said to him, “Rejoice in Paradise.”

Ḥāṭib replied, “A paradise of harmel!” And Bishr b. Abīriq, Abū Ṭuʿma;

Ṭuʿma b. Abīriq; Qazmān, an ally of the Banū Ẓafar; and al-Ḍaḥḥāk b. Khalīfa.

Saʿd b. Zarāra, who used to make poetic insinuations about the Messenger of God ﷺ; ʿUqba b. Kudaym; Zayd b. ʿAmr; Qays b. ʿAmr b. Sahl, the grandfather of Yaḥyā b. Saʿīd al-Anṣārī; al-Jadd b. Qays, who said, “Give me leave, and do not put me to trial” (9:49); and ʿAdī b. Rabīʿa. He was the one who threw excrement at the Messenger of God ﷺ. He was blind. His son was Suwayd b. ʿAdī. Also, ʿAbd Allāh b. Ubayy b. Salūl, who was the chief of the hypocrites; Suwayd and Dāʿis, from among the Jews of Banū Qaynuqāʿ; and Mālik b. Abī Qawqal.

Al-Kalbī said: Among the hypocrites were al-Ḥumayyer b. al-Ḥumayyer; Abū ʿĀmir ʿAbd ʿAmr b. [Ṣayfī] b. al-Nuʿmān, the father of “Ḥanẓala b. Abī ʿĀmir, the Washed One”;

Abū Rāfiʿ; Bakhdaj; Mālik b. al-Dukhshum; Abū ʿĀmir al-Rāhib, the father of “Ḥanẓala, the Washed One”; Aws b. Qays; and ʿĀmir b. Khālid.

And Mālik b. ʿAmr; Mālik b. Khālid; and Abū al-Nuʿmān al-Anṣārī.

He said: Among the Jewish rabbis of Banū Qaynuqāʿ who entered Islam and sought refuge in it were Saʿd b. Ḥanīf; Zayd b. al-Laṣīb; al-Nuʿmān b. Awfā; Rāfiʿ b. Ḥuraymila, about whom the Messenger of God ﷺ said at Tabūk, “Today a great hypocrite has died”; Rifāʿa b. Zayd b. al-Tābūt; Silsila b. Barhām; and Kināna b. Ṣuwayrā. All of these used to listen to the Muslims’ conversations while outwardly professing Islam.

Those who drank wine undiluted until they died in anger and pride

ʿAmr b. Kulthūm al-Taghlibī. Kings used to send him their gifts He was at home, without having gone to him. When his son al-Aswad ibn ʿAmr became pre-eminent, one of the kings sent him a gift, just as he had sent one to his father. ʿAmr grew angry and said:

“Has he made me equal to my heir?”

He swore that he would not taste any fatty food until he died. He began drinking undiluted wine without food. When this went on for a long time, his wife, the daughter of al-Thuwayr, prepared some fat for him so that he would crave meat and eat it. He got up, struck her, and said:

God forbid! He calls me to break my oath,

Though I should go hungry for days, subsisting on scraps.

He continued drinking until he died.

And (Zuhayr) ibn Janāb ibn Hubl. He drank until he died after his nephew, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUlaym ibn Janāb, opposed him. He has a story. He said of him: “A man’s enemy is his nephew—though he will not leave his uncle’s killer unavenged.”

And (al-Burj) ibn Mushir al-Ṭāʾī. He awoke during the night, having been drinking.

He heard his sister al-Fuqāṭah urinating. He said: “I hear a trickling sound; I must give it a good shake.” Then he leapt upon her. She said: “Woe upon you! I am your sister.” But before long he had deflowered her. When morning came, he fled headlong to Syria, where he settled and became a Christian.

Then al-Ḥuṣayn ibn al-Ḥammām satirized him and mentioned what he had done to his sister. He drank undiluted wine until he died. His son Ḥassān ibn al-Burj was one of the leaders of the Khārijites; he was killed on the day of Nahrawān. Ḥuṣayn ibn al-Ḥammām said:

Do not suppose, brother of al-Fuqāṭah, that I am

A man who knows your story but is not all-knowing.

They brought you down, and you had stained her waistband

With your mother’s daughter, while the sands lie ever still.

And (Abū Barāʾ) ʿĀmir ibn Mālik ibn Jaʿfar. He had sent word to the Messenger of God ﷺ asking him to send them men who would instruct them and present Islam and its ordinances to them. He sent them a group of his Companions. On the day of the well of Maʿūnah, ʿĀmir ibn al-Ṭufayl waylaid them and killed them all ﵃. Only one man escaped. Abū Barāʾ was grieved that ʿĀmir ibn al-Ṭufayl had violated his pledge of protection regarding the Messenger of God’s Companions ﷺ. The Banū ʿĀmir heard that ʿĀmir ibn al-Ṭufayl had died while returning from the Messenger of God ﷺ and they wanted to move away from their place, so they began preparing to depart. ʿĀmir ibn Mālik said: “What are the people doing?” He was told:

“They are departing because of what has happened among the people.”

He said: “Without my command?” One of his nephews said: “They claim that some affliction has come upon your mind because you sent word to this man.” He called for Labīd and summoned two of his singing girls. He drank, and they sang to him. He said: “Labīd, tell me: if something should happen to your uncle, what would you say?

“Your people claim that my mind has gone, and death is better than the loss of reason.” Labīd said:

Two women journeying with the wailing women,

At a funeral whose departure is hastened,

In black garments and in worn cloaks,

They mourned Malāʿib, master of the spears:

O ʿĀmir! O ʿĀmir of the morning!

O ʿĀmir of the mighty company!

He completed this and other elegies. When the drinking weighed heavily upon him, he leaned on his sword until he died. He said: “There is no good in life when ʿĀmir has disobeyed me.” The ʿĀmir claim that he died a Muslim and did not kill himself.

Names of those whose hearts were reconciled, from Quraysh and others

(Abū Sufyān) ibn Ḥarb ibn Umayyah; and (Muʿāwiyah), his son.

And (al-Ḥārith) ibn Hishām ibn al-Mughīrah; (Ḥakīm) ibn Ṭalīq ibn Sufyān ibn Umayyah; (Khālid) ibn Asīd ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ; (Saʿīd) ibn Yarbuʿ al-Makhzūmī; (Ṣafwān) ibn Umayyah ibn Khalaf al-Jumaḥī; (Suhayl) ibn ʿAmr, of the Banū ʿĀmir ibn Luʾayy; and (Ḥuwayṭib) ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā al-ʿĀmirī.

And (Ḥakīm) ibn Ḥizām ibn Khuwaylid ibn Asad; (Abū Sufyān) ibn al-Ḥārith ibn ʿAbd al-Muṭṭalib; (ʿUyaynah) ibn Ḥiṣn ibn Ḥudhayfah al-Fazārī; (al-ʿAbbās) ibn Mirdās al-Sulamī; (Mālik) ibn ʿAwf al-Naṣrī; and (al-ʿAlāʾ) ibn Jāriyah al-Thaqafī; and (al-Aqraʿ) ibn Ḥābis al-Mujāshiʿī.

The Messenger of God ﷺ gave each of them one hundred she-camels, except Saʿīd ibn Yarbuʿ and Ḥuwayṭib ibn ʿAbd al-ʿUzzā, to each of whom he gave fifty she-camels.

Names of the Messenger of God’s disciples, all of them from Quraysh

Ibn Ḥabīb said: al-Musayyib related, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muʿādh al-Ṣanʿānī, from Maʿmar, that they were: Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿAlī, Ḥamzah, Jaʿfar, Abū ʿUbaydah ibn al-Jarrāḥ, ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān, ʿUthmān ibn Maẓʿūn, ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf, Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ, Ṭalḥah ibn ʿUbayd Allāh, and al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām—may God have mercy upon them. Ibn al-Kalbī said: al-Zubayr alone was a disciple. was the only disciple. Abū Saʿīd said: “Al-Musayyib himself related this to me.” Ibn Ḥabīb used to say, “al-Musayyib related.”

Notable teachers and jurists

(Bishr) ibn ʿAbd al-Malik al-Sakūnī, brother of Ukaydir, lord of Dūmat al-Jandal; a pre-Islamic figure.

(Sufyān) ibn Umayyah ibn ʿAbd Shams; a pre-Islamic figure.

(Abū Qays) ibn ʿAbd Manāf ibn Zuhrah; a pre-Islamic figure.

(Ghaylān) ibn Salamah ibn Muʿattib al-Thaqafī; a Companion of both the pre-Islamic and Islamic periods.

(ʿAmr) ibn Zurārah ibn ʿAdas ibn Zayd; a pre-Islamic figure. He was called “the scribe.”

(al-Ḥajjāj) ibn Yūsuf ibn al-Ḥakam ibn Abī ʿAqīl al-Thaqafī, governor of Iraq.

(al-Ḍaḥḥāk) ibn Muzāḥim, teacher of a group. Al-Faḍl ibn Dukayn related from Bishr ibn Salmān: “I was in a school.”

(ʿĀmir) al-Shaʿbī, with the sons of ʿAbd al-Malik.

(Abū Ṣāliḥ) [the companion of] al-Kalbī (Bādhām), client of Umm Hāniʾ, a teacher. Muḥammad ibn Bakkār mentioned this, saying: ʿAnbasah ibn ʿAbd al-Wāḥid al-Qurashī related to me, from Ismāʿīl ibn Abī Khālid. And (Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān) al-Sulamī. And (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Ḥabīb, teacher of al-Ḥasan and al-Ḥusayn.

(ʿAbd Allāh) ibn al-Ḥārith, a teacher. ʿĀṣim al-Aḥwal, ʿAlī ibn Zayd ibn Judʿān, and Qatādah related from him.

(ʿUbayd) al-Muktab ibn Mihrān. He related from Ibrāhīm, Mujāhid, and others.

(Ḥusayn) al-Muʿallim. Shuʿbah, ʿAbd al-Wārith, Yazīd ibn Zurayʿ, and others related from him.

(Ismāʿīl) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī al-Muhājir, tutor to the sons of ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān.

(Abān) ibn Taghlib, a teacher. Shuʿbah and Sufyān ibn ʿUyaynah related from him.

(ʿAbd al-Wāḥid) ibn Qays, tutor to the sons of Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik. Abū Mushir said: Ṣadaqah ibn Khālid related to me, saying: Marwān ibn Janāḥ related to me, from ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Qays, who said: “I told Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik: ‘I do not take anything from you for teaching the Qurʾān. I take from you only for teaching other things.’”

(Abū Saʿīd Muḥammad) ibn Abī al-Waḍḍāḥ. He related from ʿAlī ibn Badhīmah, Sālim al-Afṭas, and Khuṣayf.

(Yūnus) ibn Ḥabīb, the grammarian. He related from Ḥammād ibn Salamah, ʿAbd al-Wārith, and Ḥammād ibn Zayd.

(Abū Ismāʿīl Ibrāhīm) ibn Sulaymān. He related from ʿAṭiyyah al-ʿAwfī, al-Aʿmash, and others.

(Ḥabīb) ibn Abī Buqayyah, a teacher.

Ḥammād ibn Salamah, ʿAbd al-Wārith, Ḥammād ibn Zayd, and others related from him.

(Hārūn) ibn Mūsā al-Aʿwar al-Qāriʾ, a teacher. ʿAbbād ibn al-ʿAwwām related from him.

(ʿUmar) ibn al-Faḍl al-Baṣrī, a teacher. ʿAbd al-Wārith related from him.

(Muḥammad) ibn Shihāb al-Zuhrī, a teacher.

(Ismāʿīl) ibn Jaʿfar ibn Abī Kathīr al-Madanī.

(Ḥajjāj) ibn Muḥammad al-Aʿwar, a teacher. Yaḥyā ibn Maʿīn and Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal related from him.

(Yūnus) ibn Muḥammad, a teacher.

(Sufyān) ibn Ḥusayn, a teacher.

(Shaybān al-Naḥwī) Abū Muʿāwiyah, a teacher.

(al-Qāsim) ibn Mukhaymirah, a teacher.

(al-Kumayt) ibn Zayd al-Asadī, the poet, a teacher.

(Qubayṣah) ibn Dhuʾayb al-Khuzāʿī, teacher of a group.

ʿUthmān ibn Abī Shaybah related this, from Isḥāq ibn Manṣūr, from Muḥammad ibn Rāshid, from Jaʿfar ibn ʿAmr ibn Umayyah, who said: “Qubayṣah ibn Dhuʾayb was a teacher in a school.” (Salamah) ibn al-Faḍl al-Anṣārī al-Abraṣ. Al-Mahdī appointed him judge of al-Rayy. (ʿAbd al-Karīm) ibn Umayyah ibn Abī al-Mukhāriq, a teacher. Ḥammād ibn Salamah related from him. (Ṣāliḥ) ibn Kaysān. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān attached him to his son ʿUmar. When al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik became caliph, he ordered ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, who was his governor in Medina, to send Ṣāliḥ to him. He did so. Ṣāliḥ was assigned to his son ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Umm al-Banīn, daughter of ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān. (ʿAlqamah) ibn Abī ʿAlqamah al-Madanī. Mālik ibn Anas and Muḥammad ibn Isḥāq related from him. He related from his mother, from ʿĀʾishah; and he was a teacher.

(Abū ʿUbaydah) ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAmmār ibn Yāsir was tutor to ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik after Ṣāliḥ ibn Kaysān.

(ʿAwn) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUtbah ibn Masʿūd was tutor to Ayyūb ibn Sulaymān ibn ʿAbd al-Malik. One day he came to him, but Ayyūb kept him waiting outside. ʿAwn then stopped visiting him. Ayyūb grew angry with him, so ʿAwn went to him and reproached him, and Ayyūb reproached him in turn. ʿAwn went in to Sulaymān and said: “You have saddled me with a man who, if I visit him, keeps me waiting; if I stop visiting him, he gets angry; and if I reproach him, he reproaches me.”

(al-Mundhir) al-Afṭas al-Ṣanʿānī. And (Maymūn) ibn Mihrān, tutor to the sons of ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz.

(ʿAṭāʾ) ibn Abī Rabāḥ.

(al-Ḥasan) ibn ʿArafah.

He related from al-Mubārak ibn Saʿīd, brother of Sufyān al-Thawrī, and from Khalaf ibn Khalīfah. (ʿUbaydah) ibn Ḥumayd al-Ḥadhdhāʾ al-Naḥwī. He was tutor to Muḥammad ibn al-Rashīd, Commander of the Faithful. Abū Tawbah al-Naḥwī, Maymūn ibn Ḥafṣ, informed me that ʿUbaydah taught Muḥammad until he reached Sūrat al-Ḥadīd, whereupon al-Rashīd awarded him seventy thousand. ʿUbaydah died a few days after receiving it. (ʿAbd al-Raḥmān) ibn Hurmuz. (Shaybah) ibn Niṣāḥ.

Muʿāwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān appointed (Daghfal), the genealogist, as tutor to his son Yazīd.

(Abū ʿAbd Allāh) al-Muʿallim.

(Maymūn) ibn Abī Shurāʿah. Shuʿbah, Yazīd ibn Zurayʿ, and others related from him.

(Isḥāq) ibn Abī Isrāʾīl. He taught a group in Baṣrah at the gate of Ḥammād ibn Zayd. Al-Qawārīrī mentioned him.

Names of notable people who were crucified

The Messenger of God ﷺ killed (ʿUqbah) ibn Abī Muʿayṭ ibn Abī ʿAmr ibn Umayyah ibn ʿAbd Shams at ʿIrq al-Ẓabyah on his return from Badr. He ordered that he be crucified and so he was the first person crucified in Islam. This is related on the authority of Abū ʿAbd Allāh al-Qaṣṣāb.

On the day of al-Rajīʿ, Hudhayl captured (Kh ubayb) ibn ʿAdī al-Anṣārī and (Ibn al-Dathinah) al-Anṣārī, after giving them a guarantee of safety and pledging not to betray them. Once they were in their hands, they betrayed them and sold them to Quraysh, who crucified them at al-Tanʿīm. May God have mercy upon them.

Ziyād ibn Abīhi crucified (Muslim) ibn Zaymar and (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Nujayy, both Ḥaḍramīs, at their respective doorways for several days in Kūfah. They were both Shīʿīs. This was by order of Muʿāwiyah. Al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī ﵄ counted them among the charges against Muʿāwiyah in his letter to him: “Are you not the one responsible for Ḥujr and the two Ḥaḍramīs, concerning whom Ibn Sumayyah wrote to you that they followed ʿAlī’s religion and opinion? You wrote to him: ‘Kill and mutilate anyone who follows ʿAlī’s religion and opinion.’ So he killed them and mutilated them at your command. The religion of ʿAlī, and the cousin of ʿAlī—the one on whose account your father used to beat your father, and on whose account your father gave you the seat you now occupy. Had it not been for that, the greatest distinction of you and your father would have been enduring the two journeys whose burden God lifted from you—” This was in a lengthy letter in which he rebuked him for claiming Ziyād as his own and appointing him governor over the two Iraqs.

Khālid ibn al-Walīd crucified (ʿAqqah) ibn Jashm ibn Hilāl al-Namrī at ʿAyn al-Tamr while on his way to Syria. He took its women and children captive and killed its fighting men. Among those captives were Sīrīn, father of Muḥammad ibn Sīrīn, and Ḥumrān, client of ʿUthmān.

ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Marjānah crucified (Hāniʾ) ibn ʿUrwah al-Murādī in the market of Kūfah, and (Muslim) ibn ʿAqīl as well. Muslim ibn ʿAqīl ibn Abī Ṭālib had been hiding with him when al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī ﵄ sent him.

He also crucified (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn ʿAfīf al-Azdī, then al-Ghāmidī, at al-Sabkhah in Kūfah. His story was that when ʿUbayd Allāh had overcome al-Ḥusayn and his family ﵃ he ascended the pulpit and said: “Praise be to God, who has made the truth prevail and granted victory to the Commander of the Faithful, Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiyah, and his party over Ḥusayn and his followers.”

This ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAfīf sprang up. His left eye had been lost on the day of the Camel while he was with ʿAlī ﵁ and the other had been lost on the day of Ṣiffīn. He used to remain in the congregational mosque, praying there until nightfall. He said: “Ibn Marjānah, you and your father are liars, as are the man who appointed you and his father. You kill the sons of prophets and speak as the truthful do?” Ibn Ziyād had him brought to him and said: “Enemy of God, what do you say about ʿUthmān?” He replied: “I say of him that he was a man who did good and did evil, who set things right and corrupted them. God is the master of His servants, and judges ʿUthmān and others with truth and justice. But if you wish, ask me about yourself, your father, Yazīd, and his father.” He said: “I will not ask you— “Until death [uncertain reading]—” He said: “I had prayed to God to grant me martyrdom, before your mother bore you, at the hands of the creature of God most hostile to Him and most hateful to Him.

“When I lost my sight, I despaired of it. Praise be to God, who granted it to me after I had despaired, and showed me that my prayer had been answered.”

Al-Ḥajjāj ibn Yūsuf crucified (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn al-Zubayr upside down in Mecca during the caliphate / of ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr crucified his brother (ʿAmr) ibn al-Zubayr in Mecca, then took him down. During the governorship of Muṣʿab ibn al-Zubayr over Iraq, al-Qāsim ibn Muḥammad ibn al-Ashʿath al-Kindī crucified (Mazīd) and (ʿAbd Allāh), the sons of Khayrān ibn Jābir, of Banū Ḥanjūr ibn Jundub. They had claimed that they killed Muḥammad ibn al-Ashʿath along with al-Mukhtār. He slaughtered them in the cemetery of Kinda and crucified them.

Al-Ḥantaf ibn al-Sijif al-Tamīmī crucified (Ḥubaysh) ibn Dulja al-Qaynī at al-Rabadhah. He was Marwān’s governor over Medina, while Ibn al-Zubayr was in Mecca. He and al-Ḥantaf met at al-Rabadhah, where al-Ḥantaf killed him and routed his army. ʿAbd Allāh was then under siege in Mecca, during the first siege.

They exhumed (Misrif) ibn ʿUqbah al-Murrī, the commander at the Battle of al-Ḥarrah, [who] had been sent to Mecca to fight Ibn al-Zubayr, on behalf of Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiyah, and crucified him at al-Mushallal.

During the caliphate of Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, Maslamah ibn ʿAbd al-Malik crucified (Yazīd) ibn al-Muhallab ibn Abī Ṣufrah at the Bridge of Babylon, and hung a pig, a fish, and a wineskin beside him.

Al-Ḥajjāj ibn Yūsuf crucified (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn al-Jārūd, (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Ḥakīm al-Mujāshiʿī, and (al-Hudhayl) ibn ʿImrān ibn al-Fuḍayl al-Burjumī at Rustaqābādh.

Ziyād ibn al-Muhallab crucified (al-Khayyār) ibn Sabrah al-Mujāshiʿī in Oman. Al-Ḥajjāj had appointed him governor there, and he oppressed the Azd. When Yazīd rebelled, he appointed his brother Ziyād over Oman.

So Ziyād crucified al-Khayyār there.

During the caliphate of Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar al-Thaqafī crucified (Zayd) ibn ʿAlī ibn al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib in al-Kūfah, at al-Kunāsah. Crucified with him were (Muʿāwiyah) ibn Isḥāq ibn Zayd ibn Jāriyah ibn ʿĀmir ibn Mujmaʿ ibn al-ʿAṭṭāf ibn Ḍubayʿah ibn Zayd ibn Mālik ibn ʿAwf / ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿAwf ibn Mālik ibn al-Aws al-Anṣārī, and (Naṣr) ibn Judhaymah al-ʿAbsī, who had commanded Zayd’s right wing. He too was exhumed and crucified with him. It was because of Zayd that the Rāfiḍah got their name. They had pledged allegiance to him ﵁ but later put him to the test.

He declared his loyalty to Abū Bakr and ʿUmar, so they repudiated him. That day they were called “Rāfiḍah” (the repudiators).

During the caliphate of Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Qasrī crucified (al-Mughīrah) ibn Saʿīd al-Bajalī, then al-Aḥmasī, who had rebelled against him in al-Kūfah, calling people to follow Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥasan and claiming that he was the Mahdī. He overcame him and his followers. He crucified al-Mughīrah and burned his followers in fire. They are the ones called the Mughīriyyah.

(Yahyā) ibn Zayd ibn ʿAlī ibn al-Ḥusayn ibn ʿAlī rebelled against [al-Walīd] ibn Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik. He had fled to Khurāsān during Hishām’s time, and continued moving from place to place among its districts until al-Walīd became caliph. Naṣr ibn Sayyār was then in charge of Khurāsān, and he dispatched Salm ibn Aḥwaz al-Māzinī, of Banū Tamīm, to fight him.

Near Herat, Sūrah ibn [Muḥammad] ibn ʿAzīz al-Kindī killed him and crucified him at al-Jūzjān, in one of its arches. Opposite him, in another arch, he crucified an Arab man called (Maṭar) ibn Muṭarrif, or Muṭarrif ibn Maṭar, with a road running between the two.

He remained crucified until Abū Muslim came, took him down, concealed his body, led the prayer over him, and buried him. Abū Muslim then seized everyone who had gone out to fight him. He reviewed the register and looked at everyone who had been in the expedition, killing them except those he could not catch. The people of Khurāsān wore black garments in mourning for him, and this became their distinctive dress.

After fighting him, Naṣr ibn Sayyār crucified (Judayʿ) ibn ʿAlī al-Kirmānī al-Azdī. He fought him for a long time, so Naṣr made peace with him. When Judayʿ felt secure, Naṣr betrayed him and crucified him on the parade ground at Marw.

The people of Oman crucified (al-Qāsim) ibn Saʿr al-Saʿdī. Al-Ḥajjāj sent his brother Mujāʿah ibn Saʿr against them. When he arrived, he found his brother crucified. His companions wanted to take him down, but Mujāʿah refused and ravaged their territory; afterward, he took him down.

Marwān ibn Muḥammad ibn Marwān crucified (al-Aṣbagh) ibn Dhuʾālah al-Kalbī at Ḥimṣ, and crucified his companions with him.

Marwān also crucified (Yazīd) ibn Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Qasrī at the Gate of al-Farādīs in Damascus. The man who killed him was Zāmil ibn ʿAmr al-Judhamī; Marwān was in Palestine.

Marwān also crucified (al-Simṭ) ibn Thābit ibn Yazīd ibn Sharāḥīl al-Kindī.

While in Palestine, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-ʿAbbās killed eighty men from the Banū Umayyah, including (al-Ghamr) ibn Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, in a single gathering by the River Abū Fuṭrus. Ḥafṣ al-Umawī said this elegy for them:

Where are the two peaks of ʿAbd Shams? Where are they?

Where are those of breadth and noble lineage?

Tell whoever asks about them: they are

Corpses gleaming above the wood.

Milk all you wish in your courtyard;

You will be made to drink the dregs of that milk.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī crucified (ʿAmr) ibn Suhayl ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān, when ʿAbd Allāh captured Damascus. Marwān ibn Muḥammad had left al-Walīd ibn Muʿāwiyah ibn Marwān ibn ʿAbd al-Malik as governor there when he departed for Palestine.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAlī killed him and captured (Yazīd) ibn Muʿāwiyah ibn Marwān ibn ʿAbd al-Malik and (ʿAbd al-Jabbār) ibn Yazīd ibn Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān. He sent them to Abū al-ʿAbbās, Commander of the Faithful, in the year in which the oath of allegiance was given to him. He killed them and crucified them at al-Ḥīrah.

/ Commander of the Faithful Abū al-ʿAbbās also killed (Sulaymān) ibn Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik and his two sons after granting them safe conduct. His wife Umm Salamah had interceded with him on their behalf. When Sudayf ibn Maymūn urged him in a poem, he killed them and crucified them.

He also crucified (Sulaymān) ibn Ḥabīb ibn al-Muhallab ibn Abī Ṣufrah and (Sulaymān) ibn Salm ibn Kaysān al-Kalbī. The latter had been among those who fought Zayd ibn ʿAlī alongside Yūsuf ibn ʿUmar.

Abū Jaʿfar al-Manṣūr crucified (ʿAbd al-Jabbār) ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Azdī. He had appointed him governor of Khurāsān, but he rebelled. Al-Manṣūr overcame him and crucified him in al-Kūfah.

Al-Manṣūr also crucified (Khālid) ibn ʿUthmān ibn Khālid ibn al-Zubayr.

He had rebelled with Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ḥasan, but was overcome.

Shaykh ibn ʿUmayrah al-Asadī crucified (Abū Sārah) al-Sāmī, who had rebelled against al-Manṣūr.

Al-Mahdī crucified (Yūsuf) al-Narm, who had rebelled in Khurāsān.

He overcame him and crucified him in Baghdad.

Asad ibn al-Marzubān, al-Manṣūr’s commander, crucified (al-Mughīrah) ibn al-Qazaʿ al-Saʿdī in al-Baṣrah. He had rebelled with Ibrāhīm ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ḥasan.

Al-Rashīd crucified (Jaʿfar) ibn Yaḥyā ibn Khālid ibn Barmak al-Bahrānī in Baghdad, cut him into three pieces, then joined his head to the upper part of his body, leaving it in two pieces, and then burned him.

Al-Rashīd also crucified (al-Simṭ) ibn Thābit ibn Zayd ibn Sharāḥīl al-Kindī. He had rebelled.

His son (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn al-Simṭ was crucified by ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿīd al-Ḥarashī in Ḥimṣ.

(Al-Hayṣam) al-Hamdānī was crucified in al-Raqqah. He had rebelled in Yemen, and Ḥammād al-Barbarī fought him, captured him, his son, and his nephew, and crucified them all.

(Tamrūn) and his two sons were also crucified in al-Raqqah, on the timber of Ibn Abī Shaykh.

/ He was a king among the kings of Khurāsān. He rebelled, and al-Ḥarashī captured him.

The people of Oman crucified (ʿĪsā) ibn Jaʿfar ibn Sulaymān ibn ʿAlī.

Al-Rashīd had appointed him governor of Oman. He set out with the people of al-Baṣrah, who committed sexual outrages against women along their route and robbed them. When the people of Oman heard of this, they fought ʿĪsā and prevented him from entering their land. They overcame him and crucified him, and they defied the sultan and refused to give him their allegiance.

Ṭāhir ibn al-Ḥusayn ibn Muṣʿab crucified (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Khālid ibn Khārijah, of Banū Jaḥwān ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥabīb ibn ʿAmr ibn Shaybān ibn Muḥārib ibn Fihr al-Fihrī, at the Gate of al-Anbār, while he was fighting al-Amīn.

Al-Maʾmūn crucified (Yaḥyā) ibn ʿĀmir ibn Ismāʿīl al-Muslī in Khurāsān.

He had entered upon him with Harthamah ibn Aʿyan. He spoke harshly to him [and said]: “You are only the commander of the Magians.” Al-Maʾmūn killed him and imprisoned Harthamah until he died.

Al-Ḥasan ibn S[ahl] al-Sarī ibn Manṣūr al-Shaybānī, known as Abū al-Sarāyā, was crucified. He had rebelled in al-Kūfah, calling people to support the descendants of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁. He fled from Harthamah when al-Kūfah was taken. Ḥammād al-Kandagūsh seized him and sent him to al-Ḥasan ibn Sahl, who killed him and crucified him in Baghdad in two pieces: one in the eastern quarter and one in the western quarter.

Al-Maʾmūn crucified (Ibrāhīm) ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Wahhāb ibn Ibrāhīm al-Imām, who was known by the name of his grandmother, ʿĀʾishah bint Sulaymān ibn ʿAlī. He crucified him in Baghdad for part of a day and then took him down. He had ridden out to him at night and killed him in the prison. Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm al-Ifrīqī al-Sadūsī was killed with him.

During al-Maʾmūn’s reign, Abū Isḥāq al-Muʿtaṣim crucified (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Ḥalbas al-Hilālī and (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Khālid al-Judhamī on the Bridge of Miṣr, in Egypt. (ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz) ibn al-Wazīr ibn Janābī, who was al-Jarawī, was crucified in Egypt.

Al-Maʾmūn crucified (Yaḥyā) ibn ʿAdī and (ʿAbd al-Ḥamīd) ibn ʿAttāb, both of the Lakhm, in Egypt. They had rebelled there.

Al-Muʿtaṣim crucified (al-Māziyār) al-Ṭabarī, (Bābak) at Sāmarrāʾ, and (ʿAbd Allāh), Bābak’s brother, in Baghdad. He sent their heads to Khurāsān.

Al-Wāthiq crucified (Aḥmad) ibn Naṣr ibn Mālik ibn al-Haytham al-Khuzāʿī at Sāmarrāʾ and displayed his head in Baghdad.

Notables whose heads were displayed

Al-Kalbī said, in what he was told: The Messenger of God ﷺ used to say to Quraysh: “What do you think: if I kill (Umm Qirfah), who was Fāṭimah daughter of Rabīʿah ibn Badr ibn ʿAmr ibn Juwayyah ibn Lawdhān, will you believe?” They would say, “Could such a thing happen?” When [she was killed] by Zayd ibn Ḥārithah al-Kalbī, the Messenger of God ﷺ ordered that her head be taken around Medina to make her death known. And the Messenger of God ﷺ spoke the truth. Her husband was Mālik ibn Ḥudhayfah. She bore him thirteen sons, all of whom had taken up the sword of leadership. She was formidable and incited people against the Messenger of God ﷺ. When a dispute arose among the Ghatafān, she would send her headcloth to be set up between them on a spear, and they would make peace.

Muʿāwiyah displayed the head of (ʿAmr) ibn al-Ḥamq al-Khuzāʿī, who was a Shīʿī.

His head was taken around the marketplace. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Umm al-Ḥakam had captured him in al-Jazīrah.

Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiyah displayed the head of (al-Ḥusayn) ﵁. Those killed with him were (al-ʿAbbās, Jaʿfar, ʿUthmān, ʿAbd Allāh, Muḥammad, and Abū Bakr), the sons of ʿAlī ibn Ṭālib ﵃; (Abū Bakr) ibn al-Ḥasan; (al-Qāsim and ʿAbd Allāh), the two sons of al-Ḥasan; (ʿAlī and ʿAbd Allāh), the two sons of al-Ḥusayn; (ʿAbd Allāh, Jaʿfar, and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān), the sons of ʿAqīl ibn Abī Ṭālib; (Muḥammad) ibn Abī Saʿīd ibn ʿAqīl; (Muslim) ibn ʿAqīl; and (Muḥammad and ʿAwn), the two sons of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar ﵃. Their heads were carried to Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiya, who displayed them in Syria. He sent al-Ḥusayn’s head ﵁ to be displayed in Medina.

Al-Mukhtār ibn Abī ʿUbayd displayed the head of (ʿUbayd Allāh) ibn Marjāna, the head of (al-Ḥuṣayn) ibn Numayr al-Saksakī, and the head of (Shurayḥbīl) ibn Dhī al-Kalāʿ al-Ḥimyarī. Ibrāhīm ibn al-Ashtar had killed them on the day of al-Ḥāzir and sent their heads to him. He sent their heads to Ibn al-Ḥanafiyya, and they were displayed at the gate of the Sacred Mosque. Ibn al-Ḥanafiyya came out from circumambulating the Kaʿba, saw them displayed, and praised and glorified God. Those who brought the heads to him on al-Mukhtār’s behalf were: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿUmayr al-[Thaʿlabī]¹, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Shaddād al-Jushamī, al-Sāʾib ibn Mālik al-Ashʿarī, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Bakr al-Ṭāʾī.²

When Muṣʿab ibn al-Zubayr killed (al-Mukhtār), he sent his head to ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr, who displayed it at the gate of the Sacred Mosque. An iron nail was driven into al-Mukhtār’s hand beside the mosque—the Mosque of Kūfa. It remained nailed there until al-Ḥajjāj arrived, saw it, and asked about it. He was informed about it, and ordered it removed.

Muṣʿab ibn al-Zubayr displayed in Kūfa the head of (ʿUbayd Allāh) ibn al-Ḥurr al-Juʿfī.

ʿAbd al-Malik displayed the head of (Ibrāhīm) ibn al-Ashtar al-Nakhaʿī and the head of (Yaḥyā) ibn Jaʿda ibn Hubayra ibn Abī Wahb al-Makhzūmī.

ʿAbd al-Malik displayed the head of (Muṣʿab) in Egypt. Then he brought it back and displayed it in Damascus. ʿĀtika daughter of Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiya took it, washed it, embalmed it, and buried it.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān displayed the head of (ʿUmayr) ibn al-Ḥubāb al-Sulamī / in Damascus.

Al-Walīd ibn Yazīd displayed the head of (Yaḥyā) ibn Zayd ibn al-Ḥubāb al-Sulamī / in Damascus.

Al-Walīd ibn Yazīd displayed the head of (Yaḥyā) ibn Zayd ibn ʿAlī¹ ﵁. Naṣr ibn Sayyār had sent him the head from Khurāsān.

Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik displayed the head of (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr. Bishr ibn Ṣafwān al-Kalbī had sent it to him from Ifrīqiya, accusing him of killing Yazīd ibn Abī Muslim, the client of al-Ḥajjāj ibn Yūsuf.

Yazīd al-Nāqiṣ displayed the head of (al-Walīd) ibn Yazīd in the Damascus Mosque, on the side facing the Bāb al-Farādīs. He also displayed the head of (Yūsuf) ibn ʿUmar al-Thaqafī in Damascus.

Abū al-ʿAbbās, Commander of the Faithful, displayed the head of (Marwān) ibn Muḥammad ibn Marwān in Kūfa.

Al-Hādī displayed the head of (Daḥya) ibn al-Muʿaṣṣib ibn al-Aṣbagh ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān. He had been [killed] in Egypt, and his head was displayed in Baghdad.¹

Mūsā ibn ʿĪsā ibn Mūsā killed (al-Ḥusayn) ibn ʿAlī ibn Ḥasan ibn Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī at Fakh during the pilgrimage season. Al-Hādī displayed his head in Baghdad on the bridge. Then he sent it to Khurāsān. His mother was Zaynab daughter of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ḥasan.

Ṭāhir ibn al-Ḥusayn ibn Muṣʿab displayed the head of (Muḥammad) ibn Hārūn al-Amīn in Baghdad, at the gate of the garden of Muʾnisa. Then he sent it to al-Maʾmūn in Khurāsān, where al-Maʾmūn displayed it.

Al-Maʾmūn sent to al-Ḥasan ibn Sahl, who was at the mouth of al-Ṣulḥ in Khurāsān, the head of (ʿAlī) ibn Abī Saʿīd, the head of (ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz) ibn ʿImrān al-Ṭāʾī, the head of (Khalaf) the Egyptian, and the head of (Muʾnis) the merchant.

He accused them of responsibility for al-Faḍl ibn Sahl’s death. Al-Ḥasan ibn Sahl displayed them there.

Al-Maʾmūn displayed in Baghdad the heads of (ʿAlī) and (al-Ḥusayn), the two sons of Hishām. He had sent them from Adhana.

Al-Wāthiq displayed the head of (Aḥmad) ibn Naṣr ibn Mālik al-Khuzāʿī in Baghdad.

Al-Mutawakkil displayed the head of (Isḥāq) ibn Ismāʿīl al-Tiflīsī in Baghdad, at the Bāb al-ʿĀmma. Bughā the Elder had sent him from Armenia.

The Fugitives¹

Among them was (ʿAbd Allāh) ibn Muṭīʿ ibn al-Aswad al-ʿAdawī. He had fled from the army of Muslim ibn ʿUqba al-Murrī on the Day of al-Ḥarra. When al-Ḥajjāj ibn Yūsuf besieged ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr in Mecca, he fought al-Ḥajjāj’s troops and said:

I am he who fled on the Day of al-Ḥarra,and a free man flees only once.I shall surely follow flight with a charge;how fine the charge after the flight!

He continued fighting until he was killed.

(Zufar) ibn al-Ḥārith al-Kilābī fled on the day of Marj Rā[hiṭ]¹, leaving behind his father [? his son] Wakīʿ and his client Mishkān. They were killed. He said of this:

Should one single day, if I fled it, outweighmy good days and my fine deeds?No misfortune had befallen me before this—my flight, and the killing of my companion and my retainer.²At evening I rode through the villages, seeing no onethere but a guardian or a protector.

(Ḥabīb) ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Aʿlam al-Hudhalī, brother of Ṣakhr al-Ghayy, of Banū Kināna, fled and said:

When I saw the people onthe high ground, below the lofty summits,I was overcome with fear, and neithercould I shoot nor bid a companion farewell.They urge their companion against usfervently, and I urge him on without deceit.I urge Abū Wahb to make themfail, while they stretched out the milk camels,like a man shouting to cattle, with the pledgeof pasturing them each evening away from the herd./ He urges Judhayma, and the mount,as if he were on a high-humped camel, a traveler.Lean as the stalk of the lote tree, itoutstrips the wild asses and the noble camels.

A broad-backed, dark-gray mare appeared to him,whose loved ones were in al-Baḍīʿ.I feared the blow of a small spear,though I had endured every trial,lest I become their prey upon itand fall to famished hyenasas carrion, and to birds of prey,wolves, and foxes.A swift she-wolf would dragmy flesh to her lair, amid thorny thickets,black, sleek wolves, as thoughtheir skins were a monk’s robes.When they are close, their earsare like the ears of a victim.They strip a man’s skin,as a smith strips away the old layers.I turned my eyes toward the Ḥijāz,toward people renowned for noble deeds,and remembered my family in the open countryand the need of the disheveled, wandering women—those deprived of their ancestral wealth,who look toward their kinsmen.

(Tamīm) ibn Asad al-Khuzāʿī, of Banū Nufātha, fled. His wife reproached him, for he had left her brother behind, and he was killed. He said:

She reproached me; had she witnessed it, her rebukewould have been like urine wetting the joints of a sandal.God knows that I left him,though my heart was at ease. Ask my companions.

When I saw Banū Nufātha coming,rushing at every swift, lean horse,/ I remembered an old blood-debt they held,from bygone ages in the distant past.I knew¹ that whoever they caught would be brought downas carrion for the ravening beast and the eagle’s young.I smelled the breath of death coming toward them,and feared the blow of a sharp Indian sword.²I raised a leg that I did not fear would stumble,and cast my clothes across my bare back.

Banū Ḍamra ibn Bakr raided Banū Jusham ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Tamīm ibn Saʿd ibn Hudhayl. The latter were warned of them, so they went out in pursuit and killed them all except (Ḥuṣayb) al-Ḍamrī. He escaped at a run. When he returned, his wife reproached and taunted him. She said, “I see you are unscathed, while your people have been struck down!” He said— Ḥuṣayb al-Ḍamrī:

Khalīda said when I came to her at night:“This is Ḥuṣayb, unscathed, who was not struck down.”What oath did she swear to frighten me with—white swords, ornamented with sword-belts?

Ḥuṣayb also said:

When I recognized the Banū ʿAmr and their allies,I knew I was their quarry in this encounter.I raised my leg, turning aside from no one,like a solitary wild man who turns his back.I escape to the plain, but escape to no one,as though my garment, from what I sought to avoid, were torn to pieces.Alas for me—and alas, of no avail—and is there any refuge from God’s decree?A people, no cowards, whom the calamities of deathhave afflicted, and a time that has no end.

They were the secret treasure of my heart, but I lost them;every hoarded provision has a bitter end.I met Khuthaym, then Mulaytha,like lions, with felt cloaks on their shoulders.Alas for my mother—a grief no lie—for the noble band of Ḍamra, when they perished.Were it not that sorrow is widespread among people,my liver would split whenever I remembered them.

(Ḥabbān) ibn al-Ḥakam ibn Mālik ibn Khālid ibn Ṣakhr ibn al-Sharīd, who in the pre-Islamic period was called “the Fugitive,” fled from Banū ʿAwf. He said:

When I saw Banū ʿAwf and their horsesannouncing Bishr with a cry, not a cheer,I urged her on, then let the reins go,as though she were a palm shoot, withered and trailing.I [urged] her on, the friend; I turned aside from no one,and neither my urging nor my speech could be heard by them.

[He said]¹ also:

How many horsemen have I met with horsemen,until, when the two forces joined, my hand reached them;I left them with the spears piercing their backs,some slain, and others propped upright.Will it benefit me that their women say,when I have killed the last of the fugitives, “May you not be far away”?

It is said that the Messenger of God ﷺ said to Banū Sulaym: “Choose your leader according to the custom you had in the pre-Islamic period, and appoint over you as leader the one who used to lead you in the pre-Islamic period.” A man from Banū Sulaym stood and said, “Messenger of God, the Fugitive ibn the Fugitive used to lead us.” It was as if the Messenger of God ﷺ disliked this. He repeated it to them. The Sulamī said those words a second time. He repeated it, and the man replied to him a third time, saying, “Ḥabbān ibn al-Ḥakam used to lead us.”

[ʿAmr ibn Jaʿda]² al-Khuzāʿī and ʿUmayr ibn Jaʿda, of Banū Liḥyān, fled on the day of Khushāsh, below Waddān. His wife reproached him. He said:

Umayma turned away from me—this was no time for turning away— and warned my companions that it was time to depart.

O Umayma, do you know how many companions I parted from on the day of Khushāsh, none of them weak?

He rejoices when the smoke of cooking is welcome and they are stricken by drought, among the tribe, unlike Kabinna ʿAlfūf.23

The drinking companion is given drink when he forgets his fellows, like the child’s mother in her enveloping garment.24

When I saw them, it was as though their arrows, at al-Jizʿ by Naqrī al-Ḥibā, were autumn rain.

I knew that whoever they caught would be left to the hyena, or would spend the summer in the worst of summer places.

I was certain nothing could save one from them except to meet the ordained end of every pastern.25

I lifted a leg, unafraid it would stumble: escape belongs to the fearful one who is swift.26

Whenever I saw a shape before me, I thought it a man, then it whirled like the blur of a spinning top.

Al-Ḥārith b. Hishām b. al-Mughīrah fled on the day of Badr, and Ḥassān b. Thābit said:

If what you told me is false, then may you have escaped as al-Ḥārith b. Hishām escaped:

He left his loved ones rather than fight to defend them, and escaped with his head on a tall mare, with bridle.

He befouled the high-bred steeds in a barren place, like the passage of al-Damūk through Maḥṣad and Rijām.27

The two breaches were crushed by him, and it laid him down, while his loved ones came to rest in the worst of places.

Had flight and her gallop not been there, she would have left him as prey for beasts of prey, and birds of prey would have stained him.28

Praise be to God alone. Its copying was completed on the eleventh of Jumādā al-Ākhirah, in the year seven hundred and fifty-one.
